# Backlog

Maintained by the loop of [LOOP.md](LOOP.md). Ordered by priority; `ready` items first.
Statuses: `ready`, `in_progress`, `done <hash>`, `blocked: <reason>`, `parked`,
`dropped: <reason>`. Ids are monotonic and never reused.

### B-373 — `weida-http`: a reset stream frees no place in the HTTP/2 accept hand-off
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 3ae9464 | needs: [B-367]
acceptance: the HTTP/2 server driver refuses a new stream with REFUSED_STREAM while `max_concurrent_streams` exchanges wait for the accept queue, so a client that opens and resets streams while the application is not accepting grows `pending_exchanges` to at most that number; a unit test over `tokio::io::duplex` (a raw peer opens and resets 3 × the limit with nobody accepting, then sees REFUSED_STREAM for every stream past the limit); INVARIANTS and `docs/libraries/http.md` §5 (hyper's `max_pending_accept_reset_streams`) and §9 name it. Found in the review pass after B-370: RST_STREAM removes a stream from `streams`, so the admission check no longer counts its exchange, which stays queued in the driver while the application's accept queue is full.

### B-379 — A `send` into an outbox nobody will drain fails instead of hanging
kind: code | size: 20 | status: done 5842ff1 | needs: []
acceptance: once every address of a `Pusher`/dialling `Pair` is given up (`PeerEvent::GaveUp`), `send` fails with `ConnectionLost(cause)` whether or not the outbox still holds bodies, as it already did on an empty outbox, and a sender blocked at the outbox bound (`OutboxFull::Block`) is woken with that error instead of waiting forever; bodies already queued stay for a later `connect` to drain. Test: unit tier, paused clock, a redial that gives up while a third `send` waits at a bound of two.

### B-380 — A swarm fetch costs linear time in its pieces, not quadratic
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 6430a09 | needs: []
acceptance: `swarm/schedule.rs` no longer scans every piece of the blob for every order: the scheduler keeps the free pieces (missing, no order fetching them) as a bit map with a count, a claim probes it at random and falls back to one pass over the free and HAVE maps only when the probes miss, keeps a seeder that held none of the free pieces out of the scan until a piece is freed again or it says HAVE anew, and takes endgame copies from the orders in flight; `Fetch::next` asks a count instead of reading the delivered map whole. 0040's rules stay: uniformly among free pieces the seeder holds, the endgame only when no piece is free, least-copied first. Tests: a seeded random walk over answered, failed and released orders, seeders gone and HAVE anew, checks after every step that a piece is free exactly when it is missing and unordered, then finishes the blob from one full seeder; a seeder with nothing free claims pieces once another seeder's orders fail.

### B-351 — A busy seeder makes a session wait instead of dropping it
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done d9fdd29 | needs: []
acceptance: `swarm/seed.rs` `read_sessions` takes a place before it takes the next request, so the 65th pending session waits in the route's queue (`endpoint_queue`) and then on its stream, as a replier's request does, instead of being dropped and read by its fetch as a version-1 or failed seeder; integration test `a_busy_seeder_makes_a_session_wait_rather_than_drop_it` (MAX_PENDING_SESSIONS + 16 fetches against a seeder that accepts late, every fetch completes, exactly one session per fetch); 0041 §4.2, INVARIANTS, PATTERNS §6.5 say "waits"; full gate green.

### B-338 — The owner's decisions on the hardening deviations
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done c7fe805 | needs: [B-331, B-330]
acceptance: weida-zmq's `ipc://` bind takes a live endpoint over as libzmq does, through `BoundUnixSocket::bind_replacing` (weida and weida-nng keep refusing a live listener); a broker consumer whose delivery stalls or fails at the transport has its connection closed with `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` through `weida::Consumer::close`, so its redial re-subscribes; the per-message overhead measured (about 1.7 KB while out for delivery, 136-240 B queued) and the constant left at 1024 by the owner.

### B-339 — Review pass over the hardening night, and its fixes
kind: review | size: 90 | status: done 8a8b1ee | needs: [B-334]
acceptance: four read-only reviews of a9b7b6f..e6ccb07 (broker/raft 8 findings, client drivers 11, weida 9, zmq/nng 7, none critical) and every finding fixed with a regression test: the broker tells a per-message refusal from a transport failure (`MAX_REFUSALS`), cancels a requeued delivery's watcher, shares admissions per connection (`ConnectionId` on incoming streams) and scans only what is new to a subscription; raft refuses engine RPCs from learners, checks URL length and membership size atomically and finishes an abandoned joint config; NATS reports recoverable `-ERR`s (`Connection::refusals`) instead of closing; AMQP link operations are cancel-safe and a final outcome cannot be evicted; MQTT bounds `stalled` and refuses an authenticator without a method; weida holds a local transfer's place until its drain ends, decodes headers inside their slot, charges segment budget before sending; zmq counts IPv6 per /64 and judges slicing by the real allocation; a dialling NNG socket flushes on drop.
note: done in 1eb20cf..8a8b1ee. Gate green on the tip: fmt, clippy (workspace and seven feature runs), 2515 workspace tests (0 failed, 39 ignored), the four blocking runs (465, 327, 207, 187), ten doc runs; 371 s after a `cargo clean`.

### B-334 — Python bindings: no interpreter freeze, no lost result, no secret in a repr
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done e6ccb07 | needs: [B-325, B-326, B-327, B-328]
acceptance: findings #1, #3, #4, #5, #7, #8, #11-#14, #17, #19 of the 2026-10-15 Python binding audit, for `weida-py-core` and the mqtt, nats, nng and amqp bindings: no getter takes a lock with the GIL held, a value computed for a cancelled asyncio task is re-stashed rather than dropped where it consumed a message, completions reach the loop without a reactor worker blocking on the GIL, unbounded blocking waits are interruptible by signals, Python-supplied durations and counts raise instead of panicking, one `Slot`/`Lease` in `weida-py-core`; each binding's `develop.sh` suite green.
note: done in part at the session's time box: #1, #4, #8, #12, #13, #17, #19 fixed; #3 restores a consumed value in nats, mqtt, amqp (async) and nng (PULL, SUB, PAIR, BUS; the state-machine sockets document why not); #7 for nats, mqtt, amqp; #11 everywhere except mqtt's `disconnect`, `ping`, `reauthenticate`; #5 for amqp's async and sync `send`. #14 not started; the rest moved to B-335. Python suites ran in private venvs. Rust gate for the seven PyO3 crates green: clippy, 31 tests, rustdoc.

### B-335 — weida-py and weida-zmq-py: the binding findings left for after the library merges
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done b3dda8e | needs: [B-334]
acceptance: audit findings #2 (`SyncRuntime.bind` holds its mutex across `py.detach`), #9 (`CurveKey` repr, hash and equality leak or compare a secret key), #10 (zmq sync sockets hold their mutex across a blocking `recv`, so `close` from another thread hangs), #11 for weida-py and zmq-py and mqtt's `disconnect`/`ping`/`reauthenticate` (the `interruptible` helper of B-334), #14 (`weida_mqtt::Message::payload` as `Bytes`), #15, #16 (`IncomingStream.read(max_bytes)` allocates `max_bytes` up front and aborts on a huge value), #18 (poison-tolerant locks), #3 for amqp's sync surface, and zmq-py moved onto `weida-py-core`'s `Lease`; both `develop.sh` suites green.
note: `CurveKey` redacted, keyed hash, constant-time equality; `SyncRuntime` on an `RwLock` taken inside the released section; zmq sync `close()` wakes a parked call; `interruptible` in weida-py, zmq-py and the three mqtt calls; `weida_mqtt::Message::payload` is `Bytes`; datagrams carry `Bytes`; `IncomingStream.read` reads pieces of at most 64 KiB; poison-tolerant locks in weida-py; amqp's sync `next_delivery` polled on the calling thread; zmq-py on `weida-py-core`'s lease.

### B-336 — The leftovers of the hardening pass, each with its failure
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 7a0a709 | needs: []
acceptance: (1) `weida::Pem::Bytes` holds its key bytes in a zeroizing buffer; (2) `weida-zmq` honours `ZMQ_TCP_KEEPALIVE*` (the rows are refused today, so an idle half-open TCP peer holds a slot until the heartbeat, which is off by default); (3) a zmq proxy gives each downstream peer a share of the upstream subscription table and forwards a prefix once however many subscribers hold it; (4) `weida-nats`: `close()` signals after the state change; (5) `weida-runtime`'s resolver: a connect deadline also ends the `getaddrinfo` it is waiting on, or the doc says the thread outlives it; (6) the raft tests B-330 did not write: stalled outsider refusals beside a served member, the scripted forward redirect, the `targets` prune, and a `TooManyNodes` integration test that does not hang; (7) `weida-nng::PeerKey::host` and `weida-zmq`'s `counted_address` are one function in `weida-core`; (8) `cargo clippy -p weida --no-default-features` without `--all-targets` fails on `identity.rs` `Kind::Ephemeral` never constructed, a pre-existing red the gate's `--all-targets` run hides.
note: done in dd9720f..7a0a709 with the review's unfiled notes: `Pem::Bytes` zeroizing; `MAX_OUTSTANDING_LOOKUPS` (64) bounds system lookups and every deadline's doc says the lookup thread outlives it; `Kind::Ephemeral` behind `generate`; `weida_core::host_key` shared by zmq and nng; `ZMQ_TCP_KEEPALIVE*` honoured (also from Python); a zmq proxy forwards a prefix once and gives each subscriber a share of the upstream table; NATS `close()` after the state change; the raft tests, plus `Error::ResponseTooLarge`, a snapshot over the leader's cap not streamed, a stale-vote answer read while the body is still written; the broker settles on `Processed` only when the offset covers the body. Gate green: 2553 workspace tests 0 failed 39 ignored, blocking 465/349/208/187, ten doc runs, 176 s.

### B-337 — Allocation work that needs a type redesign
kind: code | size: 90 | status: ready | needs: []
acceptance: `DataHeader` borrows its strings for encoding so a send builds no owned copy per field (Protocol audit #11); `OutgoingTransfer::finish` stops boxing quinn's `stopped()` future per message (WeidaConn audit #12); `weida-nats` publish takes `Bytes` so a payload is copied once into the write buffer; AMQP encodes a message straight into its frame buffer. Each measured before and after with the existing benches; numbers into IMPLEMENTATION.md.

### B-333 — zmq sockets: one peer snapshot, a prefix tree, a proxy that survives
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 7b171f5 | needs: []
acceptance: findings #1-#4 and #6-#14 of the 2026-10-15 `weida-zmq` sockets audit: the peer set is one `Arc` snapshot rebuilt on change and waits are allocation-free without lost wake-ups; XPUB never clones subscription tables per event and synthesizes unsubscribes only for what its application was handed; queue accounting charges frame and message overhead; a proxy counts and skips per-message refusals; subscription matching is a compressed prefix tree; a blocking send waits for room for its own size; `router_bind_identity_to_user` (opt-in); `SocketOptions` redacts `plain_password`.
note: done on a worktree branch (0352c8c) and merged. The merge also makes an XPUB's receive queue block whatever its options say, so a subscription notification is never dropped (session.rs awaits it on that peer's session since 497a735). Left: per-downstream quota and upstream dedup in the proxy (B-336).

### B-332 — weida fan-out: pruned registry, an index, concurrent waits
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4fa1e39 | needs: []
acceptance: findings of the 2026-10-15 weida pub/sub audit except the sequencer: `SubRegistry` holds a path only while a filter holds it; exact and `#`-ending filters are matched through an index; stalled subscribers are waited for together under one deadline; a refused copy does not end a subscription; sequence numbers per (publisher path, topic); `RuntimeConfig::receive_timeout` for the blocking receives and `weida serve --sink`; `SendHalf::write_chunk` so fan-out shares one payload; one subscriber budget per connection; a refused datagram flow backs off; the CLI caps `--framing length` and escapes topics; the handoff decoder bounds count, size and destinations.
note: done on a worktree branch (9f0cbff) and merged with B-329's conn.rs and config.rs.

### B-331 — zmq engine: linear decode, CURVE without leftovers, ZAP as libzmq asks
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 497a735 | needs: []
acceptance: the 19 findings of the 2026-10-15 ZMTP/engine audit: accept loops survive EMFILE, `Multipart::decode` is linear, a per-address peer cap and stalled-handshake eviction, a persistent heartbeat timer and writes in their own arm, 64 KiB handshake frames and 64 metadata properties, ZAP asked by libzmq 4.3.5's measured rule, `X-Local-Principal` cannot be posed by a PLAIN username, ZAP takes no socket slot, CURVE on `curve25519-dalek` and `crypto_secretbox` with every secret zeroized and small-order points refused, `Message` is `Bytes`-backed, batched writes; the `command` fuzz target compiles again and every zmtp target ran 120 s clean; libzmq interop 19 passed.
note: an ipc bind no longer steals a live endpoint (libzmq unlinks it): the runtime refuses a live listener since 074ffc9, and the zmq tests and parity row say so.

### B-330 — broker, raft, openbao: nobody waits on a peer
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 074ffc9 | needs: []
acceptance: the 2026-10-15 infrastructure audit: the broker's queue loop never awaits a peer (reads in their own task under a deadline and a minimum rate, one delivery writer per subscription, a stalled consumer dropped and its deliveries requeued), the delivery scan is no longer O(queue x subscriptions x unsettled), `max_unsettled_per_consumer`; raft refuses what followers would refuse, bounds refusals and snapshot buffering, needs a voter for membership changes and resolves redirects through its own membership; openbao sends no token across hosts, refuses `http://` unless allowed, caps response bodies, wraps secrets in a zeroizing `Secret`; the runtime's unix bind never shows the node in the umask's mode.
note: `PER_MESSAGE_OVERHEAD` went from 256 to 1024, which cuts a queue's message-count ceiling about four times.

### B-329 — weida connections: handshakes, headers, local peers, resumption
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done f1135d7 | needs: [B-324]
acceptance: the 2026-10-15 connection audit: QUIC Initials refused at `max_connections` and `max_handshakes`, a stateless Retry above `retry_above_handshakes`; frame heads read as they arrive under `header_timeout_ms`, at most `max_header_streams` at once; UNSUBSCRIBE releases only what it holds; no TLS 1.3 tickets and no server session storage, so withdrawn client trust is not bypassed by resumption; consumers recorded per filter; local connections hold a `max_connections` place, a preamble deadline and are torn down when the peer leaves; a second HELLO is a violation; redial backoff survives short-lived successes; `collect` reads without a 64 KiB scratch; connection ids are a process serial; every DATA, SUBSCRIBE, CREDIT and FLOW header is checked before it is written.

### B-328 — nng: one slow peer no longer stalls a socket or device
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 41a2482 | needs: []
acceptance: the 20 findings of the 2026-10-15 NNG audit: TLS handshakes off the accept loop under a deadline and `max_tls_handshakes`, REP/RESPONDENT drop a reply to a full pipe, `device()` pumps both directions independently, one pipe snapshot and socket-wide signals, `Bytes` bodies, a too-deep stack costs the message not the pipe, PUB and PUSH discard what peers write, `max_pipes_per_peer`, TCP keepalive; NNG interop 11 passed, weida-sp fuzz targets 60 s clean, `develop.sh` 56 passed.
note: REP dropping a reply at a full pipe is a decision, not a measured NNG behaviour; `docs/libraries/nng.md` says so.

### B-325 — nats: bound what a hostile server can make the client hold or wait for

### B-322 — Raft admission by membership, bounded RPC reads, and a stopped signal
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 57ad19e | needs: [B-224]
acceptance: [requirements/griasdi-raft-admission.md](requirements/griasdi-raft-admission.md) §5: once a node's effective membership is non-empty, every raft RPC whose proved key is not in it is refused with `Rejected` before a byte of it is read, and an uninitialized node still accepts engine RPCs from anyone; the length prefix, envelope and end-of-envelope check are read under `election_timeout_max`, a snapshot body under `install_snapshot_timeout`, the envelope allocated as it arrives, and `ConsensusConfig::max_concurrent_rpcs` handlers at most, one more refused; `Consensus::stopped()` resolves when the engine ended on its own. Tests in `server.rs` and `consensus.rs` named in the item's note.
note: filed from griasdi's proposal; griasdi-august binds its raft port on loopback until this ships.
note: done. `Ops::admission` reads `membership_state.effective()` through `with_raft_state` (`Member`, `Outsider`, `Uninitialized`); `server::handle` checks it before reading, bounds the envelope, snapshot body, forwarded operation and reply by deadlines, allocates the envelope as it arrives, and `serve` caps handlers with a semaphore and refuses the surplus inline; `ConsensusConfig::max_concurrent_rpcs` (1 to `Semaphore::MAX_PERMITS`); `Consensus::stopped()`. `admission` returns `Box<Fatal<_>>` because clippy's `result_large_err` refuses the 280-byte variant. Tests: `an_outsider_cannot_unseat_or_write_to_an_initialized_node`, `a_stalled_envelope_ends_at_its_deadline_and_the_cap_refuses_more` (`server.rs`), `a_store_failure_stops_the_engine_and_stopped_fires` (`consensus.rs`), and the rewritten `an_rpc_claiming_another_sender_is_refused`, now against an uninitialized node; acceptance 2 is covered by the existing `tests/cluster.rs` joiner and snapshot tests, which go through admission.

Test in `crates/weida/tests/radio.rs`: `an_empty_segment_arrives_as_an_empty_layer_zero` — a radio segment and a `Peer::segment`, each finished unwritten, reach their receiver with `layer == Some(0)` and an empty body.

### B-274 — Identity and trust as sources: ephemeral, static, files with reload, live rotation
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 3e6828b | needs: []
acceptance: [0032](decisions/0032-identity-sources-and-the-handoff.md) §4.1, §4.2, §4.4 in `crates/weida`: `IdentitySource`/`TrustSource` traits with a current value and a change signal; `Static`, `Ephemeral` (feature `generate`) and `Files` (bootstrap `0700`/`0600`, mtime reload at an interval) sources; `ServerTls::from_source` and `ClientTls::with_identity_source` alongside the value constructors, which become the static source; a rustls resolver on the binding that serves the rotated certificate to the next handshake with no re-bind; `IdentityEvent` on a bounded stream. Tests: a binding whose files are replaced under it serves the new certificate to the next connection and the old connection is unaffected; a key replaced on disk yields `KeyChanged` and a pinning client's redial reports `PeerChanged`; the bootstrap writes owner-only files and a second start loads them with the same fingerprint.
note: the owner's position, after 0031 made a stable key a precondition of the redial: the identity implementations belong in weida, the default stays ephemeral, and an application connects a store. The taxonomy in 0032 §2 is what decided the three sources — SPIFFE, ACME and cert-manager all end in a file somebody else writes, so a reloading file source covers them without a client for any of them.
note: done 3e6828b. `crates/weida/src/identity.rs`: `IdentitySource` and `TrustSource` as cheap-to-clone handles around a `RwLock`ed value, a generation counter and a 64-event broadcast; `ServerTls`/`ClientTls` now hold sources and take `impl Into<…>`, so every existing call site that passed an `Identity` or a `Trust` compiles unchanged — the whole workspace did, Python binding included. Two decisions the acceptance line did not make. **Equality by content for static sources, by identity otherwise**: the pool keys on `Arc<ClientTls>`, and two subscribers configured alike must still share one connection (the collision test depends on it), while two file sources on two directories must not. **A static value's material is read at first use**, not at construction: `From<Identity>` cannot fail, and `a_binding_with_unreadable_tls_material_fails_before_serving` still passes because the resolver's first build is at the bind. The resolver is one `LiveKey` for both `ResolvesServerCert` and `ResolvesClientCert`, caching a `CertifiedKey` per source generation; the binding's client verifier rebuilds its root store per trust generation the same way. `Files` polls modification times on use, not on a timer — no task, no reactor, no inotify. One finding from the tests: a same-key certificate verifies under the **old** certificate as an anchor, because an anchor is a name plus a key and both are unchanged, so the test observes rotation through the SANs the new certificate no longer carries. `tests/identity_source.rs`, four tests in 1.4 s.

### B-275 — `weida-openbao`: the client and the three auths, hand-off included
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 9e24256 | needs: [B-274]
acceptance: [0032](decisions/0032-identity-sources-and-the-handoff.md) §4.3 first half: `crates/openbao/weida-openbao` with `reqwest` (rustls, no default features) and `serde_json`, both justified in the manifest; `Auth::Token`, `Auth::AppRole { role_id, secret_id }` and `Auth::Handoff { wrapping_token: Source }` where the source is a path or `$CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY/<name>`; unwrap before any other request; a renewal task on `auth/token/renew-self` at half the TTL; a failed unwrap is `IdentityEvent::HandoffStolen` and an error. Tests against a scripted HTTP server in the crate for every auth and for the stolen hand-off (unwrap answers 400 "wrapping token is not valid or does not exist"), and one `#[ignore]`d test against `bao server -dev` on the local binary that mints a wrapped token, unwraps it, and sees the second unwrap fail.
note: the owner's flow, in his words: a root controller with a token that may only mint child tokens naming the service; the child handed over wrapped, seconds-lived and single-use; the service redeems first or dies; theft is therefore loud. Response wrapping is the tool, confirmed by the owner; systemd's credentials directory is the delivery.
note: done 9e24256, with B-276 to B-278 in the same commit. `crates/openbao/weida-openbao`: `OpenBao` over `reqwest` with a rustls `ClientConfig` built on `ring` and handed in through `use_preconfigured_tls`, so no process-wide provider is installed; `webpki-roots` for an OpenBao behind a public certificate, `Config::ca` for an internal one, `Config::from_env` reading what the `bao` CLI reads. `Auth::{Token, AppRole, Handoff}`; `HandoffSource::{File, Credential, Token}` — never the environment, which every process of the uid can read. `unwrap` keys on the one error string that means "already redeemed" and nothing else is `HandoffStolen`. The renewal task holds the shared session and leaves when it is the last holder. `post_wrapped` and `revoke_accessor` are the controller's half for a controller that is itself Rust. `tests/scripted.rs` — six tests against an HTTP/1.1 server of 200 lines in `tests/common` — and `tests/bao_dev.rs`, two `#[ignore]`d tests against the local `bao` 2.6.0 in dev mode, both run and green here: a wrapped child token redeemed once, refused the second time in the exact words, revoked by accessor with the service's `renew-self` then answering 403.

### B-276 — `PkiSign`, `PkiAnchor`, `Kv`
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 9e24256 | needs: [B-275]
acceptance: [0032](decisions/0032-identity-sources-and-the-handoff.md) §4.3 second half: `PkiSign` builds a CSR from a `Files` key (rcgen), calls `pki/sign/<role>` with `use_csr_sans`, installs the chain, and renews at a configured fraction of the TTL with `Renewed`/`RenewalFailed`; `PkiAnchor` is a `TrustSource` from `pki/cert/ca` (or `ca_chain`) reloaded at an interval; `Kv` reads chain and key from a KV v2 secret. The role settings of 0032 §2 are checked where they can be (`key_type` mismatch is an error naming the role), and stated in the crate's README where they cannot. Tests: scripted server for all three; the `#[ignore]`d `bao -dev` test enables a PKI mount, a role, signs weida's CSR, and a client trusting `pki/cert/ca` connects to a binding whose identity came from `PkiSign`, then the binding rotates and the client's next connection verifies against the new chain with the same fingerprint.
note: `PkiIssue` is named and refused in the note: it rotates the key, and under pinning a new key every TTL is a new peer every TTL.
note: done 9e24256. `PkiSign::start(client, &key_source)` reads the key of a `files` source, builds the CSR with rcgen over it, posts `pki/sign/<role>` with `use_csr_sans`, and installs `certificate` plus `ca_chain` as the chain into an `IdentitySource::external`; a task renews at `renew_at` (two thirds) of the lifetime and retries a failure after a tenth of what is left, one second to a minute, with `RenewalFailed` on the stream and the certificate in service untouched. Against the real server: a role with the settings of 0032 §2 signed weida's CSR, a client anchored through `PkiAnchor` connected to a binding served from the source, the renewal three seconds later arrived as `Renewed` under the same fingerprint, and the next connection verified against the new chain. `PkiAnchor` refreshes only on a changed chain — the first version reported `TrustRefreshed` on the first unchanged refresh, and the scripted test caught it. `Kv` reads two fields of a KV v2 secret and, with `refresh`, updates on change.

### B-277 — The systemd hand-off, end to end
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 9e24256 | needs: [B-275]
acceptance: `crates/openbao/weida-openbao/examples/handoff/`: a unit file with `ExecStartPre=` minting the wrapped child token (`bao token create -role … -wrap-ttl=5s`), keeping its `wrapped_accessor`, `ExecStopPost=` revoking by that accessor, `ProtectProc`, `NoNewPrivileges`, `Restart=on-failure`; a service binary that unwraps, signs its identity and binds; a README that states the policy the controller token needs and the role the service token has. Proved on this workstation by running the unit under a user manager against `bao server -dev`, once cleanly and once with the wrapping token consumed by a second `bao unwrap` first.
note: the controller side is a `bao` invocation and a unit file, not a Rust program; 0032 §5 says why.
note: done 9e24256, and proved as the acceptance line asks, with two findings the unit now records. **The credentials directory is read-only for `ExecStartPre=`** as it is for the service, so the wrapping token cannot be delivered through `SetCredential=`/`LoadCredential=` from the pre-start; it travels through `RuntimeDirectory=` — `0700`, the unit's uid, removed with the unit — and the service unlinks it once redeemed. The controller token itself does ride `LoadCredential=`, which is the direction that works. **The controller keeps the child's accessor from the minting answer** (`wrapped_accessor` in `wrap_info`), not from the service: so a theft, where the service never gets far enough to write anything, still ends with the thief's token revoked. The clean run: `redeemed: policies ["default", "weida-handoff"]`, the fingerprint, `listening on 127.0.0.1:4433`, and `weida request weida://<fp>@127.0.0.1:4433/echo` answering. The theft run, with a `sleep 3` drop-in after the mint and `bao unwrap` on the file inside the window: `ExecMainStatus=3`, `Success! Revoked token` from `ExecStopPost=`, and the thief's `bao token lookup` failing. One thing not seen: the service's own `ALARM:` line on stderr did not reach the journal in that run while its `redeemed:` lines did; the exit status is the signal the README tells a deployment to alert on. The unit and the dev controller token were removed from the workstation afterwards.

### B-278 — Documents for 0032
kind: docs | size: 30 | status: done 9e24256 | needs: [B-274]
acceptance: [0032](decisions/0032-identity-sources-and-the-handoff.md) §6's four edits — PATTERNS §1.9, INVARIANTS, GUARANTEES §6 peer-identity row, and `docs/libraries/weida-openbao.md` once B-275 exists.
note: done 9e24256: PATTERNS §1.9 gained the paragraph and the test list, INVARIANTS the bounds of a source and of the crate's tasks, GUARANTEES §6's peer-identity row the source and the unchecked-revocation sentence, and `docs/libraries/weida-openbao.md` with its index row — a library document for something that is not a foreign protocol, which its own index row says.

### B-270 — A dialled address outlives its connection: slots, redial policy, events
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 76cbb28 | needs: []
acceptance: [0031](decisions/0031-transparent-redial-and-the-sender-outbox.md) §4.1, §4.5, §4.7, §4.8 and §4.10 in `crates/weida`: `PeerSet` holds slots keyed by URL and trust, a per-slot task redials with `ReconnectPolicy` (backoff with jitter, `stop` causes, `give_up`), `ReconnectPolicy::never()` reproduces today's tests unchanged, a redial answering with a different fingerprint does not go live and reports `PeerChanged`, and every transition is delivered on a bounded, lag-counting `PeerEvent` stream per endpoint. On `weida+inproc://` the redial is a wait on the name registry rather than a timer; on `AF_UNIX` and named pipes it is the same policy as QUIC. Tests: a pusher whose QUIC server restarts delivers again with no second `connect`; the same over `AF_UNIX` with the listener re-bound on the same path, and over inproc with the bus dropped and re-registered under the same name, each asserting the same `Lost`, `Retrying`, `Connected` event order; a policy of `never` reports `ConnectionLost` as before; a QUIC server that comes back with a new key yields `GaveUp { PeerChanged }`.
note: the owner's premise, in two steps. First: weida is ZeroMQ's idea and transparent redial was central to that idea, so the core withholding it while `weida-zmq` (B-073) ships it is the wrong way round. Second, withdrawn by the owner in the same conversation: retaining completed streams for resend up to a size threshold, because the threshold makes behaviour depend on payload size rather than on what the caller asked for. What survived is the rule that responsibility moves at the API boundary — `send` is a message, `open` is a stream — and the note is built on it.
note: done 76cbb28, with B-271 to B-273 in the same commit because the four are one change to `crates/weida/src/stream.rs`: `PeerSet` became a list of slots — URL, path, `Live`/`Down`/`Gone` — behind an `Arc<PeerShared>` that the per-slot `watch_slot` task and the outbox drain hold weakly, with a `watch` channel in `Peer` that ends every task at the endpoint's drop. `ReconnectPolicy` (`crates/weida/src/reconnect.rs`: `initial`, `max`, `jitter`, `max_attempts`, `stop_on_peer_closed`, `never()`) lives on `RuntimeConfig::reconnect`; `PeerEvent::{Connected, Lost, Retrying, GaveUp, Missed}` ride a 64-deep broadcast per endpoint, `Missed(n)` being what a lagging reader is told. **Three findings from the tests rather than the design.** The pool compares a redial against a peer's *live* connections only, so after a total loss it would have accepted a stranger: the slot pins the first-proved key into the redialled `EndpointAddr` and the TLS verifier refuses the newcomer in the handshake — `GaveUp { PeerChanged }` with no connection ever opened (0031 §4.7 rewritten to say so). On `AF_UNIX` nothing travels on the control connection after the HELLOs, so the dialling side had no way to notice a peer's death: `Grouped::open_transfer` now marks the link closed when nobody answers on the address, which is the local shape of "learned at the next operation" (PATTERNS §1.10) and what starts the redial. In process the one shared close cell could not say which end closed, so a client's own `drain` looked like a peer close and was redialled: `LinkState` records the closer's id and reports `LocallyClosed` to that end, as `conn_error` does on QUIC. `NameRegistry::wait_bound` (weida-runtime) is the inproc redial: no timer, a bind wakes it, and the test's policy of a minute never elapses. `tests/reconnect.rs`, ten tests in 2.1 s; `after_the_server_restarts_the_pusher_must_reconnect` became `a_server_that_goes_away_is_reported_as_connection_lost` under `never()`, and `idle_timeout_reports_loss_within_the_window` now asserts the cause on the event and the next request on the redialled connection.

### B-271 — `open` waits for a live peer; `NotConnected` means never connected
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 76cbb28 | needs: [B-270]
acceptance: [0031](decisions/0031-transparent-redial-and-the-sender-outbox.md) §4.4: `PeerSet::pick` awaits a peer-list change when every slot is down, bounded by `RuntimeConfig::send_timeout`, and returns `NotConnected` only for an endpoint with no slot. `after_the_server_restarts_the_pusher_must_reconnect` is replaced by a test that opens a transfer during the outage and sees it complete on the redialled connection; a `send_timeout` test sees the timeout error carrying the last `LossCause`.
note: this is the "awaitable peer-list change" [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §6c point 1 sketched and deferred; the deviation-from-ZeroMQ paragraph there is rewritten by the same item.

### B-272 — Re-subscribe on the redialled connection
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 76cbb28 | needs: [B-270]
acceptance: [0031](decisions/0031-transparent-redial-and-the-sender-outbox.md) §4.6: the redial task performs the route registration, reverse-pool fill and filter re-send that `Subscriber::connect` performs today, factored so there is one implementation of that sequence. Tests: a subscriber whose publisher restarts receives the next publish after the restart with no application call; a filter subscribed *during* the outage is present after it.
note: 0008 §4.5 is untouched by this — the filters are the subscriber's own state, re-sent by the subscriber, and the server keeps nothing. B-078's finding for `weida-zmq` ("re-subscription on reconnect needed no reconnect logic") is the shape.

### B-273 — The outbox: `send` returns when the runtime owns the body
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 76cbb28 | needs: [B-270, B-271]
acceptance: [0031](decisions/0031-transparent-redial-and-the-sender-outbox.md) §4.2 and §4.3 for `Pusher` and a dialling `Pair`: the fast path is byte-identical to today's `open` + `write_all` + `finish`; with no live peer the body is copied into a per-endpoint outbox bounded by `RuntimeConfig::outbox_messages` and `RuntimeConfig::outbox_bytes`, drained in order after the redial, with `Block` at the bound by default and a counted drop under `OutboxFull::Drop`; a body over `outbox_bytes` is refused at the call. Tests: 100 sends during an outage arrive in order after it; the fourth send at a bound of three blocks until the drain begins; a `Drop` endpoint counts what it discarded; a body written before the restart is delivered once, asserted by the puller's sequence after the redial.
note: at-most-once on purpose. Retaining until the transport receipt and resending would be at-least-once, would need the 0001 counter to survive the reconnect and a dedup window at every receiver, and would collapse `Indeterminate` for a request; 0031 §5 names it and leaves it to a guarantee-set dimension.
note: two corrections to the acceptance line, both found by writing it down in code. The bounds are `RuntimeConfig`'s, not `Limits`': `Limits` is a per-connection profile by its own module doc and an outbox is per endpoint. And the behaviour at the bound is a local `OutboxFull { Block, Drop, Reject }` rather than the negotiated `Backpressure` dimension — the first test that declared `Drop` on the pusher failed its handshake against a `core` puller, which is 0006's rule working as designed and the wrong rule for a setting a puller has no stake in. `Requester::request` has no outbox: its caller awaits the reply, so the wait in `open` (B-271) is the same wait with nothing to copy. The one copy per body on the slow path is `Bytes::copy_from_slice`; the fast path allocates nothing it did not before.

### B-255 — The guide: the C8B question, and chapter 1
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done c1975a7 | needs: []
acceptance: `docs/GUIDE.md` exists and is the document that **teaches**, in the form [0026](decisions/0026-the-guide-and-the-c8b-question.md) fixes: §0 states the C8B question with the arithmetic that follows from this repository's own measured numbers — each figure naming the item that measured it, each derivation saying it is one, and the gaps named rather than rounded — and §1 teaches one transfer end to end: a transfer is a stream, the peer is a key, the outcome is a value, how far the far end got, what a receipt costs. **Every program in the chapter is a file in the tree and a test drives that file**: `crates/weida/examples/guide_one_transfer.rs` with its outcomes as values, included as a module by `crates/weida/tests/guide.rs`, which asserts each numbered claim — plus one test that counts the chapter's claims, so a claim added to the prose without a program and an assertion fails the gate. The refusal race of [0005](decisions/0005-refusal-race.md) is taught by demonstration rather than smoothed over. The guide is published on the site, first in the weida group.
note: the owner named both halves in one message: the zguide is the model for the documentation this project owes, because its patterns teach *ways of constructing networked software*; and C8B is the question, this project's C10K. What makes the form worth the cost is that this repository already runs nine zguide recipes as examples asserted against the guide's own claims (B-090..B-092) — the discipline existed, it had just never been pointed at weida's own documentation.
note: done c1975a7. `docs/GUIDE.md` at 19 KB: §0 with the arithmetic and §1 with five numbered claims, `crates/weida/examples/guide_one_transfer.rs` with five `pub` entry points that return their outcomes as values, `crates/weida/tests/guide.rs` with **7 tests**. Three things the chapter taught its author. **The refusal race is demonstrable rather than describable**: running the chapter three times gives `Delivered`, `Delivered`, `Refused` for a push to a path nobody serves — [0005](decisions/0005-refusal-race.md)'s race, on the page, with the remedy (ask a question instead of making a statement) in the next paragraph and a test that pins what survives the coin flip rather than one side of it. **The cap is a conversation**: the chapter's second transfer offers 64 MiB to `collect(1 MiB)`, and the interesting half is not the reader's `LimitExceeded` but the sender's `Error::Rejected` **mid-write** — the refusal travels, so a cap is never a silent truncation. And **the receipt's cost is two orders of magnitude**, measured by the chapter itself rather than quoted: 165.9 µs for eight 1 KiB pushes without the receipt against 19.98 ms with it, which agrees with [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §3's ~26 ms and is why fire-and-forget is the default. The claim-counting test is the mechanism that makes the form worth its cost: `every_claim_the_chapter_makes_has_a_program_and_a_test` reads the document, counts the lines beginning `**Claim §1.` and fails on a sixth claim written without a program.

### B-262 — A connection's negotiated guarantee set is not observable
kind: design | size: 30 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §4 describes a negotiated set as a property of a connection — "both sides declare, and the intersection decides" — and `GuaranteeSet::intersect` is public, so a caller can compute what *would* be agreed from two offers. What no public accessor reports is what a **live** connection actually agreed to: `Agreed` and `ConnCtx::guarantees` are `pub(crate)`, and nothing on `Runtime`, `Listener` or any `Endpoint` exposes them. Either an accessor exists (and says which connection, since a multi-path peer has several) or the documents stop describing the agreed set as observable. A decision note is not required if the answer is the accessor; it is required if the answer is "no", because then three documents need the sentence that says so.
note: found by writing [GUIDE.md](GUIDE.md) §4.5, which set out to print what a chain had negotiated and could not. The chapter names the gap and its test asserts it (`observable_on_a_connection == false`), so closing this item **fails that test on purpose** and forces the paragraph to be rewritten rather than left quietly wrong. Worth noting what is *not* missing: the negotiation itself is implemented, enforced and tested — a set that does not reach what a peer requires fails the connection with `NEGOTIATION_FAILED` and there is no downgrade path. This is an observability gap, not a protocol one, which is why it is `design` rather than `code`: the interesting half is what a caller may ask about *which* connection.
note: done, answered **no accessor**, and the answer is arithmetic rather than taste ([0027](decisions/0027-the-negotiated-set-is-the-configured-set.md)). `RuntimeConfig::guarantees` is what a runtime offers *and* requires — one setting, by [0006](decisions/0006-guarantee-sets.md) §4.4 — so for any **live** connection `agreed = min(mine, theirs)` and `agreed ≥ mine`, or there would be no connection, therefore **`agreed = mine`**. A `connection.guarantees()` would hand a caller its own configuration back plus a branch that can never be taken. What a peer requiring more than its peer offers receives is not a weaker connection but **none**, measured against a core listener at this commit: `core` connects, the same runtime with `delivery: AtLeastOnce` or `acknowledgement: Accepted` is refused with `Error::Negotiation`. So the observation an application needs is the connection having succeeded. **Two findings on the way.** HELLO keys `5` and `6` **are** on the wire — encoded, decoded, and a `core` set omitted so a v0 HELLO stays byte-identical, with a header requiring more than it offers rejected by the *decoder* — which retires a stale "spec ahead of code" caveat that [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §4 had been carrying. And the refusal's **wording is a race**: `negotiation failed` when the dial has read the peer's CONNECTION_CLOSE reason, `connection lost` when the local teardown wins, both observed from the same program on the same commit — so the test asserts the refusal and that it carries a reason, never which one, exactly as [0005](decisions/0005-refusal-race.md) established for a misrouted push. [GUIDE.md](GUIDE.md) §4.5 now teaches the property instead of naming a gap, and its program demonstrates the refusal against a real handshake.

### B-256 — Guide chapter 4: depth, and what a chain may claim
kind: code | size: 90 | status: dropped: general cross-protocol bridge product retired | needs: [B-255]
acceptance: the chapter that can be written **now**, ahead of chapters 2 and 3, because its machinery exists and is tested: a message enters through one protocol and leaves through another, every hop's guarantee set is intersected rather than downgraded ([0006](decisions/0006-guarantee-sets.md)), and the chain's honest end-to-end claim is `BestEffort` — which `crates/interop/cross-tests` already asserts rather than implies. Programs: the existing cross-protocol chain driven as the chapter's own example, plus one that shows a hop refusing to claim what it cannot ([adapters/zmtp.md](adapters/zmtp.md) §9's named losses at the boundary). The chapter must end with the number §0.1 makes load-bearing: at two to four hops, an end-to-end acknowledgement is 1.1 TB for one message, so a chain's claim is the weakest hop's and nothing composes it upward.
note: filed before chapters 2 and 3 on purpose — [0026](decisions/0026-the-guide-and-the-c8b-question.md) §4.4 orders the slices by what can be asserted, not by chapter number. Chapter 2 needs B-247's fan-out measurement and chapter 3 needs B-203's acknowledgement.
note: done. `docs/GUIDE.md` §4 with five claims, `crates/interop/cross-tests/examples/guide_depth.rs`, and `crates/interop/cross-tests/tests/guide_depth.rs` with five tests. The chapter lives in the interop crate for the reason that crate exists: a chain through both adapters belongs to neither, and the claim **count** stays in `crates/weida/tests/guide.rs` because it is a property of the document rather than of the chain. **The chain is harsher than a weida deployment on purpose** — ZeroMQ → ZMTP bridge → weida → SP bridge → nng, three protocols and two translations, both foreign ends the real implementations — because a system that reaches everybody does not get to choose everybody's protocol. §4.1 makes the translation visible through the topic, which all three protocols express differently: two ZMTP frames become `sport.football\0goal`, one flat SP body, and **the frame boundary the sender used does not exist at the far end**. §4.2 is the chapter in two booleans: the ZeroMQ send succeeded, nothing arrived at the SP end, and neither protocol lied — `BestEffort ∩ BestEffort = BestEffort`. §4.3 shows the smaller ceiling deciding at the first hop that sees it, and the far hop buffering **nothing**, which is the difference between one node's bad configuration and a whole tree's. §4.4 is the composition rule as a loss: a two-frame message has no weida counterpart, so it dies at the first hop although SP could have carried the bytes perfectly well — a chain's capability is an intersection, not a union. **§4.5 found a real gap** and is the reason B-262 exists: `GuaranteeSet::intersect` is public and the arithmetic is a caller's to do, but **no public accessor reports what a live connection negotiated** — `Agreed` is `pub(crate)`, and three documents describe the agreed set as a property of a connection. The chapter names it and its test asserts `observable_on_a_connection == false`, so closing B-262 fails that test on purpose.
note: retired 2026-09-18 with the general bridge product category. The cross-protocol crate and
guide chapter were removed; chapter 4 now waits for a native weida/broker multi-hop example
([0026](decisions/0026-the-guide-and-the-c8b-question.md) §4.4).

### B-257 — Guide chapter 2: many peers, and the drop
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: [B-255, B-247]
acceptance: selection (round-robin, fan-out, every-live-peer), the drop-versus-block decision per pattern with the counted drops per cause that make a starving subscriber nameable, and what one slow reader does to everybody else on the same connection ([PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.3). The chapter states a **measured** fan-out width and the memory it costs, replacing §0.1's derived 10³-10⁴, and says which of the two bounds — transport state or the subscriber byte budget — binds first at that width.
note: this is the chapter the C8B arithmetic rests on, which is why it is blocked rather than written from the defaults. Guessing the width here would put an estimate in the one place the guide promises none.
note: done, and the chapter is longer than planned because the programs refused two versions of it. `docs/GUIDE.md` §2 with five numbered claims, `crates/weida/examples/guide_many_peers.rs` with five `pub` entry points, five tests in `crates/weida/tests/guide.rs` (12 in the file now), and the claim counter generalized to one row per written chapter so a sixth claim in either fails the gate. **§2.1 is the table a ZeroMQ reader recognises**: six pushes over three pullers is `[2, 2, 2]`, two publishes to three subscribers is `[2, 2, 2]`, three BUS members sending once each is `[2, 2, 2]` — one call shape, three peer sets, and the selection policy is a property of the endpoint rather than of the send. **§2.2 is where the chapter earned its keep.** The isolation claim is demonstrable exactly as stated — the reading subscriber received all 512 messages while its neighbour read nothing at all — but the first two attempts at demonstrating it were both wrong and both are recorded in the program: pacing the publisher to the reader reported **zero drops** for a subscriber that had read nothing for 2 MiB, because a budget permit is released when the bytes reach the **wire** and QUIC absorbs the rest; and cutting the budget to four messages to force the cliff starved the *reader* too (43 of 64), which proves nothing about isolation. The sentence that came out of it is the chapter's headline: **overload is a rate, not a backlog.** **§2.3 settles the bound question with one variable**: same payload, same queue, budget 64 KiB versus the 8 MiB default, and the counter names a different ceiling each time — the byte budget below the connection's 0.6-1.5 MiB absorption band, the queue above it. That also explains why B-247's bench sees the queue at the defaults. **§2.4** quotes the measurement rather than the program's debug timings, and the program says so when it is a debug build. **§2.5** is the pattern that has to answer "what if a peer takes the message and says nothing": a respondent that accepts a survey question and holds it is `asked 3, answered 2, silent 1`, and the deadline is the caller's. Both new tests bite, checked by mutation: recording a budget refusal as a queue refusal fails `each_ceiling_names_itself_when_it_is_the_one_that_binds`, and turning the selection cursor's `fetch_add` into a `load` fails `the_pattern_chooses_the_peer_set` with `[6, 0, 0]`.

### B-258 — Guide chapter 3: a hop that takes responsibility
kind: code | size: 90 | status: blocked: needs B-203, so that a queue's promise survives a consumer that fails | needs: [B-255, B-203]
acceptance: the broker as a hop rather than a component: what `Accepted` means and what it does not, credit as the consumer's own bound on what it will be sent, redelivery as the queue's answer to a delivery that failed, and the sentence a queue may not say before it has a store. Programs against `weida-broker`, asserted the way chapter 1's are.
note: half of this can be written today and the half that matters cannot: a chapter about responsibility whose queue cannot survive a consumer's death would teach the wrong lesson.

### B-259 — Guide chapter 5: a peer that comes back
kind: code | size: 60 | status: ready | needs: [B-255, B-270, B-272]
acceptance: what a redial restores and what it does not: the runtime redials and re-sends the subscriber's own filters ([0031](decisions/0031-transparent-redial-and-the-sender-outbox.md) §4.6), a reconnecting peer is the same key on a new connection ([0008](decisions/0008-session-identity.md) §4.5) and therefore nothing server-side is resumed — no session, no sequence position, no message published during the gap — with the program that watches `PeerEvent` instead of looping on `connect`, and the one rule a caller gets wrong: a re-sent subscription is a new subscription.
note: the chapter exists to say what does not come back, which is the half a reader assumes wrongly.
note: re-scoped by 0031: the first acceptance line taught "reconnection as the application's decision" and blocked on B-018, which was done — the real blocker was that the runtime had no redial to teach. Now it has one to wait for.

### B-263 — An acknowledgement vocabulary the user names, and the broker that relays it
kind: spec | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the decision note that settles what a completion level *is* to a caller and what a broker does with one, because the mechanism exists and the rule around it is currently self-contradictory. Three parts. **(1) The levels are the user's and the numbering stays.** `CursorLevel::Application(u64)` at or above `APPLICATION_FLOOR` already carries any level an application names, "carried and ordered, never interpreted" ([0023](decisions/0023-completion-is-a-cursor.md) §4.4); the note states that a *name* is the API's business and never the wire's, so nothing is added to the header for it, and each binding is free to hand the caller labelled constants over the same integers. **(2) The broker relays the consumer's report to the producer, and that is not a broker guarantee.** [0018](decisions/0018-minimal-broker.md) §4.3 currently says the broker "MUST NOT relay" the consumer's `Processed`, on the grounds that guarantees are hop-local — but a cursor is a **report**, not a guarantee, and 0023 §4.5 already separates the two: the negotiated `acknowledgement` dimension of HELLO is the guarantee and "a cursor is never load-bearing". So §4.3 is amended to the rule it meant: the broker's own *certificate* to the producer stays `Accepted`, and the consumer's report may travel end to end **as the consumer's**. The note states the three mechanical consequences: a message is two transfers, so the broker translates the producer's order into its own order on the delivery; relay re-emits the `(level, offset)` pair on the producer-facing cursor stream under the report id the **producer** allocated, because a report id is per connection and per allocating side; and relay is honest only while the body is byte-identical across the hop — a broker that ever re-encoded a payload must stop relaying offsets rather than renormalise them silently. **(3) An ordered level nobody reports is silence, and that is the answer.** A producer that orders a level no hop implements gets it absent from the final snapshot, which is already implemented and already tested; the note says so as a rule so that no later slice invents a validation handshake for it.
note: filed out of the owner's own framing, which is sharper than the note it corrects: the broker exists to extend the zmq patterns with a persistent queue, so producer and consumer are the two ends of **one** pattern and the queue is the durable middle rather than a third party with opinions. What 0018 §4.3 was protecting is still protected by the amendment — nobody certifies a hop they do not own — and what it was accidentally forbidding is the thing the cursor stream was built for. Blocks B-203, which needs both halves of this (settlement *and* relay) before it can be built.

note: done. [0029](decisions/0029-a-report-is-relayed-a-certificate-is-not.md), `provisional`, and the distinction that resolves the contradiction was **already in the tree**: 0023 §4.5 separates the negotiated `acknowledgement` dimension (a guarantee, hop-local) from a cursor (a report, "never load-bearing"), and GUARANTEES §2's invariant is about the first only. So a queue re-emits its consumer's report as the **consumer's** while its own certificate stays `Accepted`, with two prohibitions that keep a claim from drifting: a hop may not emit a level it did not receive for that message, and may not re-emit a report that reached it as a re-emission — a report travels at most one queue from its observer. What changed beside the new note: 0018 §4.3's "MUST NOT relay" sentence is amended to the rule it meant, 0023 §4.4's "loses its end-to-end reading" paragraph keeps the half that was right and names the half that was wrong (treating "not a guarantee" as "not transportable"), GUARANTEES §2 gains the normative guarantee-versus-report paragraph, and PROTOCOL §6.2 gains **DATA key `12` `delivery_attempt`** as a count with "absent means `1`" plus the rule that a relayed record rides the **reader's own** `report_id`. Two alternatives are refused in writing rather than left open: attribution bytes on the record (16 bytes is the whole record, and `Processed`'s own definition already names whose statement it is) and a broker-specific restatement level (a producer would order a private number to learn a standard thing). Site: 57 pages, link checker clean, 17 site tests green.

### B-264 — Which runtimes Phase C reaches, in which order, and through what
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the decision note [LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §9's one-line ordering has never had. It records the order the owner set — **Python, Node, Erlang, C, Java** — and the reason each position is where it is, measured against [0026](decisions/0026-the-guide-and-the-c8b-question.md) §0's question rather than against popularity: how many runtimes can call this, and through what substrate. The facts the note is built on: the Python row is finished and five bindings wide on one three-part foundation, which is the evidence that a per-language foundation is the right unit; an N-API addon is **one** artifact for Node, Bun and Deno, so "Node" is one row and not three; `rustler` reaches Erlang and Elixir with a mailbox as the completion mechanism, which is the cheapest bridge of any target because `enif_send` replaces the loop-wakeup machinery every other binding needs; a **C ABI** is the substrate for the targets with no Rust-native path (Go, .NET, C, Zig, Lua) and is therefore the fourth position rather than the first, because every binding here is written by this project and Rust-native is cheaper wherever it exists; and Java is **last** rather than dropped, with the honest reason recorded — the reach is real and the maintenance is not, since nobody here writes Java and a binding nobody maintains is worse than none. [0019](decisions/0019-jvm-binding.md) stays as the design it is and gains a status line pointing here.
note: the ordering in LOOP §9 was an assertion from the first week and no document ever justified it; 0019 answered only *how* a JVM binding would be built. The owner reordered it in conversation, and an order without a written reason is exactly what this repository turns into a note.
note: done. [0030](decisions/0030-which-runtimes-and-in-which-order.md), `provisional`: **Python, Node, Erlang, C, Java**, each position with its cost rather than its popularity. Two things the writing sharpened. **Erlang would be second on cost alone** and is third only because Node's reach is wider — `enif_send` delivers a completion into a process mailbox, so the machinery that costs the Python row its ~78 µs per operation (one loop wakeup per `await`, IMPLEMENTATION §4) is not needed at all, `ResourceArc` replaces the generation-tagged handle table 0019 designs for the JVM, and refcounted binaries beat CPython's forced outbound copy. And **"Node" is one row for three runtimes**, because N-API is a stable ABI that Bun and Deno implement — with the caveat written into the note rather than assumed: each runtime gets its own smoke test, the way `package.sh` proves the wheel instead of trusting maturin. 0019 keeps its design and gains a status line saying it answers *how* and never *whether*. What remains the owner's: the one-line ordering in `LOOP.md` §9, which this note supersedes and which only they edit.

### B-265 — Erlang distribution over weida: the sheet before the adapter
kind: research | size: 60 | status: parked: the owner — not now (2026-09-15); revives with the Erlang row | needs: []
acceptance: `docs/research/erlang-dist.md` in the shape every adapter here got one, answering what a `-proto_dist weida` adapter would have to implement and what it would prove. The claim to test is already written in this repository: [0022](decisions/0022-consensus-topology.md) §4.5 argues against Erlang distribution's shape — "RabbitMQ's groups ride Erlang distribution and Kafka's replication one TCP connection per broker pair; weida's transport is QUIC, where **one connection carries independent streams**" — so an adapter would demonstrate that argument in the ecosystem that owns it, where OTP 22 had to add message *fragmentation* precisely because distribution is one TCP stream. What the sheet must settle before any code: the distribution callback surface (`listen`, `accept`, `accept_connection`, `setup`, `close`, `select`) and whether a pure-Erlang module over an active-mode NIF socket is enough, as `inet_tls_dist` is pure Erlang over `ssl`; **the ordering question, which is the hard one** — Erlang guarantees signal order per sender/receiver process pair while independent QUIC streams give no cross-stream order, so the sheet names the options (one stream per process pair, a hashed stream pool with head-of-line blocking inside a bucket only, or a sequence-and-reorder buffer over `PerProducer` ordering) with the cost of each; which weida surface carries it (the raw L0 `Peer`/`Acceptor`, or PAIR, which is the closest pattern — and **not** the broker, because distribution is peer to peer and a queue in the middle would break `nodeup`/`nodedown` and monitors); and the handshake, tick and version negotiation with the OTP release each was read against.
note: the owner's interest is the distribution adapter rather than only the binding, and the two are separable: the binding (B-266's row) is `rustler` plus a mailbox, while this is a protocol adapter against a documented-in-corners protocol where a mistake means "the VM does not cluster". Sheet first, as [LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §5 requires of every `research` item. A separate idea the sheet names rather than solves: weida as a bridge **between** BEAM clusters, which is a broker question and not a distribution one.

### B-266 — Does a second Python client earn its keep on PyPy
kind: spec | size: 15 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the question answered with a number rather than a preference, and recorded wherever the answer belongs — `docs/libraries/weida-py.md` §10 if it is "no". The facts: the wheel is `abi3` from CPython 3.9, and the limited API is CPython's, so it does not load on PyPy at all; PyPy's supported route is `cffi` over a **C ABI**, which is the fourth position of B-264, so a PyPy client is a consumer of `weida-c` and not a second implementation of anything. Against it: this binding's measured cost is ~78 µs per operation dominated by **one event-loop wakeup per await** ([IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §4), not by bytecode, so the interpreter PyPy replaces is not where the time goes — and a second Python surface to keep in step with the first is exactly what [0013](decisions/0013-competitor-libraries.md) §4.4 exists to prevent. For it: a `cffi` shim over a stable C ABI is a few hundred lines and no new protocol logic. The note also names the two questions that matter more than PyPy and answers neither by assumption: **free-threaded CPython**, where the stable ABI's availability per version has to be read rather than guessed, and GraalPy.
note: raised by the owner while discussing the C ABI, and it is the right instinct to ask before building: the answer decides whether `weida-c` has one consumer more, and it is cheap because the measurement that decides it already exists.
note: done, answered **no**, and recorded in [libraries/weida-py.md](libraries/weida-py.md) §10 where a reader of the parity table meets it. The decisive number was already in the tree: ~78 µs per operation dominated by **one event-loop wakeup per `await`** ([IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §4), so the interpreter PyPy replaces is not where the time goes. **And the round found something sharper than its own question**, read out of PyO3 0.29.2's selector rather than assumed (`pyo3-build-config-0.29.2/src/impl_.rs:96-132`): `applicable_stable_abi` returns `None` for a **free-threaded** interpreter below 3.15, because the free-threaded stable ABI starts there — so this wheel's headline claim, "one `abi3` wheel for CPython 3.9 and up", holds for **GIL-enabled** CPython and not for 3.13t or 3.14t, and §2's row said so unconditionally. It says so conditionally now. PyPy and GraalPy take the same branch from the other side: a stable ABI is selected but pinned to the interpreter's own version, so each would be its own artifact. The route if it is ever wanted stays a `cffi` shim over the C ABI of [0030](decisions/0030-which-runtimes-and-in-which-order.md) §3.4 — a consumer of that row, not a binding of its own — and free-threaded CPython revisits itself when `abi3t` becomes the floor.

### B-253 — The website in the tree: the documents, rendered
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 6038c51 | needs: []
acceptance: `homepage` has named `https://weida.doodleshnookie.net` since B-068 and nothing is there. A workspace member renders this repository's own documents into a static site — `README.md` as the landing page, the normative documents, every decision note, every library parity document, every adapter mapping — and **writes no prose of its own** beyond navigation, a stylesheet, a per-page heading index and one pre-release notice, so that no claim on the site can disagree with the document beside it ([0025](decisions/0025-the-website.md)). Every document under `docs/` is published **or** excluded with its reason recorded, and one that is neither fails a test; a link to an excluded document resolves into the repository rather than to a missing page; a link that resolves to nothing fails the build. The notice — no crate on any registry, no tag, no binary — comes from one constant and appears on every page. Output is files under `target/site` that work over `file://`: no server, no JavaScript, no web font, no analytics. `cargo test -p weida-site` and the workspace gate cover it.
note: filed on the owner's instruction that the site lives in this tree while work continues locally, which is what settles the shape: a site that is the document set cannot go stale against it, and a site with its own summary of the guarantees would be the first page to.
note: done 6038c51 (and `aa43c3f`, `d3c256b`, `e2a3c68`, `6b5643a`, `b5db80c`). This status line said `ready` for three rounds after the site shipped, which is the loop's own arithmetic going wrong in exactly the way a document's counts do — found while recounting for this round rather than by reading the entry. What exists: `crates/site`, one member, one dependency, **55 pages** (the guide first in the weida group), 17 tests, no JavaScript, no web font, `file://`-able, and a link checker that fails the build on a dangling link — which found 58 broken links in the notes on its first run and one more in [0027](decisions/0027-the-negotiated-set-is-the-configured-set.md) today. Serving it is B-254, blocked on the owner.

### B-254 — Serving the site, and what a release form would be
kind: code | size: 45 | status: dropped: 0033 — the site is built and deployed from its own repository, so this tree has nothing to serve | needs: [B-253]
acceptance: `weida.doodleshnookie.net` serves what `cargo run -p weida-site` produces, from a named host with a named mechanism — the Forgejo host `sadala` behind its existing reverse proxy, a static host, or a push target — with the build reproducible from a clean checkout and the deployment step written down where the next person finds it rather than in somebody's shell history. Two things the site must gain **before** it is public and not before: a quick start that names a real release form (there is none — the workspace version is `0.1.0`, unpublished and untagged) and the overhead numbers of B-246 re-measured, because bytes on the wire are the first thing a visitor compares.

Nothing in the tree publishes anything today, which is the honest state and is what every page says.
note: **parked by the owner (2026-09-15), and the reason is the same one the acceptance's own precondition states**: a site is a promise of something to install, the workspace is `0.1.0`, unpublished and untagged, so there is nothing to promise yet. The work this item would do is not lost — `cargo run -p weida-site` renders 56 pages from the tree on demand and its link checker fails the build on a dangling link, so the site stays correct while it stays local. It revives when a release form exists, and the two preconditions above (a quick start naming that form, B-246's re-measured overhead numbers) revive with it.
