### B-245 — Over `AF_UNIX` a cancelled transfer reads as a complete one
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: a transfer abandoned over `weida+unix://` — `OutgoingTransfer::cancel`, a drop without `finish()`, or a producer process that dies mid-payload — surfaces **at the reader** as `Error::Canceled` from `collect`/`read_capped` and as `io::ErrorKind::ConnectionReset` on the `AsyncRead`, and never as `Ok(0)` or a short `Ok(bytes)`. Today it is the opposite: `crates/weida/src/unix.rs` implements the `grouped::Stream` hooks `finish` and `reset` identically — both `drop(writer)`, the code discarded — and `grouped::LocalRecv` maps a zero-length read to a clean end of stream with no reset check, so an application over `AF_UNIX` takes a truncated payload for a whole one with nothing on the wire to warn it. The chosen shape **matches the named-pipe transport rather than adding a second convention**: per-write framing as `crates/weida/src/pipe.rs` frames it (a kind byte plus a `u32` length, a FIN chunk, a RESET chunk carrying the code), so the two socket transports share one framing and the reader keeps its single-pass read; the alternative and its cost are already written down ([0012](decisions/0012-local-connection-grouping.md) §4.7(e): one end-of-stream marker per transfer is one byte on a clean end and nine on a reset, and it costs the zero-copy read, because the reader must hold back nine bytes until EOF). Tests beside the existing local-transport ones in `crates/weida/tests/transports.rs`: a cancelled transfer over `AF_UNIX` observed as `Canceled` at the reader and never as EOF, a clean transfer still arriving byte-for-byte under the framing, and the named-pipe and in-process behaviour unchanged. The per-write overhead is measured and recorded rather than assumed.
note: the sharpest finding of the review that filed this, because three documents asserted the guarantee for every transport and one transport silently did not have it — the failure mode is an application reading half a message as a whole one, which no error ever names. The claim is now **qualified** in [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.5 and §1.10, [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6's transport row and [FAILURE_MODEL.md](FAILURE_MODEL.md) §4; this item is what lets it be unqualified again. Linux has no kernel-level abort for a stream socket — `SO_LINGER` with a zero timeout plus `close` is byte-for-byte a plain close at the reader — so the distinction can only live in the payload, which is why this is framing work rather than a socket option.
note: done, and the shape is the first of [0012](decisions/0012-local-connection-grouping.md) §4.7(e)'s two: **per-write framing, the named pipe's, now shared rather than copied**. The framing moved out of `crates/weida/src/pipe.rs` into `crates/weida/src/chunked.rs` as `ChunkWriter<W>`/`ChunkReader<R>`, and both socket transports use it — one convention, one reader, which is the reason the loss existed at all (a convention per transport is how a distinction gets lost). The pipe's behaviour is unchanged byte for byte; `AF_UNIX` gained the RESET chunk it had no kernel equivalent for, and `unix.rs` grew a `UnixLocal` wrapper carrying the `Exec` the marker's write needs — the same shape `PipeStream` has always had, because ending a payload is synchronous at the call site and a write is not. Three tests in `crates/weida/tests/transports.rs`, all `#[cfg(unix)]`: a **cancelled** transfer is `Error::Canceled` at the reader, a transfer **dropped** without `finish()` is too (the case an application hits by accident rather than on purpose), and a payload written in three chunks — one of them 96 KiB, past the read buffer — still arrives byte for byte, which is the regression the framing could have caused. Both loss tests bite: making `reset` write a FIN again fails exactly those two. Four documents lose their qualification and say why rather than going quiet: [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.5 (retitled — it was "never EOF **except over `AF_UNIX`**"), §1.10, §1.11 and §4's streamed-fan-out row, [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6's transport row, [FAILURE_MODEL.md](FAILURE_MODEL.md) §4 and its cancellation paragraph, and 0012 §4.7(e) records which of its two shapes was taken and why. What is **still** a named loss on both socket transports, unchanged: the reader's refusal has no carrier, so a reader that stops **drains** and the writer learns from the reply (0012 §4.7(b)).

### B-201 — `weida-broker`: a queue at an endpoint path, and `Accepted`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: [B-198]
acceptance: the admission half of [0018](decisions/0018-minimal-broker.md) §4.1-§4.6 in a new crate `crates/broker/` → `weida-broker` ([ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §4), depending on `weida` and depended on by nothing. A queue is registered on an endpoint path from the broker's configuration — no declare frame, §4.5 — and a producer's **exchange** to that path is answered on the reply half with the achieved level `Accepted` (§4.6), or with ERROR `{REJECTED}` when the queue is at `queue_bytes` and ERROR `{UNKNOWN_ENDPOINT}` when no queue is registered, both from [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §9.4's existing vocabulary; a one-way transfer to the same path is admitted with no confirm. The three bounds of §4.8 — `queue_bytes`, `max_queues`, `max_unacked` — exist with defaults and are documented beside the per-runtime numbers of [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §10. Tests assert what a producer observes: a confirm that arrives with **no consumer registered at all**, which is the temporal decoupling a queue exists for; a refusal at the byte bound that leaves the connection alive; and that no code path can report `Stored`, asserted by its absence from the type rather than by a comment.
note: the first slice deliberately delivers nothing. A queue that admits, confirms and holds is verifiable on its own, and splitting admission from delivery keeps each slice's acceptance observable.

note: done fd67d65. `crates/broker/` → `weida-broker`, whose whole dependency list is `weida`: **one acceptor per configured queue path**, which is what lets one path take both stream kinds under the existing "one answer per (stream kind, path)" rule — a bidi exchange is admitted *and confirmed*, a uni transfer is admitted silently, and neither needed a new registration concept. The confirm is **DATA key `8`** on the reply half (`achieved`, the `Acknowledgement` ladder value), golden vector `57 01 03 A1 08 02`: a whole publisher confirm in three header bytes, which is the argument for spending a reply half on it rather than a frame kind. An undefined level is a **decode error**, not a skipped key — reading an unknown value as the weakest would invent a claim about a producer's message, so this is the one narrow exception to §5's skip-the-unknown rule. `Stored` is refused **by the type**: `Achieved` has one variant, so no code path can issue a durable certificate. **The bound needed a number the note did not have**: `queue_bytes` alone bounds nothing against a producer of empty messages (zero payload bytes, one live `QueuedMessage` each), so every message is charged `PER_MESSAGE_OVERHEAD` = 256 B plus payload plus retained labels, which makes `queue_bytes / 256` the ceiling on the message count too; the payload is read under the queue's *remaining* budget rather than the producer's advisory `content_len`. **The refusal is the opposite choice from B-096's** and the note says why: a ZeroMQ queue that refused the only message in an empty queue would deadlock a pattern with nowhere else to put it, while a broker has somewhere to put the refusal — the exchange's reply half — so an over-budget message is refused rather than accepted past the bound. 12 tests (8 admission over real QUIC, 3 queue unit, 1 doctest), mutation-checked: removing the confirm fails two, removing the push check fails one, and the read cap is pinned by its own test. Docs: PROTOCOL §6.2 key `8` + §8 vector + §9.4 queue rows + §10 broker profile + §11 rewritten from "absent" to "one of four, and no frame", ARCHITECTURE §1/§4 (whose crate map was six crates stale), GUARANTEES §1, INVARIANTS, IMPLEMENTATION phase 6 `first slice done`.

### B-202 — Frame kind `5`: the L2 credit frame, and delivery under it
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: [B-201]
acceptance: frame kind `5` decoded and encoded in `weida-protocol` with the fields [0003](decisions/0003-credit-unit.md) §4.2-§4.3 fixed and [0018](decisions/0018-minimal-broker.md) §4.4 confirms — a subscription and an **absolute** delivery limit — with golden vectors in [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §8's shape and a fuzz target beside the other header targets; the frame rides the connection of the path it names ([0011](decisions/0011-answered-where-it-arrived.md) §4.3). A consumer registers with SUBSCRIBE on the queue's path and receives **nothing** until it grants credit (initial credit zero, §4.4); a message is delivered to exactly one consumer as a one-way transfer; the broker stops at the limit and resumes when the limit is raised. Tests assert the three properties a credit scheme is bought for: a duplicated or reordered credit frame changes nothing; a consumer at its limit receives no further delivery while another consumer with credit does; and credit lowered to `0` pauses delivery without resetting a stream or closing the connection.
note: the roadmap gated Phase D on this frame having "a consumer on both ends" ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §9). This is the end inside weida; the adapters named in §5 of [0003](decisions/0003-credit-unit.md) are the other.

note: done. **The frame's fields were fixed; its *interpretation* was not, and that is where the work was.** 0003 §4.3 says an absolute limit makes a lost or duplicated frame a no-op — but every control frame rides its own unidirectional stream and QUIC orders no stream against another, so a *reordered* grant could lower a limit the consumer had already raised, and 0018 §4.4 wants credit `0` to pause. Both cannot hold with last-write-wins. The rule that satisfies both: **the broker keeps the highest limit it has seen, and a consumer pauses by restating the count it has already been delivered** — AMQP 1.0's `link-credit = 0` against an absolute baseline, so a fresh subscription's pause is the `0` it starts at. No new field, and 0003's fields untouched; written into PROTOCOL §6.6 as the three rules of the scheme. **The test for it had to be rewritten twice**: "a stale grant delivers nothing" passes under last-write-wins too, because a lowered limit also delivers nothing — only feeding *new* messages afterwards distinguishes a limit that is still 4 from one that fell to 3, and the mutation now fails as it should. Wire: `FrameKind::Credit` = 5 (kind `6` is the new first free number), `CreditHeader{endpoint,filter,limit}` with all three keys required — an absent limit and `0` would otherwise be the same frame, and `0` is the pause — two golden vectors (`57 05 12 A3 …` and the zero-limit `57 05 0C A3 …`), a fuzz target beside the other header targets. `weida` gained the L2 hooks: `Incoming::{Subscribed,Credit,Unsubscribed}` on a raw acceptor, `Consumer` with `open`/`deliver`, `Subscriber::grant`, `TransferMeta::topic` (a producer can now label a message, which is what a consumer's filter selects on), and `Namespace` tracks which queue paths a connection consumes on so a **closed** connection tells the queue, not just an UNSUBSCRIBE. Two refactors the slice forced rather than chose: `handle_subscription` became route-aware (a publisher's path keeps its subscription in `SubRegistry`, a queue's arrives as an event) and **`matches_filter` moved from `weida::pubsub` into `weida_protocol::filter::matches`**, because a second matcher for one grammar is the drift a single definition exists to prevent. Delivery scans past a message no consumer filters for — strict FIFO would let one unmatched head block the queue forever — and requeues at the head when a write fails. 13 broker tests (7 delivery, 8 admission... 15 total with the queue units), 2 new hostile tests replacing `the_first_retired_frame_kind_is_now_unknown`, which pinned a fact that is no longer true: kind 5 is live, so what is worth testing is that a credit frame for a path with no queue is **ignored** and the connection survives, and that a malformed credit header still closes it. Linux 1734.

note: **half of that rule was wrong, and the wrong half is withdrawn.** Monotone-highest-wins stands, and it is what makes a reordered grant harmless. "A consumer pauses by restating the count it has already been delivered" does not: `Consumers::grant` ignores any limit that is not strictly greater than the standing one, so restating the delivered count changes nothing unless the consumer had already exhausted its credit — in which case nothing was being delivered anyway. The claim was stated as normative in four places ([PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.6 rule 3, `CreditHeader`'s doc, `weida-broker`'s module doc and `Subscriber::grant`'s doc) and is now replaced by what the code does: credit is cumulative and monotone, the only pause is the `0` a fresh subscription starts at, and lowering a standing limit would need a wire field that tells a newer statement from an older one — without which a "pause" could be undone by a delayed earlier grant. A consumer that wants to stay in control grants in increments it is willing to receive.

### B-203 — Settlement and requeue: a delivery is not a deletion
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done c7ea8cb | needs: [B-202, B-263]
acceptance: **the first slice of the outcome half: settlement and requeue.** A delivery orders `Processed` on its own DATA header and the broker keeps the `Cursors` handle it gets back; the message does **not** leave the queue at delivery but moves to an `unsettled` table **keeping its charge against `queue_bytes`**, because a message that may come back is memory the queue still owes. The consumer's `Processed` settles it — dropped, charge released — and the end of the report without one (subscription gone, connection dead) **requeues** it at the head. `max_unsettled` becomes live in the same commit that renames it from `max_unacked`, as a per-subscription bound in the delivery scan: a subscription at its bound is not eligible, which is the bound `INVARIANTS.md` currently says is dead. The queue's event loop gains one internal channel of exactly `max_unsettled` capacity so a settlement is an event of the **one** loop rather than a second writer — one watcher task per outstanding delivery, which is the shape the fan-out's writers and a survey's collectors already have. Tests: a consumer that reports sees the message once and the queue empty; a consumer whose subscription goes without reporting makes the message reappear for another consumer; a subscription at `max_unsettled` receives nothing more while its outstanding deliveries stand, and resumes when one settles; an unsettled message still counts against `queue_bytes`. **Split out of this item, in order:** B-267 (`delivery_attempt` on the wire), B-268 (the relay of 0029), B-269 (`retire`).
note: done c7ea8cb. **The delivery loop grew a second event, and that is the whole design.** `serve_queue` was a loop over one acceptor; it is now a `tokio::select!` over the acceptor and an `mpsc` of `Settlement`, so an outcome nobody asked for is applied by the same task that admits and delivers — one order, one budget, one writer. Each delivery keeps the `Cursors` it gets back (`weida::Consumer::deliver` drops them, hence a four-line local `deliver`), hands the message to `Queue::hold` still charged, and spawns **one watcher task** that reads that one report and sends `Settled` or `Lost`. `Lost` is the interesting one: `Cursors::changed` resolves to `None` for a finished reporter, a reset stream **and** a dead connection, three cases 0023 made deliberately indistinguishable and all three meaning the same thing to a queue, so the requeue needs no timer and this slice invents none. Four tests, each **mutation-checked**: disabling the requeue, removing the bound and dropping the charge each fail exactly the test that claims it (the third fails all four, because the charge underflows). Two things the writing found. **The attempt counter was wrong where it stood**: incremented before the write, a delivery whose write failed locally made the next one claim to be a repeat, which B-267 would have put on the wire — it is now counted after the bytes are out. And **the channel's capacity argument was false**: `max_unsettled` is per subscription, so a queue with several consumers can fill it; harmless, because the receiver is the queue's own loop and never waits on a watcher, but the comment claiming it "cannot fill" is gone. The rename landed in all four documents, and 0018 §5's snapshot of the item as it was *proposed* is kept with a pointer rather than rewritten: what the note asked for stays readable.
note: this is the slice that makes the broker worth its hop. Without a consumer acknowledgement the queue is RabbitMQ's `no-ack` mode, which its own documentation calls unsafe.
note: **unblocked by the owner (2026-09-15) and every open choice is now answered.** The two the item was parked for were answered by the cursor stream (see the note above). The four that were left are answered here, in the owner's words where they are theirs. **(1) The relay is intended**: the consumer's report travels to the producer as the *consumer's*, while the broker's own certificate stays `Accepted` — producer and consumer are the two ends of one pattern and the queue is its durable middle, which is why B-263 amends [0018](decisions/0018-minimal-broker.md) §4.3 rather than this item working around it. **(2) No timer, and the reason is that there are three failure modes with three answers**: packet loss is QUIC's retransmission, a dead consumer is the requeue on subscription loss, and a consumer that holds a delivery without reporting is bounded by `max_unsettled` — a visibility timeout would invent duplicates weida did not have to invent, and it stays a named option in B-263's note rather than code. **(3) The names**: the state is `Unsettled` and the bound is **`max_unsettled`** rather than `max_unacked`, because there is no ack here, there is a report; the wire flag for a repeat is **`delivery_attempt`**, a counter rather than a boolean (DATA key `12`, since 9-11 are the report order), because a count detects a poison message and costs the same varint; and the queue drain is **`retire(deadline)`** returning `Retired { settled, unsettled, requeued }`, because [0009](decisions/0009-drain.md) §4.7 forbids presenting it as the L0 drain. **(4) No new types on the client side**: a consumer is an ordinary `weida::Subscriber` that grants credit, and settlement is the `Reporter` the delivery's `IncomingTransfer` hands out — so the parked branch `broker-b203-wip`'s `Consumer`/`IncomingDelivery` rename is void, and that branch is to be discarded rather than rebased.
note: **from the review pass at `d666c1a`: the rename is part of this item, in code and in four documents.** `BrokerConfig::max_unacked` exists today with a default of `256` and **no reader** — [INVARIANTS.md](INVARIANTS.md) says so in as many words, because a cumulative credit limit cannot be capped without capping a subscription's lifetime count — and [0029](decisions/0029-a-report-is-relayed-a-certificate-is-not.md) §4.7 names the live version `max_unsettled`, on the grounds that nothing here is an ack. So the tree currently has two names for one bound. This item renames the field, and with it the four places that name it: `PROTOCOL.md` §6.6's prose and §10's table, `INVARIANTS.md`'s queue paragraph, and 0018 §4.8. The field stops being dead in the same commit that renames it, which is the only honest moment to do it.

note: **the two design choices this item was parked for are now answered, and it stays `blocked` on the owner's word rather than on them.** (1) A delivery does **not** become bidirectional: consumer settlement is a cursor on a cursor stream, so a delivery keeps the one-way topology a queue delivery had, and the `Consumer`/`IncomingDelivery` rename the parked work-in-progress introduced is no longer forced by an ack needing a reply half. (2) The alternative 0018 §6 named — a frame kind `6` carrying batched acks in RabbitMQ's `multiple` shape — is **spent**: kind `6` is the cursor stream, and a cursor is already cumulative because it is an absolute offset, so batching needs no `multiple` flag. One mechanical consequence for the parked branch `broker-b203-wip`: its `redelivered` DATA key must move from `9` to **12**, because 9, 10 and 11 are the report order (`docs/PROTOCOL.md` §6.2).
note: **blocked by me, on the user.** Started and then stopped mid-slice on their word: they have design changes for the broker and name components that are still missing, and the two choices this slice would fix in place are exactly the reversible ones — whether a delivery is an **exchange** (the ack needs a reply half; the alternative 0018 §6 itself names is a kind `6` carrying batched acks in RabbitMQ's `multiple` shape) and whether a queue consumer is its own client endpoint or a `Subscriber` with an answer. The work in progress is uncommitted and does not compile: `weida-protocol` DATA key `9` (`redelivered`), delivery turned bidirectional, `Consumer` renamed to `ConsumerHandle` with a new client-side `Consumer`/`IncomingDelivery` in `crates/weida/src/consumer.rs`, and in the broker a `Bytes` payload with an attempt counter, `Outstanding`/`Outcome`/`Settled`, `ack_wait` (30 s), `max_unacked` as a bound on *unsettled* deliveries and an event loop with an outcome channel and a task per delivery. Unwritten: `Broker::drain`, the requeue tests, the documentation. `main` is green without any of it.


### B-267 — A redelivery is visible as one: DATA key `12`
kind: code | size: 60 | status: ready | needs: [B-203]
acceptance: `delivery_attempt` reaches the wire and a consumer can read it. `DataHeader` gains `delivery_attempt: Option<u64>` with the encoder writing it only from `2` upwards ("absent means `1`", [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.2), a golden vector in §8's shape, and the fuzz target extended; `TransferMeta` and `IncomingMeta` carry it, so a `Subscriber` reads `meta.delivery_attempt` without a new call. `weida-broker` counts an attempt per requeue on the `QueuedMessage` and writes it on the next delivery. Tests: a consumer that lets one delivery go unsettled and takes the redelivery reads `2`; a first delivery carries **no** key at all, asserted on the bytes rather than on the decoded value; the count survives two requeues. The Python surfaces get the field with the other `IncomingMeta` attributes and its parity row.
note: split out of B-203 at its start, because requeue is verifiable without the label — the same payload arriving twice is the proof — while the label needs a codec change, a vector and a fuzz target of its own. That is a wire slice and this repository has never mixed one into a behaviour slice.

### B-268 — The broker relays its consumer's report to the producer
kind: code | size: 90 | status: ready | needs: [B-203]
acceptance: [0029](decisions/0029-a-report-is-relayed-a-certificate-is-not.md) §4.3 and §4.4 in code. The broker reads the **producer's** report order (keys `9`-`11`), answers itself what it can (`Accepted`, already done), and for a level only a consumer can answer **orders it on the delivery** at its own report id; when the consumer's record arrives it re-emits the same `(level, offset)` pair on the producer-facing cursor stream under the report id **the producer** allocated. Two prohibitions are tests rather than prose: a level the broker never received for that message is never emitted, and a report that arrived as a re-emission is never re-emitted — so a claim travels at most one queue from its observer. The producer's own certificate stays `Accepted` however the consumer answers, which is the hop-locality [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §2 keeps. Tests: a producer that orders `Processed` and a consumer that reports it sees it, at the payload's own offset; a producer that orders it against a consumer that never reports sees **nothing** and no error; a producer that orders nothing gets no cursor stream at all; and the relayed offset equals the producer's payload length, which is the byte-identity condition §4.3 makes the rule.
note: split out of B-203 at its start. It needs settlement to exist first — there is nothing to relay until a consumer's report is being read — and it is the half that touches two connections at once, which is where the report-id scoping of PROTOCOL §6.2 earns its rule.

### B-269 — `retire(deadline)`: a queue's drain is not the transport's
kind: code | size: 60 | status: ready | needs: [B-203]
acceptance: the four steps of [0018](decisions/0018-minimal-broker.md) §4.7 as `Broker::retire(Duration) -> Retired { settled, unsettled, requeued }`: stop admitting (new messages refused with ERROR `{REJECTED}`, as a full queue already is), keep delivering under credit already granted, wait up to the deadline for outstanding deliveries to settle, then requeue what is still unsettled and report the three counts. The deadline is **mandatory and finite** ([0009](decisions/0009-drain.md) §4.3) and the name is `retire` rather than `drain` because 0009 §4.7 forbids presenting it as the L0 drain — its documentation says so in one sentence and says that it certifies nothing about any peer. Tests: a queue with a silent consumer terminates at its deadline and reports one requeued; a queue whose consumer settles inside the deadline reports one settled and nothing requeued; a producer's send after `retire` begins is `Rejected`; and `retire` on an empty queue returns three zeros without waiting out its deadline.
note: split out of B-203 at its start, and it is last of the three because it is the only one that needs all of settlement, the unsettled table and the counts to exist before its own report means anything.

### B-211 — The JVM foundation: `weida-jvm-core`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: blocked | needs: []
acceptance: `crates/jvm/weida-jvm-core` and its `weida-jvm-core` jar, versioned and released as one pair, holding the four things of [0019](decisions/0019-jvm-binding.md) §4.2 and no protocol: the errno-to-exception-class generator (one class per variant under one base, the shape `crates/py/weida-py-core/src/errors.rs` has for Python), the `CompletableFuture` bridge of §4.3 over `weida_runtime::Exec` with `defaultExecutor()` and `newIncompleteFuture()` overridden, cancellation propagated into a Rust task abort, a panic surfacing as an exception rather than a hang, and the generation-tagged handle table with `AutoCloseable` plus a `Cleaner` backstop. `[dependencies]` is `jni` and `weida-runtime`; `weida`, `weida-protocol` and every protocol library are absent, asserted by a test over the manifest as the Python foundation's is. Bytecode target 17. Three JUnit tests that would fail on a plausible bug: a future completed from a reactor thread whose `thenApply` continuation observes the caller's executor and never a reactor thread; a cancelled future whose Rust task is observably dropped; a forged and a reused handle both raising the named exception instead of dereferencing anything.

note: **blocked on a toolchain this workstation does not have.** `javac`, `java`, `gradle` and `mvn` are all absent, so a JNI slice can be neither compiled nor verified here, and installing a JDK is a `sudo pacman -S jdk17-openjdk gradle` the loop does not get to make. ### B-218 — The discovery form: a portless URL resolves a node set
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: `weida://name/path` without a port resolves SRV `_weida._udp.name` and, on an empty answer, A/AAAA of the same name plus the default port, bounded by `max_resolved_addresses` and with SRV priority honoured and **weight ignored** ([0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.2); a URL with a port resolves nothing and dials exactly one endpoint. `Discovery::{Aware, Single}` on `RuntimeConfig` with `Aware` the default. Tests drive a stub resolver rather than DNS: a set of three answers dialled in priority order, an empty SRV answer falling through to A/AAAA, an answer longer than the bound truncated at it, and a portless URL under `Single` refused at parse with a message naming the reason.
note: the one slice that is useful before any cluster exists — a portless URL against a headless service already selects one of several equivalent brokers.

note: done. **The SRV half turned out not to be needed for the case that motivated it, and that is a correction to [0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.2 rather than a shortcut.** A headless Service's A/AAAA answer already *is* the node set — the Kubernetes documentation's own words — and every Pod of one Service listens on the same container port, so the port SRV would carry is a constant: `DEFAULT_PORT` = **7443** in `weida-core`. That works with the resolver the runtime already has (`tokio::net::lookup_host` returns every address a name answers with), while SRV needs a **DNS client** — neither std nor tokio resolves it — and therefore a dependency decision, filed as B-226 rather than taken here. What landed: `EndpointAddr::port` is now `Option<u16>`, and a portless authority is a *statement* rather than a missing value; `Display` round-trips it (a printed address that gained a port would mean something else); the pinned form works portless too. `Discovery::{Aware, Single}` on `RuntimeConfig`, `Aware` by default, and the whole client half of §4.2 is one testable function — `dial_port(written, discovery, host)` — which uses a written port as written, discovers `DEFAULT_PORT` under `Aware`, and **refuses** under `Single` with a message naming the host and the mode. Mutation-checked: with `Single` also discovering, the test fails. One existing test had pinned the old rule — `weida://127.0.0.1/x` sat in the reject list — and it moved out with the reason written where it was, because the rule changed deliberately. 4 new tests, workspace **1738**.

### B-229 — Several groups on one connection, and a queue group created by the control group
kind: code | size: 90 | status: blocked | needs: [B-224, B-227]
acceptance: `weida-raft` carries **more than one group** over one connection per peer pair — a group identifier in the RPC envelope, one exchange per RPC as B-224 built it, and a test asserting that a stalled RPC in one group does not delay another group's heartbeat ([0022](decisions/0022-consensus-topology.md) §4.5). The broker then creates a queue's group from a committed control-group entry: a three-node cluster where a queue is created, replicated, and its group dissolved when the replication factor drops to 1, with the queue still serving throughout.
note: this is the item that makes the topology of [0022](decisions/0022-consensus-topology.md) real rather than described; it needs the transport and the log first.

### B-230 — The availability split: administration stops, messaging does not
kind: code | size: 60 | status: blocked | needs: [B-229]
acceptance: the property of [0022](decisions/0022-consensus-topology.md) §4.7 proved rather than claimed: with the **control group** below quorum, no queue may be created, deleted or reconfigured — each refused with a message naming the reason — while every existing queue keeps admitting and delivering under its own group's quorum. And the converse: a queue whose own group is below quorum refuses admission and pauses delivery while every other queue on the same nodes is unaffected. Two tests, each killing a different majority.
note: this is the reason the topology has two kinds of group at all, so it is the test that earns the design. Deliberately unlike Kafka's fencing, and the note says why.

### B-239 — The three ack modes, including `fin_only`
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: [B-233]
acceptance: a sender states per message which levels it wants and in which mode — `per_chunk`, `coalesced { bytes, interval }`, `fin_only` ([0024](decisions/0024-three-families-one-back-channel.md) §4.3) — and the reporting side honours it, with `coalesced` proved lossless: a reporter that coalesces 100 advances into 3 frames leaves the receiver with the same final offset per level as one that reports all 100, because cursors are absolute. `fin_only` produces **exactly one** cursor frame per level and is the mode an adapter with a whole-message ack maps to; a test asserts that `fin_only` plus a `Stored` level is byte-for-byte the classic confirm, so the migration story is real rather than asserted.
note: the mode is configuration, not protocol: the frame of B-233 is unchanged by all three.

note: done. **Two modes reached the wire, not three, and the third was a contradiction inside its own acceptance.** `coalesced { bytes, interval }` as a *wire* mode puts the granularity in the sender's header — which is a negotiated granularity, and [0023](decisions/0023-completion-is-a-cursor.md) §4.5 says it is "the reporter's own number, never negotiated". Both cannot hold, so `ReportMode` carries `progress` and `final-only`, and `Reporter::with_granularity(bytes, interval)` carries how finely: `per_chunk` is that granularity set to zero, the default is 1 MiB or 100 ms whichever comes first, and 0024 §4.3 is corrected rather than worked around. Lossless is **measured**, not asserted: the same hundred advances emit 2 records coarse and 100 fine, and both leave the reader at offset 100 — the count comes from a raw peer reading the cursor stream, because the reader-side API keeps only the latest value per level, which is the property under test. `final-only` is exactly one record per level (twenty calls, two records). The acceptance's last clause is **withdrawn**: `final_only` plus `Stored` is not byte-for-byte the classic confirm and cannot be — a cursor is a CURSOR stream, the classic confirm is DATA key `8` on an exchange's reply half. The migration story is the shape, not the bytes, and the confirm itself stays untouched (B-201), which M4's broker test pins.

### B-240 — The cursor stream, orthogonal to every pattern
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: [B-239]
acceptance: the reporting side opens a uni cursor stream per payload stream it reports on, and the payload topology is **unchanged** in every case ([0024](decisions/0024-three-families-one-back-channel.md) §4.4a). Proved where it is surprising: a Push transfer with cursors ordered is still **one unidirectional** payload stream with unchanged round-robin selection, refusal behaviour and delivery semantics, plus a reliable verdict on a separate stream; with none ordered it is byte-for-byte today's transfer. An exchange reporting both directions has **two** cursor streams and one bidi, and a test asserts that each side's own `report_id` space disambiguates them without a direction field. Two load-bearing negatives: a stalled or never-read cursor stream blocks no transfer, and a peer that ignores cursor streams entirely completes every transfer and every guarantee.
note: the reply half stays the application's, so the broker's confirm (DATA key `8`, B-201) is untouched and `fin_only` on an exchange needs no cursor stream at all.

note: done. The stream half landed with the cursor API in `weida`: `CursorSet`, `Cursors`, `Reporter`, `TransferMeta::with_report`, `OutgoingTransfer::cursors` and `IncomingTransfer::reporter`, with `crates/weida/tests/cursors.rs` proving the topology against a **raw** peer — the library's own API cannot assert "one uni stream and no bidi one". Both load-bearing negatives have a test: a sender that never reads its cursors still completes a 1 MiB transfer, and a receiver that never reports fails nothing. One thing building it added that no acceptance asked for: `Cursors::changed()` watches the **connection** as well as the channel, because a peer that simply never reports opens no stream and FINs nothing — without that leg a reader would wait for an end that is not coming.

### B-241 — ARCHITECTURE §1: three vocabularies instead of an onion
kind: spec | size: 30 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §1 no longer opens with "weida is three layers" and a stack diagram, but with the surface a user actually chooses from — stream, message, broker — the overlap between the families and the reason for it, and the dependency direction as a single sentence rather than a picture. The L0/L1/L2 names survive only where they name crate boundaries, which is what they are.
note: done. The onion was never wrong about dependencies and always wrong as a user's mental model, and the document said so itself two screens further down: "L2 is a layer, not a fork".

### B-236 — PAIR: one connection, one peer, a narrower API
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the pattern [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §6b maps as "one connection, one exchange or one one-way transfer each way", built as the API surface the owner asked for rather than as new protocol. Exactly one peer: a second connection to a bound `Pair` is refused with a stop code and the first is **kept** (ZeroMQ's PAIR drops the new one silently; refusing and saying so is this repository's rule for a capacity decision, [decisions/0005](decisions/0005-refusal-race.md)). Both directions usable concurrently, cancellation and the delivery receipt behaving exactly as the primitives already do, and a test proving the pattern adds no wire vocabulary: a `Pair` talks to a bare `Peer`/`Acceptor` on the same path.
note: architecturally identical to what exists, which is why it is the smallest of the three and the right one to build first: it proves the pattern layer is API and nothing else.

note: done. `weida::Paired`, `Runtime::pair` and `Listener::pair`, with **no wire vocabulary added** — a test has a pair talking to a bare `Peer` and `Acceptor` on the same path. Two corrections the acceptance needed. It is **one-way transfers in both directions**, not "one exchange or one one-way transfer each way": an exchange's reply half would make one side a replier, and PAIR is symmetric. And a **bound** pair learns which connection its peer is on only when that peer first speaks, so its first send waits for the peer rather than buffering — the accepting side of this library has no other way to address a peer it has not heard from, and a queue there would have been a guarantee nobody asked for. The refusal needed one thing beyond the pattern: `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` as a **stop** code had no `StopReason`, so a sender read it as `Transport("code 5")`; `weida-core` gains `StopReason::LimitExceeded` — additive, two match sites, both inside core and protocol — so a refused newcomer reads `Error::LimitExceeded`.

### B-237 — SURVEY: fan-out of exchanges with a deadline
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: `Surveyor::survey` opens one exchange per connected respondent and yields replies as they arrive until a deadline, then stops; late replies are dropped and **counted**, not delivered, and the count is observable the way the fan-out drop counter already is ([GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6). A respondent that refuses, a respondent that dies mid-reply and a respondent that answers after the deadline are three separate tests with three distinct outcomes; a survey with no respondents returns an empty result rather than an error, because "nobody answered" is an answer. The deadline is the caller's and is not negotiated.
note: the only pattern of the family with a time dimension, which is why it is worth a type: the deadline, the partial result and the late-reply rule are exactly what an application otherwise rebuilds wrongly.

note: done. `weida::Surveyor`, `SurveyRun`, `weida::Respondent`, `Runtime::surveyor` and `Listener::respondent`. **One primitive was missing, not a pattern's worth**: `Peer::open_bi` picks *one* peer, which is Req/Rep's selection policy, so the body moved into `stream::open_exchange_on` and a survey is a fan-out of it plus a deadline. A respondent's route is **byte-for-byte a replier's**, so the pattern adds no routing at all. Two rules are where a hand-built survey goes wrong and both have a test: a late reply is counted **where it arrives** rather than where the caller reads, so `late()` means "after the deadline" whatever the caller does with its handle; and a survey with no respondents is an empty run rather than `NotConnected`, because "nobody answered" is an answer. `next(max_bytes)` reads the reply under the caller's cap, so nothing is buffered before a cap exists.

### B-238 — BUS: n peers, each bound and connected
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: [B-236]
acceptance: the mapping of [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §6b made real — each member holds a `Peer` per remote plus an `Acceptor` on its own path — with the property that distinguishes a bus from a fan-out: a message sent by a member reaches every **other** member and never the sender itself. Joining and leaving are ordinary connect and disconnect, a member that dies is dropped from the set without affecting the others, and the fan-out is best effort per peer with the same counted drops as Pub/Sub. No relay: a bus of *n* members is *n* × (*n*-1) deliveries and weida does not forward on anyone's behalf, which is the trade nanomsg's BUS makes too.
note: completes the nanomsg pattern set (PAIR, REQREP, PUBSUB, PIPELINE, SURVEY, BUS) and with it [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §6b's table.

note: done. `weida::BusMember` and `Listener::bus(path, tls)` — the only factory that takes both a path and dialling terms, because a bus member is the one role that is bound and dialling at once. A message reaches every **other** member **structurally**: `send` writes to the peers this member dialled, and a member does not dial itself, so nothing filters a copy out because no copy is ever addressed to it. **The acceptance's "same counted drops as Pub/Sub" turned out to require Pub/Sub's shape, not just its counter**: with an inline send, a member that stops reading stalls its stream tasks, the sender exhausts `max_concurrent_uni_streams` and `open_uni` blocks — so one writer task per member, drop-and-count at the queue when it is full and at the wire when a write fails. A test with a one-slot queue and 1 MiB payloads observes both, and asserts every copy is either accepted for delivery or counted. BUS is the second pattern to need the per-peer writer, which is what makes it a rule rather than a Pub/Sub detail.

### B-242 — The failure matrix `PATTERNS.md` §1.11 claims
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: [B-236, B-237, B-238, B-240]
acceptance: `crates/weida/tests/interrupted.rs`, one test per row of [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.11's per-pattern table, each asserting **both** sides' observation: a transfer interrupted before FIN is a definite `ConnectionLost` for the sender; a reader never sees an interrupted stream as complete — `collect` with `Error::Canceled`, `AsyncRead` with `ConnectionReset` and **never** `Ok(0)`; a Req/Rep requester learns `Indeterminate` and can re-issue; a Push producer is the only side that can reschedule; a Pub/Sub copy that cannot be taken is counted and never re-sent; and a cursor reported before the break survives it, which is the whole value of a cursor over a verdict. Plus one liveness test, because a reliable work chain depends on bounded detection: a bound side observes a dead dialler within the `idle_timeout`, with both knobs shortened in the test's own `RuntimeConfig`. Adds no API.
note: these were documented claims, not tested ones. The file proves the document rather than the other way round.

note: done. Seven tests, and three of them needed the failure to be **made** rather than described. A dead dialler cannot be simulated by dropping a handle — that closes politely — so the test gives the dialler its own reactor with `Runtime::owned` and drops it: the threads that drive the connection stop, no FIN and no close frame is ever sent, and the bound side is left with exactly the case an idle timeout exists for. An unanswered request cannot be simulated by dropping the `IncomingRequest` either, because that sends ERROR `NO_REPLY`, which is a *definite* answer; the replier leaks it instead, so the requester learns nothing and reads `Indeterminate`. And a Pub/Sub drop is deterministic only because `publish` is **synchronous**: it charges the subscriber's byte budget before a writer task can run, so a 128 KiB burst against a 4 KiB budget drops without a race, and the test then asserts that exactly the copies that were sent arrive — a dropped copy is never re-sent.

note: **the second of those seven claims is transport-qualified, and the acceptance above does not say so.** `a_reader_never_sees_an_interrupted_stream_as_complete` runs over QUIC, and over `AF_UNIX` the same interruption is read as a complete, truncated transfer, because a stream socket has no abort — B-245. The test is right about what it tests; the sentence would be wrong if it were read as a statement about every transport.

### B-246 — Every DATA header pays for a `traceparent` nobody asked for
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: `crates/weida/src/transfer.rs`'s `data_header` sets `traceparent: Some(trace.to_traceparent())` on **every** DATA header and mints a fresh root context when the caller supplied none, so every transfer on every pattern pays a fixed 55-byte W3C string plus its CBOR framing whether anything traces or not — on a 64-byte push that is the largest single item of a 135-byte DATA frame, against ZMTP's one to nine bytes of framing per message and SP's eight ([IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §4). No decision note justifies making it unconditional; §6 only defers the OpenTelemetry exporter, which is a different question. A **decision note first**, because the bytes a peer observes change either way: propagation stays unconditional, becomes conditional on the caller having supplied a `TraceContext`, or becomes a runtime setting — with the cost of each named, including what a broken trace chain costs an operator and what an absent `traceparent` means for a receiver that reads `IncomingMeta::trace`. Then the chosen behaviour is implemented and the overhead number is **re-measured** wherever the documents quote it, not adjusted by arithmetic.
note: filed rather than fixed, because "always propagate" is a defensible answer and the item is the decision, not the deletion. Whichever way it goes, a second implementation reading [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.2 needs to know whether DATA key `3` is always present — today it is on every frame this library sends, and nothing says so on purpose.
note: done, decided as **propagate only** ([0028](decisions/0028-trace-propagation-is-the-callers.md)). The bytes made it urgent and they are not what decided it: **a minted root is not a safe default, it is a fabricated fact** — a hop that received a context and forgot to propagate it emitted a *new trace* rather than nothing, so a collector saw two unrelated traces instead of one visible gap. "Always mint" fails as a lie that looks like data; "propagate only" fails as a gap that looks like a gap. A runtime setting was rejected too: it would make the bytes a peer observes depend on a local configuration nobody declared, which is the class of thing [0006](decisions/0006-guarantee-sets.md)'s negotiated dimensions exist for, and its "on" position is the behaviour being removed. Implemented: key `3` is written exactly when the caller supplied a context, on every pattern including both publish paths; `weida::new_trace()` is public so starting a trace is one deliberate line; `OutgoingTransfer::trace()` returns `Option<TraceContext>`. **Re-measured rather than derived**, two runs, same desktop as B-009: a 64-byte push is **76 B** at **192.6-195.9 Kmsg/s** against **136 B** at 168.9-171.2 Kmsg/s with a caller's context — so the context costs 60 B of frame (58 of header, 2 of the frame's length field) and **13-14 % of the message rate**, both variants in one run because this machine's spread is wider than the effect. The headline: **twelve bytes of framing around 64 bytes of payload**, half of it the endpoint path, 84 % of the wire payload — weida is now in ZMTP's and SP's class on small messages instead of 71 bytes worse. Two secondary findings. The bench's `wire_bytes` helper **hardcoded** a `traceparent` and would have kept reporting 135 B after the code stopped writing one, so it now builds the header the send path builds and the bench gained a `push_64b_traced` variant. And one existing test pinned the old behaviour (`sub_meta_carries_topic_and_trace` asserted `meta.trace.is_some()`); it is rewritten to assert the difference between `publish` and `publish_with_trace` rather than re-pinned to the new text. Three tests bite the rule, checked by restoring the mint.

### B-247 — What per-subscriber fan-out costs, measured against the alternative
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: weida gives every subscriber its own stream, which buys the isolation one shared queue per socket cannot ([PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.3, §4) — and nothing in this repository measures what that costs when the fan-out is large. A benchmark in the shape the existing measurements have (`crates/weida/tests/streams.rs`'s numbers are quoted with their conditions) reporting **per-subscriber latency, median and tail, and publisher throughput at a stated fan-out** — at least 1, 16 and 256 subscribers, one payload size, loopback, with the runtime configuration and the machine stated beside the number so it can be reproduced or dismissed. The result lands as a number in [STATUS.md](STATUS.md) and, where it changes an argument, in the document making that argument; a fan-out at which `publish` stops keeping up is reported as the number it is rather than as a caveat.
note: the isolation argument is sound and unpriced, which is exactly the shape of claim this repository otherwise refuses. Deliberately not a design item: nothing here proposes a shared queue, only that the trade be quantified before anyone is asked to accept it.
note: **the C8B question makes this the load-bearing measurement of the whole guide.** [0026](decisions/0026-the-guide-and-the-c8b-question.md)'s arithmetic says two to four hops reach eight billion people, and that depth rests entirely on the fan-out width one node can hold — derived in [GUIDE.md](GUIDE.md) §0.1 as 10³-10⁴ from B-011's per-connection memory and the default 8 MiB subscriber budget, and **never measured above eight subscribers** (B-021's bench). So this item now decides two things beyond its own acceptance: whether the depth argument survives contact with a real fan-out, and which of the two bounds — transport state or the byte budget — binds first at width. Guide chapter 2 (B-257) is blocked on it rather than written from the defaults, because an estimate there would be the one thing §0 promises the guide contains none of. Worth extending the acceptance when it is taken: report the width at which each bound binds, not only the latency at three widths.
note: done. `crates/weida/benches/fanout.rs`, one client runtime per subscriber as in B-011, both ends in this process, three consecutive runs; the table is [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §4's new section and the argument it settles is [GUIDE.md](GUIDE.md) §0.1's. **The isolation is cheaper than the derivation assumed and the limit is somewhere else entirely.** Per-subscriber transport state is **396-436 KiB** at widths 16 and 256 (both ends together), publisher CPU is **88.8 ns per subscriber** per message (22.94 µs at width 256 against 287 ns at width 1 — the marginal cost *falls* with width, because the fixed cost amortizes and the registry walk gets its cache), and idle latency is **507-539 µs** median at 256 against 34.7-37.9 µs at 1. So neither memory nor CPU binds at 10⁴ subscribers: 64 GiB is ~1.5·10⁵ subscribers of transport state and 10⁴ subscribers is 0.9 ms of publish. **What binds is the delivery rate, 276-279 Kcopies/s**, which is 36 ms of a node's capacity for one message to 10⁴ subscribers and puts a width-10⁴ node at about 27 messages per second. The guide's width interval survived — 10³-10⁴ — for a completely different reason than the one it was derived from, and §0.1 now says which. **Two measurement mistakes are recorded in the bench's own comments** because both are easy to repeat: the first version published 64 messages back to back and called the arrival delay "latency", which is a **backlog** (21.5 ms median at width 256 against 507 µs for the same path with one message in flight — two orders of magnitude, and the wrong number to plan with); and the first stalled-subscriber run reported an accepted count without naming the drop **cause**, which is the only thing that distinguishes the two ceilings. Fixed: two labelled latency phases, and `dropped_on` read for the cause. **The bound question has a clean answer and it is not the payload size.** A copy's budget permit is released when its bytes reach the wire, and a connection absorbs 0.6-1.5 MiB before it stops, so a budget *below* that band is spent while QUIC still accepts (the budget refuses, measured at 64 KiB and 256 KiB) and a budget *above* it never fills (the queue backs up and refuses, measured at the 8 MiB default, 1153-1537 messages of 1 KiB). One correction for anyone sizing a publisher: `subscribers × subscriber_buffer_bytes` **overstates** it, because one `publish` is one `Bytes` every copy shares — 16 stalled subscribers accounted 18-24 MiB of budget while holding 5.4-19.0 MiB resident in total.

### B-248 — One `unsafe` guard covers one crate while seven manifests claim it
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: every crate that sets `unsafe_code = "allow"` is covered by a guard that fails when a hand-written `unsafe` block or `unsafe impl` appears in its own `src`, and the guard is **one mechanism rather than seven copies** — a shared helper each crate's test calls, or one test that walks the manifests that set the allowance. Today `crates/py/weida-py-core/tests/no_handwritten_unsafe.rs` scans `Path::new(env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR")).join("src")`, so it checks exactly one crate, while `weida-zmq-py`, `weida-nng-py`, `weida-mqtt-py`, `weida-amqp-py`, `weida-nats-py` and `weida-py` each set the allowance under a comment asserting that no `unsafe` is written by hand in that crate: six claims with nothing behind them. The guard walks `src` recursively rather than one directory deep, so a module directory added later is not a silent hole, and it keeps the existing test's two assertions — that it scanned something at all, and that a doc comment mentioning the word is not code. Verified by making it red once per covered crate: a hand-written `unsafe {}` in that crate's `src` fails that crate's guard and no other.
note: the reason the property is worth a test is in the existing test's own module documentation — "we allowed it for the macros" stops being true the moment somebody writes an `unsafe` block under that allowance and nothing complains. Six crates are in exactly that position, and a PyO3 crate cannot use `forbid` because `#[pyclass]` expands to `unsafe impl`.
note: done, and the shape is the second of the acceptance's two: **one test that walks the manifests**, not a helper seven tests call. `crates/weida/tests/unsafe_guard.rs` finds every crate under `crates/` that sets `unsafe_code = "allow"` — seven today — and scans each one's `src` **recursively**, which the guard it replaces did not do: that one read a single directory level, so a module in a subdirectory was unchecked. A crate that gains the allowance tomorrow is covered by the fact of having it; nobody has to remember to copy a file, which is the property the item was really about. `crates/py/weida-py-core/tests/no_handwritten_unsafe.rs` is deleted rather than left beside it — two mechanisms for one invariant is the thing being fixed. **A second test came out of the walk and is the better half:** a manifest that says *nothing* permits hand-written `unsafe` by default and claims nothing at all, which is worse than the seven that allow it under a comment, because there is no comment to falsify. Every crate in the tree takes a position and `every_crate_takes_a_position_on_unsafe` keeps it that way. Two deliberate exclusions, named in the file: the six `fuzz/` crates declare their own `[workspace]` so no workspace lint ever applies to them, and the walk reads `src` only. Both tests bite — an `unsafe {}` in `weida-py`'s `lib.rs` fails the first, and turning one `forbid` into a `deny` fails the second.

### B-249 — The parked receipt set is polled under a `std` mutex, and the peer sizes it
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: `ConnDrain::park` runs inside `Drop for Delivery`, i.e. on the fire-and-forget path of `Pusher::send`, the Pub/Sub fan-out, `Consumer::deliver` and `BusMember::send`. At its cap it calls `parked.retain_mut(|r| settled(r).is_none())`, which polls **every** parked receipt while the `std::sync::Mutex` is held, and each poll of a `quinn` `stopped()` future takes `quinn`'s own connection-state lock. Two things are then true and neither is written down: the cap (`max_concurrent_uni_streams + max_concurrent_bidi_streams`, 3072 at the defaults) bounds *inbound* streams, while how many receipts can be pending at once is bounded by what the **peer** granted this side; and the sweep is O(parked) under a lock on the hot send path. Either show, with a number in the shape [STATUS.md](STATUS.md) §5's measurements have, that a full sweep at the cap costs less than a send, or make the set amortize it (a settled-count hint, a second deque, or a sweep budget per call) — and state which quantity the cap actually bounds.
note: filed rather than fixed because the fix without a measurement would be a guess about which side of the trade hurts. The sweep only runs at the cap, so an application that awaits its receipts never reaches it; what is unpriced is the fire-and-forget producer at full stream budget.
note: done, and the answer is the acceptance's first branch: **shown with a number that the sweep is not a problem**, no restructuring. The reason is structural rather than lucky — a receipt settles when the peer's transport acknowledges the FIN, and the stream's concurrency slot frees at the same moment, so the parked set and the in-flight stream count are bounded by the same quantity and `open` waits for a slot before the set can outgrow it. Measured through the public surface, because `Drained::outstanding` counts every receipt evicted at the cap: **20 000 fire-and-forget sends at 43.5 Kmsg/s leave 2 656 receipts parked against a cap of 3 072 and evict zero**, twice in a row, deterministically (`a_fire_and_forget_producer_never_fills_the_parked_receipt_set` in `crates/weida/tests/drain.rs`). Reaching the O(parked) sweep needs a peer that **stops acknowledging while this side keeps opening streams**, which the stream budget makes a bounded window rather than an unbounded one. The second half of the item — that the cap is taken from this side's `max_concurrent_*_streams`, which is what this runtime grants *inbound*, while what bounds a parked set is what the **peer** granted this side to open — is now written down where the field is declared: the cap is a **local heuristic**, not a derived bound, and it stays one because reading the peer's transport parameters is something `quinn` does not expose. Nothing changed in the code but the documentation and the test, which is the honest outcome of an item whose own note said a fix without a measurement would be a guess.

### B-250 — A DATA header is encoded into one `Vec` and copied into another, per message
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: `write_data_preamble` calls `DataHeader::encode`, which builds a fresh `Vec` from empty — so a ~70-byte header pays the 8/16/32/64/128 growth chain — and then allocates a second exact-capacity `Vec` and copies the whole header into it. Per one-way transfer the send path allocates: the endpoint `String` (re-owned from an `Arc<str>` the peer set already holds), the 55-byte traceparent `String` (B-246), the header `Vec` plus its growth, the preamble `Vec`, and one boxed future for the receipt. Acceptance: an `encode_into(&mut Vec<u8>)` on each header type beside the existing `encode`, the preamble written into the same buffer ahead of the header, and one reusable buffer per send path — with the message rate at a 64-byte payload measured before and after, in [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §4's shape.
note: the protocol crate owns the first half (an `encode_into` that cannot regress the canonical form) and `weida` the second. Worth doing with B-246, because the two together are what a small-message benchmark actually pays.
note: done, and the honest half of the result is that **the rate did not move**. What changed: `encode_into(&mut Vec<u8>)` on all six header types, with every `encode` now a thin wrapper over it so the canonical form has exactly one implementation — two encoders that could disagree about key order would be a wire divergence rather than an optimisation; `write_varint` into a slice and `preamble_bytes` returning a fixed array, so building a preamble allocates nothing; and `data_frame` encoding the header **after** a reserved `MAX_PREAMBLE_LEN` and right-aligning the preamble against it, so a send is one allocation with the right capacity, no copy of the header and one contiguous write. Gone per message: the header `Vec`'s 8/16/32/64/128 growth chain, the second exact-capacity `Vec`, and the copy between them. **Measured before and after on B-009's desktop**, `push_64b_keys0` 5.105-5.193 µs before and 5.067-5.256 µs after — inside the run-to-run spread, which is the expected answer once B-246 made the frame 76 bytes: a 64-byte loopback push spends ~5 µs in QUIC and two small allocations are tens of nanoseconds. The frame bytes are unchanged at 76/136/157, which is the point rather than a caveat. Kept anyway because the code is simpler — one buffer, one write — and because the allocation count is a property somebody will care about at a message rate this bench cannot reach. **One regression this shape can cause and a test for exactly it**: a preamble aligned one byte off has the same length and a different meaning, so `a_data_frame_is_built_in_one_buffer_and_is_byte_identical` compares the one-buffer bytes against `encode_frame(Data, &header.encode())` at six path lengths spanning the varint's width boundary, and re-parses the preamble to check the length field agrees with what follows it. Off-by-one in the alignment fails it. **Not done, and named**: the endpoint `String` is still re-owned from an `Arc<str>` the peer set already holds, which needs the decoder's owned form to change shape and is a bigger surgery than this item.

### B-251 — One refusal table, not one per stream kind
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: `dispatch` decides the refusal for a one-way transfer — `UNKNOWN_ENDPOINT` for no route, `UNSUPPORTED` for a `Request`/`Pub` route, `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` for a taken `Pair` — and `handle_bi` decides it again for an exchange, each pairing of `ErrorCode` and stop code written out by hand at its call site. [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §9.3's table is therefore enforced in two places, and a seventh `Route` variant added to one match and not the other is a silent divergence between what a Push sender and a Req sender learn about the same misroute. Acceptance: one function mapping a route mismatch to its `(ErrorCode, stop code)` pair, called from both dispatchers, with §9.3's table as its doc comment and a unit test per row.
note: the two agree today. This is about which change breaks them, and it is the next pattern's registration.
note: done. One `Refusal { code, stop }` with three named rows — `UNKNOWN`, `WRONG_SHAPE`, `PAIR_TAKEN` — and one `refusal_for(route, wanted)` that both dispatchers call before they hand anything over, so neither writes a stop code at a call site any more and the arms that used to decide are now `unreachable!` with the reason. [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §9.3's table is the function's doc comment. The third row is the one worth separating: a pair that already has its peer is a **capacity** decision (`LIMIT_EXCEEDED`) rather than a routing mistake, so a sender can tell the two apart — which is why it is a constant beside the table instead of a variant of the wrong-shape row. Two unit tests: one walks every pairing of route and stream kind, the other asserts the three rows are distinct on the wire. The first bites — dropping `Route::Pair` from the one-way arm fails it, which is exactly the divergence the item was filed about: the next pattern's registration reaching one dispatcher and not the other.

### B-252 — One workspace run in about twenty reports a failure nobody has seen
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the failing test is **named**, and then either fixed or filed as its own item. Two occurrences are on record, one per platform — Linux `cargo test --workspace --no-fail-fast` reported `1 failed` among 1827, and Windows reported `1 failed` among 1805 — and in both cases the output was consumed by the pipeline that was *counting* the results, so no test name, no assertion and no source line survives. It did not reproduce in twenty-six further Linux runs (twelve ordinary, eight at `--test-threads=32`, three under sixteen busy-loop processes, three plain) or four further Windows runs. Acceptance is met by a capture, not by a green run: `cargo test --workspace --no-fail-fast 2>&1 | tee /tmp/ws.log` in a loop with `grep -E '^test result: FAILED|^---- '` over the **file** rather than the stream, left running until it fires; then the name decides whether this becomes a fix or a second item.
note: filed rather than hunted further because the loop that found it had already spent thirty runs on both platforms, and because the shape is known from B-182: a timing assertion whose wait and whose assertion do not agree fails at a rate like this under load. What is *not* known is which one, and guessing at a suspect would be the mistake B-182's own note warns about ("it did not reproduce in four runs here, which is what a one-in-three race looks like from the wrong side of it"). Two rules for whoever takes it: never count test results through a pipeline that discards the failure block, and prefer `--no-capture` so a panic's own message reaches the log.
note: **done, and it was a port race rather than a timing assertion.** The run that finally captured it was not a hunt: the website's own gate run (B-253) happened to keep its log, and the failure named itself — `an_nng_push_reaches_a_zmq_pull`, `crates/interop/cross-tests/tests/cross.rs`, `zmq.rs binds: Network(AddrInUse)`. The cause is one helper, `free_port`, used by eight of the nine cross tests: bind `127.0.0.1:0` with a `TcpListener`, read the port, **drop the listener**, then hand the number to `zeromq` or `nng` to bind. Between the drop and that bind the port belongs to nobody, and this workspace's suite binds loopback ports by the hundred in parallel — which is exactly a one-in-twenty rate and exactly why it never reproduced when the cross tests ran alone. **Neither foreign library needed the guess**, which is the part worth keeping: `zeromq`'s `bind` returns the endpoint it resolved ("port # resolved, for example", says its own doc), and an `nng` listener reports the URL it bound. So the helper is gone and both halves ask the library that did the listening. One trap found on the way, and it is now a comment in the helper: `nng`'s `LocalAddr` option on a listener bound to port `0` answers with a port **nothing is listening on** — `tcp://127.0.0.1:49074` against the `Url` option's `45759` in one measured run — so the first version of this fix made the three cross tests that assert an *arrival* fail and would have let the three that assert a *loss* pass vacuously. `Url` is the option that tells the truth. Also fixed beside it: the late-puller re-listen in `the_chain_is_best_effort_end_to_end` swallowed its bind error with `let _ =`, so a failed bind would have proved the test's negative for the wrong reason; it is now retried briefly and asserted. Nine cross tests, five consecutive runs, green.

### B-244 — PAIR, SURVEY and BUS in `weida::blocking` and `weida-py`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 5cce24c | needs: [B-236, B-237, B-238]
acceptance: the three new patterns reach the surfaces this plan deliberately did not touch: `crates/weida/src/blocking.rs`, which mirrors each pattern explicitly rather than generically, and both halves of `crates/py/weida-py` (asyncio and `sync`). `Paired`, `Surveyor`/`SurveyRun`/`Respondent` and `BusMember` each gain their blocking twin with the same value types, and the Python surfaces follow their library as the other five bindings do. Tests: one round trip per pattern per surface, plus the one rule each pattern has that a caller can get wrong — a second peer refused for PAIR, the deadline for SURVEY, "never your own message" for BUS.
note: filed rather than built with the patterns because each new pattern costs three surfaces there (blocking, asyncio, `sync`) and a survey's deadline is the first weida concept whose Python shape is a design question rather than a translation.
note: done 5cce24c. `crates/weida/src/blocking.rs` gained `Paired`, `Surveyor`, `Respondent` and `BusMember` with `Runtime::pair`/`surveyor` and `Binding::pair`/`respondent`/`bus`; `crates/py/weida-py/src/patterns.rs` is the asyncio half and `sync.rs` the synchronous one, thirteen classes each surface now. Tests: three in `crates/weida/tests/blocking.rs` (7 in the file), three in `tests/test_patterns.py` (9) and three in `tests/test_sync.py` (8) — 25 Python tests, `smoke.py` resolving all 46 names through a freshly built wheel. **The design question the note flagged is answered: a survey is a value, not an iterator, on both Python surfaces.** `weida.Survey` carries `replies`, `asked`, `failed`, `late` and `silent()`, and the reason is mechanical rather than aesthetic — the only channel out of a bridged future is the errno family, so `async for` would need a second error channel invented for `StopAsyncIteration` alone. The Rust `SurveyRun` stays where answers arrive one at a time. Two things the blocking facade taught: a **bound pair must be handed back from a worker thread, not dropped** (`PairState::Drop` unregisters the route, so a dropped bound pair turns its peer's next send into `UnknownEndpoint` — the first version of the test failed exactly there), and `BusMember::send` returns a count without awaiting a receipt because a fan-out has no single peer to get one from. `blocking::Paired` also needed `path()` and `peer_count()` and `blocking::Surveyor` `peer_count()`, which the facade had skipped. Mutation-checked: `asked` off by one, the deadline ignored (the case then fails on its own 15 s bound, so the 0.5 s deadline is load-bearing), the refusal class remapped, and the bus reach count off by one — four mutations, four intended failures. Gate: 1876/0/37, `--features blocking` 282, 56 pages.

### B-243 — The cursor API in `weida-py`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 9bbebcc | needs: [B-240]
acceptance: `crates/py/weida-py` reaches the cursor surface from both of its halves, the asyncio one and `sync`, because it mirrors the pattern surface explicitly in each and neither inherits from the other: `TransferMeta`'s report order, the cursor handle a send returns (an async iterator on the asyncio side, a blocking `changed()` on the `sync` one) and the reporter an inbound transfer hands out. `IncomingMeta` gains the three fields it already carries in Rust. Tests: a Push producer that orders `Accepted` observes it, a receiver that never reports fails no transfer, and an application level round-trips uninterpreted — one pair per half, as `tests/test_pubsub.py` does for the fan-out.
note: filed rather than built with B-240 because a binding costs three surfaces (asyncio, `sync`, the value classes both share) and a cursor is the first weida concept whose reader outlives the handle it came from — which is a lifetime question in Python, not a translation.
note: done 9bbebcc, and the acceptance's "async iterator" is **not** what was built: `weida.Cursors` is a handle with `snapshot()`, `offset(level)` and `changed()`, read as `while (set := await cursors.changed()) is not None:` — for the reason B-244's `Survey` gives, that the only channel out of a bridged future is the errno family, so `StopAsyncIteration` would need a second one invented for it. The surface, on both halves: `report=`/`mode=` on `pusher.send`, `pusher.open` and `paired.send` (the two **one-way** senders, since an exchange's reply is its verdict and a fan-out copy has no single handle), `recv_reporting(max_bytes)` handing back `(payload, meta, reporter)`, `stream.cursors()`/`stream.reporter()` on the streamed form, `weida.Cursors` and `weida.Reporter` on each surface, and `IncomingMeta`'s three report fields. **A level is an integer**, not a class: the level space is open above `weida.APPLICATION_FLOOR` and a class would close what the protocol leaves open; the named rungs are module constants and an undefined value in the reserved range is `weida.Protocol` at the boundary. The facade needed the slice first, as B-244's order predicted: `weida::blocking` gained `Cursors`, `Reporter`, `Pusher::send_reporting`, `Paired::send_reporting` and `Puller`/`Paired::recv_reporting`. **The finding worth the round is the deadline.** A synchronous `changed()` with no deadline is a hang, and not a theoretical one: a mutation test parked for 300 s because a peer that never reports opens no stream and a connection both sides keep alive never closes. So `crate::Cursors::changed_within(deadline)` exists — `Reported::{Changed, Waiting, Ended}`, three outcomes because a deadline is not an ending — the blocking `changed(deadline)` is the only mandatory-deadline call this surface adds, and `weida.sync.Cursors.changed(seconds)` raises `TimeoutError`; the same mutation now fails in 15 s with that message. Mutation-checked four ways: the level boundary opened (the reserved-value case fails), the snapshot not updated after a change (`offset` returns `None`), the report order hidden from the metadata (three cases fail), and the deadline removed (the hang above). Also fixed in passing: `Exec::within`'s doc comment had been attached to `Exec::resolve`, leaving a public method undocumented and another documenting the wrong one. Gate: 1878/0/37, `--features blocking` 284, 32 Python tests, 56 pages.

### B-233 — Frame kind `6`: the cursor stream
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: `FrameKind::Cursor` = 6 on a **unidirectional** stream, as CREDIT is: a head frame naming the payload stream it reports about — QUIC's own `StreamId`, which `quinn` exposes as `SendStream::id()`/`RecvStream::id()` and which is a varint both ends already know — followed by `(level, offset)` records until FIN, where the level is the `uint` of [0023](decisions/0023-completion-is-a-cursor.md) §4.3a and the offset is absolute. Encoded and decoded in `weida-protocol` with golden vectors in §8's shape and a fuzz target beside the other headers. The receiving side keeps the **maximum** offset per level, so a duplicated or reordered record changes nothing — mutation-checked, as B-202's credit rule was. Hostile tests: a cursor record whose offset goes backwards is ignored rather than fatal, a cursor stream naming a stream id that does not exist on the connection is a protocol violation, and **[PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §4.1 is untouched** — a test asserts that a reply half still carries exactly one DATA or ERROR.
note: a cursor never shares a stream with payload, which is what makes the frame independent of the pattern: the same stream kind reports on a one-way transfer and on either direction of an exchange.

note: done. `FrameKind::Cursor` = 6, `CursorHeader`, `CursorLevel`, `ReportMode`, DATA keys `9`-`11`, four golden vectors and the `cursor` fuzz target; `PROTOCOL.md` gains §6.7 and the reserved range moves to `7..=255`. **One clause of this acceptance was wrong and is corrected in [0024](decisions/0024-three-families-one-back-channel.md) §4.4 rather than worked around: the head frame names a `report_id` the ordering DATA header allocated, not QUIC's `StreamId`.** A `StreamId` exists only on QUIC — `handle_local` has no stream-kind vocabulary at all, so inproc, `AF_UNIX` and named pipes would have had no identifier — and it is chosen by the *peer*, so a receiver would map an id it never issued; a `report_id` is allocated by the side that orders the report, so an unknown id allocates nothing and the stream is reset with `CANCELED`. The level space is open, and the split is enforced at the decoder: `0..=15` is weida's ladder, `16..` is the application's, and an **undefined value below 16 is a violation** rather than an opaque stage — otherwise a later version of this specification could not spend the number. Records are QUIC varint pairs, not CBOR: 3 bytes for `(Accepted, 64)` against a map per record. Seven decoder rules have one rejection test each, and the order is canonical because the array must **strictly ascend**, which makes duplicate detection free.

### B-234 — Reporting a cursor, and the number that says how often
kind: code | size: 60 | status: blocked | needs: [B-233, the Phase 5 store]
acceptance: a hop with a store reports `Stored(Written)` and, where it flushes, `Stored(Flushed)` up to a byte offset on the reply half, at a granularity from its own configuration — "every *N* bytes or every *T* milliseconds, whichever comes first", with a default and the rule that more often is always allowed and less often never ([0023](decisions/0023-completion-is-a-cursor.md) §4.5). A peer that negotiated `TransportReceipt` receives **no** cursor frames at all; a peer that negotiated `Stored` and talks to a hop with no store fails the negotiation rather than being sent nothing ([0006](decisions/0006-guarantee-sets.md) §4.4).
note: the granularity number is the whole of the configuration surface, and it is named here so that no slice invents its own.

### B-235 — What an interrupted stream means, per pattern
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) gains the section a stream-shaped system needs and did not have: a stream is unfinished by definition, so the question is not "was it delivered" but what a half-sent stream means — **within a connection QUIC retransmits, and when the connection ends weida does not resend**. The three answers an application has (cancel, reschedule, reconnect-and-continue) with when each is right and what it costs, and which one each pattern usually needs: Req/Rep reschedules because it has a reply half, Push/Pull reschedules and only the sender can, Pub/Sub cancels because a copy is best effort by definition, and a queue cancels on the way in and redelivers on the way out.
note: done, docs-only, and it replaces an item that was wrong: the first draft filed "resumption" as a protocol feature, which presumes a resend layer this system deliberately does not have.
