The correction came from the owner, and the sentence that makes the whole bridge is now in the document: **a message is a stream that reached FIN** — so a queue's unit is a completed stream, an interrupted admission needs no vocabulary of its own because nothing was a message yet, and a redelivery is an ordinary new stream rather than a continuation.

### B-232 — PROTOCOL said no sender writes the sequence key, and a publisher does
kind: docs | size: 15 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.2 stops claiming that DATA keys `6` and `7` are both "coded but unused", because key `6` is written by a publisher whose connection negotiated `PerProducer` ordering (`crates/weida/src/pubsub.rs`: the number is assigned once per published message, before fan-out, and both the whole-message and the streamed path put it in the header). The three other places that repeated the claim — §8's vector comment, §8's decoded description and §9.5's ordering paragraph — say the same thing, and the `DataHeader::sequence` doc comment in the codec with them.
note: found by the owner's question about whether the ZeroMQ analogies still hold, which is exactly the reader this document has: a second implementation reading "written by no v0 sender" would have concluded that the key never appears on the wire and that a hole in a subscriber's sequence is impossible. Key `7` (`producer`) is still genuinely unwritten and now says so on its own rather than as half of a pair.

### B-231 — The bounds a cluster adds, each with the failure it prevents
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: ready | needs: []
acceptance: the numbers of [0022](decisions/0022-consensus-topology.md) §4.8 written into [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §10's broker profile with a default and a named failure each: the Raft connection's stream budget (bounds in-flight RPCs across all groups between two nodes), the election and heartbeat ticks (one setting per node, not per queue), the per-group log segment size and retained-segment count, and the refusal of a two-member group or a two-node cluster. Nothing is implemented by this item; what it prevents is a number being invented per slice.
note: filed because [0022](decisions/0022-consensus-topology.md) §4.8 names which numbers exist and deliberately not what they are, and a number chosen under load is a number chosen badly.

### B-228 — A resolver the application may replace
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: [B-218]
acceptance: `weida_runtime::Resolver` as a public trait — one method, `resolve(exec, name, port: Option<u16>, max_addresses)`, returning addresses **in dial order** — with `SystemResolver` as the default and `RuntimeConfig::resolver` holding it, so what a `weida://` authority means is the deployment's decision rather than this library's ([0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.2). The contract is documented where it is implementable: order matters, `None` means the authority names a set and the resolver picks the ports, `max_addresses` is a cap and not a hint, and an empty answer is an error rather than an empty set.
note: done. The seam exists because of the case the owner named: a cloud load balancer hands out **one address** with several forwarded ports, which A/AAAA cannot express and SRV is shaped for — so rather than choose between "no SRV" and "a DNS stack for everybody", the choice moved to the application. `Exec::resolve` stays as the **system** convenience the five competitor libraries use (a foreign-protocol client has no reason to let an application replace resolution) and now delegates to `SystemResolver`: one implementation, two entry points. `SharedResolver` compares by **pointer identity**, because the connection pool keys on configuration and two different resolvers must not be treated as one even when they answer alike today. Nine tests, including the load-balancer shape — one address, three ports, order preserved — and the cap honoured by a replaced resolver; the three `Exec::resolve` tests moved to the trait rather than being duplicated. Workspace **1747**.

### B-226 — An SRV resolver, as a convenience over the seam
kind: code | size: 60 | status: ready | needs: [B-228]
acceptance: an SRV-capable [`Resolver`](../crates/runtime/src/resolve.rs) implementation — `_weida._udp.<name>` first, A/AAAA second, `DEFAULT_PORT` third, priority honoured and weight ignored ([0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.2) — shipped behind a **non-default feature** so the DNS stack it needs (`hickory-resolver`; neither std nor tokio resolves SRV) is in nobody's dependency graph by default. Tests drive the resolver against a stub DNS answer rather than a network: three targets with two priorities ordered correctly, an empty SRV answer falling through to A/AAAA, one host with three ports producing three addresses — the load-balancer shape — and an answer longer than `max_resolved_addresses` truncated at it.
note: restated after B-228 made the seam public: this is no longer "the SRV half of discovery" but a convenience implementation of a trait an application can already fill itself. The load-balancer case the owner named — one address, several forwarded ports — is what A/AAAA cannot express and what SRV is shaped for.

### B-219 — Trust is a set, and a redirect may only be followed inside it
kind: code | size: 60 | status: ready | needs: [B-218]
acceptance: `Trust` accumulating several pins is already expressible (`and_pin`); this item makes it the documented cluster form and proves the negative: a redirect to a node whose fingerprint is not in the set fails with `Error::Untrusted(fp)` and **does not retry**, and a DNS answer naming an untrusted host is not authority for anything ([0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.4). One shared cluster key is refused by documentation rather than by code, with the accounting reason (`max_connections_per_peer` would count the cluster as one peer).
note: this is the half of "aware" that is a security property rather than a performance one.

### B-220 — The not-the-leader answer, with a bound on following it
kind: code | size: 90 | status: ready | needs: [B-219]
acceptance: the redirect of [0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.3 on the wire — an ERROR code on an exchange's reply half plus a hint carrying `host`, `port` and the **term** it was learned in — with golden vectors in [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §8's shape, the client following at most **two** hints per operation, an older term's hint discarded rather than followed, and a client that ignores the hint entirely still being served. Tests assert all four properties, including the loop bound, which is the thing AMQP 1.0 left unspecified.
note: needs no cluster to test: a node that claims another node is the leader is one configuration flag.

### B-221 — Payload replication as a stream, with the commit record after it
kind: code | size: 90 | status: blocked | needs: [B-220, the Phase 5 store, B-222]
acceptance: the leader streams a message body to each follower as an ordinary one-way transfer and the consensus log carries only the commit record — queue, offset, digest — never the bytes ([0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.5), with the ordering proved rather than assumed: a test that drops a follower's payload stream mid-write and asserts that **no commit record naming it is ever committed**, and a test that asserts `Replicated(n, flushed)` reports `n` achieved at the commit with the leader counted, per [0004](decisions/0004-durability-levels.md) §4.2.
note: blocked on the store and on [0021](decisions/0021-consensus-openraft.md)'s first slice; this is the item where a mistake loses data that was reported replicated, so it gets the harshest test of the phase.

### B-222 — `AsyncRuntime` over `Exec`, and openraft compiling inside `weida-broker`
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: openraft 0.9 in `crates/broker/Cargo.toml`, a `WeidaRuntime` implementing its nine associated types and eight functions over `weida-runtime`'s `Exec` — `spawn` entering the runtime's handle rather than calling `tokio::spawn`, which is [0013](decisions/0013-competitor-libraries.md) §4.2's rule — and a single-node `Raft` instance that elects itself leader and commits one entry, driven entirely through our own types. **No `openraft::` type in any public signature** of `weida-broker`, asserted by a test that only touches the public API. `cargo geiger` run once and its `unsafe` count for the new subtree recorded in the item's note.
note: this is the go/no-go for [0021](decisions/0021-consensus-openraft.md): if `AsyncRuntime` cannot be implemented cleanly over `Exec`, the note is reopened in favour of its Option B.

note: done. **The go/no-go passed, and it corrected two things the note had predicted wrongly.** (1) The sixty-line `AsyncRuntime` delegation type **cannot exist**: its functions are associated functions, not methods — `fn spawn<T>(future: T)` takes no `self` — and the trait is chosen at the type level through `RaftTypeConfig`, so there is no instance to carry an `Exec`. What decides where openraft's tasks run is the **ambient** runtime at the construction site, so the rule became a construction-site invariant with `AsyncRuntime = openraft::TokioRuntime`, and it is **tested**: a plain `#[test]` with deliberately no ambient runtime builds `weida::Runtime::owned`, starts the engine inside `exec().spawn(…)` and waits for a committed entry on a channel — without inheritance the `spawn` inside openraft panics and nothing arrives. (2) The v2 storage traits are **sealed unless the `storage-v2` feature is on**; v1 + `Adaptor` is otherwise the only option, and v2 is what 0.10 makes the sole API. Also found: **`LogFlushed::new` is `pub(crate)`**, so a third-party store cannot construct the flush callback its own `append` receives — a store's `append` is only exercisable through a live `Raft`, which constrains how B-223's store is tested. **Measured, not assumed:** openraft adds **39 crates** to the lockfile because 0.9 depends non-optionally on `clap` (derive, env), on `byte-unit` (→ `rust_decimal`, `borsh`, `schemars`, `arrayvec`) and on `chrono` (→ five `windows-*` crates on Linux), with no `default` feature to trim them — and **0.10 drops `clap` and `byte-unit` outright**, which turns the migration from a risk into the fix. `cargo geiger` is not installed and installing a tool is not this loop's call, so the `unsafe` surface was counted over the vendored sources instead: **openraft's own code has zero `unsafe` in 208 files**; every occurrence in the new subtree is transitive (`arrayvec` 58, `byte-unit` 21, `chrono` 12, `ref-cast-impl` 10, `borsh` 7, `dyn-clone` 5, `derive_more` 5). Consequence taken rather than noted: openraft is **optional behind a non-default `cluster` feature**, so a broker that leads no cluster links none of it. `crates/broker/src/consensus.rs` holds the type config, an in-memory v2 store (the reference B-223's store must match) and a single-node group that elects itself and commits; **no `openraft::` type appears in a public signature**. 7 module tests; default workspace **1734**, and the two commands the gate does not run were run here: `clippy -p weida-broker --features cluster --all-targets -D warnings` and `test -p weida-broker --features cluster` (**23 tests**), plus rustdoc with the feature.

### B-223 — The store interface the consensus engine requires
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done | needs: [B-222]
acceptance: the Phase 5 store's interface written down **before** the store, as the thirteen methods of [0021](decisions/0021-consensus-openraft.md) §4.3 translated into requirements on a log: append, truncate backwards from a log id, purge forwards up to a log id, ranged and byte-limited reads, two durable cells with their ordering rule, and a snapshot that is a stream rather than a value. Each requirement names the failure it prevents, and the document states which of them an append-only file cannot satisfy without a rewrite path.
note: filed because choosing a library inverted a sequencing assumption: the store is built first and its interface is decided by this note, not by the store.

note: done, docs-only: **[STORE.md](STORE.md)**, a new normative document beside ARCHITECTURE and PROTOCOL, because Phase 5 will need it as a contract rather than as a note's section. It says the store is **two things behind one crate** — a log of small consensus entries and a payload store of unbounded bodies — with different sizes, lifetimes and durability questions, which follows from [0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.5 keeping payload out of the log. Each of the sixteen methods becomes a requirement plus **the failure it prevents**, quoting the three correctness rules openraft states for `append` (readable when it returns, persisted when the **callback** fires, never a hole "because Raft only examine the last log id") and the one for `limited_get_log_entries` ("must not return empty result if the input range is not empty"), which is how a replication read stays bounded. §5 is the single ordering rule across the two halves — a commit record may be proposed only after its payload is durable on the proposer, and acknowledged only after it is durable on the follower — and it is what makes `Replicated(n, flushed)` true rather than optimistic. §7 is the part that earns the item: **three requirements have no implementation in a plain append-only file** — `truncate` discards a suffix, `purge` discards a prefix, and the two durable cells are overwritten in place rather than appended — so the shape is a **segmented log plus two small cells**, with RabbitMQ's numbers carried in as the warning (512 MiB WAL cap, node memory 3-4x, >=32 B metadata per message, throughput falling with message size and member count).

### B-224 — The Raft transport, and `weida-raft` generic over an application's types
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 3e80585 | needs: [B-222]
acceptance: `weida-raft` becomes what its name says — openraft plus I/O — in two halves. **The transport:** `RaftNetworkFactory`/`RaftNetwork` implemented over weida, one exchange per RPC on the separate ALPN `weida-raft/0` with its own connections (never a client connection, [0002](decisions/0002-control-and-bulk-separation.md) §6.2), `RPCOption`'s timeout honoured, the snapshot install carried as a **streamed transfer** rather than chunked calls, and node addresses taken from the `Node` payload. **The types:** the crate stops fixing `D` and `R` to its own example — an application declares its request and response types while keeping this crate's `NodeId` (the proved fingerprint, [0020](decisions/0020-cluster-and-discovery.md) §4.4, with the all-zero digest as the `Default` openraft requires and refused as a real id), `Node`, `Entry` and `SnapshotData`. A three-node cluster on loopback elects a leader, replicates entries and survives the leader being killed; a test asserts that a client connection carries no Raft frame and a Raft connection no client frame.
note: restated after the owner's argument reshaped [0021](decisions/0021-consensus-openraft.md) §4.1: the transport is not a detail of the broker but the whole reason `weida-raft` exists, so it is unblocked and comes before the store rather than after it.
note: the example `Command`/`Applied` and the in-memory store stay, as the reference a real store is measured against and as the crate's own test fixture.
note: done, with two owner decisions that replace parts of the acceptance line. **Opaque payloads instead of generic `D`/`R`:** one concrete `TypeConfig` with `Request(Vec<u8>)`/`Response(Vec<u8>)`, so the transport and the codec are written once; `Command`/`Applied` are gone and `MemoryStore` stays as the reference store over the new types. **Its own port:** `ConsensusConfig::listen` is a second QUIC binding, and weida gained a closed ALPN choice (`Alpn::{Weida, Raft}` on `ServerTls`/`ClientTls`, part of the pool key; PROTOCOL.md §2.1 rewritten), so a mismatch fails the handshake. `Consensus::{start, bootstrap, write, read_barrier, add_node, remove_node, leader, id, node, shutdown}`; `NodeId` is the proved fingerprint and every engine RPC is refused unless its vote names the key that proved itself; follower operations are forwarded at most three hops; a second member is parked as a learner and a removal that leaves two voters is refused (`Error::TwoVoters`). openraft's `generic-snapshot-data` makes `full_snapshot` the only snapshot path, carried as the trailing bytes of one exchange. The public minicbor codec (`weida_raft::codec`) shares `weida_protocol::skip_value`. Tests: `crates/weida/tests/alpn.rs`, `crates/raft/weida-raft/tests/cluster.rs` (single node, restart, three nodes with a follower write and a read barrier, leader killed, snapshot to a new member — verified to fail when the server refuses snapshots — no cross-ALPN dial, construction site), the impersonation test in `server.rs` and the codec's bounds.

### B-227 — The segmented log, so a `weida-raft` user needs only a state machine
kind: code | size: 90 | status: blocked | needs: [B-224]
acceptance: the log half of [STORE.md](STORE.md) implemented in `weida-raft` as a **segmented log plus two durable cells**, satisfying `RaftLogStorage` — append with the flush callback fired after the bytes are on the medium, `truncate` discarding a suffix, `purge` dropping whole segments, ranged and byte-limited reads — with the three things §7 says a plain append-only file cannot do proved by tests: a truncate across a segment boundary, a purge that frees a segment, and a crash-and-reopen that agrees with the last committed cell. A user of the crate then implements `RaftStateMachine` and nothing else.
note: this is what turns "openraft plus I/O" into the claim the crate's own documentation makes. The state machine is inherently the application's; the log is not, and every user writing their own would be the duplication this crate exists to remove.

### B-225 — What the log holds, and what a leader change preserves
kind: code | size: 90 | status: blocked | needs: [B-224, the Phase 5 store]
acceptance: the list of [0021](decisions/0021-consensus-openraft.md) §4.5 as the replicated state machine — queue registry, membership, leadership and term, the per-message commit record, and **consumer state**: subscriptions, credit grants and the settled/unsettled position of every delivery. The user-observable properties are the tests: an acknowledged delivery is never redelivered after a leader change, an unacknowledged one always is, and a `Replicated(n, flushed)` report is never stronger than what happened ([0004](decisions/0004-durability-levels.md) §4.2). Re-testing Raft's own invariants is explicitly **not** in scope: that is the dependency's test suite.
note: this is where B-203's parked in-memory table of outstanding deliveries reappears as a replicated structure, which is why that item was stopped rather than finished.

### B-217 — The ZeroMQ sheet has no JVM implementation
kind: research | size: 45 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: [research/zeromq.md](research/zeromq.md) §13 gains the JVM row it does not have — the sheet contains no occurrence of "Java" — naming each implementation, its licence, its version, whether it is a pure-Java implementation of ZMTP or a binding over libzmq, and what it does not implement, each with a source in §14's form. That row is what makes [0013](decisions/0013-competitor-libraries.md) §4.7 clause 4 answerable for the JVM binding; until it exists, [0019](decisions/0019-jvm-binding.md) §4.6's named loss stands.

note: done, docs-only. §13 gains five entries, and the premise held: the sheet contained no occurrence of "Java", "JVM", "jzmq", "JeroMQ", "Kotlin" or "Clojure" before this. **JeroMQ** (`zeromq/jeromq`) is the one that matters: a pure-Java ZMTP implementation under **MPL-2.0**, 0.6.0 on Maven Central 2024-02-06, no native artifact — so a JVM caller already has a complete-enough incumbent and a permissive licence is not a differentiator against it (libzmq itself only reached MPL-2.0 in 4.3.5). The two JNI routes are the weak ones: **jzmq**'s newest release is 3.1.0 from **2014-07-11** and its `ZMQ.java` has no `HEARTBEAT_*` option at all, so ZMTP 3.1 heartbeating cannot be configured through it however new the libzmq underneath; **JCZMQ** has no published artifact at all. Two corrections to the item's own premise, both verified here rather than taken from the worker: `github.com/zeromq/jczmq` is a **404** — zeromq.org's Java page identifies JCZMQ as `zeromq/czmq/bindings/jni`, which is what the sheet cites — and `ptitjes/kzmq` (Kotlin, Apache-2.0) has **zero tags and zero releases** although its README documents Maven Central coordinates, so the sheet says no released version could be established. Re-verified against primary metadata: both licences, both release dates, all three default-branch last-commit dates (2025-03-02, 2020-10-27, 2025-11-24) and the 404. This is what makes [0019](decisions/0019-jvm-binding.md) §4.6 answerable.

### B-204 — `weida-py` slice 2: Pub/Sub and the streaming surface
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: [B-200]
acceptance: the half B-200 named and did not build, so that a Python caller reaches every pattern the library has: `Publisher` with `publish(topic, payload)` returning the subscriber count and `open(topic)` returning a `FanOut` with `write_within(chunk, seconds)`, `write_now(chunk)`, `finish()` and `subscribers`; `Subscriber` with `subscribe`/`unsubscribe`/`recv(max_bytes)` and the topic and gap on the metadata; and the streamed *transfer* surface for the two patterns that already exist — `Pusher.open()` and `Requester.open()` returning an object a caller writes in pieces and finishes, plus a reader that yields chunks instead of one `bytes`, since a payload that does not fit memory is the reason `INVARIANTS.md` forbids materialization. Tests for a published message reaching two subscribers, a filter that excludes, a streamed publish larger than `subscriber_buffer_bytes` (the B-064 claim, from Python), and a chunked request. The wheel's smoke script gains the fan-out round trip.
note: filed by me on finishing B-200, which named this slice in `crates/py/weida-py/src/lib.rs`'s module documentation rather than hinting at it. The ordering is deliberate: Req/Rep and Push/Pull are the shapes a caller reaches for first and they need no ceiling arguments beyond the ones slice 1 has, while fan-out needs the `FanOut` handle whose two write forms are a decision a binding must not paper over (`write_now` drops, `write_within` waits — [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §4.1).
note: done. `Publisher` (publish, open, subscriber_count, filter_count, dropped, dropped_on with all three causes), `Subscriber` (connect, subscribe, unsubscribe, recv, recv_stream, peer_count), `FanOut` (write_within, write_now, finish, subscribers, topic) and the streamed transfer surface for the other two patterns: `Pusher.open()`, `Requester.open()` returning both halves of an exchange, `Puller.recv_stream()` and `Subscriber.recv_stream()`, with `OutgoingStream`, `IncomingStream` and `Reply`. **Both write forms are exposed and neither is a default**, because they mean different things (`write_within` waits up to a bound and then drops that subscriber; `write_now` never waits) and a binding that picked one would make either a video publisher wait on its slowest viewer or a payload above the budget undeliverable to anybody. `write_now` is a coroutine anyway, and the doc comment says why: two coroutines may hold one `FanOut`, and the lock is the only thing it can wait for. Six new tests (14 in the suite, green four runs in a row), the B-064 claim among them — 10 MiB through an 8 MiB budget to two subscribers, both receiving it whole, with `publish` of the same size asserted to raise `LimitExceeded`. **The test that first hung taught the harness rule**: waiting for a subscription by *publishing a probe* leaves copies in the subscribers' queues, which is exactly the budget the streamed test is about, so `filter_count()` was added to the publisher and every test now polls it — the same instrument `await_filters` uses in the Rust suite. The wheel's smoke script gained the fan-out round trip and prints the 10 485 760 bytes it carried with no toolchain on `PATH`.

### B-205 — `weida.sync`: the binding's synchronous surface
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done | needs: [B-200, B-194]
acceptance: `weida.sync` beside the asyncio surface, in the shape every other binding in this tree has (`weida_zmq.sync`, `weida_nng.sync`, `weida_mqtt.sync`, `weida_amqp.sync`, `weida_nats.sync`): the same objects with no coroutines, for a Python process with no event loop, built on `weida::blocking` rather than on a second `block_on` of its own — so the reactor-refusal of B-194 and the explicit receive ceilings hold identically, and nothing is decided twice. The same value classes are shared with the asyncio surface, and the two differ only where they must. Tests mirroring the asyncio suite's round trips with no `asyncio.run` anywhere, plus the wheel's smoke script exercising the synchronous path.
note: filed with B-204. The other five bindings' sync surfaces all came *after* their library's blocking facade, which is the order that builds the facade once ([0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) §2); B-194 landed this session, so weida's binding can follow the same order rather than inventing a `block_on` in Python.
note: done. `weida.sync` as a real submodule (in `sys.modules`, so `import weida.sync` works and a traceback names `weida.sync.Runtime`), built on `weida::blocking` and **not** on a second `block_on`: the facade owns the reactor, the `block_on` and the refusal of a call from inside a Tokio runtime, so this file is argument conversion and `Python::detach` around it — the GIL is released while a call waits, which is what makes one thread per endpoint the shape it is supposed to be. Nine classes: Runtime, Binding, Requester, Pusher, Subscriber, Replier, Request, Puller, Publisher, sharing `Trust`, `Identity` and `IncomingMeta` with the asyncio surface rather than copying them. The streamed surface has no synchronous form and the module doc says why: the facade takes whole payloads by design, and a `sync` module that invented streaming would be inventing a second facade. **The suite found a real API collision**: `Error::Runtime`'s class was named `Runtime`, which is also the runtime class — a module cannot have one name for two things, and the loser was silent. The error class is `weida.RuntimeFailure` now, with the rename carried by the macro that writes the name list (an entry may be `(Variant)` or `(Variant, "ClassName")`) so the compiler still checks exhaustiveness, and the smoke script asserts both names, because a wheel could lose the rename. 19 pytest tests green three runs in a row; workspace 1709.

### B-206 — `weida-py` has no parity document, and the index has five
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done | needs: [B-200, B-204, B-205]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/weida-py.md` in the shape `zmq-py.md` set, plus its row in `docs/libraries/README.md`'s Documents table — with the one thing that makes it unlike the other five stated in its own §1: the other five compare against a foreign reference implementation (pyzmq, pynng, paho-mqtt, python-qpid-proton, nats-py), and this binding has none, because weida has no other Python client. Its reference is therefore the Rust API it binds: every public surface of `crates/weida` present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason. Every count counted out of the tree rather than estimated.
note: filed by me on finishing B-205, from the index: five bindings had a parity document and the sixth — weida's own — did not, which is a gap in the filing rather than in the work.
note: done, docs-only: 363 lines, twelve tables, and the reference column is the Rust API rather than a foreign client, stated in §1 as the one way this document differs from its five siblings. Counts, each with how it was counted: **25** `#[pyclass]`es, **41** names in `weida.__all__`, **9** in `weida.sync.__all__`, **22** exception classes (21 variants plus the base), **19** test functions across three files, **8** of `IncomingMeta`'s 9 Rust fields surfaced (`tracestate` is the one that is not), and **1** of `RuntimeConfig`'s 9 fields reachable from Python. **It found three text claims that had fallen behind the code, and all three are fixed rather than recorded as defects**: the module documentation still said the synchronous facade "is B-205" while `weida.sync` was registered; `pyproject.toml` and `Cargo.toml` still described the module as "Req/Rep and Push/Pull" when Pub/Sub and the streamed surface had landed; and the `Typing :: Typed` classifier was declared while no `.pyi` or `py.typed` ships — the classifier is **removed**, because a classifier is a claim, and §10 names the stub file as the follow-up. §10 also names what a caller does not get and where it would be added: the raw L0 surface, a Python server on the three local transports, and eight of nine `RuntimeConfig` fields.

### B-191 — 0015's consequences in the normative documents
kind: spec | size: 30 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the five doc edits [0015](decisions/0015-peer-authorization.md) §5 names, each in the document that owns the claim: [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §11's "Authentication beyond TLS" entry gains its reason and its positive half (authorization is the acceptor's decision on the proved identity, expressed with §9.4's refusals, and HELLO key `7` is what a later version would take); §9.4's dispatch table says it *is* the authorization surface — a registered path MAY refuse an accepted stream with `REJECTED`, and an acceptor that prefers to hide a path answers `UNKNOWN_ENDPOINT`; [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §5's "authentication is not authorization" paragraph gains the per-path MAY/MUST NOT rules of 0015 §4.3-§4.4 and says master doc §46's hooks are unimplemented **by decision**; [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.9 gains the application-side shape (authorize in the handler on `meta().peer`; for a token flow a companion Req/Rep path plus `ServerTls::require_client`); and [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6's peer-identity row states what an identity is *not* — a grant. No code.
note: filed by the loop when 0015 landed: a provisional note whose consequences nobody transcribes is a decision that exists only in `docs/decisions/`, and the rule the next reader needs ("never authorize on a claimed value", "never read structure into a path") belongs in INVARIANTS' and PROTOCOL's own words.
note: done, docs-only. All five edits, each in the document that owns the claim: [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §9.4 now says the dispatch table **is** the authorization surface, with the four MAYs and the MUST NOTs in the protocol's own words and the deliberate indistinguishability of `UNKNOWN_ENDPOINT` from "not for you"; §11's "Authentication beyond TLS" entry carries the decision, its positive half and HELLO key `7` as the price of changing it; [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §5 says master doc §46's hooks are unimplemented **by decision** and lists the per-path rules; [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.9 gained the application-side shape — authorize on `meta().peer` plus the dispatched path, and for a token flow a companion Req/Rep path with `ServerTls::require_client` — plus the one cost stated where a reader meets it: a subscription cannot present a token, because SUBSCRIBE has no reply half; and [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6's peer-identity row ends with "an identity is not a grant". §1.9 also gained the `PeerIdentity::Windows` variant it had been missing since B-039.

### B-192 — 0016's consequences, and the drop-end nobody had written down
kind: spec | size: 30 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the edits [0016](decisions/0016-conflation.md) §5 names. The one that matters most is one sentence: [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6's Pub/Sub drop bullet must say **which end** of the queue is dropped — the arriving copy, never the queued one — because that single unwritten fact is what made conflation look answered when it was not. Beside it: §3's backpressure paragraph carries the reservation's definition (a keyed queue at a hop, the key being the topic) so `Coalesce` stops being a name with no referent, and says why §6's row omits it; [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.5 gains the forbidden combination (`backpressure` `4` with `ordering` `2` — reassembly would wait for numbers that are never coming) and names the DATA `topic` key as the coalescing key if the level is ever honoured; [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §4.1 gains the one-sentence recipe (single-slot latest value, `open` plus `write_now`, `subscriber_buffer_bytes` sized to one value); [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §1's "Coalescing backpressure" deferral gains this note as its reason, in the shape its streaming-fan-out neighbour now has; [SYNTHESIS.md](research/SYNTHESIS.md) §5 P4 gains the drop-new qualification. No code.
note: filed with 0016. The note itself says no code falls out of it, which is exactly why the doc half has to be done deliberately rather than assumed.
note: done, docs-only, and the sentence the item was filed for is written: [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6 now says **the end dropped is the arriving copy, never a queued one** — drop-new, where conflation is drop-new at depth one *per key*, "which is why the two look alike and are not". Beside it: §3's backpressure dimension defines `Coalesce` (a keyed queue at a hop, depth one per key, the key being the DATA `topic`) and says why §6's row omits it, with `Spill` given the same treatment since it is the same kind of reservation; [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.5 gained two rules — `backpressure` `4` with `ordering` `2` is a **framing violation**, because reassembly waits for numbers a conflating hop guarantees will never come, while `ordering` `1` is the intended pairing, and the coalescing key is the DATA `topic` rather than a declared value; [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §4.1 carries the recipe in one paragraph; [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §1's coalescing deferral names the note and the refusal; [SYNTHESIS.md](research/SYNTHESIS.md) §5 P4 gained the drop-new qualification and the streaming answer for a payload above the budget.

### B-193 — Research: a per-subscription verdict, or the silence stays
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the wire question [0015](decisions/0015-peer-authorization.md) §5 explicitly leaves open, answered in a decision note: **SUBSCRIBE cannot be refused individually** ([PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.4 gives it no reply half), so a publisher that admits some subscribers and not others has only silence or a connection close with `LIMIT_EXCEEDED`, while MQTT answers per filter with SUBACK reason codes `0x87`/`0x8F` (3.9.3) and NATS answers per subject permission. The note decides whether weida grows a per-subscription verdict — a reply half for SUBSCRIBE, a new frame kind, or nothing — with the cost of each named against the wire (the first free frame kind is `6`; kind `5` is reserved for the L2 credit frame) and against [0011](decisions/0011-answered-where-it-arrived.md) §4.1's rule that a frame naming a path rides that path's connection. If the answer is "nothing", it says what a subscriber may conclude from silence and what it may not.
note: the one follow-up 0015 named and deliberately did not take: it is a wire question, not an authorization question, and answering it inside a note about credentials would have been the wrong document. It is also the last unanswered question of the two requirement notes.
note: done. `docs/decisions/0017-subscription-verdict.md`, `Status: provisional`: **nothing on the wire**, and the reason is stronger than a preference — no legal v0 frame can answer a SUBSCRIBE (ERROR is legal only on a bidi reply half, PROTOCOL §4.1/§6.3), and a new frame kind would not be additive but **fatal**: kind `6` is unknown to a v0 peer, which closes the connection with `PROTOCOL_VIOLATION` (§3.2). So the note pays for the silence by defining it: it means "received and not refused", a subscriber may **not** infer that anything will ever be published to it, and the three silences v0 conflates — recorded-not-yet-published, ignored because this side publishes nothing, recorded-with-every-copy-declined — are named as indistinguishable. The `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` close keeps its place with the cases where it is legitimate written down. Two findings worth the read: a declined fan-out copy is **not** a verdict (per copy, counted, the subscription survives), and before any wire question there is no *local* decision point at all — `Publisher` exposes counters and a fan-out handle, and the subscription is recorded without asking the application, so a verdict would need that hook first. Coherence with this repository's own `weida-zmq::XPubSocket::refuse` is argued rather than asserted: 37/ZMTP's ERROR is fatal to its peer, so that refusal is connection-level too — the same granularity under a different name. Every wire number in the bill was read off PROTOCOL's tables: frame kind `6`, ERROR code `6`, SUBSCRIBE key `2`, DATA key `8`, and no new QUIC application code, since `REJECTED` and `UNKNOWN_ENDPOINT` already exist.

### B-194 — weida's own blocking facade
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the debt entry "No synchronous API wrapper" of [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §6 closed for `weida` itself, in the shape the four competitor libraries already have (`weida-zmq`'s and `weida-mqtt`'s `blocking` feature over the same engine, no second implementation of any pattern): a `blocking` feature exposing `Runtime`, `Requester`, `Replier`, `Pusher`, `Puller`, `Publisher` and `Subscriber` with synchronous methods over the async core, every timeout explicit, no reactor of its own unless asked (`Runtime::owned`'s discipline), and a compile-fail test for the one mistake that matters — calling a blocking method from inside the reactor's own threads. Tests for one Req/Rep, one Push/Pull and one Pub/Sub round trip under the facade; the gate gains the feature's clippy and test runs like `weida-zmq`'s.
note: my own judgement, and the CLI is the evidence: every verb in `crates/weida/src/bin/weida.rs` is an `async fn` driven by `#[tokio::main]` for no reason a script cares about, and a user who wants weida from a synchronous program — the shape `weida-zmq::blocking` exists for — has to build that bridge themselves. `weida-zmq` proved the pattern is one module over the same engine, not a second library.
note: done. `crates/weida/src/blocking.rs` behind the non-default `blocking` feature: `Runtime` over `Runtime::owned`, `Binding` with `replier`/`puller`/`publisher`, and `Requester`, `Pusher`, `Subscriber`, `Replier`, `Request`, `Puller`, `Publisher` — every method a `block_on` around the asynchronous endpoint's own, and every one of them handing out the async endpoint (`endpoint()`) for the streaming surface a facade must not re-derive. Payloads are `Vec<u8>` with an **explicit byte ceiling on every receive**, passed through to `read_capped` rather than defaulted, because the caller is the one who knows how much there may be. **The compile-fail test the acceptance asked for turned out to be the wrong instrument and the item is better for it**: calling a blocking method from inside a reactor is not a type error — `futures::executor::block_on` compiles anywhere — it is a deadlock, so the facade *detects* it instead, checking `tokio::runtime::Handle::try_current()` at every entry point and failing with `Error::Runtime` naming the fix. That is testable, and `the_facade_refuses_to_block_a_reactor_thread` tests it in both shapes (the constructor and a call on an endpoint built outside). Three round trips beside it — Req/Rep across two threads, Push/Pull with the receipt awaited, Pub/Sub with a non-matching filter asserted to reach nobody — all plain `#[test]`, no `#[tokio::test]` anywhere, which is the point. Gate: workspace 1704 tests, `--features blocking` 199, clippy in all three configurations, rustdoc with the feature. IMPLEMENTATION §6's "No synchronous API wrapper" entry is replaced by what exists, including why its own reasoning ("a binding-layer concern, Phase 10") was wrong twice.

### B-195 — The CLI has no `--version`, and `--help` exits 2
kind: code | size: 15 | status: done | needs: [B-060]
acceptance: `weida --version` prints the crate version and exits `0`; `weida --help` and `weida <verb> --help` print the usage and exit `0`, not `2`, because a help request is not a usage error; `weida` with no arguments keeps exit `2`. The usage text names the four verbs and every option each takes.
note: found by using the binary the hour it was written: `--help` shares the `usage()` path, which exits 2, and `--version` does not exist at all. Both are one-line fixes and both are the first thing a script or a packager tries.
note: done. `--version` prints `weida 0.1.0` from `CARGO_PKG_VERSION` and exits `0`; `--help`, `-h` and `help` print the usage **on stdout** and exit `0`, before a verb is even required, because asking for help is not a mistake; no arguments still exits `2`. The usage text grew an options section naming every flag, which is what made the other two CLI items obvious.

### B-196 — A regression net for the CLI
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done | needs: [B-060]
acceptance: `crates/weida/tests/cli.rs` drives the binary through `std::process::Command` with `env!("CARGO_BIN_EXE_weida")` — no new dependency — and asserts what a user observes rather than how it is built: a `serve --echo` process whose printed address a `request` round-trips against, with the payload on stdout and nothing else on it; the exit code for an unknown endpoint (`4`) and for an untrusted key (`6`); and one local-transport run (`weida+unix://` on Unix, skipped elsewhere with a message). Each child process is killed on drop and every wait is bounded, so a hang is a failure rather than a stalled suite.
note: B-060 was verified by hand — four verbs, two transports, four exit codes — and that verification is not repeatable by anyone else. The exit-code mapping in particular is a contract a script depends on and nothing checks.
note: done. `crates/weida/tests/cli.rs`, four tests, no new dependency: the echo round trip (payload on stdout and **nothing else** on it, which is the property that makes the binary composable), the exit codes for an unknown endpoint, an untrusted key and a usage error, `--version`/`--help` as answers rather than errors, and a `send` into a `--sink` over `AF_UNIX` asserting the bytes came out of the sink's own stdout. Every child is killed on drop and every wait is bounded by a 20 s deadline on a thread, so a binary that hangs fails the test instead of owning the suite; the whole file is behind `cfg(feature = "generate")` because the binary is, so `--no-default-features --all-targets` stays green. The address line is read by the reader thread and **handed back** with the reader, which is the one thing that needed care: taking `child.stdout` for the first line would have thrown away the buffered bytes the sink test then reads.

### B-197 — `weida sub` cannot frame a payload that contains its separator
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done | needs: [B-060]
acceptance: `weida sub` either frames its output unambiguously or says plainly that it does not. Today every payload is followed by a newline, so a subscriber of binary or multi-line messages cannot tell one message from the next. The fix is the caller's choice made explicit: keep the newline as the default for the log-like use, add a `--raw` that writes payloads with no separator (for `--count 1`, where framing is the process boundary) and a length-prefixed or NUL-separated form for a stream a script parses; whichever set is chosen, the help text and the module documentation state what the framing is, and one test asserts that a payload containing the separator survives a round trip.
note: the CLI's one real defect, found while writing it and recorded rather than papered over: `MESSAGE_SEPARATOR` is a newline because "a subscriber's output is a log to look at", which is true and is not framing.
note: done. `--framing line|raw|nul|length`, with `line` the default and each form documented in the help text and in a `Framing` enum whose variants carry their own reason. `length` is the one that is unambiguous for anything — eight bytes big-endian before the payload — and it is *not* the default because it is the only form that has to hold the payload to know its size, which the doc comment says outright. Verified by publishing a payload containing a newline and reading the length-framed output as octets: `00 00 00 00 00 00 00 18` then the 24 bytes, no separator.

### B-198 — Research: what the minimal L2 broker is
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the decision note Phase D needs before any code, per the roadmap's own condition ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §9: "only once B1-B3 have green cross-adapter tests and the credit of 0003 has a consumer on both ends" — both now true). It answers what the broker *is* in weida's terms and what it is not: which of the reserved acknowledgement levels it implements first (`Accepted` alone, or `Accepted` plus `Stored(Written)` — [0004](decisions/0004-durability-levels.md) defines all four), whether the L2 credit frame of [0003](decisions/0003-credit-unit.md) §4.2 is part of the first slice or follows it, where a queue's identity lives given that endpoint paths are opaque ([0007](decisions/0007-topic-namespace.md) §4), what it means for the drain of [0009](decisions/0009-drain.md) (a broker has a queue to drain, which the note says is a *different* operation), and what the persistence boundary is so that Phase 5 is not pre-empted. It names the first three code slices with their acceptance lines, so the backlog can be replenished from it.
note: the roadmap's next unstarted line, and the one item on it that is not a large code slice. The user has twice been offered the choice between Phase D and Phase C's Java row; this note is what makes the Phase D half of that choice concrete rather than a heading.
note: done. `docs/decisions/0018-minimal-broker.md`, `Status: provisional`, 447 lines. **Level:** the first slice issues `Accepted` on the producer hop and `Processed` on the consumer hop, and leaves `Stored(Written|Flushed)` and `Replicated` reserved — because reporting `Stored` for an in-memory buffer is prohibited by GUARANTEES §1 and 0004 §4.1, and the store it needs is Phase 5. **Credit:** the L2 credit frame is *in* the first slice, as the frame kind PROTOCOL §4/§11 already reserves, with initial credit zero and an absolute idempotent per-subscription delivery limit riding that path's connection — the roadmap gated Phase D on exactly that, and the per-connection alternative cannot be withdrawn without closing streams. **Queue identity:** an ordinary opaque endpoint path with one dispatch answer per (stream kind, path), never a topic and never a third namespace, created from the broker's own configuration rather than by a declare frame, following AMQP 1.0's out-of-scope node lifecycle. The note also settles the two things a reader would assume: a **queue drain is a different operation** from `Runtime::drain` (stop admitting, keep delivering under granted credit, requeue what is outstanding at a mandatory finite deadline, report a count), and §4.9 enumerates what the broker is **not**, each pointing at the phase or note that owns it — clustering to Phase 7, durability to Phase 5, exchanges to 0007/D2, consumer groups refused outright for want of a position identifier. Three code slices filed as B-201..B-203. Its own open questions are marked: the publisher-confirm encoding (the reply half of the producer's exchange) is the note's proposal rather than a quoted rule, and the three new bound names are proposals too.

### B-199 — Research: how a JVM binding reaches these libraries
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: the decision note Phase C's second row needs ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §9, [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) §2: "Python first, then Java, then Node"), answering the one question that decides everything after it: **JNI or the Panama FFI**, against a stated MSRV-equivalent floor for the JVM (a Panama binding needs JDK 22+, which excludes the LTS a user may be pinned to), what the `weida-py-core` equivalent is for the JVM (error mapping, the reactor bridge, the `byte[]`/`ByteBuffer` boundary) and whether it is one shared artifact as `weida-py-core` is, how a Java caller gets an executor bridge without a second reactor, and how the binding is built and published (Gradle, Maven coordinates) given that the Python row's answer was maturin plus a wheel per binding with a smoke test that needs no toolchain. It names the first binding slice with its acceptance line.
note: filed so that the other half of the user's Phase D / Phase C choice is also a concrete first step rather than a heading. Deliberately a research item: the Python row's shape came out of 0014 and worked; the JVM's constraints (LTS pinning, two FFI mechanisms, a build system per ecosystem) are different enough that guessing would cost more than the note.
note: done. `docs/decisions/0019-jvm-binding.md`, `Status: provisional`, 450 lines. **JNI, not Panama**, and the reason is a floor rather than a preference: the Panama FFI is final only from JDK 22 (JEP 454), which sits above two of the three LTS releases a deployment may be pinned to, so the note states **JDK 17** as the bytecode floor — the MSRV-equivalent of the workspace's `rust-version = "1.88"` — and wants virtual threads without requiring them. One shared foundation per language, `weida-jvm-core`, shipped as a versioned Rust-crate and jar pair, and it carries one concept `weida-py-core` did not need: a **generation-tagged handle table** with `AutoCloseable` and a `Cleaner` backstop, because JNI has no `#[pyclass]` refcount and a raw `long` is a forgeable pointer. The bridge is a `CompletableFuture` subclass overriding `defaultExecutor()`, so reactor threads complete futures and never run a caller's continuations, and the blocking surface waits in Java rather than in a native frame — a native frame pins a virtual thread. `byte[]` costs exactly one copy each direction for the reason `weida-py-core`'s `bytes.rs` gives for CPython; a caller-supplied direct `ByteBuffer` removes the allocation, not the copy; wrapping Rust-owned memory is refused. ZeroMQ gets the first binding, because it is the only one of the five whose interop bench needs no third process. Seven slices filed as B-211..B-217. Fourteen external claims are marked `[inference]` in the note, JNI's thread functions and the LTS designation among them, because the JDK specifications are not in this repository.

### B-200 — A Python binding for weida itself
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done | needs: [B-194]
acceptance: the gap the binding row makes visible: five Python surfaces exist for the *competitor* libraries and none for weida, so a Python caller can speak ZeroMQ, SP, MQTT, AMQP and NATS through this tree but not weida's own protocol. Slice 1 on `weida-py-core`, asyncio first exactly as [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) §2 requires: `Runtime`, `Requester`/`Replier` and `Pusher`/`Puller` with `Trust` and `Identity` as objects, `IncomingMeta` including `peer`, payloads at the `bytes` boundary, the error vocabulary as exception classes generated from `weida_core::Error` by the same macro shape the other bindings use, and a wheel built and smoke-tested with no Rust toolchain on `PATH` like the other five. Pub/Sub and the streaming surface (`FanOut`, `OutgoingTransfer`) are a second slice, named here and not built.
note: `needs` B-194 rather than nothing: the four existing bindings all sit on their library's blocking facade for the synchronous half, and building weida's binding before weida has one would either invent that facade inside the binding — which 0013 §4.4 exists to prevent — or ship an asyncio-only surface that the other five do not.
note: done, slice 1. `crates/py/weida-py` (crate `weida-py`, Python module **`weida`**): `Runtime` over `Runtime::owned`, `Binding` with `local_addr`/`fingerprint`/`url`/`replier`/`puller`, `Requester`, `Pusher`, `Replier`, `Request`, `Puller`, plus `Trust` and `Identity` as classes and `IncomingMeta` — endpoint, topic, the **proved** peer, the sequence and the gap — on every arrival. One reactor, not two: the asyncio bridge drives the runtime's own executor, which is what `Runtime::exec()` was added for, with the doc comment saying it exists for this caller and why a second executor would be the cost 0013 §4.2 names. **Every receive takes a ceiling in bytes**, passed straight to `read_capped`, because a default would be a decision about how much memory a stranger may make a Python process allocate — `test_a_payload_above_the_ceiling_is_refused_rather_than_held` pins it. Eight pytest tests, green first run, both halves of every exchange this library: the round trip, Push/Pull with its metadata, the ceiling, `Rejected` against `UnknownEndpoint`, a wrong key refused with the fingerprint that answered in the message, one reply per exchange, the module surface and a drain reporting its counts. The wheel is built and smoke-tested exactly as the other five are: `weida-0.1.0-cp39-abi3-manylinux_2_34_x86_64.whl` installed into a throwaway virtualenv and run with `PATH=/usr/bin:/bin` — Req/Rep, Push/Pull and the untrusted-key refusal, with no `cargo`, `rustc` or `maturin` reachable. Pub/Sub and the streaming surface are the second slice, named in the module documentation rather than hinted at. Two PyO3 constraints shaped the code and are recorded where they bite: one `#[pymethods]` block per type and no macro items inside it, so `connect` is written out twice against one documented rule.

### B-010 — Measure: reassembly buffer under cross-stream reordering
kind: measure | size: 60 | status: done a236cbb | needs: []
acceptance: a probe in `crates/weida/tests/streams.rs` opening N transfers, finishing them in reverse order, and measuring at the puller how many arrive out of dispatch order and the peak count held back by an application-side reorder buffer keyed on a payload sequence; N = 16, 256; numbers recorded.
note: queueing the reverse FINs back to back does not reverse the arrival order — quinn transmits pending streams in its own order — so the probe carries a second mode that awaits each FIN's transport receipt, which does reverse it and reaches the N − 1 bound. Run at N = 16 for that mode; N = 16 and 256 for the batched one.

New bench target `crates/weida/benches/connections.rs`, which B-012 extends.

kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 2a43e70 | needs: [B-006]

Encoding is fixed by [PROTOCOL §6.2] and 0008 §4.4: `sequence` a `uint`, `producer` a `bstr` capped at 32 B and absent whenever the producer is the connection peer. note: merged `--no-ff` as 2a43e70 and gated here (232 tests). `producer` is `Option<[u8; 32]>` rather than a capped `Vec`, which is the stronger shape: a wrong length cannot be constructed at all, and the decoder rejects a `bstr` of any other length. That sharpened PROTOCOL §6.2 from "cap 32 B" to *exactly* 32 B — a spec change I accepted and carried into the key table, because a truncated digest names nobody. §8 gained a sequenced and a relayed vector; the pinned v0 vectors are byte-identical, which is the "no runtime behaviour change" half of the acceptance.

### B-014 — Wire: HELLO guarantee declarations and intersection
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4d62746 | needs: [B-006, B-013]
acceptance: `Hello` carries ordering mode, dedup on/off plus window, producer naming, control-isolated flag; `negotiate()` computes the intersection and fails on a requested level the peer does not offer; `Agreed` exposes the result; hostile tests for mismatches; golden vector for the extended HELLO.
note: delegated to the parallel worker on branch `b014-hello-guarantees`, branched from `b020-segment-filter`. The wire is already specified: HELLO keys `5`/`6` and the guarantee-set map of [PROTOCOL §6.5], with the per-dimension intersection and the fail-rather-than-downgrade rule of §2.3 steps 5-7. Absent keys mean `core`, so a v0 HELLO must stay byte-identical — the existing golden vector is the regression guard.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 4d62746, gated here (253 tests at that point). **The acceptance line asked for two dimensions my PROTOCOL §6.5 did not have** — producer naming and a control-isolated flag — and the worker added them as guarantee-set keys `8` (unordered, like backpressure) and `9` (ordered, so the intersection is a logical AND), amending §6.5. Accepted: the item asked for them, `control_isolated` is exactly what SYNTHESIS §8.2 said an adapter multiplexing foreign sessions would need to require, and both default to the `core` value so a v0 HELLO stays byte-identical — the extended vector proves it. Two further rules the worker added are right and were missing: an encoder MUST NOT write a dimension left at its `core` level, so `core` and absent are indistinguishable on the wire; and when the intersection weakens `acknowledgement` below `Stored`, the `durability` and `replicas` axes it qualified are dropped rather than carried. The debt this creates is that GUARANTEES §3 still lists five dimensions — filed as B-035.

Deduplication is **not** in it and is B-034 per LOOP §1.3.

### B-034 — Runtime: bounded deduplication with a time window
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 842fd24 | needs: [B-015]

Three conflicts, all additive and all kept: `Limits` gained `max_dedup_entries` (4096) beside my `max_resolved_addresses`, and both INVARIANTS and PROTOCOL §10 had to carry the two new bounds together. One choice worth recording because it is not obvious: a **suppressed transfer is drained to EOF**, so the sender still sees a normal receipt — a duplicate is not a refusal, and making it one would let a receiver's dedup window look like a network failure. The branch also fixed a real `GapDetector` bug it found on the way: a below-expected arrival advanced `expected` by one and silenced the *next* gap, and `an_out_of_order_arrival_is_not_a_gap` now fails on the old code. The worker's note that `is_duplicate` boxes its scope on every call is filed as B-040.

### B-017 — Bulk connection per path, bound by fingerprint
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done d624720 e1134c3 | needs: [B-014, B-011, B-012]
acceptance: pool tiers per 0002 §7; HELLO on the control connection; bulk connections keyed by path and bound by fingerprint per 0008 §4.2, with a fingerprint mismatch refused and a test for that refusal in `crates/weida/tests/identity.rs`, and anonymous clients (no proved fingerprint) never treated as one peer; `Limits` profiles `control` and `bulk` in `RuntimeConfig` with defaults chosen from B-011/B-012 numbers; stream probes updated; `a_stalled_stream_does_not_block_its_siblings` extended to show a control frame crossing while bulk is stalled.
note: **the item's control half is not built, deliberately, and the title changed to say so.** I wrote `Limits::CONTROL` and the second `RuntimeConfig` profile first, then found what they would buy in v0: nothing. Every connection performs its own HELLO (PROTOCOL §2.2), the credit frame of §11 does not exist at wire version 0, and SUBSCRIBE cannot move to a control connection because a publisher writes fan-out on the connection the SUBSCRIBE arrived on and **no field tells it which of a peer's per-path connections to use instead** — the question 0002 §7 left open as "a HELLO field, or the fingerprint alone". A control connection in v0 would therefore cost one handshake per peer and carry nothing, so I reverted my own profile work rather than ship a knob nothing reads. Filed as B-044 (the addressing decision) and B-045 (the tier). The delivered half: one connection per dialled path, so two paths share no receive window — `a_stalled_path_does_not_stall_another_path` fills one path's window until a write parks and then sends on the other, and it times out under a mutation that restores one connection per peer, which is exactly the coupling 0002 set out to remove. The fingerprint binding compares a fresh connection against this peer's **live** connections (not a remembered value: once the last connection is gone, a replacement server with a new key is a new peer) and refuses a mismatch with `Untrusted(fp)`; proved by swapping a raw server's identity between two handshakes on one socket, which is the load-balancer case and cannot be built from two `quinn` endpoints. `max_connections_per_peer` (64, B-011's measured number) now exists because per-path connections let the dialling side choose the count; it counts per proved fingerprint, and anonymous connections are each their own peer — the "never treated as one peer" half of the acceptance line, which had no code to attach to before. `Limits` became a genuinely per-connection profile on the way: `max_connections`, `endpoint_queue` and `max_resolved_addresses` moved to `RuntimeConfig`, `keep_alive` and `idle_timeout` moved into `Limits`.

kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 6250448 | needs: [B-006]
acceptance: `matches_filter` in `crates/weida/src/pubsub.rs` becomes the allocation-free, backtracking-free segment walker of 0007 §4.3, and its doc-comment objection is rewritten rather than deleted; filter grammar validation lands in `weida-protocol` beside the other header rules so an invalid filter (`*` not alone in its segment, `#` not final) is rejected at the codec boundary; `Subscriber::subscribe`'s documentation states the grammar; golden vectors for a literal filter, a middle-segment `*`, a trailing `#`, the empty filter and a topic containing a literal `*`; `subscribe_prefix_filters_topics` is renamed and extended, including the boundary case a byte prefix over-matched (`sensors.temp` must not select `sensors.temperature`).

It is the one item that makes the implementation agree with [PROTOCOL §6.4] again, so the §8 golden vectors and the `px.` note in that section are part of it; the existing `subscribe_prefix_filters_topics` must fail before the change and pass after it in its extended form.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 6250448 (it carried B-013 with it) and gated here: 244 tests. Verified: the walker is one left-to-right pass with no backtracking and no allocation, `weida_protocol::filter::validate` rejects `*` that does not own its segment and `#` that is not final, `SubscriptionHeader::decode` turns that into `HeaderError::InvalidFilter` at the codec boundary, `Subscriber::subscribe` refuses locally before the frame is written, and the renamed `subscribe_filters_topics_by_segment` asserts that `sensors.temp` does not select `sensors.temperature` — the assertion that fails on the old matcher. ### B-021 — Measure: segment matching in the fan-out path
kind: measure | size: 45 | status: done 2c157ac | needs: [B-020]
acceptance: a criterion bench comparing byte-prefix `starts_with` against the segment walker at a realistic subscriber and filter count (the objection recorded in `crates/weida/src/pubsub.rs` asserts a cost without a number); publish-to-all-drained time per subscriber count for both matchers; numbers in IMPLEMENTATION.md verified results, which is what 0007 §6 asks for.
note: `matches_filter` is `pub(crate)` and B-020 documents why it lives beside the fan-out it serves, so the comparison is made through the public API rather than by moving code for a bench: one connection holds N filters that the topic matches none of, and running N = 1 against N = 64 subtracts every fixed cost of `publish` out. Per-filter cost is 14.3-15.1 ns and **shape-independent**, so the grammar is not the cost — registry iteration is. The byte prefix it replaced is measured as a pure function, since it no longer exists in the library.

### B-022 — Reassembly mode, capped, and subscriber-side drop detection
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done b93d5ca | needs: [B-015]
acceptance: `PerProducer(reassemble)` holds out-of-order transfers and releases them in sequence order, with the hold bounded by a named `Limits` field and the bound enforced by refusing or releasing out of order rather than by growing — B-010 measured the peak at N − 1 of the transfers in flight, and 84 of 256 with no adversarial pattern, so the cap is a configured number and not an assumption about arrival order; the cap appears in INVARIANTS' named bounds; a subscriber in detect or reassemble mode reports a Pub/Sub drop through `IncomingMeta`; a test drives reordering with the two FIN modes of `reverse_order_completion_measures_the_reorder_buffer`.
note: delegated to the parallel worker on branch `b022-reassemble`, from `b025-dedup-window`. The hold is the one structure B-010 measured rather than guessed: reverse-order completion reached N − 1 and 84 of 256 were held with no adversarial pattern, so the cap is a configured `Limits` field and the behaviour **at** the cap must be stated (release out of order, or refuse) rather than left to grow. `dedup.rs` and `ordering.rs` are the shape to follow, including the capacity assertion that proves nothing is allocated when the level is not negotiated.
note: merged `--no-ff` as b93d5ca and gated here: **284 tests**. Two conflicts, both additive: `Limits` gained `max_reorder_hold` (256) beside `max_dedup_entries` and `max_resolved_addresses`, and the INVARIANTS enforced cell now carries the reassembler, the hold and the resolver bound together, so the named-but-unimplemented list is down to two rows. Beyond acceptance, and worth keeping: **a held transfer is an unread stream**, so reassembly materializes nothing and what the hold pins is quinn's window rather than an application buffer — a documented deviation from 0002 §6.6, which asked for eager reads into an application-owned buffer, refused because that would materialize the payload. The review pass also caught a real defect in the first cut: a repeated number went through `BTreeMap::insert` and silently replaced the held transfer; a repeat is now passed through, proven by a test that fails on the old code.

### B-023 — Foreign-executor coverage for Push/Pull and Pub/Sub
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 7ae9292 | needs: [B-016]
acceptance: `crates/weida/tests/foreign_executor.rs` gains a Push/Pull and a Pub/Sub round trip as plain `#[test]`s under `futures::executor::block_on`, so the claim "weida needs no ambient reactor" is proved for every pattern rather than for Req/Rep alone; each test bounds every await with the suite's deadline helper; a pattern that cannot run without a tokio context is a bug in `runtime.rs`, not a reason to skip the test. Proposed by the B-016 worker.
note: the Pub/Sub test needed one thing the tokio suites get for free — waiting for a subscription to land. ### B-024 — An example without `#[tokio::main]`
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 11c31d0 | needs: [B-016]
acceptance: one existing example (or a new small one) drives a full exchange from a plain `fn main` using `Runtime::owned`, showing what a caller with no async runtime of its own writes; the README's build-and-run section names it; `cargo run -p weida --example <name>` works. Proposed by the B-016 worker, and it is the user-visible half of `Runtime::owned` — the API exists but nothing in the tree demonstrates it.
note: a new example, `crates/weida/examples/owned_runtime.rs`, rather than a converted one: the existing five all demonstrate a pattern and would have lost that focus. It joins the two halves instead of spawning them, because an executor with no reactor has nothing to spawn onto — which is the lesson the example exists to teach. The README gained a "No reactor of your own" section naming all three constructors. Verified by running it: it prints the pinned URL and `HELLO FROM A PLAIN MAIN`.

### B-025 — Do not resolve a literal address on every connect
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 52ebbbc | needs: []
acceptance: `Exec::resolve` (or its caller in `crates/weida/src/pool.rs`) short-circuits a host that is already an IP literal — `host.parse::<IpAddr>()` — so no string is allocated, no task is spawned and no join handle is awaited for `weida://127.0.0.1:7443/x`; name resolution keeps its current shape for real hostnames, including the spawn that `lookup_host` needs for its Tokio context; `connect/cold_handshake` returns to ~1.05 ms in `cargo bench -p weida --bench connections` and the number replaces the regression paragraph in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4 (B-012). Found by B-012: the current path costs +12 % of a cold handshake (1.02-1.10 ms to 1.18-1.24 ms, p = 0.00) for a lookup that a literal address does not need.
note: no new test — `resolves_ip_literals_without_dns` in `runtime.rs` already pins the contract for both literal families and now exercises the short-circuit, and the whole suite dials `127.0.0.1`, so the branch is covered 222 times over. The proof is the bench: 1.05-1.09 ms, `change: −11.6 % (p = 0.00)`.

### B-026 — Decision 0009: a bounded drain at shutdown
kind: research | size: 45 | status: done dec2c62 | needs: []
acceptance: `docs/decisions/0009-drain.md`, accepted, closing SYNTHESIS §8.6: whether `Runtime::shutdown` gains a bounded drain and whether the drain belongs to the runtime or to L2, decided against the evidence the entry already names — ZeroMQ's `ZMQ_LINGER` with its infinite default that can hang forever, RabbitMQ's requeue-on-channel-close, AMQP 1.0's `drain`/`echo` quiescence point, NATS Lame Duck Mode, and D12's finding that none of them is a drain *acknowledgement*; a bound in wall-clock time is mandatory if a drain exists at all, because an unbounded one is the ZeroMQ failure mode; SYNTHESIS §8.6 closed, README row added, and the consequences name what PATTERNS §1.1 and the ZMTP mapping's loss L9 must then say.
note: the evidence turned up a defect while it was being gathered: `Runtime::shutdown` already waits on `wait_idle()` with **no bound**, so today's tree has ZeroMQ's failure mode in a place where it buys nothing. The note names it and B-031 fixes it separately, ahead of the drain feature itself (B-032) and its spec sync (B-033). The ZMTP mapping's loss L9 was updated in place, since "SYNTHESIS §8.6, still open" had become false.

### B-027 — Decision 0010: the local transport, per platform
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done 3ec89b2 | needs: []
acceptance: `docs/decisions/0010-local-transport.md`, accepted, closing SYNTHESIS §8.8 and unblocking roadmap A9: the Linux default (`AF_UNIX` `SOCK_STREAM` against `SOCK_SEQPACKET` and the abstract namespace), the macOS default, the Windows default (named pipes against Windows `AF_UNIX`), the fallback chain, and whether a bulk payload ever travels by handle passing rather than by bytes — each with the cost `docs/research/ipc.md` §11 already records; inproc binding is named as the first slice because it needs no platform decision at all; SYNTHESIS §8.8 closed and README row added.
note: two decisions the acceptance line did not anticipate, both forced by the evidence. **The OS connection is the stream** — one local connection per transfer — which is what lets a local transport exist without reimplementing QUIC's stream layer; the price is a named `max_local_streams` bound, because Windows caps pipe instances at 1-255. And **no TLS locally**, which makes the kernel the prover and turns `IncomingMeta::peer` into a sum of key and local principal; that **amends 0008 §4.1** in the way INVARIANTS requires — the rule "proved, never claimed" is untouched, only the claim that the fingerprint is the *only* identity gives way. The note also fixes the address syntax (`weida+inproc://`, `weida+unix://`, `weida+pipe://`, with the `sha256:…@` form forbidden), rejects automatic fallback to loopback TCP as a silent change of who may connect, and defers handle-passing zero-copy with its measured prize (270 ns against 5,910 ns at 32 B). Every question in SYNTHESIS §8 is now closed.

### B-028 — Preserve why a connection was lost
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 2214019 | needs: []
acceptance: `PeerSet::pick` stops flattening every closed peer into `Error::ConnectionLost`: the `quinn::ConnectionError` of the peer it rejected is mapped through the existing `conn_error` so an idle timeout, a peer-initiated close and a transport error are distinguishable through the pattern APIs; a test drives the idle-timeout case and asserts the specific error rather than `ConnectionLost`; the debt entry in IMPLEMENTATION.md §6 is removed rather than annotated. Found by the review pass in the debt list: an application deciding whether to redial cannot currently tell why it lost the peer.
note: the acceptance line under-described the work: `conn_error` already mapped `TimedOut`, `Reset` and an unrecognized application close to the **same** bare `ConnectionLost`, so having `pick` consult `close_reason()` would have preserved nothing — the distinction did not exist in the vocabulary at all. `ConnectionLost` therefore carries a `LossCause` (`IdleTimeout`, `PeerClosed`, `LocallyClosed`, `Reset`, `TransportError`): a payload rather than five new top-level variants, because the *outcome* is identical in every case and only the next action differs, and a second outcome vocabulary would have been the wrong shape. Five causes, each changing what an application should do. `idle_timeout_reports_loss_within_the_window` now asserts the cause and was verified by mutation — blanking the cause in `pick` fails it.

### B-029 — Try more than the first resolved address
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 3d71f32 | needs: [B-025]
acceptance: `Exec::resolve` returns the resolved addresses in order and the dialling path tries them until one connection succeeds, so `weida://localhost:7443/x` reaches a server bound to `127.0.0.1` on a host where `localhost` also resolves to `::1`; the number tried is bounded (a hostile resolver is remote input) and the bound is named in INVARIANTS; the last error is reported when all fail; the IP-literal short-circuit of B-025 is untouched; the debt entry in IMPLEMENTATION.md §6 is removed rather than annotated.
note: the loop alone made it **worse**, and the measurement caught it: an address that answers nothing gives QUIC no refusal to observe, so trying `::1` first cost a full handshake timeout — the regression test took **30 s**. Every attempt but the last is therefore bounded by a new `RuntimeConfig::connect_attempt_timeout`, defaulting to RFC 8305's 250 ms Connection Attempt Delay, which brought the same test to **1.3 s**; the last address keeps the whole budget, so an IP literal and a single-address name behave exactly as before. Sequential rather than RFC 8305's parallel happy-eyeballs, because the failure being fixed is silence and not slowness. `Exec::within` is the one place the crate bounds an await on wall-clock time, beside `sleep`, for the same reason.

### B-030 — ZMTP codec: sans-I/O, golden vectors, fuzz target
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 84244f4 | needs: [B-018]
acceptance: `crates/adapters/weida-zmtp`, no I/O and no weida dependency in the codec itself: the 64-octet greeting, version negotiation, the NULL handshake with `READY` metadata, short and long frames with the MORE and COMMAND flags, `SUBSCRIBE`/`CANCEL`, `PING`/`PONG`; the golden vectors of `docs/adapters/zmtp.md` §10 byte-exact in both directions, including the 255/256-octet frame boundary; a fuzz target over the decoder that caps before allocating, since a ZMTP frame may declare up to 2^63-1 octets and `ZMQ_MAXMSGSIZE` is the only defence; no bridge, no sockets, no interop bench yet — those are slices 3 to 5.
note: the acceptance line pointed at "the golden vectors of zmtp.md §10", but §10 only *listed* which vectors to build — the octets were nowhere. So the vectors are published as §10.1 first and asserted from there: the document is the publication and `tests/golden_vectors.rs` is the enforcement, the same split PROTOCOL §8 has. Two places where 37/ZMTP contradicts itself surfaced while writing it, both decided for libzmq: **command names are length-prefixed** (`05 READY`), not "a printable command name, a null octet separator, and data" as the prose claims, because the ABNF and the reference implementation agree against the prose; and an **`ERROR` reason may contain a space**, which `VCHAR` excludes while libzmq's own reasons read "Unknown mechanism". Both are recorded in §10.1, in the crate docs and in IMPLEMENTATION §5, because an interop bug found later must trace to a decision. Beyond the line: `[dependencies]` is empty — not even `weida-core` — so the codec cannot quietly be checked against our reading of the specification instead of the specification; the cap is an argument to every decode entry point rather than a constant, bounded by `min(argument, 2^63-1)` so a caller cannot switch it off, which also leaves §11's `max_message_bytes` question open on purpose rather than answering it by accident; and `FrameKind` makes MORE-on-a-command-frame unrepresentable instead of checking for it. Five `cargo fuzz` targets plus a stable-Rust `fuzz_smoke.rs` (700k iterations) carry the properties; 64 tests here, **348** in the workspace.

### B-031 — Bound every wait in shutdown
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 10b3120 | needs: []
acceptance: `Runtime::shutdown` stops awaiting `wait_idle()` without a bound — today it can hang on a peer's behaviour, which is the one failure mode the whole catalogue warns about ([0009](decisions/0009-drain.md) §4.4, [zeromq §12/P17]); the wait is capped (QUIC's own closing and draining periods are "at least three times the current PTO interval", so a cap in the hundreds of milliseconds is generous), the cap is a `RuntimeConfig` field with a stated default, and a test proves that shutdown returns even when the peer never acknowledges — a server whose process is suspended or a connection to a black hole. This is a defect in shipped code and does not wait for B-032.
note: the number that justifies the default was measured rather than guessed: against a peer that has gone silent, the draining period alone is **96 ms** on loopback, so a 1 s cap is generous where a clean close was possible at all. The test asserts **relatively** — a 1 ms cap must be at least twice as fast as a 10 s one, measured in the same run — because an absolute bound would pin this machine; verified by mutation, the unbounded version reports 106 ms against 95 ms and fails it. `shutdown`'s doc comment now also says *abortive*, which is the half of 0009 that needed no new API.

### B-032 — `Runtime::drain(Duration)`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4e3d7b6 | needs: [B-031]
acceptance: `Runtime::drain(Duration)` per [0009](decisions/0009-drain.md) §4.1-§4.6: admission stops first (bindings accept no new connection, a new inbound stream on an existing connection is refused with `SHUTDOWN`), then transfers already `finish()`ed are awaited to their transport receipt, then the same close as `shutdown` runs; the deadline is a mandatory `Duration` with no infinite variant; the return value counts what reached the peer's transport and what was still outstanding, and an expired drain is not an error; `shutdown`'s documentation names itself **abortive**. Tests: a finished transfer that would be cut short by `shutdown` arrives under `drain`, and a drain against a peer that reads nothing returns at its deadline with a non-zero outstanding count.
note: merged `--no-ff` from `b032-drain` and gated here: **354 tests**.
