Three conflicts, all mine to resolve: `Limits` keeps the per-connection shape B-017 gave it (`max_resolved_addresses` stays on `RuntimeConfig`), the accept loop combines the drain's admission refusal with B-017's per-peer ceiling, and the INVARIANTS hot-path row is theirs — it is the one that is now true. The part worth keeping: the first cut parked receipts behind a **runtime-wide** mutex and cost ~3 % on the header bench; per-connection parking is back inside the noise, and "dropping a `Delivery` is free" was rewritten everywhere rather than left as a claim the code no longer supports. Admission refusals are not covered by a test (it needs a second frozen peer) — stated in IMPLEMENTATION and filed as B-046.

### B-033 — Spec sync: the drain of 0009
kind: spec | size: 30 | status: done 4e3d7b6 | needs: [B-032]
acceptance: PATTERNS §1.1 keeps "the one thing that cuts a finished transfer short" for `shutdown` and gains `drain` as its counterpart; GUARANTEES §3 notes that the drain waits on the transport receipt and inherits its meaning, including that a drained transfer may still have been discarded by the peer's application (0005 §4.2); PROTOCOL §11 lists drain as a *local* operation with no wire representation, so nobody invents a quiescence frame; `grep` shows no document still calling §8.6 open.
note: landed in the same merge. PATTERNS §1.1 now carries `drain` as `shutdown`'s counterpart with the `Drained { delivered, outstanding }` pair, GUARANTEES §3 says the drain inherits the transport receipt's meaning and no more — a drained transfer may still be discarded by the peer's application — PROTOCOL §11 names drain a *local* operation and forbids inventing a quiescence frame, and the ZMTP mapping's loss L9 points at the drain that now exists. Nothing calls SYNTHESIS §8.6 open any more.

### B-035 — Spec sync: the two guarantee dimensions B-014 added
kind: spec | size: 30 | status: done 69ee8e5 | needs: []
acceptance: GUARANTEES §3 gains `ProducerNaming` (`Fingerprint` | `Stable`, unordered) and `ControlIsolated` (`No` | `Yes`, ordered) as dimensions beside the five it lists, each with its levels, its ordering and the decision it comes from ([0001](decisions/0001-sequence-field.md) §7.3 and [0008](decisions/0008-session-identity.md) §4.3 for the first, [0002](decisions/0002-control-and-bulk-separation.md) §6.1 for the second); the guarantee-set subsection stops saying a set has one level per dimension of the *five* and says what it now is; [0006](decisions/0006-guarantee-sets.md) §5 gains a consequence line recording that PROTOCOL §6.5 keys `8` and `9` extended the vocabulary with B-014, so "a guarantee set introduces no new words" reads as "no new words without a dimension entry"; `grep` shows no document listing five dimensions where there are seven. Found by reviewing B-014's delivery: the wire and the code carry two dimensions the guarantee vocabulary does not.
note: two things the acceptance line did not ask for and the change needed. The backpressure subsection said it was "the one dimension that is not ordered" — now producer naming is the other, so that sentence was corrected rather than left to contradict the new one. And §6 gained a status row per new dimension, because the table is the normative source for `core` and a dimension absent from it cannot be part of the default. 0006's §2 evidence line still says "the five dimensions": that is a true statement of what GUARANTEES §3 held when 0006 was written, and accepted notes are history — the new §5 bullet is where the extension is recorded.

### B-036 — Spec sync: the local transport of 0010
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done debdd4b | needs: []
acceptance: ARCHITECTURE §2 stops implying QUIC is the only transport and carries the identity sum of [0010](decisions/0010-local-transport.md) §4.4 (key or local principal, inproc `None`), §3 gains the three schemes of §4.8 with their validation rules (107 B on Linux, 104 on macOS, both checked after percent-decoding; the `sha256:…@` form rejected); PROTOCOL §2.1 states QUIC for the network transport beside a local-transport paragraph — same frames, same HELLO, no TLS and therefore no ALPN, with the version fenced by `versions` in HELLO; INVARIANTS names `max_local_streams` before the code exists and records who proves a local peer; GUARANTEES §6 gains the local identity kinds and the note that a local transfer has no connection window; 0008 §4.1 carries the amendment marker pointing at 0010 §4.4; `grep` shows no document claiming the fingerprint is the only identity.
note: writing it exposed a hole the decision had not closed. A local connection is bidirectional by nature and carries no equivalent of QUIC's stream kind, but §9.4 dispatches on stream kind *and* path — so locally the path alone must decide whether a reply is expected, which the registered pattern already knows. PROTOCOL §2.1 now states that, with the existing `UNSUPPORTED` refusal covering the mismatch; without it the spec would have been silent on how a local Req/Rep differs from a local Push. Also generalized §1's definition of "peer", which said "the other side of a QUIC connection". PATTERNS is deliberately untouched: it documents built behaviour, and the A9 slices will bring it.

### B-037 — Local transport slice 1: inproc
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4c875c1 | needs: [B-036]
acceptance: `weida+inproc://<bus>/<path>` binds and dials inside one process with no socket, no TLS and no credentials ([0010](decisions/0010-local-transport.md) §4.1, §4.4): the bus name is at most 256 bytes, a transfer is one channel pair so the "OS connection is the stream" rule of §4.2 holds by construction, `IncomingMeta::peer` is `None`, and the transport boundary in the runtime is exercised by driving Req/Rep, Push/Pull and Pub/Sub over it with the same tests the QUIC path uses; peer loss is a dropped channel half reported as `ConnectionLost`; `max_local_streams` bounds live transfers.
note: delegated to the parallel worker on branch `b037-inproc`, from `b032-drain`. Two things landed since the acceptance line was written and both touch this slice: `Limits` is now a per-connection profile and the runtime-level numbers (`max_connections`, `endpoint_queue`, `max_resolved_addresses`, `max_connections_per_peer`) live on `RuntimeConfig`, so `max_local_streams` belongs with the latter; and the pool keys connections on the dialled **path**, which an inproc "connection" must respect too — the path is the key, not the bus.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 4c875c1 and gated here: **390 tests**, no conflicts left for me — the worker had already merged main and kept B-017's per-path pool and the fingerprint admission through the conflicts in `limits.rs`, `listener.rs`, `stream.rs`, INVARIANTS and PROTOCOL. The shape worth knowing: `transport.rs` holds `Link`/`SendHalf`/`RecvHalf` as **enums rather than trait objects**, so the payload path stays a direct call and nothing above the boundary knows which transport it is on; `inproc.rs` is a process-global bus registry with one `tokio::io::duplex` pair per stream, sized by `stream_receive_window`, and it keeps the wire's own codes for the local equivalents of STOP_SENDING/RESET/FIN. `tests/transports.rs` runs one Req/Rep, Push/Pull and Pub/Sub body over **both** transports through one harness, which is what makes "the same tests the QUIC path uses" true rather than claimed; peer loss is `ConnectionLost(PeerClosed)`, and the guarantee negotiation and the drain are exercised over inproc too. `max_local_streams` (255) is enforced with `LimitExceeded` and lives on `Limits`, since it bounds one connection's live streams.

### B-038 — Local transport slice 2: `AF_UNIX` on Linux and macOS
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done b232909 | needs: [B-047]
acceptance: `weida+unix://<percent-encoded-path>/<path>` per [0010](decisions/0010-local-transport.md) §4.2, §4.5 **and the grouping 0012 decides**: `SOCK_STREAM`, mode set explicitly rather than inherited from `umask`, unlink-then-bind with the directory-permission requirement stated in the doc comment, the path budget validated after decoding (107 B Linux, 104 B macOS), and the peer identity from `SO_PEERCRED` (`uid`/`gid`/`pid`) or `LOCAL_PEERCRED` (`uid`/groups, **no PID**), captured at connect time and never authorized on by PID; a test asserts the identity a local peer presents and that a second bind over a stale socket file succeeds; `#[ignore]` with the reason on any platform where the suite cannot create the socket.
note: **blocked on a design hole the worker found rather than papered over.** With "one OS connection per transfer" (0010 §4.2) the *accepting* side cannot open a stream toward a client, which HELLO and Pub/Sub fan-out both need; inproc hid it because a duplex channel pair is symmetric. The alternatives it ruled out: `socketpair` plus `SCM_RIGHTS` needs `unsafe` and a new dependency and has no Windows equivalent, and a per-transfer HELLO would reset the per-connection ordering and dedup scopes on every transfer. So 0012 decides the grouping first (B-047) and this slice implements it. Delivered so far and not stubbed: the address half, `weida_core::UnixAddr`, commit 9e5434c on `b038-af-unix`.
note: delivered on `b038-af-unix` and merged here. 0012 §4.1-§4.3 built: the first connection is the peer's control connection and carries HELLO both ways, every further one opens with `0x02` and the server's 16-byte group token and is admitted only if the token names a live peer **and** the kernel credentials match (uid always, pid where reported), and a transfer connection is dispatched by path alone. `IncomingMeta::peer` is now `Option<PeerIdentity>` — `Key(Fingerprint)` on QUIC, `Local { uid, gid, pid }` here — so a local peer is proved by the kernel and never claimed. Two departures from the acceptance line, both right: the suite is `#[cfg(unix)]` rather than `#[ignore]`, because `UnixStream` does not exist off Unix and an ignored test would not compile; and **Pub/Sub is refused with `Unsupported` rather than supported**, because fan-out needs the reverse pool 0012 §4.4 leaves to B-048 — `Subscriber::connect` refuses at subscribe time instead of starving. The residual hygiene race is named rather than hidden: the mode is set after `bind`, so the directory the socket lives in is load-bearing, which the doc comment states and the suite honours with a private directory.

### B-039 — Local transport slice 3: named pipes on Windows
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done f3a3cbe | needs: [B-047, B-188]
acceptance: `weida+pipe://<name>/<path>` mapped to `\\.\pipe\<name>` and never a UNC path, per [0010](decisions/0010-local-transport.md) §4.5: message mode, an explicit DACL rather than the default that grants read to Everyone and the anonymous account, `PIPE_REJECT_REMOTE_CLIENTS` always set, both accept-loop races handled (`ERROR_PIPE_CONNECTED` treated as success, `ERROR_PIPE_BUSY` retried after `WaitNamedPipe`), the client token SID through `ImpersonateNamedPipeClient` as the identity with `RevertToSelf` guaranteed, and `max_local_streams` reconciled with the 1-255 instance cap; the tests are `#[ignore]` with the reason off Windows, and CI parity is out of scope until a Windows runner exists.
note: unblocked by 0012, which it inherits unchanged (§4.6): the first pipe instance is the control connection, further instances carry the same preamble and token, and the credential check reads the client's token through `ImpersonateNamedPipeClient` instead of `SO_PEERCRED`. No descriptor passing anywhere, which is what makes the shape portable — that constraint is why Option A was rejected rather than something to discover here.
note: **blocked on verification, not on design.** 0012 §4.6 leaves nothing to decide and the shape is inherited whole, so this item is writable today — and that is exactly the problem: every line of it is `#[cfg(windows)]`, so this tree's gate cannot compile it, cannot run it, and cannot fail on it. What would land is a slice whose only evidence is that it looked right, in the one area where the platform details are hostile: a DACL that must not grant Everyone, two accept-loop races, `RevertToSelf` on every path out of an impersonation, and the 1-255 instance cap meeting `max_local_streams`. `AF_UNIX` found a **design hole** (0012) that no amount of reading had found; the Windows slice would find its holes in the same way and nobody would see them. So it waits for a runner that can execute it, and `weida+pipe://` stays an address form 0010 decided and nothing implements. Revival is mechanical: a Windows CI runner, or a local Windows host the loop can reach.
note: **unblocked on 2026-09-13: a Windows runner exists.** `ssh win11-geselle` is a headless Windows 11 VM (build 26100, 8 threads, 16 GB) with MSVC 14.44, Rust 1.98.1 `stable-x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`, Git, Python 3.12, uv and CMake, a checkout at `C:\work\weida` and the gate script `C:\work\gate.ps1`. `needs` gains B-188 so the pipe code lands on a gate that is already green there.
note: **done f3a3cbe, gated on both platforms** — Linux 1680 tests, Windows 1658 across 119 binaries, fmt, clippy in both configurations and rustdoc green on each. Three things differ from the acceptance line, each for a reason recorded in IMPLEMENTATION's seventeenth increment: **byte mode, not message mode**, because 0012 made the framing the protocol's own and `mio` turns a message longer than its buffer into `ERROR_MORE_DATA`; **a bounded retry on the runtime's timer instead of `WaitNamedPipe`**, which blocks a thread; and **the 1-255 cap is not a cap** — 255 is `PIPE_UNLIMITED_INSTANCES`, so `max_local_streams` is the only ceiling and INVARIANTS closes its pending entry. What the acceptance did not anticipate: a pipe has **no half-close**, so the transport carries each stream in chunks (`0x00` payload, `0x01` FIN, `0x02` RESET with a code) and a stopped reader drains rather than closing the handle the reply still needs — stated in PROTOCOL §2.1 as the pipe's own loss. The grouping was generalised rather than copied: `grouped.rs` over a `Stream` trait, `unix.rs` down to eighty lines. And one crate may use `unsafe`: `weida-winpipe`, fifteen Win32 calls behind a safe surface, empty off Windows, because no dependency exposes a DACL and a token SID read safely. The tests are `#[cfg(windows)]` rather than `#[ignore]`d, the same shape as the unix suite.

### B-040 — Measure: the dedup key's allocation per call
kind: measure | size: 45 | status: done e46e67e | needs: [B-034]
acceptance: a criterion bench over `DedupWindow::is_duplicate` at a realistic entry count showing what the per-call `scope` boxing costs against a borrowed-key lookup, in the shape B-021 used for the filter matcher (measure through the public surface where possible, and say plainly where a `pub(crate)` item forced a different approach); the number goes in IMPLEMENTATION.md verified results, and it decides whether the key is worth restructuring — if it is not, the note in `dedup.rs` says so with the number beside it. Noticed by the worker while writing B-034 and deliberately not fixed there: an unmeasured allocation is not a reason to complicate a key.
note: the measurement says **do not restructure the key**, which is the answer the worker suspected and would not assert. The allocation is real and costs **~10 ns** per call (41.2 ns against 31.4 ns on a miss, isolated by probing the same table with a key built once), but the obvious fix does not pay: the candidate two-level `HashMap<Box<str>, HashMap<(producer, sequence), _>>`, whose outer lookup borrows `&str`, measures **42.0-42.8 ns** against the flat key's **41.1-41.3 ns** on a miss and the same within noise on a hit — a second hash lookup costs what the allocation cost. Not measurable through the public surface, since `DedupWindow` is `pub(crate)` and one loopback message would bury a nanosecond answer, so the two shapes are measured as pure functions in the shape B-021 used for the byte prefix, and the note says so plainly. For scale: 10 ns is 0.13 % of a 1 KiB push, and dedup is opt-in. Numbers in IMPLEMENTATION §4 with the command; `dedup.rs` carries the verdict beside the field it is about.

### B-041 — ZMTP bridge slice 2: inbound, foreign peers into weida
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done e517a90 c8f2a89 | needs: [B-030]
acceptance: a ZMTP listener that terminates the protocol with `weida-zmtp` and speaks weida onward, per [adapters/zmtp.md](adapters/zmtp.md) §1-§3: a foreign REQ reaches a weida `Replier` and its reply comes back; a foreign PUSH reaches a `Puller`; a foreign SUB receives from a weida `Publisher` with the filter translated per §6. One ZMTP message is one weida transfer and therefore one stream (§3); the socket-type check of §2 uses `SocketType::accepts` and answers a mismatch with `ERROR` then close; a **multipart** message is refused rather than flattened, which is loss L1 and the configured default (§9); the adapter's own `max_message_bytes` bounds what it buffers for a ZeroMQ peer that cannot be handed a body before it is complete, and a weida payload beyond it is refused with `STOP_SENDING(REJECTED)` and never truncated. No outbound direction, no interop bench yet.
note: the codec is finished and has no I/O; this slice owns the sockets, the handshake *driving* (send greeting, read greeting, send `READY`, read `READY`, then traffic), the subscription table with ZeroMQ's reference counting (loss L3: two SUBSCRIBEs need two CANCELs), and the weida-side reconnect loop that re-sends subscriptions — reconnect is not re-registration ([0008](decisions/0008-session-identity.md) §4.5). The codec deliberately left the state machine out, so its shape is this item's decision and should be recorded as one.
note: `crates/adapters/weida-zmtp-bridge`, its own crate rather than a feature of the codec — a feature would put a weida dependency in the codec's manifest, and that manifest being empty is the whole reason the codec is checkable against 37/ZMTP. Nine tests cover the three patterns and the four refusals: a REQ peer's exchange (with the empty delimiter consumed on the way in and mirrored onto the reply), a PUSH peer's messages, a SUB peer's subscriptions translated per §6 with the topic written as its own frame so the peer's prefix match lands on the topic, the socket-type mismatch answered with `ERROR` before the close, a multipart message refused rather than flattened, a mid-segment subscription refused with the connection kept alive, the §9.3 opt-in re-filtering locally, the `core` guarantee set enforced at configuration time, and an over-large declaration refused from the frame header alone. **Writing the tests found a real bug**, which is the thing worth remembering: the PUB loop selects over the socket and the weida subscriber, so the framed reader's `fill` is dropped routinely — and growing the buffer before the await and truncating after it left zero-filled slack behind on cancellation, which decodes as a stream of empty message frames. The bridge then refused its own buffer with "a SUB peer sent a message". Reading into scratch and appending after the read completes is what makes "cancel-safe" true, and the comment now records the version that looks equivalent and is not. The test peer is built on our own codec, which is faithful on the wire and *not* independent — §10 item 3 now says exactly that, so B-043's zmq.rs run is owed for the right reason rather than as a formality.

### B-042 — ZMTP bridge slice 3: outbound, weida endpoints to foreign peers
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done d6647db | needs: [B-041]
acceptance: the four inbound cases of B-041 with the directions reversed, plus DEALER/ROUTER against weida's concurrent exchanges ([ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §6b), per [adapters/zmtp.md](adapters/zmtp.md) §10 item 4: a weida `Requester` reaches a foreign REP, a `Pusher` reaches a foreign PULL, a weida `Subscriber` receives from a foreign PUB with the prefix boundary rule of §6, and envelope frames the pattern itself defines (REQ's empty delimiter, ROUTER's identity frame) are consumed rather than forwarded. `ZMQ_ROUTER_MANDATORY` surfaces as an error rather than a silent drop (loss L5); `ZMQ_HEARTBEAT_IVL` is enabled on the adapter's own sockets rather than trusting TCP (§3), and neither side's liveness timer is translated into the other's.
note: the mirror is not symmetric and that is the finding: inbound the bridge binds on the ZeroMQ side, outbound it binds the weida endpoint, because the endpoint path is what weida applications address. Req/Rep dials DEALER, not REQ, since a weida `Replier` takes concurrent exchanges and REQ is lockstep; the 28/REQREP id frame is what pairs a reply, tested with four exchanges answered in reverse. `ZMQ_ROUTER_MANDATORY` could not be honoured as the acceptance line assumed — it is an option on the ROUTER socket, which outbound belongs to the peer — so L5 was re-derived: the loss arrives as silence, and a per-exchange deadline turns it into `ERROR{NO_REPLY}`. That needed `IncomingRequest::refuse`, the public API 0005 §4.3 always implied and nothing had yet used. zmtp.md §8 L5 and §10 item 4 corrected; the deadline's value is filed as §11's new open question for B-043.

### B-043 — ZMTP interop bench against the pure-Rust `zeromq` crate
kind: measure | size: 90 | status: done d6f8a35 99c63a6 | needs: [B-042]
acceptance: the bench of [adapters/zmtp.md](adapters/zmtp.md) §10 items 3-6 against the pure-Rust `zeromq` crate as the foreign peer, started as a supervised process with a `ready` condition and stopped in the same item ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2): the inbound and outbound matrices as tests, every observable named loss of §8 asserted rather than described, and round-trip latency plus messages per second for REQ/REP and PUSH/PULL through the adapter against a direct `zeromq`-to-`zeromq` pair on loopback, recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md verified results with the command that produced them. Two numbers are decided here from measurement rather than guessed: the adapter's `max_message_bytes` default, closing §11's first open question, and the outbound reply deadline of B-042, closing the one B-042 filed.
note: **the slice paid for itself three times, and every disagreement was in our code.** (1) The codec refused ZMTP 3.0 outright, which refuses every implementation that never adopted 3.1 — `zeromq` 0.6 announces 3.0 — so `Greeting::accept_downgrading` takes the downgrade the specification permits and *returns* the negotiated version instead of assuming it. (2) The outbound heartbeat sent `PING`, a 3.1 command, to that 3.0 peer, which answers "Unknown command received" and closes: the heartbeat that existed to detect a dead connection *was* the connection loss, so it is now gated on the negotiated version. (3) A real SUB sends ZMTP 2.0's one-frame `1`/`0` subscription rather than a `SUBSCRIBE` command, so a PUB-side bridge that accepted only commands had no subscribers at all; both forms are accepted now and the spec form is still what we send, with `SubscriptionForm` for the other. A fourth, smaller, came from the cap test: an over-cap payload ended the whole ZMTP connection, where weida keeps refusals per stream (PROTOCOL §3) — it now refuses that transfer and serves on. Eleven interop tests, and one deviation from the acceptance line: **no supervised process**, because the foreign peer is a library dev-dependency, so the run needs no install step and no `#[ignore]` — the outcome LOOP §2's supervisor rule exists to make safe. Numbers: REQ/REP round trip 20.5 µs direct against 81 µs bridged at 1 KiB, 439 µs against 3.37 ms at 1 MiB; PUSH one-way +0.93 µs per small message. Linear in size with no cliff, which decided both open numbers — `max_message_bytes` **1 MiB** (the cap bounds memory, and 8 MiB × 1024 connections was 8 GiB nobody chose) and the reply deadline **10 s, kept** at ~3000× the slowest exchange. The same arithmetic found a bound the question had not asked about: the PUB queue was 1024 *messages*, which multiplies by the cap, and is now 8 MiB of bytes.

### B-044 — Decision 0011: how server-initiated traffic addresses a connection
kind: research | size: 45 | status: done a456f72 | needs: []
acceptance: `docs/decisions/0011-<slug>.md`, accepted, answering the question [0002](decisions/0002-control-and-bulk-separation.md) §7 left open and B-017 ran into: **a publisher writes fan-out on the connection the SUBSCRIBE arrived on, and nothing on the wire lets it choose a different one of that peer's per-path connections.** Until that is decided the control tier cannot carry SUBSCRIBE, and therefore cannot be justified at all (PROTOCOL §2.5). The options, each with its cost: (a) a HELLO key naming the dialled path, which makes the tiers distinguishable on the wire and contradicts §2.5's "nothing distinguishes them"; (b) SUBSCRIBE keeps riding its path's connection, which is today's behaviour and leaves an endpoint that publishes *and* subscribes on one path able to queue its own SUBSCRIBE behind its own payload; (c) fan-out rides the control connection, which puts payload on the tier meant to be free of it; (d) the fingerprint alone, which is ambiguous once a peer holds several per-path connections. Evidence to weigh: Zenoh's ALPN traffic classes, MOQT's control stream pair and iroh's one-connection-per-ALPN [prior-art §1, §2, §4], and the rule that a subscription is addressed to a *path* while HELLO and credit are addressed to a *peer*. SYNTHESIS §8.2's closing line is amended if the answer changes what 0002 decided.
note: the question turned out to be two questions wearing one coat, which is why 0002 §7 and 0008 §4.2 both thought they had answered it. 0008 answered *which connections are one peer* — the proved fingerprint, nothing else. What nobody had answered is *which connection a side writes its own traffic on*, and 0011 §4.1 decides it: **the one the registration arrived on.** Every protocol in the catalogue already does this and the sheets made it unanimous — EMQX sends a server-initiated PUBLISH "on the stream where it received that topic's subscription", a NATS `sid` is connection-local, a RabbitMQ consumer tag is channel-scoped, an AMQP 1.0 link is attached to one connection at a time and a second attach *steals* rather than addresses it. MQTT looks like the counter-example and is not: its session owns the association, and a session is precisely what weida does not have. The general rule that falls out is worth more than the answer: **a frame that names a path is path-scoped and rides that path's connection; a frame that names only the peer is peer-scoped.** Option E was the instructive rejection — naming another connection requires a stable identifier for it, which is a session identifier, excluded by PROTOCOL §11. **Consequence I did not expect when filing this:** the rule strikes the reserved L2 credit frame out of the control tier too (it names a subscription, which names a path), which amends 0003 §4.2 and leaves the tier with no cargo at all. So B-045 is parked, not merely unblocked, with a revival condition.

### B-045 — Control connection per peer
kind: code | size: 90 | status: parked: 0011 §4.3 — no frame needs it; revives on a peer-scoped latency-sensitive frame or a measurement of the 0011 §4.4 coupling | needs: [B-044]
acceptance: one control connection per peer beside the per-path connections of B-017, per [0002](decisions/0002-control-and-bulk-separation.md) §6.3: HELLO and negotiation on it, SUBSCRIBE/UNSUBSCRIBE moved to it once B-044 says how a publisher then addresses the subscriber's per-path connection, a second `Limits` profile in `RuntimeConfig` whose four differing numbers are justified individually, the `control_isolated` guarantee declarable as `Yes` and honoured, and a test that a control frame crosses while the peer's bulk path is stalled at its connection window. Blocked rather than ready on purpose: in v0 a control connection would carry nothing — every connection performs its own HELLO, the credit frame of PROTOCOL §11 does not exist, and SUBSCRIBE cannot move — so building it first would cost a handshake per peer and buy nothing.
note: parked rather than blocked after B-044: the addressing question that blocked it is answered, and the answer removed the tier's reason to exist. Reviving it needs one of two things, and both are checkable: a frame that names only the peer and must not wait (a peer-wide pause/resume, an authorization refresh, a liveness signal with application meaning), or a measurement showing that an endpoint publishing *and* subscribing on one path is really hurt by its SUBSCRIBE queueing behind its own payload (0011 §4.4). Until then the acceptance line above is kept as the design to implement, minus the SUBSCRIBE move, which 0011 §4.2 now forbids.

### B-046 — Test the drain's admission refusals
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 120756e | needs: [B-032]
acceptance: the two admission refusals of [0009](decisions/0009-drain.md) §4.5 get tests: a binding that is draining refuses a new connection outright rather than accepting and then closing it, and a new inbound stream on an existing connection is refused with `SHUTDOWN` while the drain waits. Both need a peer that keeps a connection alive while the drain is in progress — a second client whose transfer is parked, which the drain suite's frozen-reactor peer already shows how to build. Noted as untested by the B-032 worker rather than left to be discovered: a refusal path nothing exercises is a refusal path that can rot.
note: the reason these were untested is that a drain with nothing outstanding returns at once, so there is no window to race a connect against. The `Draining` harness builds one: a runtime that is a server **and** a stalled sender — a binding plus one finished transfer to a frozen peer whose receipt can never settle — so its drain runs for its whole deadline. The connection test carries its own control (the same client dials one path before the drain and another during it, which one connection per path makes a real handshake rather than a reuse); both tests fail under mutation, each when its own `is_draining()` check is disabled. **A vocabulary gap fell out:** `STOP_SENDING(SHUTDOWN)` fell through `stop_reason` to `Other(6)` and reached the sender as `Error::Transport("peer stopped receiving with code 6")`, so the test could only have pinned a string. `StopReason::ShuttingDown` now names it and maps to `Error::Rejected` — same outcome as any refusal, and B-028's lesson says not to add a second word for one outcome. FAILURE_MODEL §4 and IMPLEMENTATION carry it; **356 tests**.

### B-047 — Decision 0012: local connection grouping and server-initiated local streams
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done b232909 | needs: [B-037]
acceptance: `docs/decisions/0012-<slug>.md`, `Status: provisional`, answering the hole B-038 hit: **with one OS connection per transfer ([0010](decisions/0010-local-transport.md) §4.2) the accepting side has no way to open a stream toward a client**, which both the server's HELLO and Pub/Sub fan-out need. The note must name the constraints the evidence already fixes — `socketpair` plus `SCM_RIGHTS` needs `unsafe` and a new dependency and has **no Windows equivalent** [ipc §11], a per-transfer HELLO would reset the per-connection ordering and dedup scopes on every transfer ([0001](decisions/0001-sequence-field.md) §7.1, [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §6.5), and 0011 §4.1 already decides that traffic a side originates rides the connection the registration arrived on — and then decide the grouping: a control connection per client carrying HELLO with transfer connections bound to it by a server-issued token checked against the kernel credentials, server-initiated streams served from spare connections the client provides, or whatever the `ipc.md` sheet's evidence supports better. Whichever it is, it must hold for `AF_UNIX`, Windows named pipes **and** inproc, since inproc already works by being symmetric and must not become the odd one out; `max_local_streams` and the identity rules of 0010 §4.4 stay as they are. B-038 and B-039 implement it.
note: delegated to the parallel worker, which found the hole while writing B-038 and stopped rather than inventing a mechanism — the right call: this is a wire-shaped question for three transports at once. Provisional on purpose, and it is the one thing in the nightlog's Review needed section for the morning: a grouping mechanism is the kind of decision a human should confirm before two platform slices are built on it. The address half of B-038 (`weida_core::UnixAddr`, 9e5434c on `b038-af-unix`) is delivered and unaffected either way.
note: delivered as `docs/decisions/0012-local-connection-grouping.md`, `Status: provisional`, and the shape is Option E: the first connection a client opens **is** the peer's control connection and carries HELLO both ways plus everything the runtime holds per peer (`Agreed`, sequence scopes, the dedup window, the parked drain receipts); every further connection proves membership with a 16-byte server-issued token **and** matching kernel credentials, because the sheets show a token alone is not how anyone does this — D-Bus's `nonce-tcp` and Tailscale's Darwin fallback both pair a token with a kernel-checkable fact. `SCM_RIGHTS` is rejected on portability, not taste: Windows `AF_UNIX` has no ancillary data, pipes have no equivalent, and weida must not acquire the property ipc.md §7 names for Wayland — "a protocol that structurally cannot run on a transport without `SCM_RIGHTS`". The provisional half is §4.4, parked reverse connections for fan-out, and the note says exactly why: the sheets record **no precedent**, because every surveyed local protocol is client-driven over one long-lived connection, so a reverse pool is weida's own construction. Filed as B-048 with the id collision corrected (0012 §5 wrote B-047, which is this item).

### B-048 — AF_UNIX: parked reverse connections for fan-out
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 3eb4ce5 | needs: [B-038]
acceptance: a subscriber over `weida+unix://` parks spare connections carrying the `reverse` preamble and its group token, bounded by the new `Limits::max_parked_reverse` and counted against `max_local_streams`; the publisher takes one per fan-out copy and the subscriber replenishes; a publisher that finds no parked connection drops that copy and counts it in `Publisher::dropped`, with a test that exhausts the pool and observes the drop; a subscriber that parks nothing is reported at subscribe time rather than silently receiving nothing; GUARANTEES §6 gains the local fan-out row and PROTOCOL §2.1 the `reverse` preamble ([decisions/0012](decisions/0012-local-connection-grouping.md) §4.4).
note: filed verbatim from 0012 §5 and delegated to the parallel worker on `b047-reverse-pool` (the branch keeps the name the worker started it under; the item is B-048, because B-047 is the decision note's own id and ids are never reused). This is the item that decides whether 0012 stays provisional: §4.4 is the part with no precedent behind it, and if the pool turns out to be the wrong shape it is §4.4 that changes while §4.1-§4.3 stand.
note: delivered on `b047-reverse-pool` and merged here, and the shape survived contact: preamble `0x03` plus the group token is a parked reverse connection, admitted by the same token-and-credentials rule as a transfer connection, and `Link::open_uni` on the accepting side pops one. An empty pool is `Error::NoParkedConnection`, which becomes a dropped copy counted in `Publisher::dropped` while the writer continues and the subscription stays alive — the honest mapping, since fan-out is already the one place weida answers overload by discarding. `Limits::max_parked_reverse` (default 8) bounds it and `StreamSlot::acquire` keeps it inside `max_local_streams`; `max_parked_reverse = 0` is refused at subscribe time with `Unsupported` rather than accepted and starved. One thing the implementation sharpened rather than inherited: a replacement is parked on the **first byte** the publisher writes, not at the end of the copy, so a long transfer does not shrink the pool for its duration. The blanket Pub/Sub refusal over `weida+unix://` is gone and the shared fan-out body now runs over all three transports, which is what took 0012 from provisional to **accepted** with three named tests as evidence.

### B-049 — Research: NNG/SP adapter mapping document
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done ce88531 | needs: []
acceptance: `docs/adapters/nng.md` plus its row in `docs/adapters/` navigation, derived from [research/nanomsg-nng.md](research/nanomsg-nng.md) in the shape [adapters/zmtp.md](adapters/zmtp.md) set: the SP protocol-number table mapped onto weida patterns, the SP header and its hop count, REQ/REP request ids and resend timers against weida's stream-is-the-correlation, SURVEY and BUS as patterns weida does not have, the transfer points where each guarantee chain ends, every named loss with the reason it cannot be hidden, and the interop bench plan against a real `nng` peer. No sentence may translate a weida concept into SP vocabulary without a section reference on both sides.
note: delegated to the parallel worker as the first item of Phase B2. Phase B1's lesson is the acceptance line here: the ZMTP document's value was not the table but the losses it named in advance (L1-L9), and the two the interop bench later corrected (L5's option lives on the peer's socket; a refusal the peer treats as fatal) were the two nobody had checked against a real implementation. So this document must say for each loss **how it would be observed**, not only that it exists.
note: delivered complete and verified against the README shape: twelve sections in the order the template fixes, §2's protocol-number table against ARCHITECTURE §6b with the bind/connect direction rule, §3's one-message-one-stream mapping with the SP header and its hop stack, §7 stating plainly that **SP has no transfer point at all** (no application acknowledgement, no receipt, no persistence signal), §8's losses each carrying the how-would-it-be-observed clause the acceptance line asked for, §10.1's twenty accept vectors and six rejection vectors, and §11 admitting a gap in the *sheet* — the SP RFCs' byte-level layouts are prose there, so the octets, the endpoint type IDs and the PAIR v1 header shape are marked `[rfc-*]`/`[nng-src]` rather than attributed to the sheet. It reached main through my own directory-wide `git add` rather than a branch (see B-051's note); the content is the worker's and unaffected.

### B-050 — SP codec: sans-I/O, golden vectors, fuzz target
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done f4b690f | needs: [B-049]
acceptance: `crates/adapters/weida-sp` with an **empty `[dependencies]`**, mirroring B-030: the SP wire header and protocol handshake encoded and decoded with no I/O and no weida dependency, cap-before-allocate on every declared length, golden vectors published in the mapping document and asserted byte-for-byte in both directions, a `cargo fuzz` target per decode entry point plus a `fuzz_smoke` test that runs the corpus under `cargo test`, and every place the specification and the reference implementation disagree recorded as a decision in IMPLEMENTATION.md rather than as a comment. Branch `b050-sp-codec`.
note: delegated. The empty manifest is the point, as it was for `weida-zmtp`: a codec that cannot reach weida's types is checkable against the foreign specification instead of against our reading of it ([ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) §4). B-043 is the evidence that this pays — the ZMTP codec's golden vectors were what made a foreign peer's disagreements interpretable rather than mysterious.
note: merged `--no-ff` from `b050-sp-codec` and gated here: **465 tests**. `crates/adapters/weida-sp` with an empty `[dependencies]` and `unsafe_code = "forbid"`, four decode entry points (protocol header, message, backtrace, PAIR), every bound an argument rather than a constant, and the 64-bit size field checked before any allocation — the same rule `weida-zmtp` follows and for the same reason. Forty-two tests including the twenty accept and six rejection vectors of nng.md §10.1 in both directions, five `cargo fuzz` targets the worker actually ran (20k iterations each on nightly, clean) beside a stable-Rust `fuzz_smoke` in `cargo test`. Four specification-versus-implementation disagreements are published as decisions rather than comments, the sharpest being **PAIR v1's initial hop count**: the RFC says 1, NNG encodes 0, both decode, and the disagreement is exported as `pair::INITIAL_HOPS` so a bridge picks deliberately. That is exactly the shape B-043 argued for — a disagreement that is nameable is a disagreement an interop run can settle.

### B-052 — SP bridge slice 2: inbound, foreign peers into weida
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done d055590 | needs: [B-050]
acceptance: the mirror of B-041 for SP, per [adapters/nng.md](adapters/nng.md) §1-§3 and §6: one listener terminates SP with `weida-sp` and speaks weida onward — a foreign REQ reaches a weida `Replier` and its reply comes back with the request id and backtrace consumed rather than forwarded, a foreign PUSH reaches a `Puller`, a foreign SUB receives from a weida `Publisher` with the **receiver-side** prefix filter mapped per §6 (SP filters at the subscriber, which is the opposite end from ZMTP and the reason §6 has no 0007 row group). One SP message is one weida transfer and therefore one stream (§3); the endpoint-role check answers a mismatch before any traffic; every named loss of §8 that is observable gets a test rather than a sentence, including the hop-count ceiling and an oversized message; `max_message_bytes`'s default follows B-043's arithmetic — the cap bounds memory, so state the product it is chosen against. Branch `b051-sp-inbound` from `b050-sp-codec`.
note: delegated. Filed as B-052 because B-051 is the review pass already in progress under that id; the branch keeps the name the worker started it under, as `b047-reverse-pool` did for B-048. Phase B1's inbound slice is the shape to follow (`crates/adapters/weida-zmtp-bridge/src/inbound.rs`), and its two hard-won lessons apply unchanged: the framed reader must be cancel-safe by appending only after a completed read, and a refusal is per-stream unless the protocol makes it fatal.
note: merged `--no-ff` from `b051-sp-inbound` and gated here: **479 tests**. `crates/adapters/weida-sp-bridge`, three loops behind one listener: `REP` in front of a `Replier` with the **tag stack carried beside the exchange and written back unchanged** — requests are served concurrently and paired by tag, which the four-answered-in-reverse test pins the way the ZMTP slice does for the 28/REQREP envelope — `PULL` in front of a `Puller` with Block backpressure end to end, and `PUB` fed by a `Subscriber` on the **empty filter**, because SP filters at the receiver: the bridge must take everything and let the peer discard, which is loss L1 rather than a choice. A retransmitted REQ becomes a second exchange (L4), since SP's resend timer is the client's and nothing downstream can tell a retransmission from a new request. The bounds are stated as a product rather than left implicit, which is the B-053/B-054 lesson arriving before the defect instead of after: `max_message_bytes × 2 × max_in_flight × max_connections` = 512 MiB worst case, twice `max_in_flight` because a request payload is held while its exchange runs and its reply while it waits for the writer, and `max_hops × 4` octets bounds the tag stack a peer can make the bridge allocate. Zero in any factor is refused at `bind`. Eleven tests against a raw-TCP peer built on `weida-sp` — faithful and not independent, exactly as the ZMTP slice was before B-043, which is what the `nng` run is owed for. One new named loss came out of writing it: **L10, a refusal carries no reason** — SP has no ERROR frame, so everything this bridge refuses is observable only as a close.

### B-063 — SP bridge slice 3: outbound, weida endpoints to foreign peers
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 4b46bd6 a429a1c | needs: [B-052]
acceptance: the mirror of B-042 for SP, per [adapters/nng.md](adapters/nng.md) §10 item 4: the three inbound cases reversed — a weida `Requester` reaches a foreign REP with concurrent exchanges (SP contexts, one request each), a `Pusher` reaches a foreign PULL, a `Subscriber` is fed by a foreign PUB with the topic split of §6 applied on the way in. The weida side binds and the SP side dials, for the same reason the ZMTP outbound slice gives: the endpoint path is what weida applications address. The request id and tag stack this side synthesizes must be what a REP peer strips, and a reply that names no in-flight request is logged rather than fatal. Loss L10 applies in reverse — an SP peer that refuses has only a close to say it with — so a weida requester learns it as a typed error rather than a hang, the way B-042 answered the same question with a deadline. Bounds as a stated product, as B-052 did. Branch `b057-sp-outbound` from `b051-sp-inbound`.
note: delegated. The id is B-063 rather than B-057: B-057 is the **cross-adapter test** (ZMTP in, SP out), which this item is a prerequisite of, so its `needs` gains B-063. The branch keeps the name the worker started it under. What B-042 learned and this inherits: the mirror is not symmetric, and the interesting half is what a protocol without an error frame does when it cannot honour a request — for ZMTP that was `ZMQ_ROUTER_MANDATORY` living on the peer's socket, and here it is L10.
note: merged `--no-ff` from `b057-sp-outbound` and gated here: **494 tests**. The answer to L10-in-reverse is **two answers rather than one**, and the second is the worker's: B-042's per-exchange deadline is kept (with its reasoning restated, because the cause differs — a ROUTER *dropped* the request there, an SP peer may simply be silent and has no way to decline), and a **peer close now ends every pending exchange at once** instead of letting each wait out its deadline. That is right for exactly the reason L10 names: the close is the only statement SP can make, so waiting past it would be inventing patience the protocol never offered. Tested with an exchange parked behind a 600 s deadline, the peer dropped, and `NoReply` expected immediately. Two more decisions worth the record. **No resend timer**, asserted as an *absence* (one request on the wire, nothing after): retransmitting would manufacture at-least-once for a requester that asked for one attempt, which is the same refusal-to-invent that made a retransmitted inbound REQ a second exchange in B-052. And `TopicSplit::{Delimiter, Fixed, Constant}` with a matching `topic_delimiter` on the inbound side, because SP has no topic field at all — the split is adapter configuration, named rather than guessed, and it is what lets two of these bridges round-trip a topic for B-057. New loss **L11**: a weida filter does not become an SP subscription, since SP filters at the receiver, so the prefixes are applied locally. Fourteen tests, and a new §11 question only the `nng` interop run can settle — whether a real REP minds a requester that never retransmits.

### B-051 — Review pass since `fe2121e`
kind: review | size: 45 | status: done bfd011b | needs: []
acceptance: the pass of [LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §8 against the anchor `fe2121e`: full gate on a clean tree; `git diff fe2121e --stat` with every public API changed since read as its user would and every new test read as a reviewer would; INVARIANTS against the code, so that any new remote-influenced allocation without a named cap becomes a `ready` item at the top of this file; the normative documents grepped for identifiers that no longer exist; the backlog reordered by dependency and value with stale items parked; the pass logged with its new anchor hash and its findings filed as items.
note: overdue and by a wide margin — the anchor is the 06:20Z pass and fifteen items have finished since, including three merges from the parallel worker, two new transports, a decision that went provisional and then accepted, and an adapter crate pair. That is exactly the span where a review earns its place, so the pass is run in full rather than as a formality.
note: **six findings, all filed or fixed.** Gate green on a clean tree (fmt, clippy in both configurations, 465 tests, rustdoc). Public API since the anchor read as a user: `LossCause` on `ConnectionLost`, `Drained`, `Gap`, the guarantee dimensions re-exported from `weida-protocol`, the three address forms, `PeerIdentity`/`LocalPrincipal`, `Limits::{max_reorder_hold, max_dedup_entries, max_sequence_scopes, max_parked_reverse, keep_alive, idle_timeout}` and `IncomingRequest::refuse` — coherent, and every one of them documented where a user meets it; the `Limits`/`RuntimeConfig` split is the only breaking shape change and `0.x` permits it. Two **unbounded remote-influenced allocations** in the ZMTP bridge, filed at the top as B-053 (the outbound `pending` exchange map) and B-054 (the inbound subscription tables, which is the denial-of-service surface the ZeroMQ sheet names in libzmq itself) — both in the adapter rather than the core, which is where the invariant had never been swept before. Identifier sweep over 195 backticked names in six normative documents: two stale, both fixed here — ARCHITECTURE named a `ConnDriver` type that does not exist (it is `conn::driver` holding a `ConnCtx`), and PATTERNS §4 cited a renamed test. Docs against code turned up three **debt entries the implementation has since closed** and which therefore lied in the opposite direction: deduplication described as absent, fan-out drops described as invisible to a subscriber, and the fuzz-target count from before two adapter crates existed; all three now state what is true and what genuinely remains. Replenish: ten `ready` items filed (B-053 to B-062) from the review's own findings, the roadmap's next lines and §4's fifth source. New anchor `bfd011b`.

### B-053 — Bound the outbound bridge's pending exchanges
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done a52356e | needs: [B-042]
acceptance: `serve_dealer`'s `pending` map in `crates/adapters/weida-zmtp-bridge/src/outbound.rs` gets a named ceiling — a config field with a documented default and the product it is chosen against, in the shape B-043 set for `max_message_bytes` — and an exchange arriving past it is refused with `ERROR{REJECTED}` immediately rather than accepted and held. A test drives more concurrent exchanges than the ceiling against a peer that answers none, and asserts that the excess is refused while the ones inside the ceiling still get their replies once the peer answers. INVARIANTS' enforced-bounds cell gains the row.
note: found by the B-051 review pass, §8 step 3. Each pending entry holds an `IncomingRequest` **and** its body, already read, up to `max_message_bytes`; the number of entries is whatever weida clients open, so the exposure is remote-influenced and has no named cap — which is the invariant "no remote input can cause unbounded memory allocation" read literally. The transport bounds concurrent streams *per connection*, not the sum across clients, and the reply deadline only bounds how long an entry lives.
note: `max_pending_exchanges`, 64, and the ordering of the two checks is the part worth reading: the ceiling is tested **before** the body is read, because a request refused early costs this side nothing while reading first would buffer up to `max_message_bytes` for an exchange about to be turned away. Refused immediately with `ERROR{REJECTED}` rather than parked, so a requester the peer could never reach does not wait out a 10 s deadline to learn it. The test fills a ceiling of two against a peer that answers nothing, asserts the third is refused *and* that nothing of it reaches the peer, then has the peer answer the two inside — which is what makes it a test about a ceiling rather than about a refusal. It fails under mutation (the ceiling check disabled: the third request hangs and the test times out). A zero ceiling is refused at configuration time beside the other four. INVARIANTS now says plainly that the adapters are inside the no-unbounded-allocation invariant and had been read as if they were not — 64 MiB against a 1 MiB cap is the stated product.

### B-054 — Bound the inbound bridge's subscription tables
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done c532eeb | needs: [B-041]
acceptance: `Subscriptions` in `crates/adapters/weida-zmtp-bridge/src/subscriptions.rs` gets the two ceilings weida already applies to the same thing — a count and a per-prefix length, `max_subscriptions` (256) and 256 B per filter per [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §10 — so a peer cannot grow `raw`, `filters` or `refilters` without limit; a subscription past either ceiling is refused with `ERROR` the way a mid-segment prefix already is. Tests: a peer that sends more distinct prefixes than the count allows has the excess refused and keeps the ones it had, and an over-long prefix is refused before it is stored. INVARIANTS' enforced-bounds cell gains the row.
note: found by the B-051 review pass, §8 step 3. ZMTP subscriptions are additive and non-idempotent by design — "N repeated SUBSCRIBE commands cost N entries" [zeromq §11] — and the sheet lists that as a documented denial-of-service surface in libzmq itself. weida caps exactly this per connection; the bridge, which is the thing exposed to foreign peers, does not.
note: 256 distinct prefixes of at most 256 B each — weida's own `max_subscriptions` and the wire's cap on a SUBSCRIBE `filter`, so the two ends of the hop have comparable ceilings and the exposure is 64 KiB of prefixes per peer. The subtlety the tests pin: **a repeat is accepted even at the ceiling**, because it allocates nothing and because loss L3's count is the thing being protected — refusing a second SUBSCRIBE would leave the first cancellable by one CANCEL where the peer sent two, which is the non-idempotence the bridge exists to preserve. So only a prefix the table does not already hold can be turned away, the length check runs before anything is stored, and a freed slot is reusable (the ceiling is a ceiling, not a lifetime quota). Both refusals are `BridgeError::Subscription`, which the PUB loop already treats as non-fatal: the peer is told with `ERROR` and keeps the subscriptions it has. Two unit tests rather than an integration test, because the wire path for a refused subscription is already covered by the mid-segment case and a second one would be padding.

### B-055 — Research: what the ZMTP implementation taught the sheet
kind: research | size: 45 | status: done 0bb5c4f | needs: [B-043]
acceptance: `docs/research/zeromq.md` updated with what building and running the adapter taught, in the sheet's own protocol-native vocabulary and with no sentence mentioning weida ([research/README.md](research/README.md) rule 1): §13's ecosystem entry for the `zeromq` crate gains what the interop run established about it — announces ZMTP 3.0, decodes `READY` only, sends and reads the ZMTP 2.0 subscription form — each marked as measured against version 0.6 rather than as a specification claim; the version-negotiation paragraph of §1 gains the consequence that a 3.1 peer meeting a 3.0 peer must suppress `PING`/`PONG`; and the subscription section records both wire forms with the RFC that introduced each. The sheet's rule 2 applies: every new claim carries its source with a version or date, and `[inference]` where it is ours.
note: the research item LOOP §9 requires after every Phase B slice, and there is real material for it: three of the run's four findings were about *the ecosystem*, not about our code's correctness, and a sheet that had said them would have saved the slice.
note: four additions, and the shape matters as much as the content: everything the run established is attributed to a **new source [40]** — the crate version, the files read and the behaviour observed — so nothing measured against `zeromq` 0.6.0 can later be mistaken for a claim about ZeroMQ or about a later release. §13 gains the four observable facts (announces 3.0; recognizes only `READY`, so `PING`/`PONG`/`ERROR` all close the connection; sends and reads the ZMTP 2.0 subscription form; collapses a repeated subscribe before the wire, which is why it cannot exercise the non-idempotent counting §11 describes). §1 gains the paragraph the specification does not have: **what a downgrade costs**, since a peer cannot discover which commands a 3.0 peer accepts by trying — an unrecognized command name may be answered by closing. §6 gains the two subscription wire forms, the fact that they are distinguishable on the wire, and the consequence that a publisher reading only one of them silently has no subscribers from an implementation that sends the other; 37/ZMTP does not mention the message form at all. The index row says which sections carry measured material. No sentence in the sheet mentions weida, which `grep` confirms.

### B-056 — Spec sync: the local transports in the normative documents
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done 74eeec5 | needs: [B-048]
acceptance: the three transports are one vocabulary in the normative documents rather than two: [PATTERNS.md](PATTERNS.md) §1.8's liveness and reconnect paragraphs say what they mean over a transport with no idle timeout, §1.3's head-of-line paragraph says what "one connection per path" means where the OS connection *is* the stream, and [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6's backpressure and drop rows name the local fan-out drop beside the subscriber-budget one. A `grep` for `quinn`, `QUIC` and `stream_receive_window` in the pattern and guarantee documents shows no claim that is true only of QUIC left unqualified.
note: found by the B-051 review pass, §8 step 4. `AF_UNIX` and inproc arrived under the transport boundary, and the transport-neutral documents still describe a QUIC-only world in several places — `a_stalled_path_does_not_stall_another_path` has no meaning where each transfer is its own socket, and the reverse pool's drop is a second drop cause GUARANTEES §6 does not list beside the first.
note: the fix is a new **PATTERNS §1.10, "what a local transport changes"**, rather than a qualification scattered through nine subsections: five statements above it mean something else locally and all five have the same cause — there is no shared connection, because one transfer is one channel pair or one OS connection. So §1.2's window arithmetic has no local analogue (a receipt is never evidence that the application is reading), §1.3's two windows collapse into one and `a_stalled_path_does_not_stall_another_path` is revealed as a statement about QUIC that is true by construction locally, §1.4's stream budget becomes a descriptor count where `open` **errors** with `LimitExceeded` instead of waiting, §1.8's liveness is the kernel's rather than a timer's — which is a better signal, and the reason no local heartbeat was invented — and §4's fan-out needs a parked reverse connection, whose exhaustion is a **second drop cause** beside the subscriber byte budget. The preamble and §1's opening no longer say "one QUIC stream" as if that were the only case, §1.9 and GUARANTEES' peer-identity row now carry the local principal and the group token, and the backpressure and control-isolation rows say what they mean where nothing shares a window. `grep` for `QUIC`/`quinn`/`stream_receive_window` in both documents leaves only claims that are explicitly about QUIC.

### B-057 — The cross-adapter test: ZMTP in, SP out
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 525db84 | needs: [B-052, B-063]
acceptance: the slice (6) both mapping documents have been promising ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §9, [adapters/zmtp.md](adapters/zmtp.md) §10 item 7, [adapters/nng.md](adapters/nng.md) §10): a message enters through the ZMTP bridge and leaves through the SP bridge, with the guarantees of *both* documents asserted — the weakest link named in the test itself, since ZMTP's `delivered()` proves the next hop's transport and SP has no transfer point at all, so the chain's honest claim is `BestEffort` end to end and the test says so rather than implying more. Both foreign ends are the real implementations (`zeromq` and `nng` crates as dev-dependencies); the losses that compose — a ZMTP multipart refused at the first hop, an SP hop-count ceiling at the second — are asserted rather than described.
note: the first item where two adapters meet, and the reason both documents were written in one shape. It needs B-052 for the inbound half of the chain and B-063 for the outbound one — "ZMTP in, SP out" is exactly one of each.
note: delegated on `b057-cross-adapter` from `b057-sp-outbound`, now that both halves of the chain exist. The `topic_delimiter`/`TopicSplit` pair B-063 added is what makes a topic survive the crossing, and it was added *for* this item rather than discovered by it. The claim the test must not overstate: ZMTP's `delivered()` proves the ZeroMQ hop's transport and SP has no transfer point at all, so end to end the chain is `BestEffort` — and the test says that in an assertion rather than in a comment.
note: **delivered on `b057-cross-adapter`, head `14c27fd`, main `e357a4e` merged in and the gate green there — not merged into main, because the session stopped first.** Whoever resumes: merge `--no-ff`, gate, close the item, then write the one "cross-adapter" line each mapping document's bench-plan section owes (zmtp.md §10 item 7, nng.md §10) — the worker left that text in its final report rather than touching the main tree after the stop. What it contains: a new `crates/adapters/cross-tests` crate (`publish = false`, no library code) with nine tests — ZMTP REQ→NNG REP with each envelope restored on its own side, ZMTP PUSH→NNG PULL, ZMTP PUB→NNG SUB through both topic conventions, the reverse PUSH and PUB chains, **`BestEffort` asserted end to end** (the ZeroMQ send succeeds while the far end is closed, and a fresh puller proves nothing arrived), a ZMTP multipart refused at the first hop with nothing reaching the second, the smaller of the two caps deciding, and an SP hop-count ceiling at the second hop reaching the first peer only as silence. Both foreign ends are the real `zeromq` and `nng` crates as the acceptance line asks, with one stated exception: the hop-count test's SP peer is raw TCP on `weida-sp`, because no single REP produces a multi-hop tag stack without an `nng_device` topology — the test says so rather than implying independence it does not have.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 525db84 and gated here: **503 tests**, one ignored (the 1 GiB memory test), no conflicts. The two "cross-adapter" lines the mapping documents owed are written ([adapters/zmtp.md](adapters/zmtp.md) §10 item 7, [adapters/nng.md](adapters/nng.md) §10 item 7) and IMPLEMENTATION.md §1 gained the sixteenth increment. One thing the merge settles beyond the acceptance line: the `nng` crate builds on this machine, so the chain's SP end is the real C library and **not** `#[ignore]`d — which makes this run the independent peer nng.md §10 items 3 and 4 were owed, and answers §11's first open question (a real `Rep0` replies to a raw requester that never retransmits). PAIR is in no chain, so the v1 hop-count disagreement stays open on the wire.

### B-059 — Measure: what the local transports cost against QUIC
kind: measure | size: 60 | status: done 6ca89ad | needs: [B-048]
acceptance: the pattern bench extended with the inproc and `AF_UNIX` transports beside loopback QUIC, in the shape `benches/patterns.rs` already uses: Req/Rep round trip and Push/Pull one-way at 1 KiB and 1 MiB over all three, plus what a local connection costs in memory against the 995 KiB per QUIC connection B-012 measured. The numbers go into IMPLEMENTATION.md verified results with the command, and they answer the question 0010 §4.2 left implicit — whether "the OS connection is the stream" pays for the connection per transfer it costs, and at which payload size the answer changes.
note: **stopped mid-item at the user's request, with the fix that is green committed (`81d70e7`) and the measurement not yet recorded.** Where it stands: the bench exists (`crates/weida/benches/transports.rs`, three transports, Req/Rep and Push at 1 KiB and 1 MiB, plus an RSS-per-live-local-connection report) and the Req/Rep rows ran — inproc 14.4 µs and unix 55.9 µs against QUIC's 67.1 µs at 1 KiB, inproc 162 µs and unix 348 µs against 2.70 ms at 1 MiB — but the **Push rows do not complete locally**, and that is the second bug this item found rather than a bench defect: a queued inbound transfer holds its stream, and locally a stream is a descriptor, so a fire-and-forget sender fills `endpoint_queue` (1024) with the 255 descriptors `max_local_streams` allows and the 384th send fails with `LimitExceeded`. The first bug — parked receipts holding every local descriptor — is fixed and has a 1000-exchange regression test over both local transports. What is left is a **design choice, not a repair**: either a local `open` waits for a free slot (which is what `Block` means in GUARANTEES §6 and would make Push/Pull behave the same on every transport) or the local ceiling stays an error and PATTERNS §1.10 plus the backpressure row say plainly that Push/Pull degrades from `Block` to `Reject` locally. I tried the cheap third option — polling a receipt at park time so a settled one is never parked — and the header bench says it is not free; it also says nothing conclusive, because a second `cargo` was running in another worktree and the same bench reported +19 % against its own baseline *after* the change was reverted. So: re-measure on a quiet machine, then choose. Nothing is stubbed and the tree is clean.
note: 0010 chose one connection per transfer on a structural argument (no multiplexer to reimplement) with no number beside it, and B-048 then added a parked pool whose default of 8 is also unmeasured. This is the item that prices both. B-043's reading applies: a one-way row measures enqueue rather than delivery, so the round trip is the honest comparison.
note: user decided: local open blocks on a free slot; in progress on branch `b059-local-block` by the parallel worker. That is `Block` as GUARANTEES §6 defines it, so Push/Pull behaves the same on every transport and PATTERNS §1.10 does not have to record a local degradation to `Reject`.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 6ca89ad and gated here: **505 tests**, one ignored, one auto-merge in IMPLEMENTATION.md and nothing to resolve by hand. The numbers, two runs, release, both ends in this process (IMPLEMENTATION.md §4 carries them with the command): Req/Rep at 1 KiB **58.3-58.8 µs** QUIC, **13.2-13.5 µs** inproc, **50.6-50.8 µs** unix; at 1 MiB **2.70-2.73 ms**, **144-145 µs**, **266-312 µs**. Push one-way at 1 KiB **7.97-8.08 µs**, **2.03-2.07 µs**, **24.0-24.1 µs**; at 1 MiB **1.36 ms**, **57.7-79.5 µs**, **130-147 µs**. A live local connection is **0-1761 B** of RSS against the **995 KiB** B-012 measured for QUIC. **The finding is the 1 KiB Push row: `AF_UNIX` is 3× slower than loopback QUIC there** (24.0 µs against 8.0), because QUIC opens a stream inside a connection it already has while `AF_UNIX` opens a connection — the same fixed ~22 µs that is invisible at 1 MiB is most of the message at 1 KiB. So 0010 §4.2's structural argument holds for bulk and for in-process work and *fails* at high message rates over `AF_UNIX`, which is the price of not reimplementing a stream layer, now with a number beside it. The `Block` decision landed as the design change the measurement forced: `StreamSlot` is a tokio `Semaphore` permit and a local `open` awaits one in `select!` against `closed()`, so a waiter holds nothing and a dropped future cancels the wait; `LimitExceeded` survives only where waiting would deadlock, the reverse-pool park, which takes its permit with `try_acquire`. `push_pull_waits_for_a_slot_over_{inproc,unix}` drive 600 concurrent sends against 255 slots and fail on the old code with "send 255 of 600 failed: LimitExceeded". A pre-existing hang in the bench's `report_memory` was fixed on the way.

### B-060 — The `weida` CLI: inspect an endpoint from a shell
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 68b1ba9 | needs: []
acceptance: the phase-11 tracker row ([IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §1) reduced to its first useful slice: a `weida` binary that can `send`, `request`, `sub` and `serve --echo` against any of the three transports from a shell, with the fingerprint printed and pinnable, so a user can exercise an endpoint without writing Rust. Payload from a file or stdin and back to stdout, so it composes; exit codes distinguish the error vocabulary a script can act on (refused, unknown endpoint, no reply, connection lost); the README's Quick start gains the two commands that replace its current "write a program first".
note: my own judgement of what a user of this tree hits first, which is §4's fifth source. Every example in the repository is a Rust file, and the fastest way to find out whether an endpoint is reachable is currently to write and compile a program. It is also the shortest path to a manual reproduction for a bug report.
note: done 68b1ba9. `crates/weida/src/bin/weida.rs`: `serve` (`--echo` replier, `--sink` puller, `--pub` publisher reading `topic body` lines), `request`, `send` and `sub` (`--filter`, `--count`), over QUIC, `AF_UNIX` and named pipes, `--ca`/`--identity`/`--cert-out` as the examples have them. Payload on stdin and stdout and nothing else on them; addresses, receipts, topics and gaps on stderr, so `weida request … | sha256sum` means what it looks like. Exit codes `3` refused, `4` unknown endpoint, `5` no reply, `6` untrusted, `7` connection lost, `2` usage, `1` everything else — `Indeterminate` deliberately in the last bucket, because a script must not be able to match it as a definite outcome. Three decisions worth the record: a **bind** address is parsed by the binary rather than by `Address::parse`, since a bind address may say port `0` and carries no fingerprint (the one it prints is its *output*); `weida+inproc://` is refused with a sentence naming why, because an in-process bus cannot be reached from another process; and stdio goes through `std` rather than tokio's `io-std`/`fs`, so a binary does not widen what every downstream crate compiles. `[[bin]] required-features = ["generate"]`, because `serve` mints an identity when given no `--identity`. Verified by running it: an echo round trip over QUIC, a `send` into a `--sink` over `AF_UNIX`, a `--pub`/`sub --count 2 --filter px.#` pair where the non-matching topic is not delivered, and all four failure exit codes. README gained the Quick start that replaces "write a program first"; IMPLEMENTATION §1 marks Phase 11 **first slice done**.

### B-061 — CI: the gate on every push
kind: code | size: 45 | status: parked: the owner is building their own CI | needs: []
acceptance: the debt entry "No CI" in [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §6 closed for the part that does not need a decision: a workflow that runs exactly the gate of [LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §6 — `cargo fmt --all --check`, `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` and again `-p weida --no-default-features --all-targets`, `cargo test --workspace`, `RUSTDOCFLAGS="-D warnings" cargo doc --workspace --no-deps` — on a Linux runner with the pinned toolchain, plus the `-- --ignored` suite as a separate, non-blocking job because the 1 GiB echo asserts an RSS ceiling a shared runner may not honour. The fuzz targets stay out: they need nightly and a time budget. Windows and macOS are named as absent rather than silently missing, which is also what B-039 waits for.
note: the gate exists only in this loop's head today, so a contributor cannot reproduce the bar the loop holds itself to. Sized 45 because it is configuration plus one honest decision — which suites block a push and which report — and no library code.
note: **parked by the owner (2026-09-15): they are building their own CI, so this item does not design one.** What it leaves behind for whatever runs it: the gate is now fully written down in [LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §6 — four workspace commands, five feature configurations, ten rustdoc runs and `cargo clean --doc` in front of the doc step (B-107, B-184) — so a runner has a specification rather than my habits to copy. The measured shape of the load: **150 s** warm for the four workspace commands on 16 cores, **89 s** for the feature runs, **8 s** for the doc matrix, and a `target/` that reached **197 GiB** before it was cleaned, which is the number a runner's disk budget has to answer for. The `-- --ignored` suite stays a separate non-blocking job for the reason the acceptance gives: the 1 GiB echo asserts an RSS ceiling a shared runner may not honour.
note: **blocked on a runner, measured rather than assumed (2026-09-13).** The Forgejo host (`sadala`) already has the hook: `/etc/forgejo/ci-runner` runs `./ci.sh` from every pushed commit in a scratch checkout and streams its output to the pusher — the `[ci] refs/heads/main: no ci.sh, skipping` line every push prints. What it does not have is a machine that can run this gate: **2 vCPUs, 3 GB of RAM, a 600 s limit per job, and a scratch checkout with no build cache**, so a cold `cargo clippy --workspace` — quinn, rustls, PyO3, the vendored NNG C library — would neither finish in the limit nor fit in memory, and `rustup` there has no default toolchain for the `git` user. A `ci.sh` that ran only `cargo fmt --all --check` would pass the hook and prove nothing the gate proves, which is the dishonest half of this item and not taken. What closes it is one decision that is yours: a runner with a cache and cores — this workstation over `ssh` from the hook (with the push blocked for the gate's ~4 min, or the hook returning after dispatching), the Windows VM's host, or a real Forgejo Actions runner — and the choice of which suites block a push. Until then the gate is run by hand on both platforms, as every item's note records.

### B-064 — Streaming fan-out: a publisher that hands out a stream per subscriber
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 600ad34 | needs: []
acceptance: a publisher can fan out a payload it never materializes — `Publisher::open(topic) -> OutgoingTransfer` or an equivalent that writes once into one stream per matched subscriber — so a message above `Limits::subscriber_buffer_bytes` (8 MiB) can be published at all; the existing whole-`Bytes` `publish` keeps its meaning and its `LimitExceeded`; the drop behaviour of GUARANTEES §6 applies per subscriber, not per publish; a test fans a payload larger than the subscriber budget out to two subscribers at different read speeds and asserts bounded peak memory the way `large.rs` does; the Phase 3 deferral in [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §6 is closed or narrowed to what remains.
note: source `docs/requirements/zeughaus-video.md`, request 1 of five and the one it leads with. Without it, push-based bulk fan-out is impossible above `subscriber_buffer_bytes`, and raising that limit buys a per-subscriber copy of the whole payload inside the publisher — the materialization INVARIANTS exists to forbid.
note: done 600ad34. `Publisher::open(topic)` returns a `FanOut`: one unidirectional stream per matched subscriber, written `write_within(chunk, limit)` or `write_now(chunk)`, finished with `finish()` and **reset on drop** so nobody mistakes a partial payload for a whole one. The per-subscriber writer grew an item enum — `Begin`/`Chunk`/`Finish`/`Abort` beside the whole message — so the byte budget now bounds a *chunk* and the payload has no ceiling; one `Bytes` is shared by every copy, and the ending slot is **reserved at `open`** (`try_reserve_owned`), because a subscriber whose queue filled mid-payload would otherwise hold an open stream waiting for a FIN nobody could enqueue. Two methods rather than one, and that is the item's real finding: a non-blocking write alone makes a payload larger than `subscriber_buffer_bytes` undeliverable to *anybody* — the publisher outruns its own budget and aborts every copy — so `write_within` takes a **mandatory finite bound**, the shape `drain(Duration)` already has, while `write_now` keeps fan-out's pure `Drop` for the case where a later chunk supersedes an earlier one. The drop is per subscriber and per transfer, counted in `dropped_on(topic)` like any other. Three tests, the second mutation-checked (with `write_now` in its place both subscribers are lost, which is what the bound buys). The wire needed **nothing**: PROTOCOL §11's "streaming fan-out is not specified" is now a paragraph saying why none was needed — the same DATA frame without `content_len`, and `RESET_STREAM(CANCELED)` for the drop.

### B-065 — Research: conflation as a transport guarantee, `Coalescing(key)`
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done | needs: [B-064]
acceptance: a decision note in `docs/decisions/` answering where "keep the newest, discard the rest" lives — the reserved broker-level `Coalescing(key)` of [GUARANTEES.md](GUARANTEES.md) §6, a fan-out drop policy beside `Drop`, or nowhere in L0/L1 at all — with the evidence from the sheets (what ZeroMQ's conflate option, MQTT's retained message and NATS' last-value bucket each actually do) and the consequence for the guarantee vocabulary spelled out; the note says plainly which layer owns the key and what a subscriber observes when a value is skipped.
note: source `docs/requirements/zeughaus-video.md`, request 2. Needs B-064 because the requester's own argument is that a private stream per consumer conflates by construction, and only fan-out makes the guarantee load-bearing: one producer and N consumers at different speeds is where the discard has to be the transport's rather than the producer's.
note: done. `docs/decisions/0016-conflation.md`, `Status: provisional`: **no key in L0/L1**, and option B — a fan-out coalescer beside `Drop` — is *refused* rather than deferred, because holding the value that supersedes another is exactly the per-subscriber copy B-064 was built to remove. The v0 answer is the API that now exists: the producer holds the single slot, `Publisher::open` + `write_now` makes the discard the transport's rather than the producer's, and `subscriber_buffer_bytes` sized to about one value is the whole configuration. `Coalescing(key)` stays reserved for L2 with its preconditions written down and its bill itemized; the one thing a key would buy is several keys multiplexed over one subscription. The claim-collision the item hints at is resolved in the order the code already has: a copy is numbered **before** fan-out, so a discarded one is a gap a `PerProducer` subscriber can see rather than a renumbering nobody can.

### B-066 — Research: peer authorization beyond certificate trust
kind: research | size: 60 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: a decision note answering whether a weida handshake carries an application credential at all — a HELLO-carried token validated by the acceptor, an application handshake on a separate path, or neither — against [0008](decisions/0008-session-identity.md)'s "the peer is its public key" and the sheets' precedents (ZMTP's PLAIN/CURVE mechanisms, MQTT 5's enhanced authentication, NATS' authorization callout); the note states what an acceptor may decide *per endpoint path* and what it may not, since paths are opaque (INVARIANTS).
note: source `docs/requirements/zeughaus-video.md`, request 3, which files it as a question rather than a blocker: certificate identity says *who* answered, nothing says *which* peer may attach to which endpoint. The requester can do it application-side if the answer is "neither", so the value of the item is the answer, not the code.
