### B-177 — Concurrent send and receive on one `weida-zmq` socket
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4e30122 | needs: []
acceptance: a socket of the types where the two directions are independent — DEALER, ROUTER, PAIR, XPUB, XSUB — can be split into a sending half and a receiving half that are usable **at the same time** from two tasks, so a task parked in `recv` no longer makes a concurrent `send` on that socket wait behind it; REQ and REP keep their alternation, because the RFC requires it and the split would be a lie there. A test parks a receive that will not complete and asserts a send on the same socket completes anyway, and `weida-zmq-py` exposes the halves so the same test passes from Python. No second implementation of any pattern behaviour in the binding.
note: requested by the user through W1 while B-113 was in flight. The limitation is the library's rather than the binding's: `weida-zmq`'s `send`/`recv` take `&mut self` and a socket is `Send + !Sync`, so the binding's one-exclusive-resource lease is a faithful projection of the Rust surface, and pyzmq permits the overlap. The fix therefore belongs here — a binding-side workaround would be a second implementation of the pattern layer, which is exactly what 0013 §4.4 exists to prevent. W1 will name it as a difference from pyzmq with this reason in `docs/libraries/zmq-py.md` under B-118 whether or not this item has landed by then, so the document is honest either way.
note: done 4e30122, and the library change is a **removal**: −691 lines against +493, because the pattern bodies the whole sockets had are now in `split.rs` and both the sockets and the halves call them. `SocketCore` is a `!Sync` handle that `Deref`s to a `Sync` inner (engine, executor, the two round-robin cursors as `Arc<AtomicUsize>`), so every send and receive takes `&self` and `split(self)` can hand out two handles onto one socket — DEALER, ROUTER, PAIR, XPUB, XSUB. REQ and REP have none, as the acceptance requires. The ROUTER's routing table is a `Mutex` shared by both halves and never held across an await, which is the only place the split needed a lock at all. From Python: ten half classes generated by a macro from `ops`' four shapes (so no send behaviour is written twice), and `await sock.split()` **retires** the object it was called on — the lease slot grew `Present`/`Leased`/`Retired` and `acquire` now answers `ENOTSOCK` naming the split instead of parking on a socket that will never come back. The acceptance's test exists on both sides — park a receive, assert it pending, then complete a full round trip on the other half — and neither could be written before. `zmq-py.md` §9.1 stopped being "the one limitation that is not deliberate"; `zmq.md` §9 gained the one API libzmq does not have and why C does not need it. Gate: 1692 tests / 0 failed / 37 ignored, Python zmq 120.

### B-178 — A device whose end cannot receive is not an error
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done ccb00b3 | needs: []
acceptance: `weida_zmq::proxy` treats a side that cannot receive as a side that never delivers rather than as a failure, so a PULL→PUSH device — the zguide's own streamer — forwards instead of dying at the first poll with `ENOTSUP`; the same holds for the steerable form and for the capture socket. A test runs a PULL→PUSH proxy end to end and asserts a message crossing, and one runs a PUSH→PULL pair in the direction that has no reverse path and asserts the proxy stays alive; the XSUB/XPUB and ROUTER/DEALER proofs keep passing unchanged.
note: found by W1 while writing B-115's Python proxy test, and it is a real defect rather than a binding limitation: `proxy` reads both sides, and `one_way_device!`'s `recv` returns `ENOTSUP`, so the whole device dies immediately on any pair where one end is send-only or receive-only. W1's test uses DEALER ends instead, so the B-115 proof stands, but the streamer of the guide cannot be built from this surface today. Small, and the kind of gap only a second user finds.
note: done ccb00b3. `Device` gained `fn receives(&self) -> bool`, `true` by default and `false` for `PushSocket` and the `publish_only` ends, and both proxy loops read a side only if it receives — a method rather than an associated const because `weida-zmq-py` erases the socket type behind a box and only knows at run time; `Erased` forwards it, so the Python `proxy` gets the same behaviour with no second implementation. A device whose two ends both never deliver is refused with `ENOTSUP` rather than parked forever (the steerable form keeps reading its control socket, so `TERMINATE` still ends it). Proved three ways: `a_streamer_forwards_through_a_side_that_never_receives` runs the guide's streamer with a PUSH capture socket and then the reversed pair under the steerable form, asserting the crossing, the copy, that the task is alive and that the PUSH frontend's inbound counter stays zero; `a_device_with_nothing_to_read_is_refused`; and from Python `test_monitor.py::test_a_streamer_device_forwards`. `zmq-py.md` §7's streamer row flips from "absent, a defect of the library" to present, the XSUB/XPUB and ROUTER/DEALER proofs unchanged. `weida-zmq` 222 tests, the binding's 115 pytest cases, clippy in both feature configurations, rustdoc.

### B-179 — An operation that does not wait should not cost a loop wakeup
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 3988356 | needs: []
acceptance: `weida-py-core`'s bridge polls a future **once inline** and, when it is already ready, hands Python an already-resolved future — which `await` consumes without yielding to the event loop — instead of always spawning onto the reactor and completing through `call_soon_threadsafe`; cancellation, the panic path and the no-running-loop error keep the behaviour B-111 and B-112 pinned, proved by those crates' existing tests passing unchanged. The measurement is the acceptance: the asyncio row of [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) §4's B-117 table is re-run and the saved wakeup is recorded beside the old number, or the item says why it did not materialize.
note: filed from B-117's measurements at W1's request. The asyncio surface costs **330 µs inproc against 67.7 µs for the sync surface and 16.5 µs for Rust**, and the bridge pays one asyncio self-pipe wakeup per await — about 78 µs on this machine — whether or not the operation had to wait at all. A `recv` with a message already queued, a `send` with room in the queue: both are the common case in a busy program and both currently take the slow path. The risk to watch is the one B-112 already found: starting the future eagerly lost a message, so "poll once inline" must mean polling the future *after* the coroutine has started, not at call time.
note: done 3988356, and the number is in IMPLEMENTATION §4 beside B-117's table. `start()` — the coroutine's first step, so after the coroutine has been scheduled, which is the B-112 rule — polls the `Guarded` future once on the loop's thread inside `exec.enter()` with `Waker::noop()`; `Ready` completes the loop's future in place with `set_result`/`set_exception` and spawns nothing, `Pending` spawns exactly what was spawned before. `Guarded` already boxed the inner future and is `Unpin`, so the inline poll costs no allocation and no `unsafe`. Measured on an idle machine in release, before and after on the same `roundtrip_cost.py`: asyncio inproc **145 → 78 µs** (two of a round trip's four awaits are sends into a queue with room, so two wakeups go: ~33 µs per wakeup here), tcp 157 → 125 µs; the sync surface unchanged at 17.8 µs, which says the saving is the wakeup and nothing else. The machine is idle now and was not when B-117 measured, which is why both baselines are recorded. Every binding's Python suite passes unchanged: zmq 115, nng 51, mqtt 45 against `rumqttd`, amqp 17, nats 36 + 2 skipped for the absent server; `weida-py-core`'s consumer test — cancellation observed by the parked future's own `Drop`, the panic as a `RuntimeError`, no-running-loop raised before anything is polled — unchanged.

### B-104 — A hand-paired session harness cannot deliver, and the reason should be written where it is tried
kind: code | size: 15 | status: done ac4a4d1 | needs: [B-084]
acceptance: the diagnosis below is written into the code where somebody would try the harness again — `Pipe`'s own documentation and the `Session`/`drive` doc comment — saying that two sessions cannot be paired through the public surface and why; plus, if it is worth it, a `#[cfg(test)]` constructor that crosses two pipes' halves so the harness becomes possible for whoever wants it. No library behaviour changes: the finding is that nothing is broken.
note: reported by the worker on delivering B-084 as a workaround rather than a fix: two of this crate's own sessions driven against each other over a TCP pair in one process never deliver a message, and it reproduces under plain NULL options, so it is not CURVE's. **What I established on merging, before diagnosing it:** socket-level delivery over TCP in one process is not affected and is tested throughout — `tests/plain_zap.rs` runs a REQ and a REP of this crate over `tcp://127.0.0.1:0` through a full PLAIN handshake and a ZAP dialog, and the REQ/REP, PUB/SUB and pattern suites do the same over TCP under NULL. So the symptom was never "this library cannot talk to itself over TCP", which is what made it worth half an hour rather than an evening: a workaround whose reason is unknown is the kind of thing that turns out to be a real bug six weeks later.
note: **diagnosed read-only on merging, so the item is now writing it down rather than investigating.** `Pipe` is oriented from the *socket's* perspective — `outgoing()` is "the queue of messages headed for this peer", `incoming()` is "received from this peer" (`pipe.rs:469-477`) — and a session pumps against that orientation: wire into `incoming`, `outgoing` out to the wire. Pairing two sessions therefore needs two pipes with their halves **crossed**, and `Pipe::new(config)` is the only constructor there is (`pipe.rs:460-465`): a `Pipe` cannot be assembled from two given queues, so the crossing is not expressible through the public API. Share one pipe instead — the obvious move, "one pipe between them" — and both sessions pop the same `outgoing` queue and push the same `incoming` queue, so a message is raced by both readers and never arrives where the test looks. **No library code is implicated:** the engine pairs a session with a *socket*, one pipe per peer with the socket holding the other end, which is why every socket-level test over TCP delivers. Two hypotheses I recorded before looking are also eliminated: the NULL handshake is symmetric — both sides write `READY` and read `READY` (`session.rs:308-311`) — so neither a shared role nor an uncompleted `HandshakeGate` can stall it, and the CURVE tests' `CodecPeer` shows the wire itself is fine.
note: done ac4a4d1, merged 261059e, no behaviour change. The diagnosis is in `Pipe`'s doc and on `drive` (which had none): a pipe is oriented from the socket's side, a session pumps against that orientation, so two sessions need two pipes with crossed halves, and `Pipe::new` builds both queues itself so the crossing is not expressible through the public API; sharing one pipe races both readers on one queue. And the constructor the acceptance said to add if it was a few lines *is* a few lines, since both halves are `Arc<Queue>`: `#[cfg(test)] Pipe::crossed(config) -> (Pipe, Pipe)`, with the two ERROR channels kept separate because a refusal belongs to one connection, pinned by `crossed_pipes_carry_in_both_directions`. Written unlinked in the public docs, since a `cfg(test)` item would be a broken intra-doc link under `-D warnings`.

### B-085 — The option table, honoured or refused
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 3411b10 | needs: [B-076, B-079, B-081]
acceptance: every `zmq_setsockopt` and `zmq_ctx_set` option is honoured or refused **at configuration time** with a message naming the reason — no transport, draft only, deprecated in favour of ZAP, or replaced by a `weida-runtime` construct — and never silently ignored; a test walks the whole table, and the two deliberate default changes (finite `ZMQ_LINGER`, bounded `ZMQ_MAXMSGSIZE`) are asserted as such.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: not started, at the main session's instruction; branch `b085-zmq-options` off the B-084 tip e3b06d0 or off main, either works — main has only docs commits on top of it. It inherits an instrument B-076 built and B-084 exercised hard: `validate_for(socket_type)` refusing at construction, which is where "honoured or refused, never silently ignored" actually lives, so this item is the sweep across the whole table rather than the mechanism.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 3411b10 and gated here: **729 tests**, one ignored. One commit (3044e8b), branched off e3b06d0. `src/optiontable.rs` is `OPTIONS`, a **96-row const table** covering every `zmq_setsockopt` and `zmq_ctx_set` option the research sheet names (§2, §4, §5, §10, §11 and §13's 4.3.5 additions) plus the option names libzmq defines for the transports and mechanisms that sheet records as absent. Each row is `Verdict::Honoured(binding)` naming where the option lives in this library, or `Verdict::Refused(Refusal)` with **exactly five reasons and no sixth**: `NoTransport`, `DraftOnly`, `ZapInstead`, `RuntimeInstead`, `Absent(what is missing)`. `ZmqOption::refusal()` and `optiontable::honoured(name)` are the answer by name at configuration time, `EINVAL` whose cause names the option *and* its reason — so 0013 §4.4 item 4's "honoured or refused, never silently ignored" is now a table a test can walk rather than a promise each slice keeps separately.
note: the shape worth keeping is that **the table decides and then proves itself**: four tests walk it and assert that every row decides something and names itself and its reason, that there are no duplicates, that both halves are populated (38 honoured, 58 refused), that each of the five reasons is used by at least one row, that a name outside the table is refused, that every option this library's own types carry is present as honoured, and that **the two deliberate default changes are asserted as deviations** — the finite close budget where `ZMQ_LINGER` is infinite, and a real `ZMQ_MAXMSGSIZE` where libzmq has "no limit" — each checked against the default it actually starts with *and* against the row that claims it. The read-only names (`ZMQ_EVENTS`, `ZMQ_FD`, `ZMQ_LAST_ENDPOINT`, `ZMQ_SOCKET_LIMIT`, `ZMQ_MSG_T_SIZE`, `ZMQ_MECHANISM`) are deliberately **not** rows, with the module documentation saying why — they are questions a socket answers, not configuration it accepts, and `ZMQ_LAST_ENDPOINT` is B-073's return value rather than a field.
note: this is also the row-by-row material B-093's parity table needs, and it arrives with the two errno deviations B-082 recorded (`EACCES` for a refused peer, `ENOCOMPATPROTO` for a malfunctioning ZAP dialog) and the two bounds that are not libzmq options at all (`max_peers` from B-099, `max_subscriptions`/`max_subscription_bytes` from B-078 and B-103) already documented as such where they are defined — so that item becomes a transcription with a test behind it rather than a fresh audit.

### B-086 — Monitor events
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 531f738 | needs: [B-079, B-077]
acceptance: the `zmq_socket_monitor` event set delivered as a typed stream **and** over the `inproc://` PAIR form the Espresso recipe reads. **Split (LOOP §1.3):** the devices — `zmq_proxy(frontend, backend, capture)` and `zmq_proxy_steerable` with PAUSE/RESUME/TERMINATE/STATISTICS — were this item's second half and are now B-105, because the monitor half was finished and proven while the devices were not begun.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b086-zmq-monitor` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-085 tip 3044e8b. Two halves with one trap between them: the typed stream is this library's own shape, and the `inproc://` PAIR form is what the Espresso recipe actually reads, so the events have to be *one* source rendered two ways rather than two emitters that can disagree — the mistake B-078 avoided for subscriptions by deciding the wire form in exactly one place. The `inproc` half exists already (B-080) and PAIR exists with its endpoint rules (B-077). `zmq_proxy` is where B-079's XPUB/XSUB earn their keep, and the capture socket is the Espresso recipe's own instrument.
note: **split rather than closed over, on the worker's own report.** It said "monitor events done and proven, devices not begun" and asked me not to close the item; that is the honest line, so the acceptance above keeps the half that landed and B-105 carries the half that did not, verbatim. I checked the claim rather than taking it: `grep` over `crates/zmq/weida-zmq/src` finds "proxy" only in prose and doc comments — no `Device` trait, no `zmq_proxy`, no stub and no export — so **nothing in the tree pretends the devices exist**, which is what makes the split clean rather than a partial delivery dressed up.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 531f738 and gated here: **735 tests**, one ignored. One commit (61814c1). `src/monitor.rs`: the fourteen stable `ZMQ_EVENT_*` ids, `MonitorEvents` as the `zmq_socket_monitor` mask, `MonitorEvent` with endpoint, value and reason, `MonitorSink` taking a closure so an **unmonitored socket formats nothing**, `Monitor` as the typed stream whose `EAGAIN` names how many events a lagging reader missed, `Monitor::resubscribe`, and `serve_pair` rendering the same stream onto a PAIR socket. The engine publishes from one place per transition: bind (LISTENING/BIND_FAILED), connect (CONNECT_DELAYED), the connecter loop (CONNECT_RETRIED with the interval), `run_session` for every transport and both roles (ACCEPTED/CONNECTED, HANDSHAKE_SUCCEEDED off the `HandshakeGate`, then CLOSED, DISCONNECTED, or the three-way handshake-failure split off the errno), and `Engine::close` (MONITOR_STOPPED).
note: **the trap I warned about was avoided and then tested**, which is the part worth keeping: `MonitorEvent::encode`/`decode` is the single place the wire form is decided — one publisher, two renderings — and `a_sockets_lifecycle_reaches_both_renderings` asserts a real PULL socket's LISTENING, ACCEPTED and HANDSHAKE_SUCCEEDED arriving **in the same order** on the typed stream and decoded off the PAIR socket, plus MONITOR_STOPPED on close. That is B-078's subscription-form discipline applied to a second surface without being re-argued. One named loss recorded in the module documentation rather than papered over: libzmq puts an errno in the value field, and this library's errors are named rather than numbered, so a third *reason* frame is an additive extension and the numeric errno is a loss — the shape B-082 used for `X-Local-Principal`.

### B-105 — The devices: `zmq_proxy` and `zmq_proxy_steerable`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4f29185 | needs: [B-086]
acceptance: `zmq_proxy(frontend, backend, capture)` and `zmq_proxy_steerable` with PAUSE/RESUME/TERMINATE/STATISTICS, over the socket pairs a proxy is actually built from — XSUB/XPUB, ROUTER/DEALER, PULL/PUSH, with PAIR for capture and for control. **ROUTER/DEALER's envelope passes through raw**: a proxy neither strips nor adds a routing-id frame, and a test asserts a reply reaching the original requester through the proxy rather than merely that bytes moved. The capture socket sees every message in both directions, and a paused proxy forwards nothing while still accepting control.
note: the second half of B-086, split out under LOOP §1.3 when that item's monitor half landed finished and the devices had not been begun. The worker's own reason for stopping rather than guessing is worth keeping as the item's first instruction: **getting ROUTER/DEALER right means the envelope passes through raw**, and it did not want to invent that. Everything the devices are built from exists and is tested — XPUB/XSUB with the subscription forwarding a proxy needs (B-079), PULL/PUSH and PAIR with its endpoint rules (B-077), the monitor for observing a proxy's own sockets (B-086) — so this is the device layer and nothing underneath it.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 4f29185 and gated here: **740 tests**, one ignored. One commit (b0b6fe7) on top of the monitor half, so the split's second half is one commit and the whole of this item. `src/proxy.rs`: a `Device` trait whose two halves are exactly what a device needs — receive one whole message, send one whole message — implemented for the typed socket surfaces a proxy is built from, with **PUSH/PUB send-only and PULL/SUB recv-only answering `ENOTSUP` on the half `zmq_socket(3)`'s own table says they do not have**. `proxy(frontend, backend, capture)` forwards both ways with the capture socket getting a clone of every message; `proxy_steerable` adds PAUSE/RESUME/TERMINATE/STATISTICS with `Steer::parse` refusing anything else with `EINVAL` naming the four, and `ProxyStatistics` encoding libzmq's eight `u64` counters one frame each, little-endian for the same reason the monitor header is.
note: **no unbounded buffering, and it is structural rather than promised** — the shape I would have asked for if the worker had not arrived at it: one message is read, copied to the capture socket if there is one, and handed to the other side by a **blocking send before the next is read**, so the proxy holds exactly one message and a slow backend's high-water mark reaches back through that send. The exception is named in the module documentation instead of being discovered later: XPUB/XSUB/PUB drop at the high-water mark per 29/PUBSUB, so a pub-sub proxy loses a message for a slow subscriber rather than stalling the others, which is the mechanism's own rule — and it is why the counters count what was *handed over*.
note: **the ROUTER/DEALER envelope is pass-through, which is the clause the worker refused to guess at and then proved end to end**: a ROUTER's `recv` prepends the routing id, the DEALER forwards every frame it was given, and the reply comes back with the same envelope for the ROUTER to strip — asserted by a REQ client through ROUTER/DEALER to a REP worker with the reply reaching the right client, not merely by bytes moving. Five tests: the eight counters both ways through the frame form plus a malformed reply refused; the four commands and nothing else; the pub-sub proxy over XSUB/XPUB with a real PUB and SUB at the ends and a PAIR capture socket over `inproc://` receiving the copy; the queue device over ROUTER/DEALER; and the steerable control loop asserting that PAUSE stops the traffic, **STATISTICS answers from the same loop that took the PAUSE**, RESUME starts it again, and TERMINATE ends the proxy handing back counters that match what went through.
note: what differs from `zmq_proxy`'s C signature is stated in the module documentation rather than left for a reader to discover: typed operands behind `Device` instead of three `void*` — so a socket that cannot receive cannot be a frontend and the **compiler** says so where C reports `ENOTSUP` at run time — `capture` as an `Option` rather than a null pointer, `ProxyStatistics` returned by *both* functions rather than only by the STATISTICS command, and a `select!` over two futures instead of a `zmq_poll` loop.

### B-087 — The blocking facade
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done fc49c3c | needs: [B-075, B-077, B-078]
acceptance: a non-default `blocking` feature giving one wrapper per socket over a `Context::owned` reactor, with `ZMQ_SNDTIMEO`/`ZMQ_RCVTIMEO` and `ZMQ_DONTWAIT`; no second implementation of any protocol behaviour, and one zguide example that reads like its C original.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b087-zmq-blocking` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-105 tip b0b6fe7. The acceptance line's hard half is "**no second implementation of any protocol behaviour**": a blocking wrapper is `Context::owned` plus `block_on`, which is what B-070 built the third reactor constructor for, and the moment a wrapper re-decides a mute action, an alternation or a timeout there are two ZeroMQs in one crate. `ZMQ_SNDTIMEO`/`ZMQ_RCVTIMEO` and `ZMQ_DONTWAIT` already exist as options the async surface honours (B-075), so the wrapper passes them down rather than interpreting them. B-102's thread rule is the other constraint worth stating up front: a socket is `Send` and not `Sync`, so a blocking wrapper owns its socket and does not lend it to a pool.
note: merged `--no-ff` as fc49c3c and gated here — and gated **twice**, because the mandated gate never compiles a non-default feature: the four commands are green (**740 tests**, one ignored), and so are `clippy -p weida-zmq --features blocking --all-targets`, `cargo test -p weida-zmq --features blocking` (**195 tests**) and `cargo doc -p weida-zmq --features blocking`; `cargo run --features blocking --example hello_world` does its ten exchanges and exits 0, which I ran here rather than taking on report. One commit (115cf94). `blocking = ["dep:futures"]`, not default: `BlockingContext` over `Context::owned` and eleven wrappers generated by four macros, so a new socket type cannot forget a method. **The hard clause holds and it is checkable rather than asserted:** every method is one line, `BlockingContext::drive(self.socket.<same method>())`, so mute actions, REQ's alternation, ROUTER's table, the subscription forms and the timeouts are decided exactly once, in the async surface; `ZMQ_DONTWAIT` is `try_send`/`try_recv`, which were already synchronous and never touch the reactor; anything pattern-specific is reached through `socket()` rather than copied.
note: **the design point, because the obvious implementation is wrong:** the facade does *not* block on the reactor. It drives the socket's future on the **calling** thread with `futures::executor::block_on` — which is exactly the contract `Exec`'s own documentation states — while the sessions keep running on the reactor. The worker wrote an `Exec::block_on` first and reverted it, for the right reason: a caller that parked a reactor worker would be waiting for the worker that has to drive what it waits for. `futures` was already in the workspace, and it is the one dependency the feature adds, optional and absent when the feature is off.
note: two findings reported rather than buried, and one of them is a fix that came in with this commit. **(1) A socket dropped before its session flushed takes its queued messages with it**: the worker's first REQ/REP test failed deterministically because the reply was `Queued`, the worker thread then returned, and `EngineInner::drop` aborted the session before the reply reached the wire. That is consistent with what B-085's table already says — `ZMQ_LINGER` maps to `close_budget` at `Context::shutdown`, not to socket drop — so it is a documented difference rather than a defect, and it is now written into the test and the example's doc comment as the reason the server loops instead of returning, which is also what makes it read like `hwserver.c`'s `while (1)`. I have put it on B-093 as a parity row rather than making it its own item. **(2) A latent lost wakeup in `Queue`, fixed in four lines of `pipe.rs`** — see B-106, which is where the missing test is recorded rather than waved at.

### B-106 — A regression test for the `Queue` lost wakeup
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done ddfdf0d | needs: [B-087]
acceptance: the hazard B-087 fixed is pinned by a test that fails on the pre-fix code: a waiter that has been polled once, a `notify_waiters()` landing between its check and its await, and the assertion that the waiter wakes. Manual polling or a paused clock, not a sleep — and if the hazard turns out not to be observable through the public surface, the item says so explicitly and the four `enable()` calls keep their comment as the only record.
note: the fix arrived inside B-087's commit and is correct — `Notify::notified()` registers its waiter when it is **polled**, not when it is constructed, so a `notify_waiters()` between the check and the await woke nobody and `Queue::send`, `Queue::recv`, `wait_for_room` and `wait_for_message` could all wait forever; the four sites now use `pin!` plus `enable()`, matching the idiom `inproc.rs:137-148` already had right. The worker reported plainly that **no test proves it** — it was not what broke its REQ/REP test — and did not invent one, which is the correct call and also why this item exists: a four-line concurrency fix with no test is exactly the change that gets reverted by accident later. That it was found by reading a comment that claimed something the code did not do is worth remembering: the comment said "this registers the waiter", and it did not.
note: done ddfdf0d, merged 261059e — **and the item's premise was wrong about tokio, which the worker measured rather than repeated.** `Notified` captures the `notify_waiters` generation when it is *constructed* (tokio 1.53.1 `sync/notify.rs`), so the pre-fix shape B-087 described — created above the check, awaited below it — never lost the wakeup: with all four `enable()` calls removed the suite stays green. What does lose it is the obvious simplification of those loops, `notified().await` written below the check, and that is what the four new tests pin: each fails on that shape with its own message (10 passed / 4 failed under the mutation) and passes on the tree. The window is intra-poll and unreachable through the public surface, so a `#[cfg(test)]` hook occupies it deterministically — a thread-local `Window` and one cfg-ed statement between check and await at the four sites, zero production code — and the tests poll by hand with `Waker::noop()`, no sleeps, no paused clock. The `enable()` calls stay, because they make the registration explicit and `notify_one` would need them, and the comment at `wait_for_room` that claimed the wrong hazard now states the true one. Recorded here as the acceptance asked: the pre-fix code cannot be made to fail, and the reason is written above the tests in `pipe.rs`.

### B-108 — What the interop runs taught the ZeroMQ sheet
kind: research | size: 45 | status: done 660423d | needs: [B-089]
acceptance: `docs/research/zeromq.md` records what running against two foreign implementations measured, in the sheet's own protocol-native vocabulary with no sentence mentioning weida (research/README rule 1), each fact attributed to a named version through a new source entry; plus the correction the third finding forces on `docs/adapters/zmtp.md` §10.1, whose published `MESSAGE` vector showed the frame header libzmq refuses.
note: the research item LOOP §9 requires after a Phase B slice, and the material is B-088's and B-089's rather than a fresh reading. §13 gains a **"Measured against libzmq 4.3.5 [41]"** paragraph with four facts none of libzmq's own pages state: the `as-server` octet that is 0 even on a PLAIN or CURVE server *and* is never read from the peer, so the field identifies nothing on the wire; the empty `Identity` property on REQ/DEALER/ROUTER, which `identity = 0*255OCTET` permits; the CURVE `MESSAGE` behind a message frame header with the command form closing the connection; and a PLAIN or CURVE server refusing everything without a ZAP handler, which 27/ZAP requires but no libzmq page states as a refusal. The rust-zmq bullet gains the measurement that its 0.10 line vendors libzmq and reports no CURVE while 0.9 links the system library, the zmq.rs list gains (e) XPUB-applies-on-`recv` and (f) its DEALER announcing no `Identity`, and §14 gains source **[41]** with the versions, the `ldd` and the method.
note: committed by explicit path, docs-only, so no gate ran: `docs/research/zeromq.md` and `docs/adapters/zmtp.md` together, because the zmtp correction is the same finding written for a different reader. §10.1's `MESSAGE` (CURVE) row carried `04 21` — the command-framed header — and now carries `00 21` with the reason beside it, and the list above the table went from three contradictions to **four**, the new one being that 26/CURVEZMQ calls `MESSAGE` a command while its reference implementation frames it as a message and refuses the command form. The body octets in that row were always right; only the header was the form nobody can use.

### B-109 — The codec's `CurveCommand::encode()` frames a MESSAGE as a command
kind: adapter | size: 30 | status: done 52e0538 | needs: [B-089]
acceptance: `weida-zmtp` stops offering an encoder that produces a frame libzmq closes the connection on — either `CurveCommand::encode()` frames a `MESSAGE` with a message header and the others with command headers, or it refuses `MESSAGE` and says the caller frames it (which is what the library already does). The golden vector for that row moves with it, so the published table and the test agree again, and the doc comment says which of the two readings the function implements and why.
note: found by me on merging B-089, while correcting the published vector the third disagreement invalidated — the worker did not report it and its own change is correct, so this is a leftover rather than a defect in its work. `CurveCommand::encode()` (`crates/adapters/weida-zmtp/src/curve.rs:454-458`) frames every variant with `FrameKind::Command`, `MESSAGE` included, and `tests/golden_vectors.rs:417-427` pins that as `04 21`. **Nothing is broken today**: the library never calls it for a `MESSAGE` — `CurveTransport::seal_frame` builds the body with `encode_body` and frames it `FrameKind::Message` (`crates/zmq/weida-zmq/src/curve.rs:906-924`) — which is why all 18 libzmq interop tests pass. What is wrong is that a *public* convenience encoder emits the one form a real peer refuses, and a golden vector asserts it, so the table and the test now disagree after B-108's correction: the table says `00 21` because that is the wire, the test says `04 21` because that is the function. One of the two has to move, and the function is the one that is wrong.
note: done 52e0538, merged 36a7366. Reading one: `CurveCommand::encode()` frames `MESSAGE` with a message frame header (`FrameKind::Message { more: false }`) and the four handshake commands with command headers, which is what `weida-zmq`'s `seal_frame` already built by hand — one framing path, and the convenience encoder now agrees with the library instead of contradicting it. The doc comment names the reading and why: 26/CURVEZMQ gives MESSAGE a command *body* but it travels behind a message header with the COMMAND bit clear, and a command-framed MESSAGE is what libzmq 4.3.5 closes the connection on. The golden vector's header moved from `04 21` to `00 21` — the row B-108 had already corrected in `docs/adapters/zmtp.md` §10.1, so the table and the test agree again — and `assert_curve` now asserts the frame kind the variant requires rather than `Command` for all five. Regression-checked: with `encode()` reverted to command framing for every variant, `golden_curve_ready_and_message` fails on the MESSAGE bytes. `weida-zmtp` 92 tests, `weida-zmq`'s CURVE tests, clippy, rustdoc.

### B-088 — Interop against zmq.rs, both roles
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 3a89eb9 | needs: [B-076, B-079, B-077]
acceptance: every pairing both implement, with our socket as the connecting side **and** as the bound side, against `zeromq` 0.6 as a dev-dependency with no supervised process; the three skews the sheet records — a 3.0 greeting, `READY`-only command decoding, the legacy subscription form — exercised rather than assumed.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 3a89eb9 and gated here: **753 tests**, one ignored, and **208** under `--features blocking` (re-run because the manifest changed). One commit (0688296). Thirteen tests in `tests/interop_zmqrs.rs` that run in the **ordinary suite** — `zeromq` 0.6.0 and `bytes` as dev-dependencies, no supervised process and no C library — so the interop evidence is produced by the same command as everything else rather than by a ritual somebody has to remember. The matrix is ten tests: REQ/REP, DEALER/ROUTER, PUSH/PULL, PUB/SUB and XPUB/XSUB, each with our socket bound *and* connecting. **PAIR gets its own test that says what it is:** zmq.rs 0.6 has no PAIR, so the pairing cannot be measured and ours is shown talking to itself — a gap in the peer named as such rather than a row quietly missing from a matrix.
note: the three skews are **exercised rather than assumed**, and the third one's failure message is the detail worth copying elsewhere. The 3.0 greeting is exercised by every test in the file, since none would connect otherwise. `READY`-only decoding by `a_heartbeat_is_suppressed_against_a_three_zero_peer`: `ZMQ_HEARTBEAT_IVL` at 50 ms, two seconds of silence — forty PINGs that must not happen — and then the same connection carrying a second message, which it could not do if one had gone out. The subscription form by a test that measures **both** settings on one publisher: with our default `SUBSCRIBE` command nothing is delivered, with `SubscriptionForm::LegacyMessage` it is — and its failure message says **the sheet's skew is stale rather than that our code is broken**, because that is what a pass turning into a failure there would actually mean. That is the right way to write an assertion about somebody else's implementation.
note: two measured facts about the peer that the research sheet does not have, both in the file's doc comment with the version and the peer's own line numbers. **(1) zmq.rs 0.6's XPUB applies a subscription only as a side effect of the application calling `recv()`** (`xpub.rs:181-204`), so a publisher that never receives has no subscribers and drops everything; libzmq applies it in the socket unless `ZMQ_XPUB_MANUAL` is set, and so do we — *for us their behaviour is the option*. The test reads the subscription on their side before publishing and says why; it cost the worker the first run of that test, which is exactly what the file exists for. **(2) Their DEALER announces no `Identity`**, so the routing id our ROUTER addresses it by is one we generated — not a disagreement, since 37/ZMTP says a peer that announces none gets one, but it is why the test asserts a non-empty id rather than a particular one. Nothing else disagreed: all ten pairings carried a message in both roles, in order where order is promised, with no adjustment on either side beyond the subscription form and that XPUB `recv`.

### B-089 — Interop against libzmq, both roles
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 73180e2 | needs: [B-088, B-084]
acceptance: the same matrix plus PAIR, PLAIN, CURVE and ZAP against libzmq through the `zmq` crate as an optional dev-dependency, `#[ignore]` with the install command in the doc comment where libzmq is absent (LOOP §2); every disagreement recorded in the sheet as measured, not inferred.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b089-zmq-interop-libzmq` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-088 tip 0688296. **libzmq 4.3.5 is installed on this machine** (`pkg-config --modversion libzmq`), so these tests will run for real here rather than only compile — which is the difference between this item and the `#[ignore]` its acceptance line allows. That allowance still has to be honoured for a machine without the library: LOOP §2 asks for the install command in the doc comment, and B-088's PAIR test is the shape for anything that cannot be measured — say what is absent and why, never leave a row quietly missing. This is also the item that decides whether B-088's two measured facts about zmq.rs earn a sentence in `docs/research/zeromq.md` §13; the worker will report the path if so, and the sheet's rules apply (protocol-native vocabulary, no mention of weida, measured against a named version).
note: merged `--no-ff` as 73180e2 and gated here, plus the three runs the mandated gate cannot see: **753 tests** one ignored in the default configuration, `clippy -p weida-zmq --features libzmq-interop,blocking --all-targets` clean, **208** under `--features blocking`, and the matrix itself — `cargo test -p weida-zmq --features libzmq-interop --test interop_libzmq -- --ignored`: **18 passed, 0 failed**, for real against this machine's libzmq (`pkg-config --modversion libzmq` = 4.3.5, libsodium-linked). One commit (43897b4). Every pairing with our socket bound and then connecting — REQ/REP, DEALER/ROUTER, PUSH/PULL, PUB/SUB, XPUB/XSUB and **PAIR**, which zmq.rs could not offer — plus PLAIN both roles, CURVE both roles, ZAP with the credential frames asserted, a ZAP refusal, and our heartbeat answered by a 3.1 peer. Where libzmq is the server the test binds a ZAP handler in its context, because a libzmq PLAIN or CURVE server refuses everything without one.
note: **CURVE interoperates in both roles, which is B-084's last acceptance clause measured rather than asserted.** A libzmq client reaches our CURVE server and our client reaches a libzmq server, so B-084's `crypto_box` boxes open in libsodium and libsodium's open in ours — the HELLO signature box, the WELCOME box, the cookie, the vouch, the READY box and the MESSAGE boxes, with the nonce prefixes and counters as 26/CURVEZMQ specifies. One dependency choice recorded where someone would otherwise "upgrade" it back: the `zmq` crate **0.10 cannot measure CURVE at all** — its `zmq-sys` 0.12 always builds libzmq from source and that build reports `zmq_has("curve") == false` and version 4.3.4 — so 0.9 is pinned, whose `zmq-sys` 0.11 links the system library through `pkg-config`, which is the library a deployment actually meets. The manifest comment says exactly that.
note: **three disagreements, each one a fix on our side, none inferred — and the monitor of B-086 named two of them without a packet capture, which is the best argument for that item anyone could have made.** (1) libzmq announces an `Identity` property with an **empty value** on every REQ, DEALER and ROUTER with no `ZMQ_ROUTING_ID`; we refused it, since a routing id is 1..=255 octets, so half the matrix failed at the handshake — but 37/ZMTP's `identity = 0*255OCTET` permits it and only a *non-empty* identity must not begin with a zero octet, so an empty property is the **absence** of one and `session.rs` reads it that way now. (2) libzmq's greeting `as-server` octet is **0 even on a PLAIN or CURVE server**, against 24/ZMTP-PLAIN and 25/ZMTP-CURVE, and it never reads the peer's octet either — each side takes its role from its own option — so our guard, which refused "both ends are the client", refused **every** libzmq server; it now refuses only two *announced* servers and leaves two clients to `ZMQ_HANDSHAKE_IVL`. (3) A CURVE `MESSAGE` travels behind a **message** frame header although 26/CURVEZMQ calls it a command, and libzmq closes the connection on the command-framed form; we send what libzmq sends and accept either kind, because the check that matters is whether the box opens. Each is pinned by a test whose failure message says **libzmq was fixed rather than that we are broken**, which is the right direction for an assertion about somebody else's implementation.
note: docs: the sheet's §13 and §14 edits landed in this tree unstaged and are committed separately as the research item **B-108**, together with a correction the finding forced on `docs/adapters/zmtp.md` §10.1. One thing the doc pass turned up that the worker did not report and that is **not** closed by this item — see **B-109**: `CurveCommand::encode()` in the codec still frames a `MESSAGE` as a *command*, and a golden vector pins that header, so the codec's convenience encoder produces the one form libzmq refuses. The library itself is correct — `seal_frame` builds the body and frames it with `FrameKind::Message` — which is why all 18 interop tests pass and why nothing here is broken today.

### B-090 — zguide I: the pirates and the load-balancing broker
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done f1b68a3 | needs: [B-088]
acceptance: lazy pirate, simple pirate over the chapter 3 load-balancing broker, and paranoid pirate with heartbeats, as examples that run in `cargo test`, each asserting the guide's own claim — an in-order reply or abandonment; a worker may crash and restart while the queue runs; the queue evicts a lost worker instead of discovering it through a failed request.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b090-zmq-zguide-pirates` by `zmq-lib`, with B-091 and B-092 queued behind it on `b091-zmq-zguide-mdp` and `b092-zmq-zguide-clone`. The acceptance line's shape is the thing to hold: each example **asserts the guide's own claim** rather than merely running, so lazy pirate proves an in-order reply *or* abandonment, simple pirate survives a worker crashing and restarting while the queue runs, and paranoid pirate's queue **evicts** a lost worker instead of discovering it through a failed request. Everything they need exists and is interop-tested: REQ/REP and DEALER/ROUTER (B-075, B-076), the monitor if a test wants to watch a reconnect (B-086), and `zmq_proxy` for the broker shapes that want it (B-105). Lazy pirate needs close-and-reopen after `EFSM`, which B-075 built and tested for exactly this reason.
note: merged `--no-ff` as f1b68a3 and gated here: **753 tests** one ignored by default, clippy clean with `blocking,libzmq-interop`, and **211** under `--features blocking`, which is where the three new tests live because two of the three recipes are synchronous in their C originals. I also ran all three examples rather than trusting "reads like its C original": `lazy_pirate` prints `W: no response from server, retrying...` twice and then `E: server seems to be offline, abandoning` with `Abandoned { attempts: 3 }`; `simple_pirate` retries once and reports `Reply { attempts: 2, out_of_sequence: 0 }` with `queue: 2 READY, 2 routed`; `paranoid_pirate` prints `W: heartbeat failure, can't reach 1 worker(s)` and ends `0 on the list, 1 evicted`. One commit (c970c54).
note: **the test file includes the example files as modules**, so what is asserted is the recipe a reader actually runs rather than a second copy written to be testable — and `simple_pirate` includes `lazy_pirate` the same way, which is how "an unchanged Lazy Pirate client" stops being a comment and becomes a fact the compiler checks. Each claim is the guide's own: lazy pirate proves **both** halves in one test, a server that crashes once still answering the retry *with the request's own sequence number* and a server that is not there being abandoned after the retry count with the elapsed time asserted, because "never blocking indefinitely" is a claim about the clock; simple pirate kills a worker on its first request, lets a replacement join while the queue runs, asserts `attempts >= 2` so the crash cannot silently not happen, and then asserts a second request needing **no** retry; paranoid pirate asserts its claim with **no request sent at all** — a worker registers, vanishes, and after `HEARTBEAT_LIVENESS` intervals the queue has taken it off its list and counted the eviction, which is exactly the difference from Simple Pirate's queue, that only notices a dead idle worker when work is sent to it.
note: two liberties, both named in the doc comments rather than quietly taken. The intervals and retry counts are **arguments rather than `#define`s** — the guide's values remain the constants and what `main` uses — so a claim about a timeout can be asserted in milliseconds. And the Paranoid Pirate worker does **not** rebuild its socket with exponential backoff, because this library reconnects a dialled endpoint with `ZMQ_RECONNECT_IVL` backoff itself (B-073): the socket churn the C needs is the engine's job here, and what stays in the worker is the liveness counting the recipe is actually about. That is the right way to port a recipe — keep what the recipe teaches, drop what the C was compensating for, and say which is which.

### B-091 — zguide II: Majordomo and Freelance
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 42d7e23 | needs: [B-090]
acceptance: MDP/0.2 client, worker and broker with the six-byte header, per-service queues, heartbeats and `DISCONNECT`, plus `mmi.service` answering 200/404; Freelance models one and two with request numbering so a stale reply is ignored; each asserting the RFC's stated guarantee.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b091-zmq-zguide-majordomo` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-090 tip c970c54, with B-092 queued behind it. B-090 set the instrument to reuse: examples included as modules by the test file, so the recipe under test is the one a reader runs. Two things this item should keep from it — the claim asserted in the guide's own terms (for MDP that is the RFC's stated guarantee, not "a message arrived"), and every liberty named in the doc comment rather than taken quietly. `mmi.service` answering 200/404 is the clause most easily faked: it wants a service that exists *and* one that does not, asked through the same broker.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 42d7e23 and gated here: **760 tests** one ignored, clippy clean with `blocking,libzmq-interop`, **218** under `--features blocking`. The seven new tests are in the **ordinary** suite, because both recipes are async in their C originals and needed no facade — which is the right reason to choose either. Both examples run: `majordomo` prints `mmi.service echo -> Some("200")`, `mmi.service nosuchservice -> Some("404")` and `worker served 1 request(s), registered 1 time(s)`; `freelance` walks past a dead endpoint and ends `stale reply discarded: … stale_discarded: 1`. One commit (be5c731). 18/MDP 0.2's broker, worker and client in one file with the C's own `#define` block quoted — MDPC02/MDPW02 and READY/REQUEST/PARTIAL/FINAL/HEARTBEAT/DISCONNECT as `\001..\006` — one ROUTER for both sub-protocols (the RFC's MAY), a waiting-worker deque and a request deque per service, `mmi.` prefixes handled internally and never queued, and the worker a DEALER **with the reason stated**: it has to keep beating while waiting for work, which a REQ cannot.
note: **seven RFC clauses asserted and not one "a message arrived" among them**, and two are the kind that catch a false pass. *Routing is by abstract name:* two workers on two **different** services, the echo one serving 1 and the reverse one 0 — so "the name routed it" cannot be confused with "there was only one worker". *LRU per service:* two workers of the **same** service and two requests come out exactly (1, 1), because the second request cannot reach the worker that just answered. Then `mmi.service` through the same broker and the same client function as a real request, 200 and 404; `DISCONNECT` and re-registration, where a worker whose own heartbeat interval is longer than the broker's patience falls silent without dying and the test waits for the DISCONNECT **both sent and received**, `registrations >= 2`, and a subsequent request served — which is the point of re-registering rather than exiting; and its converse, that any command except DISCONNECT acts as a heartbeat, proved by a beating worker surviving four lifetimes of complete idleness with `disconnects_sent == 0`.
note: 10/FLP's two models, and the second one's test is the one I would keep: model one walks past a dead endpoint with `attempts == 2`, so the dead endpoint was really tried, and a list of only dead endpoints returns `None` rather than blocking; model two sprays one copy per server from a single DEALER — which *is* what `flclient2.c` does — with the empty delimiter written explicitly because the peers are REP sockets and a DEALER writes no envelope, and the sequence number in decimal ASCII as the C prints it. **The stale-reply test is deterministic by construction rather than by racing two servers:** one server that takes 250 ms over *every* request, a first request abandoned at 100 ms, then request two collecting the sequence-one answer first — `stale_discarded == 1`. The worker's first version raced two servers and was flaky "in exactly the direction that would have passed vacuously", which is the failure mode worth naming: a racing test that usually passes is worse than no test.
note: three liberties, all named in the doc comments, and the third is a judgement I would not have thought to ask for: **the guide's own omissions stay** — no exponential backoff in the worker, empty service queues never deleted — "since a recipe that behaves better than its original teaches the wrong thing about the original". The other two: intervals are arguments with the guide's values as the constants and what `main` uses, and the broker's counts are atomics rather than `printf`, because re-registration is a claim about a count.

### B-092 — zguide III: Clone, Binary Star and the pub-sub recipes
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 02bfa16 | needs: [B-091, B-086]
acceptance: CHP's ROUTER/PUB/SUB port triple with `ICANHAZ?`/`KVSYNC`/`KTHXBAI`/`KVPUB`/`HUGZ`/`KVSET` and the strict-increment discard rule; the Binary Star state machine with its three events and the split-brain warning in the doc comment; the last-value cache and Espresso on the proxy capture socket.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b092-zmq-zguide-clone` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-091 tip be5c731. The last zguide item, and the one with the most moving parts: CHP's port triple, Binary Star's state machine, the last-value cache and Espresso. Two instruments are already built and should not be rebuilt — the **capture socket** of B-105 is what Espresso reads, and the **monitor** of B-086 is how a test watches a peer arrive or vanish without a packet capture. The strict-increment discard rule is this item's stale-reply moment, so B-091's lesson applies directly: make it deterministic by construction rather than by racing two publishers. Binary Star's split-brain warning belongs in the doc comment, as its acceptance says, and that is an honesty clause rather than a decoration — the guide is explicit that two primaries can both think they are live.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 02bfa16 and gated here: **769 tests** one ignored, clippy clean with `blocking,libzmq-interop`, **227** under `--features blocking`, and — because this item changed `session.rs` — the **libzmq matrix re-run for real, 18 passed**. All four examples run: `clone` ends `reader at sequence 3 holds ["/client/a", "/client/b"], discarded 3 update(s)` and then the delete leaves one key; `binary_star` prints `after the primary vanished, backup is Passive`, `the vote: Serve`, `backup is now Active`; `espresso` captures `A hello`, `00A`, `00B`; `last_value_cache` says `late subscriber caught up with A two, 1 message(s) from the cache`. One commit (00abdd7). 12/CHP on the wire it specifies — ROUTER at P, PUB at P+1, SUB at P+2 — with **all six commands sharing one five-frame kvmsg layout** and `ICANHAZ?`/`KTHXBAI`/`HUGZ` living in the key frame, which is *why* the layout can be uniform; the client **subscribes in its constructor**, so a caller cannot get the subscribe-before-you-ask order wrong. Binary Star is the FSM as a **pure transition function** over the three events with the clock outside it, so `event()` is testable without a wall clock and `classify()` alone turns a request into a vote after two heartbeats of silence.
note: the discard rule is deterministic by construction, as asked: the reader subscribes and **waits for a HUGZ**, so its subscription is provably live before any write happens, and the test then demands `discarded == 3` exactly — a rule that applied the updates instead fails outright rather than flaking. Its converse is asserted too, that the first update *after* the snapshot must land, because the rule is strictly-greater rather than greater-or-equal. Binary Star asserts the three-way rule in **all three** of its cases including the one `bstar.c` itself asserts — a server that has never seen its peer has no evidence of silence, so its request is *not* a vote — plus a live failover where the backup stays Passive through six heartbeats of silence *because nobody asked* and is promoted by the first client request. Dual actives report `EFSM` with the C's own words rather than aborting, and the **split-brain warning is verbatim in the doc comment** with the honest part stated: a peer cannot detect a split brain from the inside, only the dual-active case it can see.
note: **a library bug, found here and fixed here: a subscription made between `connect` and the handshake reached the publisher twice.** The socket queued the announcement into the pipe *and* the session replayed the socket's table at the handshake — and because 29/PUBSUB subscriptions **count** rather than set, the publisher then needed two `CANCEL`s for something the application holds once, so the first `unsubscribe` changed nothing and an XPUB reported nothing. It was found because Espresso's captured trace had **no unsubscriptions in it**, and confirmed with a throwaway probe before being believed: XPUB's own table still matched the prefix after the SUB had cancelled it, for both SUB and XSUB. The fix (`session.rs:185-215`) drains the pipe's queued subscription messages, keeps anything else an XSUB had queued and writes it in order afterwards, and replays the table **with its counts** — so a doubly-held subscription still survives a reconnect as two. Regression test in `src/pubsub.rs`: a subscription made in exactly that gap, one unsubscribe, and the publisher must stop delivering. The whole libzmq matrix was re-run for real afterwards to be sure the wire had not moved.
note: two more things beyond the brief. **`apply_pending`'s bound was wrong in a way worth keeping written down:** the first version read "until nothing arrives for `quiet`", which against a server that beats every 100 ms never returns — the recipe's own liveness signal turned into a hang, and it hung a test run for five minutes. It bounds total time now, with the reason in the doc comment. And one stale line in the crate documentation is corrected: `lib.rs` still said "what remains is the blocking facade" after B-087 shipped it, and now lists the facade among what the crate has, names the nine zguide examples and the module-inclusion instrument. **That closes the zguide block** — B-090, B-091, B-092 — with nothing left half-done.

### B-093 — The parity table against libzmq 4.3.5
kind: spec | size: 60 | status: done 7629829 | needs: [B-085, B-089]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/zmq.md` with `docs/libraries/README.md` as its index in the shape `docs/adapters/README.md` set, stating row by row every socket type, transport, mechanism, option, monitor event and device as present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason; no row says "partial" without naming what is missing.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: the deviations this table must carry are already discovered and documented where they live, so the item is transcription with tests behind it rather than an audit. Beyond B-085's 96 option rows: **a socket dropped before its session flushed loses its queued messages** — `ZMQ_LINGER` maps to `close_budget` at `Context::shutdown` and *not* to socket drop, found by B-087's first REQ/REP test and now the reason its example loops like `hwserver.c` rather than returning; the two errnos libzmq has no name for (`EACCES` for a peer a ZAP handler refused, `ENOCOMPATPROTO` for a malfunctioning dialog, B-082); the three bounds that are not libzmq options at all (`max_peers` B-099, `max_subscriptions` and `max_subscription_bytes` B-078/B-103, `max_message_frames` B-098); the finite close budget and the real `ZMQ_MAXMSGSIZE` default (0013 §4.4 item 5); CURVE's 72-octet HELLO padding against the prose's 70 (B-083); the monitor's reason frame as an additive extension where libzmq puts an errno in the value field (B-086); and `zmq_proxy`'s typed operands, where a socket that cannot receive cannot be a frontend at compile time rather than `ENOTSUP` at run time (B-105).
note: merged `--no-ff` as 7629829. **Docs-only, so no gate ran** (LOOP §6), and the diff is three files: `docs/libraries/README.md` and `docs/libraries/zmq.md` new, plus one row in README's Documentation table so the index is not orphaned — README's *crate* table deliberately untouched, because B-095 owns it. One commit (6c02e34), rebased onto main by the worker so it applied cleanly. **My brief's option count was wrong and the worker's is right:** I said 96, the table has **98**, and I verified it against the code rather than taking either number — 98 uniquely named rows in `optiontable.rs`, 60 `Refused` and therefore 38 `Honoured`, which is exactly what the document states. That it states the *measured* number rather than the one the brief remembered is the behaviour I want.
note: the index earns its place by doing something the adapter index does not: its first section **contrasts the two kinds of document**, so nobody writes the wrong one. An adapter mapping is about a bridge and carries the adapter-honesty invariant and a named-loss list; a library parity document is about a crate usable with no weida in the picture, mentions weida only where a weida crate replaces a foreign construct, and has **no loss list at all, because a library terminates no weida guarantee** — carrying parity honesty instead: present, refused-with-reason, absent-with-reason. Its two rules are the ones that make such a table worth reading: every row carries its evidence, and **there are no aggregate verdicts** — "mostly complete", "partial support" and "planned" are explicitly not verdicts.
note: what makes the table better than a transcription is that it asserts rather than describes in three places. All twenty rows of `zmq_socket(3)`'s table are there, and where the manual **omits** a mute action the document supplies it from the protocol RFC and says so (REP and SUB drop, per 28/REQREP and 29/PUBSUB) rather than leaving a blank; the compatible-peers row is documented as **enforced at the handshake** by `SocketType::accepts`, with the file and line, which is the difference between asserting the manual's rows and repeating them; and the option table is **generated from `OPTIONS`** rather than typed out. Two monitor deviations are rows rather than omissions: `ZMQ_EVENT_CLOSE_FAILED` is a mask bit that is **never emitted**, because no close path here can fail and be reported — and the worker did not invent an event to fill the row — while a failure carries a third frame with a sentence where libzmq's value field carries a `ZMQ_PROTOCOL_ERROR_*` number, so what a caller matching on numbers loses is named. One claim is marked `[inference]` because the monitor tests bind `tcp` only. §10 ends with the list a reader actually wants: everything this library does not have that libzmq 4.3.5 does.
note: one deliberate omission, and the reasoning is why I am not filing it as debt: libzmq's DRAFT `ZMQ_EVENT_PIPES_STATS` had a row and the worker **removed** it, because `docs/research/zeromq.md` does not cite it and an uncited claim about libzmq in a table whose whole value is that every row is sourced would cost more than the row is worth. A row for it needs a research line first, which is a choice somebody can make later rather than a gap in this item.
note: one correction to the note above, found by `git status` rather than by reading: the README Documentation row was **not** in the branch commit — it was an uncommitted change left in this tree, so the merge did not carry it. Committed separately as `0a607ba` (`docs: index the ZeroMQ parity table`), docs-only. Worth stating because the branch alone would have added two documents that nothing links to, and "the index is not orphaned" was the part of this item easiest to believe without checking.

### B-107 — The gate never compiles a non-default feature
kind: spec | size: 15 | status: done | needs: [B-087]
acceptance: LOOP §6's gate covers the `blocking` feature, or states deliberately that it does not and where that check lives instead — because as written the four commands compile `weida-zmq` **without** it, so 600 lines of `blocking.rs`, its example and 195 feature tests are invisible to the gate that decides whether a commit may land.
note: found on merging B-087, which is the first item in this repository to add a non-default feature. The worker ran the three extra commands itself and reported them, and I re-ran them here before finishing the item — `clippy -p weida-zmq --features blocking --all-targets`, `cargo test -p weida-zmq --features blocking`, `cargo doc` with the feature, plus the example — so nothing is unverified *today*; what is missing is that the next session has no instruction to do the same. LOOP.md is the user's standing instruction and not a document this loop rewrites, so this needs one sentence from them; it is `ready` rather than `blocked` because the check itself is being performed meanwhile and recorded in each item's note.
note: **answered as option A: the gate covers the features.** The owner chose "write it into the gate" over "state deliberately that it does not", so the replacement text for LOOP §6 is drafted with **measured** runtimes rather than estimates and waits only for the paste, because this loop never edits `docs/LOOP.md`. Measured on this machine, warm `target/`: the five default-off feature configurations — `weida`, `weida-zmq`, `weida-mqtt`, `weida-nng` with `blocking` and `weida-broker` with `cluster` — cost **89 s** for clippy plus test together and cover **850 tests** (284, 245, 152, 143, 26), none of which any `--workspace` command compiles. Two features are excluded with their reason rather than silently: `nng-interop` and `libzmq-interop` need a foreign C library at run time and belong to the item that touches them (their clippy runs still compile in ~2 s each), and `extension-module` is maturin's to set — a cargo run with it fails to link, which is why `develop.sh` and `package.sh` prove the wheels instead. The feature runs cost the next plain gate almost nothing: measured immediately after them, `clippy --workspace` took 3.7 s and `doc --workspace` 9.2 s, because cargo keys a fingerprint per crate **and** feature set.
note: the meanwhile-check of the note above, run again at `07f8b6f` and recorded here rather than left to memory: `cargo clippy -p weida-zmq --features blocking --all-targets -- -D warnings`, and the same for `weida-mqtt` and `weida-nng`, all three clean; `cargo test -p <crate> --features blocking` green at **245**, **150** and **138** tests. `weida-zmq`'s number moved (240 to 245) because B-177 added the split tests, which is exactly why the check belongs in the gate rather than in a note: the feature surface grows with items that do not mention it.
note: done. The owner pasted the §6 text; the gate now names the five default-off configurations and the 850 tests behind them, so the check is a rule rather than my habit. The item was open since B-087 — the first non-default feature in this repository — and what closed it is not a code change but a measurement: option B ("state deliberately that it does not") was on the table until the numbers showed 89 s for all five and 3.7 s of churn on the next plain gate, at which point covering them was simply cheaper than documenting why not.

### B-094 — The bridge rebuilt on the library
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 5b62c2c | needs: [B-088]
acceptance: `weida-zmq-bridge` keeps `Inbound`/`Outbound` and their configuration, drops `wire.rs`, the handshake driving, `Liveness` and the subscription reference counting in favour of `weida-zmq` sockets, still refuses zmtp.md §9's six configurations at configuration time, still states `core` on the weida side, and every existing bridge test and the interop bench pass with unchanged observable behaviour.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b094-zmq-bridge` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-093 tip 6c02e34, with B-095 queued behind it. **This is the item where the library pays for itself**: the bridge is its first consumer that is not a test, and "unchanged observable behaviour" is the whole claim. The thing to watch is the **deletion** rather than the addition — 0013 §5.2 names line by line what becomes the library's socket layer and what stays the forwarder's, so a bridge that kept its own `wire.rs` "just in case" would fail this item while passing its tests. The existing bridge tests are the acceptance: written against the old internals, they must pass untouched, which is what makes this a refactor rather than a rewrite.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 5b62c2c and gated here: **764 tests** one ignored, clippy clean with `blocking,libzmq-interop`, **228** under `blocking`, the libzmq matrix **18 passed** for real, and `cargo bench -p weida-zmtp-bridge --bench interop -- --test` **Success** on all four cases. One commit (30c9861). **The number that matters is the deletion: 1352 lines removed against 837 added.** `src/wire.rs` is gone entirely — `Session`, `Incoming`, the framed cancel-safe reader, `answer`, `answer_command`, `sanitize`, `DropQueue`; `Liveness` with its version gate is gone; `subscriptions.rs`'s reference counting, its `Change` enum, its `wants` re-filter and its two per-peer ceilings are gone; `SubscriptionForm` is a **re-export** of the library's rather than a second enum; and `BridgeError` lost `Handshake`, `SocketType` and three `From` impls for codec errors, "because a bridge that kept its own names for a protocol it no longer speaks would be lying". The crate's tokio features shrank from net+io-util+rt+sync+macros+time to **rt+sync+macros+time**: it has no socket of its own to read. That is 0013 §5.2's line-by-line split actually performed rather than approximated.
note: **no test file changed — I checked the diffstat rather than the claim.** Nine inbound, eleven outbound, seven interop and twelve unit tests pass with their assertions untouched, which is what makes this a refactor and not a rewrite; the ERROR naming both socket types in `tests/inbound.rs` now comes from `session.rs` and the test does not know the difference. **`Presenting::Pub` is an XPUB socket**, because the subscriptions have to reach the application to be translated — and that choice paid twice: `MidSegment::BoundaryAndRefilter`'s local re-filter became the socket's own prefix match against the topic frame, so the bridge's `wants` function and its refilter list **deleted themselves**.
note: one library capability was added to keep an existing test honest, and it is the right kind of addition: `tests/inbound.rs` asserts that a mid-segment subscription is refused **with an ERROR** and that the connection stays usable — and no ZeroMQ socket API can do that, since libzmq's XPUB application can decline to apply a subscription (`ZMQ_XPUB_MANUAL`) but cannot say why, while zmtp.md §9.3 exists precisely to forbid the silent drop. So `weida-zmq` gained `XPubSocket::refuse(reason)` over a new per-pipe refusal channel that the session turns into 37/ZMTP's `ERROR` command: **the protocol always had the command, libzmq's API just never exposed it.** Additive, with its own test in `xpubxsub.rs`, and written up as a row in `docs/libraries/zmq.md` §9 rather than left as an undocumented extra.
note: six behaviour differences, listed in zmtp.md §3 as a numbered list rather than absorbed silently — and **the interop bench found the sixth**, which is the bench earning its keep: `max_message_bytes` is a payload budget while `ZMQ_MAXMSGSIZE` counts frame headers, so a bench sending exactly 1 MiB against the 1 MiB default had its connection reset by the message's own header until the difference was named and the socket given the 32-octet allowance. The other five: a message the bridge cannot carry now ends the socket rather than one connection (no ZeroMQ socket API drops a single peer, and libzmq has none either); a DEALER peer must send the empty delimiter to a presented REP, which is what libzmq's REP requires; the outbound direction reconnects with `ZMQ_RECONNECT_IVL` instead of ending the run; a refused subscription still reaches the peer, through the capability above; and `queue_bytes` becomes `queue_bytes / max_message_bytes` messages per peer, at least one, because the HWM counts messages.
note: the worker also found the mechanism behind B-093's stray README change and reported it unprompted: **its session's cwd is this main tree**, so an `edit` with a relative header path lands here rather than in the worktree — which is what put the README row and then a zmtp.md paragraph in my tree. It moved the paragraph into the worktree copy, restored the main-tree file with a plain write rather than git (it does not run state-changing git here), and now uses absolute worktree paths for every doc edit. `git status --porcelain` was empty before this merge, which I verified.

kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done bcdfdff | needs: [B-071]
acceptance: `crates/zmq/` holds `weida-zmtp`, `weida-zmq` and `weida-zmq-bridge`; `crates/nng/` holds `weida-sp` and `weida-nng-bridge`; `cross-tests` moves to `crates/interop/`; README's crate table gains the `kind` column of [0013](decisions/0013-competitor-libraries.md) §5.5 and ARCHITECTURE §4's crate map and every stale doc path follow; no crate **name** changes, so nothing published breaks.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b095-crate-move` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-094 tip 30c9861 — the last item of [0013](decisions/0013-competitor-libraries.md)'s definition of done. The **bridge crate's rename travels in the same commit as the directory move**, at the worker's own suggestion and per 0013 §5.2, so the two can never disagree in the history. The acceptance clause that protects users is "no crate **name** changes" for the published ones: `weida-zmtp-bridge` becoming `weida-zmq-bridge` is a rename of something never published, while `weida-zmtp`, `weida-zmq`, `weida-sp` and `weida-core` keep theirs. Every doc path follows in the same commit, which is the part most easily left half-done: ARCHITECTURE §4's crate map, README's crate table with 0013 §5.5's `kind` column, and the paths quoted in zmtp.md, nng.md and the two library documents.
