One commit (c97802d): **68 files, 133 insertions, 110 deletions — a move and not a rewrite.** The tree is 0013 §4.1's layout: `crates/zmq/{weida-zmtp, weida-zmq, weida-zmq-bridge}`, `crates/nng/{weida-sp, weida-nng-bridge}`, `crates/interop/cross-tests`. Done with `git mv` throughout, and I verified the two things that claim rests on rather than accepting them: `git log --follow` still walks the bridge sources back through `30c9861` and `99c63a6`, and the manifests show **exactly two renamed crates** — `weida-zmq-bridge` and `weida-nng-bridge`, the two 0013 §5.2 prescribes — while `weida-core`, `weida-protocol`, `weida-runtime`, `weida`, `weida-zmtp`, `weida-zmq`, `weida-sp` and `weida-cross-tests` keep their published names. The fuzz directories travelled with their crates, lockfiles and corpora included.
note: **one leftover I found and removed, invisible to `git status` because it is ignored: `crates/adapters/` still existed on disk**, holding 47 MB of `weida-zmtp/fuzz/target` build output. `git mv` moves tracked files; ignored build directories stay behind, so the directory the whole item exists to eliminate was still there and still answering "no" to ARCHITECTURE §4's question. Nothing under it was tracked (`git ls-files crates/adapters` was empty), so it was regenerable output and I deleted it; `crates/` now lists exactly `core interop nng protocol runtime weida zmq`. Worth writing down because a move verified only by `git status` and a green gate would have left it indefinitely.
note: the docs followed in the same commit, which is the half most easily left undone. README's crate table gained 0013 §5.5's `kind` column **and four missing rows** — it had been listing four crates out of date, with no `weida-zmq`, no `weida-sp` and no `cross-tests` — and its intro no longer claims "the ZeroMQ adapter has its first three slices". ARCHITECTURE §4's map needed no row changed because it already described this layout, but its three paragraphs did: the one that said the codecs and bridges "still live under `crates/adapters`" now records the move and the renames, and "planned, not yet present" drops `weida-zmq` and keeps `weida-nng`. Stale paths also fixed in IMPLEMENTATION.md, STATUS.md, INVARIANTS.md, adapters/zmtp.md, adapters/nng.md and `docs/status/architecture.svg`, whose crate box still read `crates/adapters/*`. I checked what remains: the only current-document mention of the old path is **one deliberate sentence** in ARCHITECTURE §4 explaining why that name answered "no" to "does this repository ship a ZeroMQ?" — the rest are in BACKLOG.md and NIGHTLOG.md, which are append-only history, and in decision 0013, which records what was decided rather than what the tree looks like afterwards. That is the right place to stop editing.
note: **this closes [0013](decisions/0013-competitor-libraries.md)'s definition of done** — B-093 the parity table, B-094 the bridge rebuilt on the library, B-095 the move — and with it the whole B1 arc from B-070. What the repository has that it did not this morning: a ZeroMQ library with eleven socket types, three transports, three security mechanisms with the ZAP dialog, an option table decided row by row, a monitor, the devices and a blocking facade; interop in both roles against zmq.rs and against libzmq 4.3.5 with CURVE opening in both directions; nine zguide recipes that assert the guide's own claims; and a bridge that is 1352 lines smaller because the library does the protocol.

### B-110 — The zmq.rs interop tests race for a port
kind: adapter | size: 30 | status: done 559bb3b | needs: [B-088]
acceptance: the half of the zmq.rs matrix where *they* bind no longer fails when another test takes the port first — `free_endpoint` stops handing out a number it no longer holds, or every caller retries on `AddrInUse` with a fresh probe in the shape `tests/inproc.rs`'s rebind already uses. The test names stay, the assertions stay, and the retry is bounded and commented with the reason.
note: found by the gate on merging B-094, and **named by the gate rather than guessed at, which is what B-100's change to LOOP §6 bought**: `our_req_is_answered_by_a_zmq_rs_rep` failed at `interop_zmqrs.rs:180` with `AddrInUse` from zmq.rs's own bind, and the re-run was 764/0. The cause is in the helper rather than in either library (`interop_zmqrs.rs:102-109`): `free_endpoint` binds `127.0.0.1:0`, reads the port, **drops the listener** and returns the number — a textbook time-of-check-to-time-of-use window, and the workspace suite runs many binaries at once, so another test can take that port in between. zmq.rs needs a concrete port because, unlike ours, its `bind` cannot report one back (B-088's note), so the helper cannot simply go away. This is the first flake since B-100 and it arrived with a name, a file and a line; under LOOP §6 a second occurrence would make it a deterministic rewrite or a blocked item, and the deterministic rewrite is already known.
note: **a second site, observed on merging B-058 and worth the item's scope being widened before it is fixed**: `weida-zmq-bridge --test interop`'s `a_three_zero_peer_is_not_sent_pings` failed with `AddrInUse` at `interop.rs:554` in a full workspace run, passed alone, and passed on an immediate full re-run. So the race is not one helper in one file — whatever fix lands must cover every caller that probes a port and drops the listener, and the acceptance's "or every caller retries on `AddrInUse`" is now the cheaper half to satisfy across both files. Twice observed, never retried a third time: the fix is this item's, not a re-run's.
note: done 559bb3b, merged 261059e. Both sites: `interop_zmqrs.rs`'s `free_endpoint` and `weida-zmq-bridge`'s `tests/interop.rs` `free_port` are gone, replaced by a helper that probes `127.0.0.1:0`, binds *their* socket on the number, and on zmq.rs's `ZmqError::Network(AddrInUse)` probes again — bounded at eight attempts with a panic naming the last port taken, any other bind error failing at once. All twelve callers updated, names and assertions unchanged. Two facts the fix rests on were verified with a throwaway test rather than assumed: zmq.rs 0.6 reports a held port as exactly `Network(io)` with `kind() == AddrInUse`, and a zeromq socket whose bind failed that way is still usable, so the retry is live. `outbound.rs`'s `listen()` keeps its listener and has no window. 13 + 11 interop tests green.

### B-180 — The drain test is timing-flaky under load
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done d6307c1 | needs: []
acceptance: `a_draining_runtime_refuses_a_late_stream_on_an_open_connection` (`crates/weida/tests/drain.rs`) is deterministic — the late transfer is ordered against the drain by a channel, a read or a state observation rather than by a 5 s wall-clock window — or the item states why the phenomenon needs wall-clock time and reduces the window to the smallest one that demonstrates it ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2). It must still fail on the plausible bug it exists for: a draining runtime that accepts a late stream.
note: filed by me after the **third** sighting in this session — reported by W2 on delivering B-119 and by W4 on delivering B-157, both under a loaded machine, and then hit here in B-118's own gate, where it failed at `drain.rs:382` with "a late transfer must be refused", passed in isolation and passed on a clean full re-run (1324 tests). LOOP §6 says a test flaky twice gets a deterministic rewrite or becomes a `blocked` item; this is the rewrite, filed rather than retried a fourth time. It is a real assertion about a real invariant, so weakening or ignoring it is not an option — the fix is to order the events instead of racing a timer, and four parallel worktrees compiling at once is exactly the load that exposes it.
note: done d6307c1. The race was in the harness, not the assertion: `Draining::begin` spawned the drain and the test slept 100 ms hoping the task had run; under load it had not, so the "late" stream arrived before admission stopped and was accepted. `Runtime::drain` stops admission **before its first await** (`shared.drain.begin()` precedes `wait_for(...).await`), so `begin` now polls the drain future once in place — asserting `Pending`, which the stalled receipt guarantees — and only then spawns it: the late dial and the late stream are late by construction, and both sleeps are gone. Mutation-checked rather than assumed: with `drain.begin()` commented out, both tests fail with the messages they exist for, and the tree was restored. Three consecutive runs of the five-test file green, 5.0 s each.

### B-181 — `weida-nng`'s leftovers queue is bounded only while it is drained
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 39948d8 | needs: [B-134]
acceptance: the socket-level leftovers queue in `crates/nng/weida-nng/src/engine.rs` carries a **named ceiling of its own**, checked before a retired pipe's arrived messages are pushed into it, with the oldest dropped or the newest refused per the receiving protocol's `FullAction` — the same choice B-125 made one layer down — and a test stands up a peer that connects, sends its queue full, disconnects, and repeats while the application never receives, asserting the queue stops growing instead of following the peer. The doc comment's bound is corrected with it: the ceiling is not `max_pipes × RECVBUF` unless something also bounds how many times a pipe slot may be recycled.
note: filed by the review pass at anchor bac3f94, on the code B-134 added two merges earlier — and the bug is in the *reasoning*, not in the mechanism. The comment argues the queue is "bounded by what its pipes' queues could hold, which is `max_pipes × NNG_OPT_RECVBUF` and therefore already bounded", and that is true of **one instant**: `retire` removes the pipe from the table before pushing, so the slot it held is free again, and a stranger may reconnect, fill its queue, disconnect and repeat as often as it likes. Each cycle adds up to `RECVBUF` messages to a queue nothing drains unless the application receives. So the product is `max_pipes × RECVBUF × cycles`, and only the first two factors are bounded. Nothing is wrong with keeping a dead pipe's arrived messages — that fix is right and B-134's regression test earns it — what is missing is the ceiling on the place they are kept. `docs/INVARIANTS.md` is the rule this violates, and the queue's own doc comment cites it, which is the detail that makes this worth filing rather than shrugging at: the code knew which invariant it had to satisfy and satisfied it for the easy case.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 39948d8, one commit (4ba03d8), 125 insertions against 9 deletions; gate green: **1574 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. I read `store_arrived` rather than the summary, and **the fix is better than the acceptance asked for in one respect**: the ceiling is *one pipe's* incoming depth rather than `max_pipes ×` anything, because this buffer is the socket's receive buffer and not a per-pipe queue — which is the correct reading of what NNG keeps where, and it is argued in the doc comment instead of asserted. At least one, because holding the last answer is the reason the buffer exists at all. Full behaves as `SUB_PREFNEW` does at that same layer: oldest dropped for a SUB that prefers new publications, **newest refused for everyone else**, because the oldest is the answer somebody is already waiting for — and blocking, which `FullAction::receiving` would ask for, is unavailable when the pipe is gone and no reader can push back on a peer that has left. Refusals are counted into a `tracing::debug!` with both numbers.
note: the field comment no longer claims arithmetic it does not have; it points at `store_arrived`, where the reasoning now lives, and the reasoning states the exposure in the terms the review pass used — "the pipe set is bounded and the number of pipes over time is not". The test is the one the acceptance named: `a_peer_that_reconnects_in_a_loop_cannot_grow_the_buffer`, depth 4 and **sixteen** connect-send-disconnect cycles with nobody receiving, asserting exactly four held **and that they are the first four**, which pins the policy as well as the bound. Filed 06:40, fixed and merged by 07:00.

### B-182 — The survey discard test asserts an exact count it only waited for approximately
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 48a6513 | needs: [B-127]
acceptance: `the_deadline_starts_at_the_send` (`crates/nng/weida-nng/tests/survey.rs`) no longer fails when the late answer has not been discarded within its polling window — the wait and the assertion agree, so either the loop waits for the condition the assertion states or the discard is observed through something other than a polled counter (a notification, a channel, or a read that returns the discard). The deadline half of the test keeps its current strength: the surveyor must still give up ~300 ms after the *send* while the respondent answers 400 ms after receiving, which is the claim the test exists for.
note: filed by me after W4 attributed a flake it had seen as "1 target failed with no failing test result" while working on B-160. W4 then reproduced it properly: `cargo test -p weida-nng --test survey the_deadline_starts_at_the_send` three times in a row on an idle machine gave ok, ok, **FAILED**, at `survey.rs:104`, `assert_eq!(surveyor.discarded_late(), 1)` with left 0. It did not reproduce in four runs here, which is what a one-in-three race looks like from the wrong side of it. **The shape is unambiguous even without a reproduction:** lines 98-104 poll up to 100 × 10 ms for `discarded_late() >= 1` and then assert `== 1`, so the loop tolerates lateness that the assertion refuses, and the test decides by timing whether the late answer has been read off the wire and discarded yet. Two honest fixes exist and the item names both rather than picking for W2, whose file it is; what is not acceptable is widening the window, which only moves the failure rate. Worth reading beside B-180: both are wall-clock assertions about events that have an observable order, which is the class LOOP §2 asks tests to avoid.
note: **the flake was a library defect, not a test-ordering problem.** A response is only routed — and so only judged late — inside a receive that drains the pipes, and after `ETIMEDOUT` the context's survey is cleared, so the next receive returns `ESTATE` at once without draining anything: a late answer sitting in a pipe was never counted unless a *later* survey's receive happened to stumble over it. `discarded_late()` promised a count the code only kept by accident of timing. Fixed in 48a6513 by draining the pipes in the reader itself — `discarded_late()` routes whatever the pipes hold before loading the counter, and `route` already judges anything without a live survey as late — so the count is true at the moment it is read. The test now receives first (which reports `ESTATE`, the protocol's own word for a finished survey) and asserts the count second; 15/15 on Linux and green on the Windows runner. Found by running the tree on a second platform, where the race lost every time.

### B-183 — `weida-nng-py` has no parity document and the index still calls it planned
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done 70161d4 | needs: [B-138]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/nng-py.md` exists in the shape `docs/libraries/zmq-py.md` set — the binding against `pynng` over NNG 1.11.0 in the eleven-section form, every surface present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason, with the two absences B-137 named (raw sockets with `nng_device`; `tls+tcp`, where the library has the transport and this surface has no certificate API) stated as rows rather than as prose — and `docs/libraries/README.md`'s "Planned:" line loses `nng-py.md`, because it has landed. The document states which C release each claim was measured against, since the Rust suite uses 1.4.0-rc.0 and this binding's `pynng` bundles 1.11.0.
note: filed by me on merging B-150, from its index conflict. The "Planned:" line still reads "a `nng-py.md` when the Python binding on `weida-py-core` lands beside `zmq-py.md`" — and the binding landed two items ago in B-137 and B-138, with a sync surface and 51 Python tests. W1's chain filed `zmq-py.md` as its own item (B-118) and W2's did not file the equivalent, so this is a gap in the filing rather than in the work: five parity documents exist for five libraries and one of the two bindings has none. It is also the document that would record the **two-C-release** fact in the one place a reader compares implementations, which no other document can carry.

### B-184 — The gate's doc step cannot see a `cfg`-ed intra-doc link, and its cache can see nothing at all
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done | needs: []
acceptance: two holes closed, both in [LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §6's gate. **First**, every crate with a non-default or default-off feature gets a rustdoc run in the configuration that omits it — at least `RUSTDOCFLAGS="-D warnings" cargo doc -p <crate> --no-default-features --no-deps` for `weida`, `weida-mqtt`, `weida-amqp`, `weida-nats` and `weida-nng`, plus `--all-features` where a feature adds public items — because an intra-doc link to a `#[cfg(feature = ...)]` item **is a hard rustdoc error in the configuration that lacks the item** and no default-features run will ever show it. **Second**, the doc step is run against a target that cannot be stale for the crates the item touched (`cargo clean -p <crate>` before it, or a documented reason why the cache is trustworthy here), because rustdoc's incremental cache makes a doc-only change invisible: an unchanged `.rs` file that a *feature* now compiles differently is exactly the case it gets wrong. A test is not the instrument here; the acceptance is that both commands exist in the gate and the second one is stated as a step rather than a hope.
note: filed by me on merging B-152, which produced the run's **first red gate** and then, on the clean re-run, a second break nobody had seen. The first: `weida-mqtt-py`'s crate doc linked `[`sync`]` against a private `mod sync;` — W3 had run `cargo doc --workspace` in its worktree and read its silence as green, against an artifact from before the doc change. The second, found only because the fix forced a clean target: `[`TlsOptions`]` in `weida-mqtt/src/transport.rs`'s module doc, a `#[cfg(feature = "tls")]` type, which **shipped in B-147 and survived three subsequent items** through every `--workspace` doc run, because the workspace run has the feature on. Both are one-line fixes; the point is that **neither was reachable by the gate as written**, which is why this is an item and not a note. The cost of the hole was one round trip and a 8.1 GiB rebuild; the cost of the second hole was three items of a broken document nobody could see.
note: **both holes are measured and the §6 text is drafted (option A); it waits on the paste, since this loop never edits `docs/LOOP.md`.** And measuring it found the fourth instance of exactly this bug, mine from an hour earlier: `crate::blocking::Cursors::changed` in `crates/weida/src/cursor.rs` broke `cargo doc -p weida --no-default-features` with `-D warnings` while every `--workspace` doc run in this session was green — fixed in `00eff38` by writing the item as text with the reason beside it, which is the convention `weida-py`'s `sync.rs` already uses. So the hole is not theoretical and it is not shrinking: three instances before this one, and the run that proves the gate needs the command is the run that found the next one. Numbers for the text: the ten doc runs — five `--no-default-features` (`weida`, `weida-mqtt`, `weida-amqp`, `weida-nats`, `weida-nng`) and five `--all-features` (`weida`, `weida-zmq`, `weida-mqtt`, `weida-nng`, `weida-broker`) — cost **8 s** warm together. The cache half is cheaper still: `cargo clean --doc` is **0.1 s** and re-documenting the whole workspace **3.6 s** against **0.2 s** for the cached run, so the "cannot be stale" guarantee costs under four seconds and the doc step simply starts with it.
note: **the first half's defect is fixed, the gate text is still yours (2026-09-14).** Running what the acceptance asks for found a real break of exactly the predicted shape, invisible to every `--workspace` doc run this repository has ever made: `crates/weida/src/config.rs`'s `Identity` doc linked `[`Identity::generate`]`, a `#[cfg(feature = "generate")]` item, so `RUSTDOCFLAGS="-D warnings" cargo doc -p weida --no-default-features --no-deps` failed with `unresolved link`. It is now a `cfg_attr` pair — the link in the configuration that has the item, a plain code span in the one that does not — with the reason in a comment beside it. The full sweep afterwards, from a **wiped `target/doc`** (the acceptance's second half, performed by hand): `weida`, `weida-amqp`, `weida-mqtt` and `weida-nats` each in `--no-default-features` and `--all-features`, plus `weida-zmq` and `weida-nng` with `--features blocking` — ten runs, all clean. What remains is the sentence in LOOP §6 that makes the next session do it, which is the user's file.
note: done. The owner pasted the §6 text: ten rustdoc runs — five `--no-default-features`, five `--all-features` — and `cargo clean --doc` in front of the doc step, which is the second hole, the cache. Both halves are now steps rather than hopes, and the fourth `cfg`-gated link (`00eff38`) is in the text as the reason, so the next reader knows the rule was written by a failure and not by taste.

### B-188 — The gate on Windows: four failures the first run measured
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 48a6513 | needs: []
acceptance: LOOP §6's five commands are green on `win11-geselle` (`C:\work\gate.ps1` runs them in order and logs to `C:\work\gate.log`) from the same commit that is green on Linux, with nothing `#[ignore]`d to get there. Also: the `nng-interop` feature needs the C library to build under MSVC via CMake — state whether it does. IMPLEMENTATION.md records the Windows gate as a second gate with its numbers.
note: the first Windows run (2026-09-13) went build 25 s, fmt/clippy/doc green, tests 318 s with seven failures; three of them — the `ipc` tests of `weida-nng` running on a platform without `AF_UNIX` — were gated in 4c6d749 together with a `#[cfg(not(unix))]` arm for `Address::Unix` in `weida`'s dial, a unix-only bench import, three `cfg`-ed intra-doc links (B-184's class again), and two doc comments the remote-move rewrite had corrupted. Everything the Linux gate could not see. B-039 depends on this item: the pipe transport must land on a runner that is already green.
note: **done: the Windows gate is green end to end** — build 43 s, fmt, clippy both configurations, **117 test binaries with 0 failures in 131 s**, rustdoc — at 48a6513, the same commit that is green on Linux. The four, each at its cause: (1) the AMQP test asserted `is_usable()` at an instant the transport decides — Windows delivers the server's `begin` and its bad `transfer` in one read, so the session was already ending; the test now asserts the state it exists for, ended with `amqp:session:unattached-handle`, after the server's turn. (2) The TLS refusal is an abrupt close by design, and rustls reports it per platform — `ECONNRESET`/`ECONNABORTED` on Linux, `ESYSERR(unexpected EOF)` on Windows; the assertion accepts all shapes of the same refusal and says why. (3) The trybuild stderr quotes `core/src/marker.rs`, which rustc prints only with the `rust-src` component installed; the runner got it and the harness doc names the dependency. note: the open clause, measured: **the vendored NNG C library builds under MSVC via CMake**, and seven of the eleven `nng-interop` pairings pass on Windows (bus, pair v0/v1 both directions, pub/sub, push/pull, req/rep, the hop-count vector); `survey_interoperates_in_both_roles` **blocks with zero CPU** and takes the three after it with it (`--test-threads=1` shows the order). Filed as B-189; not a gate failure, because the matrix runs behind `--ignored`.

### B-189 — NNG survey interop blocks on Windows
kind: code | size: 45 | status: done 7280725 | needs: [B-188]
acceptance: `cargo test -p weida-nng --features nng-interop --test interop_nng -- --ignored` completes on `win11-geselle` with all eleven pairings green, or the survey pairing carries the reason it cannot and is `#[cfg_attr(windows, ignore)]`d with that reason in its doc comment. Found with `--test-threads=1 --nocapture`: `survey_interoperates_in_both_roles` is the eighth test in order and never returns, the process sitting at zero CPU — a wait on something that does not happen, not a slow path. Candidates to rule out in order: NNG's `SURVEYTIME` firing on a different clock under the C library's Windows build, the respondent's answer arriving after our deadline on a loopback that is slower than Linux's, and a close the C side reports differently so our side's "survey over" never observes it. The three tests after it (`the_device_forwards_between_two_nng_sockets`, `the_role_nibble_is_nngs_registry`, `the_tag_stack_survives_a_round_trip_through_nng`) are only untested because the process hangs before them; run them alone first.
note: filed from B-188's last clause on 2026-09-13. Everything else the C library touches on Windows works, which narrows it to the survey pattern rather than the FFI or the build.
note: **done 7280725 — the hang was a timeout the test could not report.** Found with an atomic checkpoint and a watchdog thread rather than prints, because prints alone made it pass: our respondent's `recv()` timed out at 5 s (the test's own `expect("a survey")` panicked), and the panic hung the process because the NNG surveyor loop in `spawn_blocking` — resurvey, wait `SurveyTime`, repeat — was unbounded, and a tokio runtime cannot shut down while a blocking task runs. **The race:** `SurveyTime` was set to `PATIENCE`, the same 5 s as our receive timeout, so a first survey that went out before our pipe existed left NNG's next survey and our timeout racing on the same clock; Windows's slower dial made our side lose about one run in four, and none of the three candidates the item named was it. The survey time is now 250 ms, so NNG resurveys well inside our timeout, and both open-ended waits in the pairing are bounded by `PATIENCE` so a failure fails. Linux 11/11; Windows **8/8 runs green**, alternating one and four test threads, where 1 of 4 hung before. The instrument is worth keeping in mind: a checkpoint in an atomic and a watchdog that aborts with its value costs nothing in timing, which is exactly what a print does not.

### B-096 — A byte ceiling for the per-peer queues, or the product stated where it is taken
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 76a98f5 | needs: [B-076, B-077, B-078, B-079]
acceptance: the exposure a socket hands a real peer is bounded in bytes rather than only in messages, or — where libzmq's message-counting HWM must be preserved exactly — the product `hwm × max_message_size` per direction per peer is stated at every place the numbers are taken and a test asserts the ceiling actually holds; the answer is the same shape B-053 and B-054 gave the bridge ([INVARIANTS.md](INVARIANTS.md)), and it says which of the two it chose and why.
note: found by B-072 rather than by a review pass. `ZMQ_SNDHWM`/`ZMQ_RCVHWM` count **messages**, so at the defaults one peer can hold 1000 × 1 MiB per direction; `DEFAULT_MAX_MESSAGE_SIZE`'s documentation states the product, which is honest but is not a bound. Deliberately after the pattern sockets exist (B-076..B-079): before them nothing hands these numbers to a stranger, and the right answer may differ per socket type — a PUB that drops is not exposed the way a PULL that blocks is.
note: the B-097 review pass found this item's other half and filed it as **B-099**: the per-peer product is one factor, the number of peers a stranger may open is the other, and `Engine::admit` bounds neither. Answer them with one number each — a ceiling on what a peer holds and a ceiling on how many peers there are — because either alone leaves the product unbounded.
note: done 76a98f5, and the item's two options were both tried on paper before one was built. Chosen: a **real byte ceiling**, `QueueConfig::max_bytes` (`DEFAULT_QUEUE_BYTES`, 8 MiB per direction per peer, the number the ZMTP bridge and `Limits::subscriber_buffer_bytes` already use for the same quantity), enforced in `Queue` beside the message count. Rejected, with the reason in the module documentation: the bridge's shape of dividing a byte budget by the message cap (B-053) works only where the messages' size is known — in the library `max_message_size` is a *ceiling*, so dividing would cut libzmq's documented default from 1000 messages to 8 for every socket, including the ones whose messages are forty bytes. `ZMQ_SNDHWM`/`ZMQ_RCVHWM` therefore still mean exactly what `zmq_setsockopt(3)` says, and what refuses is the peer that actually occupies the memory. **A queue that is empty still accepts one message of any size**, because a bound that can refuse the only message in a queue is a deadlock and not a bound, so the stated exposure is `max_bytes - 1 + max_message_size` — just under 9 MiB — and `ZMQ_MAXMSGSIZE` bounds the single-message case. The numbers are named where they are taken: the two `optiontable.rs` verdicts, `DEFAULT_MAX_MESSAGE_SIZE`'s doc comment (whose "the exposure is a product" paragraph now says the product is bounded), `zmq.md` §9's bounds table (six rows to seven) and INVARIANTS. Five tests, one of them at the socket surface with `hwm: 0` so that nothing but the ceiling can refuse, mutation-checked. The first run **deadlocked**: the refusal message re-locked the state mutex its caller already held, which a `std::sync::Mutex` answers by hanging forever — found by running the tests, fixed by passing the queued byte count in.

### B-111 — `weida-py-core`: the shared PyO3 foundation
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 9a550d8 | needs: []
acceptance: `crates/py/weida-py-core` holds what every binding of [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) shares and nothing protocol-specific — the error mapping that turns a library's errno-named enum into one distinct Python exception class per variant under a single base class, the `Runtime`/`Context` bridge that drives a library future on the caller's running asyncio loop and hands back an awaitable, Python-side cancellation propagated onto the Rust future instead of swallowed, and the `bytes` boundary that avoids a copy where PyO3 allows it and measures the copy where it does not — with `pyo3` and `weida-runtime` as its only external dependencies and neither `weida`, `weida-protocol` nor any protocol library in its manifest; one throwaway consumer in the crate's own tests proves an awaitable completing, an exception class arriving with its errno name, and a cancelled Python task cancelling the Rust future.
note: workstream W1, worktree ../weida-w1, branch b111-weida-py-core
note: merged `--no-ff` as 9a550d8, one commit (2b963ba), 1386 insertions in `crates/py/weida-py-core`; **one conflict, additive, both sides kept** — the workspace dependency list, where W2's `weida-nng` line met this crate's `weida-py-core` line. Gate green here: **815 tests**, 0 failed, 2 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The dependency-direction rule of 0014 §2 is verified with `cargo tree`, not read off the manifest: the normal-edge tree is `pyo3`, `weida-runtime`, `weida-core` and `tokio` with **no `weida`, no `weida-protocol` and no protocol library**.
note: the acceptance's three proofs are real and they are proved from Python rather than from Rust, which is the only place they could be: `tests/consumer.rs` calls `Python::initialize()`, builds a throwaway module, and runs `asyncio.run(main())` over it — an awaitable completing with a value, a `DemoError` subclass arriving with its errno name, and a cancelled `asyncio.Task` whose cancellation is observed by the **parked Rust future's own `Drop`** counter, which is the one piece of evidence a swallowed cancellation could not fake. The no-running-loop case fails at the call instead of handing back an awaitable that never completes, and a Rust panic arrives as a Python exception.
note: one thing to tidy when a binding first uses it, filed rather than fixed here: the `# and then, per method:` example in `lib.rs` is a ```ignore``` doctest, so nothing compiles it. It is a fragment of a binding that does not exist yet, so `ignore` is honest today; once `weida-zmq-py` exists (B-058) it should become a compiled example or `text`, because an `ignore` block is a claim the gate never checks.

### B-058 — `weida-zmq-py`: the module, the context and the eleven socket types

The surface is now `weida-zmq`'s rather than weida's, because W1 binds the library that is finished; and **asyncio comes first, the sync surface second** (B-116), which reverses what the original note argued — the blocking facade of 0013 §4.4 item 3 already exists in Rust, so building sync first would build it twice. A binding of weida itself is not part of W1-W4 and is not filed here.

That is **B-110's port race in a second file**, so B-110's note now records the new site rather than this item carrying a flake it did not cause.
note: the two runs the mandated gate cannot see were run here and both are green. The **non-default feature**: `clippy -p weida-zmq-py --features extension-module --all-targets -- -D warnings` passes; the feature is off by default deliberately, because cargo unifies features across a workspace and a default-on `extension-module` breaks `cargo test` for every crate that links the interpreter — `[tool.maturin] features` turns it on for the wheel alone. The **Python proof**: `sh crates/zmq/weida-zmq-py/develop.sh` in this tree built `weida_zmq-0.1.0-cp39-abi3-linux_x86_64.whl` against CPython 3.13 through the abi3-py39 limited API and ran **25 pytest tests, all passing**. The virtualenv is `uv`-managed at the repository root and `.gitignore` now carries `/.venv`, `__pycache__/` and `.pytest_cache/`, so the proof leaves the tree clean — I checked `git status` after the run.
note: **this item also changed `weida-zmq`, and the change is right.** `SocketCore::bind` and the generated per-socket `bind` stopped being `async fn(&self)` and now return `impl Future<Output = Result<Endpoint>> + Send + 'static` over a cloned `Engine`. The reason is not style: an `async fn(&self)` on a `!Sync` socket yields a future holding `&SocketCore`, which is not `Send`, so **no multi-thread executor could drive a bind at all** — the binding found a real defect in the finished library. Call sites are unchanged (`socket.bind(e).await`), so the break is limited to code naming the future's type. Two smaller consequences recorded for the next binding: the cdylib's `[lib] name` is `weida_zmq_py` rather than `weida_zmq`, because `cargo doc --workspace` refuses the rustdoc output collision, with the importable module name restored by `pymodule` plus `[tool.maturin] module-name`; and `weida-py-core`'s `ignore` doctest became a ```text``` block, which closes the loose thread B-111's note left open.

### B-112 — The asyncio surface: send, receive and the multipart message
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 6c5b9a7 | needs: [B-058]
acceptance: `await sock.send(...)` and `await sock.recv()` on all eleven types, with `Multipart` a first-class Python value sent and received atomically ("all frames or none"), `send_nowait`/`recv_nowait` for `ZMQ_DONTWAIT`'s `EAGAIN` and a timeout argument for `ZMQ_SNDTIMEO`/`ZMQ_RCVTIMEO`; payloads cross as `bytes` without a copy where PyO3 allows it and the copy's cost is recorded in [IMPLEMENTATION.md](IMPLEMENTATION.md) where it does not, and a cancelled `asyncio.Task` waiting in `recv` leaves the socket usable, which a test proves by receiving the next message on it.
note: workstream W1, worktree ../weida-w1, branch b112-zmq-py-asyncio
note: merged `--no-ff` as 6c5b9a7, one commit (7fae5de), 1346 insertions against 157 deletions, no conflicts. Gate green: **1076 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations **and** with the non-default `extension-module` feature, rustdoc with `-D warnings`; `develop.sh` ran here and **42 pytest tests pass** (25 from B-058 plus 17 new). The measurement clause is met with numbers in `docs/IMPLEMENTATION.md` §4 rather than a claim: the borrowed Python→Rust path is **flat at 16.5-16.9 ns from 0 B to 1 MiB** — a refcount and a type check, so nothing is copied — and the two forced copies are ~19 µs per MiB each, about 55 GiB/s, i.e. a `memcpy` and an allocation. The section also says what was **deliberately not done** and why: a `memoryview` would make Rust→Python zero-copy, but the buffer protocol entered CPython's limited API in 3.11 while the wheel is `abi3` from 3.9, so it would be a performance cliff across interpreters rather than a feature.
note: **the item changed `weida-py-core`'s `Bridge::awaitable`, and the reason is a real bug it found.** The future now starts at the **first await** instead of at the call, because the eager version lost a message: `create_task(sock.recv())` followed by an immediate `cancel()` cancelled a Task that had never run, while the receive it had already begun took the next message off the socket and dropped it. `tests/consumer.rs` now asserts the drop counter stays at zero for a coroutine built and closed unawaited, and `test_messages.py` asserts the message survives. One documented behaviour moves with it: "no running loop" is now reported at the coroutine's first step rather than at the call — still an error rather than an awaitable that never completes, which is the property that mattered.
note: two surface choices I checked against the acceptance and accepted: a socket type without a direction has **no method at all** rather than one that always raises `ENOTSUP` (PUB has no `recv`, PULL no `send`), which is the library's own shape; and `send_nowait`/`recv_nowait` are plain synchronous methods, because `ZMQ_DONTWAIT` has nothing to await. `Sent` and `Published` come back as reports instead of a silent success, so a dropped publication is visible from Python.

### B-114 — PLAIN, CURVE and a ZAP handler written in Python
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 63959bf | needs: [B-113]
acceptance: `ZMQ_PLAIN_*` and `ZMQ_CURVE_*` settable from Python with keys taken as 32 bytes or 40-character Z85, `CurveKey`, `RoutingId` and `ZapUserId` as distinct Python types with no conversion to or from any weida identity anywhere in the module, and a ZAP handler **written in Python** answering 200/300/400/500 over `inproc://zeromq.zap.01` for a socket of this module; a 400 from that handler refuses the connection before any message flows, which the test asserts by the absence of the message rather than by the status code alone.
note: workstream W1, worktree ../weida-w1, branch b114-zmq-py-security
note: merged `--no-ff` as 63959bf, one commit (a66a951), 694 insertions against 1 deletion, no conflicts. Gate green: **1199 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations and with `extension-module`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`, and `develop.sh` here ran **64 pytest tests, all passing** (55 plus 9 in `tests/test_security.py`). **The structural result is that the ZAP handler needs no Rust glue at all**, and it falls out of 27/ZAP rather than out of cleverness: ZAP is an `inproc://` REP dialog, so a handler written in Python is just a `RepSocket` of this module bound to `inproc://zeromq.zap.01`, and the Rust handshake talks to a *socket* instead of calling into Python — no GIL is held across an await on either side. That is the right answer and it is also the cheapest one.
note: the refusals are asserted by **absence**, as the acceptance demanded: 400, 300 and 500 each stop the connection before any message flows, proved by the receive timing out as `EAGAIN` *and* by no authorized peer appearing, rather than by the status code alone; a 200 lets a PLAIN message through with its user id readable on the peer as a `ZapUserId`; CURVE works both ends with the client's long-term key arriving at the handler as the credential; and a PLAIN server with **no** handler refuses rather than admits. The identity clause is satisfied structurally rather than by a test of omission: `CurveKey`, `RoutingId` and `ZapUserId` convert to nothing, because there is no weida identity in the module to convert to — `weida` is not in this crate's manifest.

Every implementation is mechanically rewritten with no behaviour change, and the trait's own comment — "no caller can need a `Send` bound this trait does not have" — was falsified the moment a binding existed. Two bindings, two defects of exactly this shape: the pattern is worth naming, and it is why a second consumer is worth more than a second test.

### B-116 — The sync surface over the blocking facade
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done f6376ed | needs: [B-112]
acceptance: a synchronous Python surface for the same eleven socket types over `weida-zmq`'s non-default `blocking` feature and a `Context::owned` reactor, so a Python process needs no asyncio loop at all — no second implementation of any protocol behaviour, the timeouts and `DONTWAIT` of the async surface preserved, and one zguide recipe (lazy pirate) written in synchronous Python asserting the guide's own claim, an in-order reply or abandonment, rather than merely running.
note: workstream W1, worktree ../weida-w1, branch b116-zmq-py-sync
note: merged `--no-ff` as f6376ed, one commit (992543c), 751 insertions, no conflicts. Gate green: **1301 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and with `extension-module`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`, and `develop.sh` here ran **77 pytest tests, all passing** (70 plus 7 in `tests/test_sync.py`). The manifest change is the item's substance rather than a detail: `weida-zmq-py` now takes `weida-zmq` with `features = ["blocking"]`, so the sync surface is **the library's own blocking facade** — each method calls it inside `Python::detach`, nothing is implemented twice, and the GIL is released while blocked. That last claim is asserted rather than asserted-in-prose: a test has a second Python thread keep ticking while the first is parked 300 ms inside `recv`.
note: the zguide clause is met the way 0014 §2 asks — the recipe asserts **the guide's own claim** rather than running: lazy pirate in synchronous Python, where each attempt carries its own number, the server answers the *first* attempt too late, and the test asserts the reply received answers the **last** attempt sent, so a stale reply crossing would fail it; a second test abandons a server that never answers in bounded time rather than hanging. `weida_zmq.sync` is a real submodule registered in `sys.modules`, so both `import weida_zmq.sync` and `from weida_zmq import sync` work, and it reuses the same `SocketOptions`/`ContextOptions` objects as the async surface instead of a parallel set.

The matrix is the one the acceptance asked for and it is real: **eight pairings** both implementations support × this module in either role × binding and connecting = **32 runs** against libzmq 4.3.5, plus PLAIN and CURVE with the security server on each side in turn — ours authorized by a ZAP handler written in Python, libzmq's behind pyzmq's `ThreadAuthenticator` — and one asyncio-surface exchange against libzmq.
note: the numbers are in `docs/IMPLEMENTATION.md` §4 and **I re-ran the Rust half here rather than transcribing it**: `cargo run -p weida-zmq --release --example roundtrip_cost` gives 16.1 µs inproc and 33.6 µs tcp against the recorded 16.5 and 32.4, so the table is reproducible on this machine. The comparison is the honest one — `weida_zmq.sync` at 67.7/111 µs, asyncio at 330/343 µs, pyzmq at 5.7/45.8 µs — and it says plainly that the asyncio surface is the expensive one on loopback while pyzmq's C loop wins inproc. The first full test pass hit B-110's port race again (`weida-zmq-bridge --test interop`, `a_payload_over_the_cap_reaches_no_real_zmq_peer`), passing alone and on a clean re-run; that item is already widened to cover every port-probing caller.
note: the asyncio number produced a filed item rather than a shrug: **B-179**, an operation that does not wait should not cost a loop wakeup — the bridge pays one asyncio self-pipe wakeup per await, about 78 µs here, and an operation that can complete without waiting could be polled once inline and handed back an already-resolved future, which `await` consumes without yielding to the loop.
