### B-120 — The message, the per-socket queues and `RECVMAXSZ`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 30868fc | needs: [B-119]
acceptance: a message with protocol header and application body in separate storage, delivered wholly or not at all with no streaming body; `SENDBUF`/`RECVBUF` as message depths 0-8192 with PUSH's documented default of 0 and the protocols that permit neither (REQ, one transaction per context) refusing them by name; `RECVMAXSZ` checked from the 64-bit declared length **before** any allocation; and the per-protocol full-queue action — PUSH and PAIR block or `NNG_ETIMEDOUT`, BUS drops, SUB drops oldest or rejects newest per `SUB_PREFNEW` — with one test per action.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b120-nng-message-queues
note: merged `--no-ff` as 30868fc, one commit (8807488), 1217 insertions, no conflicts; gate green here: **804 tests** (785 + 19), 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `weida-nng` itself now runs **39 unit tests plus 5 doctests**. Read rather than taken on report, because this is the item where a byte bound either exists or does not: `message::decode` hands the declared length to `weida_sp::message::decode(input, cap)` **before** any body storage exists and only then does `body.to_vec()`, `Incomplete` comes back as `Ok(None)` so a half message is unobtainable, and the `[0xFF; 8]` hostile length is `NNG_EMSGSIZE` with both numbers and the option name in the cause. The queue holds `VecDeque::new()` and grows, so a depth of 8192 is a ceiling and not an allocation. One test per full-queue action as the acceptance asks (`Block`, `Drop`, `DropOldest`, `RejectNewest`), plus depth 0 as NNG's rendezvous rather than libzmq's "unlimited", and `MAX_QUEUE_DEPTH` refused by name for both `NNG_OPT_SENDBUF` and `NNG_OPT_RECVBUF`.
note: two defaults in it are ours rather than NNG's and are documented as ours, which is the right way to carry a difference: `DEFAULT_RECV_MAX_SIZE` is 1 MiB where NNG ships unlimited (with `RECV_MAX_SIZE_UNLIMITED` kept for parity), and `DEFAULT_RECV_DEPTH` is 128 where NNG publishes no default at all. The send default stays NNG's documented 0 for PUSH.

### B-121 — The endpoint engine: dialers, listeners, pipes and reconnect
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 09d2096 | needs: [B-120]
acceptance: one socket owns many dialers and listeners, each creating pipes that map 1:1 to connections; a synchronous dial returns only once the peer's protocol header has arrived and a non-blocking dial retries, with backoff from `RECONNMINT` growing to `RECONNMAXT`; pipe add-before, add-after and remove-after events reach a callback that may not touch the socket; and the number of pipes one socket admits has a named, documented ceiling, because SP bounds nothing a stranger opens ([INVARIANTS.md](INVARIANTS.md)).
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b121-nng-engine
note: merged `--no-ff` as 09d2096, one commit (777c893), 1924 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **883 tests**, 0 failed, 2 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The acceptance's ceiling clause is the one I checked in the code rather than in the summary, because "named ceiling" and "ceiling that protects anything" are different claims: `DEFAULT_MAX_PIPES` = 1024 in `options.rs`, and `Engine::reserve` — which tests it — runs at `run_pipe`'s **first statement**, before the session starts and before the peer's header is awaited, inserting the entry with `admitted: false`. So the slot is spent from the moment a stranger's connection exists, which is the only version of the bound that bounds an attacker. Beside it `DEFAULT_HANDSHAKE_TIMEOUT` = 10 s races the handshake and retires the slot with `ETIMEDOUT`; without it the ceiling would be trivially exhausted by peers that connect and say nothing, and SP says nothing about that case at all. Both constants are documented as ours rather than NNG's.
note: two improvements on `nng_dial` that the worker documented at the call site and I am recording because they are deviations a parity table will want: the header wait is `async`, so the sheet's §1 same-thread deadlock cannot be constructed, and it is bounded by `handshake_timeout` where NNG waits for the socket timeout. The pipe callback is handed a `PipeInfo` **value and no socket handle**, which turns NNG's prose rule "the callback must not access the socket" into something unexpressible. Transports in this slice: `tcp://` only; `ipc://`, `inproc://` and `tls+tcp://` are refused by name as `NNG_ENOTSUP` until B-130 and B-131 carry them, which is the honest form of not-yet.

### B-122 — The SP session on the existing codec
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done f3cf0eb | needs: [B-121]
acceptance: both sides send the 8-octet protocol header immediately and wait for the peer's before anything else, the peer's endpoint type is checked against the pairing rule *before any traffic*, a mismatched magic, version or reserved field is answered by disconnection and nothing else — no reply, no reason code — and 64-bit framing runs through `weida-sp` unchanged; the codec's golden vectors still assert byte-for-byte, and nothing that parses SP is left in `weida-nng-bridge`.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b122-nng-session
note: merged `--no-ff` as f3cf0eb, one commit (5638047), 427 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **889 tests**, 0 failed, 2 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. Two acceptance clauses are checkable only outside the test count and both check out: `git diff` over `crates/nng/weida-sp` for this merge is **empty**, so the codec is genuinely unchanged and its golden vectors still assert what they asserted, and the tests drive a **raw `TcpStream`** rather than a second copy of the session — our eight octets are read off the wire and compared to the golden bytes per protocol before anything is sent to us, and every refusal is asserted by `read_to_end` returning **zero octets after our header**, which is the only way to prove "disconnection and nothing else".
note: one deliberate divergence from NNG, recorded here because the parity table will need it: an oversized declared length **closes the pipe** where NNG discards the message and keeps the pipe. Draining a length a hostile peer declared is the same attack in a politer shape, so the close is the right call; the test asserts it. And one acceptance clause is **not** in this item: "nothing that parses SP is left in `weida-nng-bridge`" waits for B-134, whose own acceptance drops the bridge's `wire.rs` — there is no replacement for it until the bridge is rebuilt on these sockets, and deleting it earlier would break the bridge rather than clean it.

### B-123 — REQ and REP with contexts
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 06a4268 | needs: [B-122]
acceptance: contexts that each hold one outstanding request with their own request ID and resend timer over a shared socket, the 32-bit tag stack written with the terminal bit set and the reply matched by it, requests spread over the available REP peers, REP able to send only after receiving with `NNG_ESTATE` for every other order and one pending receive per context, and a newer request cancelling interest in the older reply without withdrawing it from the replier; all three resend triggers — timer, peer disconnect, peer becoming available — with a test asserting the duplicate the sheet says a REP will see, rather than pretending retry is free.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b123-nng-req-rep
note: merged `--no-ff` as 06a4268, one commit (d6a3fa5), 1539 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **984 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The clause I asked to be an assertion rather than a paragraph is one: `the_resend_timer_makes_the_replier_see_a_duplicate` makes a REP receive the same body **with the same request ID** twice because nobody replied, so it fails if the resend stops happening *or* if the identity changes — and `a_disconnect_and_a_new_peer_both_resend_the_request` covers the other two triggers in one test, closing the peer that took the request and letting a second replier appear at an address the requester was dialling non-blockingly, with the same tag stack arriving and **no second send from the application**. The forbidden orders are `ESTATE` on both sides, one pending receive per context is asserted on both sides, and the newer-request rule is asserted with the replier still seeing both requests — cancelled interest, not a withdrawal.
note: two things in it are bounds or races rather than features and both are worth keeping in view: `Queue::offer()` now hands the message **back** when there is no room, so a round-robin that loses the last slot to another thread does not swallow it — NNG sockets are usable from several threads, so the race is real rather than theoretical. And `NNG_OPT_MAXTTL` is 8 by default over the manual's documented 1..=255 range, with `NNG_MAX_TTL` = 15 published beside it and the doc saying plainly that a value above 15 is not portable: both numbers, as `weida-nng`'s own rule requires, rather than a silent choice.

### B-124 — PUSH and PULL
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done b71265c | needs: [B-122]
acceptance: PUSH round-robins over the pullers that can accept *now* — an unavailable peer is skipped rather than held for its turn — and waits or fails with `NNG_ETIMEDOUT` when none can; PULL fair-queues arrivals with no defined order between simultaneously ready peers; PUSH has no receive and PULL no send, at the type level.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b124-nng-push-pull
note: merged `--no-ff` as b71265c, one commit (2175d2b), 446 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **990 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The clause I asked to be a test rather than a claim is `tests/pipeline.rs`'s `a_puller_that_cannot_accept_is_skipped_rather_than_waited_for`: one puller with `recv_depth` 1 that never reads, one that does, 200 messages of 32 KiB with a 5 s send timeout, asserting that **no send blocked past the timeout** and that the reading puller took at least half. It fails if the rotation holds a turn for the stalled peer, because every send after its queue and the kernel buffers fill would then time out — and the mechanism is the protocol's rather than arranged, since with NNG's documented `SENDBUF` of 0 a pipe is ready only while its write loop is parked in `recv`. Beside it: `ETIMEDOUT` on both the waiting and the non-blocking form with no puller, and PUSH **waiting rather than discarding** after the last puller leaves.
note: the type-level half is a `trybuild` harness (`tests/directions.rs`) with a third fixture that **must compile**, so the assertion is about direction rather than about a socket that can do nothing. Same standing caveat as B-102: the committed `.stderr` pins `rustc` wording, so a toolchain upgrade can redden it for a diagnostic change — regenerate with `TRYBUILD=overwrite` and read the diff, because the must-compile fixture is the half that cannot break that way. `trybuild` was added as a workspace dev-dependency, which is the version the rest of the tree already uses.

### B-125 — PUB and SUB
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done dc6cf3a | needs: [B-122]
acceptance: PUB offers every message to every connected subscriber without testing any subscription, SUB matches an arbitrary byte prefix of the body locally with an empty subscription admitting everything and several subscriptions per socket, and `SUB_PREFNEW` chooses oldest-drop or new-reject at a full queue; a test asserts the consequence the sheet measured — a subscriber that asked for one prefix is still sent the others and discards them — so the filtering locus is an assertion rather than a paragraph.
note: merged `--no-ff` as dc6cf3a, one commit (b36243b), 567 insertions against 41 deletions, no conflicts; gate green: **1080 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The sheet's consequence is the assertion the acceptance asked for: `a_subscriber_is_sent_what_it_did_not_ask_for_and_throws_it_away` publishes three topics to a subscriber holding one prefix, asserts the publisher queued a copy each time, and asserts `SubSocket::discarded() == 2` — it goes red the moment anybody teaches PUB to consult a subscription, which is the whole point of naming the filtering locus.
note: **the structural change I read before accepting, and it is right.** `SUB_PREFNEW` moved one layer up: a pipe's incoming queue now always blocks — plain backpressure, which is the only signal SP has, since it grants no credit on the wire — and the PREFNEW policy applies to the socket's queue of **admitted** publications, because a queue that dropped a *subscribed* message to make room for an *unsubscribed* one would be applying the policy to traffic the application never asked for. NNG's receive buffer sits in the same place. `prefnew_chooses_which_end_of_a_full_queue_is_lost` asserts the two policies keep opposite ends, so the choice is observable in what a reader sees rather than in a counter.
note: with it, `send_depth`/`recv_depth` became `Option<usize>` where `None` means the protocol's own default, and the send default is now per protocol: 0 where a full queue makes the sender **wait** (PUSH's documented value) and `DEFAULT_BROADCAST_SEND_DEPTH` = 128 where it makes the sender **discard** (PUB, BUS, SURVEYOR). The reason is sound: at depth 0 a dropping protocol discards every message that arrives while the writer is mid-write, which is not best effort but almost never, and a real NNG node's pipes carry a queue. Both constants are named and documented as ours. Also recorded: a repeated `subscribe` is not a second subscription, because SP has nothing on the wire to count — unlike ZMTP — and unsubscribing a prefix nobody holds is `NNG_ENOENT`.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b125-nng-pub-sub

### B-126 — PAIR v0 and PAIR v1
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done e0bdaa8 | needs: [B-122]
acceptance: PAIR v0 with no protocol header, PAIR v1 with its 32-bit word whose low octet is the hop count, `MAXTTL` between 1 and 255 enforced locally, and both readings of the initial count decoded — the RFC's one and NNG's zero — with what we send named in the doc comment and asserted by a vector; a paired socket rejects a second connection while one is live, and polyamorous mode is absent with the manual's own deprecation as the stated reason.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b126-nng-pair
note: merged `--no-ff` as e0bdaa8, one commit (85660c9), 546 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **1188 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The vector clause is met as octets off a raw `TcpStream`: `pair_v1_originates_nngs_zero_hop_count` reads `00 53 50 00 00 11 00 00` then `00..06 00 00 00 00 "hi"`, which pins the doc comment's claim that **we send NNG's zero and not the RFC's one**, and the same test feeds both readings back in, 0 and 1, with the count kept in the message's protocol header. The exclusivity is claimed at the **add-pre** hook rather than add-post, so two connections arriving together cannot both be admitted — the right ordering, and the same lesson as B-121's pipe ceiling. `a_message_past_the_local_hop_ceiling_is_dropped_and_the_pipe_kept` asserts the message gone, `dropped_over_ttl()` at 1, the pipe still there and **nothing sent back within 200 ms**, which is the silence §8 describes rather than an error the protocol has no room for.
note: polyamorous mode is absent and the absence is a **public constant**, `POLYAMOROUS_ABSENT`, carrying the manual's own deprecation and three reasons (a pipe handle rather than a routable identity, silent discard, cannot route through devices) — with a test asserting the reason text is there, so the absence cannot decay into an unexplained gap. That is the shape I would want every "we do not implement this" to take.

### B-127 — SURVEYOR and RESPONDENT
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done d184336 | needs: [B-123]
acceptance: a survey broadcast to every respondent with `SURVEYTIME` starting **at send**, at most one response collected per respondent, late responses discarded, a blocked receive expiring as `NNG_ETIMEDOUT` and a receive with no active survey returning `NNG_ESTATE`, contexts overlapping surveys with independent deadlines; a respondent that declines by silence is indistinguishable from a slow one, and the test asserts that equality rather than treating silence as failure.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b127-nng-survey
note: merged `--no-ff` as d184336, one commit (329acad), no conflicts; gate green: **1194 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The two clauses that decide this item are both tests rather than paragraphs. `the_deadline_starts_at_the_send` (`tests/survey.rs:74`) has the respondent answer 400 ms after *receiving* while the surveyor gives up ~300 ms after the *send*, so the clock cannot have started at arrival, and the following `recv` is `ESTATE` because the survey is over. `silence_is_indistinguishable_from_slowness` (`:117`) runs four surveys — a respondent that declines, one that is slow, one that closes, one that never reads — and asserts all four observations are the **same** `NNG_ETIMEDOUT`, which is the equality the sheet insists on instead of treating silence as failure.
note: the item also **deduplicated a state machine rather than copying it**, and I checked the claim that behaviour did not move: REP and RESPONDENT are the same machine — same tag stack, same answer-only-what-you-received rule, same one-receive-per-context — so it now lives once in `replier.rs` with `RepCtx` and `RespondentCtx` as aliases of `ReplierCtx`, and **all seven of B-123's tests still pass unchanged**, which is the only check that an extraction changed nothing. `NNG_OPT_SURVEYOR_SURVEYTIME` arrives with NNG's 1 s default, at most one response per pipe, and late or orphaned responses counted in `discarded_late()` rather than dropped silently.

### B-128 — BUS
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done e8023fe | needs: [B-125]
acceptance: BUS sends to every *directly connected* peer only, best-effort and non-blocking, discarding the copy for a peer that cannot receive while the send still succeeds; a three-node line asserts the far node receives nothing, which turns "a mesh must be fully connected" into a test, and raw BUS carries the ingress pipe ID and excludes that pipe when re-broadcasting.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b128-nng-bus
note: merged `--no-ff` as e8023fe, one commit (e8d68c0), no conflicts; gate green: **1199 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The mesh clause is a failing assertion rather than a paragraph: `a_three_node_line_does_not_reach_the_far_node` builds A→B←C, asserts A's send queued **exactly one** copy, B received it and C's receive expires with `NNG_ETIMEDOUT`, then dials the missing edge and asserts C receives the next one — so "a BUS mesh must be fully connected" is now something that can fail. `a_rebroadcast_excludes_the_pipe_it_arrived_on` asserts the forward queued one of two pipes, the far node got it, and the original sender's receive expires: no echo, proved by absence.
note: one design note the worker recorded and I am keeping, because B-129 depends on it: the **ingress pipe id travels in the received message's protocol header** as four big-endian octets — the shape raw BUS puts on the wire per §3 — but for a cooked socket it is **local metadata that is never sent**, a send always building its own message, and `bus::ingress_of` returns `None` for a message that did not come from a BUS receive (rebroadcasting such a message is `NNG_EPROTO`). That is what lets raw BUS keep the identical accessor when the header really is on the wire, instead of two spellings of one idea.

### B-129 — Raw sockets and the device
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done d4d3679 | needs: [B-127, B-128]
acceptance: a raw variant of each protocol that preserves the wire headers and omits the state machine, retry and matching, refusing contexts the way NNG does because the state they hold is deliberately absent; plus `device`-style forwarding between two raw sockets where the REQ/REP backtrace and the PAIR v1 hop count supply the routing and the loop control, with a test running a request through a forwarder and back and asserting the forwarder's own peer ID was pushed and popped.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b129-nng-raw-device
note: merged `--no-ff` as d4d3679, one commit (07400d7), 634 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **1206 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. Two things in it are better than the acceptance asked for. **Raw is a property of the socket rather than of the protocol** — `RawSocket::open(context, EndpointType)`, one raw variant per protocol from one type, keeping the handshake, the pairing rule, the framing and **every bound**, dropping only the state machine, the retry, the matching and all header manipulation. And **contexts are refused in the type**: `RawSocket::context()` returns `Result<Infallible>`, so the absence is in the signature where it cannot be missed, with the `ENOTSUP` cause carrying the reason that the state a context would hold is deliberately absent. The pass-through is asserted byte for byte against a cooked peer in both directions.
note: the device's routing rule is **read off the protocol rather than configured**, which is the right call for a forwarder that must not be told what it is forwarding: Rep/Respondent push the ingress pipe id onto the front of the stack, Req/Surveyor pop it and select the reverse pipe, PairV1 increments the hop count and drops past the device's own `MAXTTL`, anything else forwards unchanged, and a single-socket BUS arrangement excludes the ingress pipe. `a_request_through_a_device_is_pushed_and_popped` runs a cooked REQ through a raw REP/raw REQ device to a cooked REP and asserts the server saw **exactly one** peer id — the forwarder's, nonzero — while the reply the client received has an **empty** peer list and the same request ID; `a_device_bounds_the_pair_v1_hop_count` asserts 1 arrives as 2 and 3 at `MAXTTL` 3 is dropped silently with the pipe kept.

### B-130 — The inproc and ipc transports
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done f0a205e | needs: [B-121]
acceptance: `inproc://` as a namespace on `weida-runtime`'s registry that accepts and deliberately ignores `RECVMAXSZ` for NNG's own reason (peers share an address space), and `ipc://` on `weida-runtime`'s `AF_UNIX` hygiene — unlink-then-bind, explicit mode, socket-type check before unlinking, the path budget — with the kernel's UID, GID and PID reaching a pipe-add-pre hook as adapter policy and the PID documented as an observation that must not be authorized on ([0010](decisions/0010-local-transport.md) §4.4).
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b130-nng-local-transports
note: merged `--no-ff` as f0a205e, one commit (ac4b6d2), no conflicts; gate green: **1293 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. Both transports sit on `weida-runtime` rather than re-deriving it — `inproc://` on the `NameRegistry` (context-scoped, because a Rust library cannot own the process the way NNG's global namespace does, with `NNG_EADDRINUSE` for the second owner and a dial before the bind parking on a `Notify` instead of failing, which is what NNG's retrying inproc dialer amounts to), and `ipc://` on `BoundUnixSocket`, so the `sun_path` budget, the socket-type check **before** anything is unlinked, unlink-then-bind, the explicit 0600 after the bind and the node's removal on drop are the hygiene B-057 already paid for. Three tests hold that: the mode is 0600 and not umask's, a non-socket path is refused **and still there**, and the node is gone when the listener closes.
note: the `RECVMAXSZ` clause is **two tests rather than one**, which is the difference between testing a transport and testing a dead option: `inproc_accepts_and_ignores_the_size_limit` pushes 64 KiB through a socket configured with `recv_max_size` 16 and asserts it arrives with the pipe untouched — the option is *accepted*, not refused, so a ported program keeps its configuration — and `tcp_enforces_the_limit_inproc_ignores` asserts the same limit costs the pipe over `tcp://`. Credentials reach the `AddPre` hook as `LocalPrincipal` captured at connection time (`PipeInfo` gained a `credentials` field), with a test asserting an allow-list on uid refuses a peer by closing and **sending nothing back**; the PID is documented in three places as an observation that must not be authorized on, citing 0010 §4.4. `tls+tcp://` is now the only scheme still refused as `NNG_ENOTSUP`, and the engine test that used `inproc` for that case was moved to `tls+tcp` and additionally asserts `inproc` listens — the right correction to make when a not-yet becomes a yes.

### B-131 — The TLS transport
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done f47083a | needs: [B-121]
acceptance: `tls+tcp://` with what NNG exposes — authentication mode, CA, certificate and key, the verification result, the peer common name and its alternative names — reaching the pipe-add-pre hook where an allow-list runs; the crate's documentation states at the point of configuration that this authenticates the transport peer of one SP connection and terminates there, and no API turns it into a sender identity, because SP has none.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b131-nng-tls
note: merged `--no-ff` as f47083a, one commit (eb8e02f), 1026 insertions against 12 deletions; gate green **after one fix of mine**, at **1333 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **The fix is the first genuinely cross-workstream collision of this run and it is worth recording**: W4's B-157 and W2's B-131 each added a `tokio-rustls` workspace dependency, at different places in the file, so git merged **both** without a conflict and `cargo metadata` refused the manifest with `duplicate key`. Both requirements were identical (0.26, default features off, `ring`/`logging`/`tls12`), so I kept one entry and folded both reasons into its comment — AMQP's `%d2`/`amqps` layer and NNG's `tls+tcp` transport, one adapter, no second TLS stack and no second cryptographic backend (`fix(deps): one tokio-rustls entry for both TLS users`, be9652d). Additive-but-not-textually-conflicting is the merge case to watch when two workstreams reach for the same dependency.
note: `cargo tree -p weida-nng` links no OpenSSL and no `native-tls`, which is the claim `unsafe_code = "forbid"` depends on. All six things the sheet's §10 names are there: `AuthMode` with **`Required` as the default**, because a transport whose default proves nothing is one whose users think it proves something; `ca_pem`, `cert_pem`, `key_pem` with the key documented write-only; and `TlsPeer { verified, common_name, subject_alt_names }` reaching `PipeInfo::tls` at the add-pre hook. Five integration tests, and the two I would keep above the others are `AuthMode::None` **encrypting while `verified` stays false and the common name stays `None`** — so the field an allow-list reads says what actually happened rather than what was hoped — and a `tls+tcp` dial naming an address with no `server_name` refused as `NNG_EADDRINVAL` citing `NNG_OPT_TLS_SERVER_NAME`.
note: the identity boundary is held structurally, which is what the acceptance was really asking: there is **no conversion from `TlsPeer` to any identity type and no accessor on `Message` that returns one**, and the documentation says at the point of configuration that this authenticates the transport peer of one SP connection and terminates there — that a message on an authenticated pipe carries no claim about who composed it, and that a message through a device arrives on a pipe authenticated as **the device**. The common name is parsed by a narrow DER scan for 2.5.4.3 and documented as display-only, with the doc pointing an allow-list at the subject alternative names, which is what path validation actually checks.

### B-132 — The option table, honoured or refused
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 34d98f3 | needs: [B-129, B-130]
acceptance: every socket, dialer, listener and pipe option the sheet names is honoured or refused **at configuration time** with the reason named — no such transport, the protocol does not support it (`SENDBUF` on REQ), experimental and out of scope (ZeroTier), replaced by a `weida-runtime` construct — and never silently ignored; `RECVMAXSZ` is settable per endpoint before that endpoint starts, as the manual requires, and a test walks the whole table.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b132-nng-options
note: merged `--no-ff` as 34d98f3, one commit (0867901), 835 insertions against 6 deletions, no conflicts; gate green: **1406 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `optiontable.rs` is **the table as data** — 48 rows, each naming the option as NNG spells it, its scope, and either what honours it here or a refusal from a five-word vocabulary: `NoSuchTransport`, `NotThisProtocol`, `ExperimentalUpstream` (ZeroTier, with NNG's own word for it as the reason), `ReplacedByRuntime` (`RECVFD`/`SENDFD` replaced by the futures a reactor waits on; `IPC_PERMISSIONS` by `weida-runtime`'s explicit 0600 and the directory rule) and `Absent` — plus a note that is never empty. `require_honoured` gives **three** answers rather than two: `Ok`, `NNG_ENOTSUP` carrying name, reason and note, and `NNG_EINVAL` for a name `nng_options(5)` does not define at all, which is the distinction between "we refuse this" and "there is no such option".
note: the in-crate walk asserts every row names something and carries a note **longer than 20 characters**, and it earned its place immediately: it caught **fifteen notes too thin to act on**, which is exactly the decay a table like this suffers. The outside walk counts 25+ honoured and 8+ refused, so the table cannot quietly become a stub. The per-endpoint clause is real API rather than a promise — `EndpointOptions { recv_max_size }` with `dial_with`/`listen_with` on the engine and on every socket type, applied when the endpoint is created and **never after** — and `recvmaxsz_is_settable_per_endpoint` stands up one socket with a 1 MiB limit, one listener overridden to 64 bytes and one on the socket's own number, then asserts 4 KiB costs the pipe on the first and arrives on the second: the two trust boundaries §11 describes, on one socket, in one test.

### B-133 — Interop against the `nng` crate, both roles
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done ddc95ce | needs: [B-123, B-124, B-125, B-126]
acceptance: every pairing this library and NNG both implement, with our socket dialling **and** listening, against the `nng` crate over its vendored C library — its blocking calls kept off the reactor's threads and `RecvTimeout`/`SendTimeout` set so a stall is an error rather than a hang, `#[ignore]` with the reason where no C toolchain is present ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2); the two things the sheet says only the implementation can settle are exercised rather than assumed — the PAIR v1 initial hop count and the endpoint-type role nibble — and every disagreement is recorded as measured against a named version.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b133-nng-interop
note: merged `--no-ff` as ddc95ce, one commit (915748b), 751 insertions against 41 deletions, no conflicts; gate green: **1407 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **The feature run the standing gate cannot see, run here for real:** `cargo test -p weida-nng --features nng-interop --test interop_nng -- --ignored --test-threads=1` → **11 passed, 0 failed in 14.1 s**, against **NNG 1.4.0-rc.0** through the `nng` crate 1.0.1 over `nng-sys`, which needed `cmake` and a C compiler and built the vendored C library. Every pairing in both roles — REQ/REP, PUSH/PULL, PUB/SUB, PAIR v0, PAIR v1, SURVEYOR/RESPONDENT, BUS — plus the raw device forwarding a cooked NNG request through two of our raw sockets to a cooked NNG replier and back. Every `nng` call is on `spawn_blocking` with `RecvTimeout`/`SendTimeout` set, so a stall is an error rather than a hang, which is LOOP §2 satisfied by construction.
note: **the item found a real disagreement and fixed it on our side, and the fix changes an already-merged crate**: `weida_sp::pair::INITIAL_HOPS` was `0` on the strength of a reading of NNG's `pair1/pair.c`, and a *running* NNG 1.4.0-rc.0 sends **1**, which is also what the RFC says. So the constant is now 1, and with it golden vectors 18/19 in `weida-sp`, `weida-sp`'s notes, `weida-nng`'s `pair.rs` doc and unit test and `tests/pair.rs`'s wire vector — the B-126 vector I recorded five items ago as pinning "NNG's zero" now pins the measured one, which is exactly why that vector existed. **Nothing about interoperability turned on it** — a receiver only compares the count to its own `MAXTTL`, so both readings were always accepted in both directions — but the documentation was wrong, and `weida-sp` gained a test asserting the other reading still decodes. This is the second time a running implementation corrected a reading of its source in this repository, and both times the vector was already there to be corrected.
note: the sheet's second open question is settled too: `the_role_nibble_is_nngs_registry` reads the eight octets NNG puts on a plain TCP connection for **all eleven protocols** and they match `weida_sp::ProtocolHeader` exactly. Both findings need `docs/research/nanomsg-nng.md` to cite a new numbered source (B-136 will add it as source 31 and mark the two §11 questions settled), and the two deliberate divergences — an oversized declaration closing the pipe, the 1 MiB `RECVMAXSZ` default — are not observable from the wire and belong in the parity table (B-135).

### B-134 — The bridge rebuilt on the library
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done c9540c2 | needs: [B-133]
acceptance: `weida-nng-bridge` keeps `Inbound`/`Outbound`, their configuration and their refusals and drops `wire.rs`, the handshake driving, the tag-stack bookkeeping and the local prefix matching in favour of `weida-nng` sockets; it still states `core` on the weida side and SP's absent transfer point on the other ([adapters/nng.md](adapters/nng.md) §7), `max_message_bytes` becomes `RECVMAXSZ` on the socket with the bridge keeping its own weida-side cap, and every existing bridge test **and** the cross-adapter suite of B-057 pass with unchanged observable behaviour.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b134-nng-bridge-rebuild
note: merged `--no-ff` as c9540c2, one commit (4cbe344), **780 insertions against 731 deletions** — which is what a rebuild should look like — and `wire.rs` is **gone** from the bridge, closing the clause B-122 deferred. Gate green: **1481 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `Inbound` and `Outbound` are now written on `weida-nng`'s sockets (REP/PULL/PUB listening, RAW/PUSH/SUB dialling) and **all 19 pre-existing bridge tests pass unchanged**, which is the only evidence that a rewrite kept its observable behaviour.
note: **the rebuild exposed and fixed two real library bugs, which is the argument for rebuilding rather than keeping a parallel implementation.** A **retired pipe used to discard messages that had already arrived** — so a REP peer that answered and hung up lost its answer, the single most ordinary shape in request/reply — and the engine now keeps them in a socket-level leftovers queue that `try_take_any` drains first, with a regression test driving the peer by hand. And the socket's wait helpers **parked on closed pipes' queues**, spinning the send loop. Neither was reachable from the library's own tests; both were reachable from a second consumer with a different usage pattern. That is now three defects this session found by giving a finished library a second user (B-058's `bind`, B-115's `Device` trait, these two), and it is the strongest argument in the run for 0013's forwarder-on-the-library shape.

The **`MAXTTL` 255-versus-15 question stays open, with exactly what would settle it written down**: a raw PAIR v1 send with count 15 and a sixteen-element REQ stack against cooked NNG sockets, observing delivery, drop or close. An open question with an experiment attached is worth more than a closed one with a guess in it. §13 also records **what the run does not establish** — mangos, later releases, the TTL ceiling — which is the half a summary usually drops.

kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 85d244e | needs: [B-111, B-133]

note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b137-nng-py-asyncio
note: merged `--no-ff` as 85d244e, one commit (4d008d3); gate green: **1655 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in the workspace configuration **and** with `weida-nng-py`'s non-default `extension-module`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`, plus `develop.sh` run here — **42 pytest tests pass** across `test_sockets`, `test_options`, `test_contexts` and `test_interop`. Eleven socket classes, one per SP protocol, with the three `Context` constructors; `nng_ctx` arrives as **three** Python classes rather than four, because `RepCtx` and `RespondentCtx` are one type in the library after B-127's deduplication — the binding inherits the shape rather than re-deriving it. All 23 `NNG_E*` names as exception classes under one base, and the 50-row option table reachable from Python with **options refused where they are written**: `max_ttl` 0 or 256, a depth past `MAX_QUEUE_DEPTH`, a `send_depth` on REQ by `NNG_OPT_SENDBUF`'s own name, a subscribe list on a PUSH socket by `NNG_OPT_SUB_SUBSCRIBE`'s.
note: **the library is now measured against two different C releases**, which is worth more than either alone: the Rust suite runs against NNG 1.4.0-rc.0 through the `nng` crate, and this binding's six interop tests run against **1.11.0** through `pynng`, which bundles and builds its own copy. Both roles for REQ/REP, plus PUB/SUB filtering, PUSH/PULL and SURVEYOR/RESPONDENT. **Two absences are named rather than half-offered** — raw sockets with `nng_device`, and `tls+tcp`, where the library has the transport but this surface has no certificate API yet and the URL is refused with `EINVAL` naming the missing configuration — in both the README and the module documentation.
note: **and the binding found a fifth library bug**, in the same class as the four before it: a send or receive on a **closed** socket waited out `SENDTIMEO`/`RECVTIMEO` instead of answering `NNG_ECLOSED`, and with NNG's default of *forever* it would never have answered at all. `SocketCore::ensure_open` is now checked on each turn of both loops and a close wakes the waiters, with two regression tests. Five defects in one session found by second consumers of finished libraries — three by bindings, two by the rebuilt forwarder — and not one of them was reachable from the library's own tests.

### B-138 — `weida-nng-py`: the sync surface
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 93dc1be | needs: [B-137]
acceptance: the synchronous Python surface over the same sockets with no asyncio loop required in the process and no second implementation of any protocol behaviour, send and receive timeouts turning a stalled exchange into an exception instead of a hang; B-137's round trip runs unchanged in synchronous form.
note: workstream W2, worktree ../weida-w2, branch b138-nng-py-sync
note: merged `--no-ff` as 93dc1be, one commit (715e643); gate green: **1668 tests**, 0 failed, 17 ignored, fmt, clippy in the workspace configuration **and** `-p weida-nng --features blocking --all-targets`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`, plus **51 Python tests** (42 unchanged, 9 new). Two pieces, and the split is the right one: `weida-nng` gains a `blocking` feature whose wrappers are `block_on` over the async sockets **entering the context's reactor for the poll** — without that enter a bind panics for want of a reactor, which is the one thing the ZeroMQ precedent did not have to solve — and `weida_nng.sync` is argument conversion over it with the GIL released on every blocking call. No protocol behaviour is implemented twice. B-137's round trip runs unchanged in synchronous form, a second test asserts `asyncio.get_running_loop()` **raises** in the process while the exchange happens, two tests pin that a stalled receive and a stalled send each return `ETIMEDOUT` inside two seconds rather than parking the thread, and one test drives a **sync socket against an asyncio socket**, which is the observable proof that there is one implementation underneath.
note: one gate subtlety the worker fixed the right way rather than the easy way: the compile-fail harness asserted "no method named `recv` found for struct `PushSocket`", and with the binding in the workspace cargo unifies features, so `blocking` is on and rustc prints `weida_nng::PushSocket` instead — the namesake removes rustc's path trimming. **The feature set was made deterministic rather than the test weakened**: `weida-nng` now lists itself as a dev-dependency with `features = ["blocking"]`, so the harness sees the same names under `cargo test -p weida-nng` and under the workspace run, and both `.stderr` files carry the qualified form. Weakening a compile-fail assertion to match whichever features happened to be on would have been invisible and wrong.
note: **this closes W2 — B-119 through B-138, twenty items, nothing unstarted** — and the workstream's ledger is worth reading as a whole: a library, a rebuilt forwarder, two Python surfaces, a parity table, a sheet update, interop against **two** C releases, and **five** library defects found by second consumers rather than by its own tests. W2 was hard-aborted shortly after this delivery, so **B-182 has no owner**: it is a flaky test in `weida-nng`'s own suite, attributed by W4, and it stays `ready` for whoever takes the next NNG item.

The **client** side comes first, per [LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §9 B3 as amended by [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md), so the document must say for each rule which half binds a broker and which a client — the broker half is Phase D, and the client half is what W3 is read against.
note: the next protocol in the roadmap and the first whose mapping is genuinely hard: MQTT has a session, weida deliberately does not, and QoS 2's "exactly once, strictly per hop" is the case SYNTHESIS §7.2 already calls the weakest link of the MQTT-weida-AMQP chain. Better written before any code than discovered inside a bridge.

Docs-only by the diff — `docs/adapters/mqtt5.md` new, `docs/adapters/README.md` the row plus the rewritten "Planned" paragraph, no Rust line touched — so LOOP §6 asks for no gate; the full gate ran anyway on the merge result and is green (785 tests, 0 failed, 1 ignored). The worker corrected its own golden vector 20 on the second commit: `80 01` is the *minimal* encoding of 128, so the non-minimal examples are `81 00` and `80 00`, and the check the row now states — more than one byte with a trailing `0x00` — is the right one for [MQTT-1.5.5-1].

### B-139 — `weida-mqtt-codec`: the fixed header, the type system and CONNECT/CONNACK
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done ee84ff2 | needs: [B-062]
acceptance: `crates/mqtt/weida-mqtt-codec` with an **empty `[dependencies]`**: the one-byte type-and-flags header, Remaining Length as a Variable Byte Integer of 1-4 bytes refused when not minimally encoded, UTF-8 strings and binary data with their 65,535-byte bound, the property framework with its Variable Byte Integer identifiers, and CONNECT/CONNACK with every property of 3.1.2.11 and 3.2.2.3 encoded and decoded; golden vectors follow the normative body's payload order (Client Identifier, Will Properties, Will Topic, Will Payload, User Name, Password) rather than Appendix B's, a fuzz target rides along, and nothing is allocated before a declared length is checked against a caller-supplied maximum packet size.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b139-mqtt-codec-connect
note: merged `--no-ff` as ee84ff2, one commit (968aafd), 5601 insertions; **two additive manifest conflicts** (member list, dependency list) against W1 and W4, both sides kept, the member line grouped with the other protocol families. Gate green: **977 tests** (889 + 88), 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `cargo tree -p weida-mqtt-codec` prints exactly one node, so the empty `[dependencies]` of 0013 §4.3 holds for the third codec in the tree.
note: the bound clause is satisfied the way the mapping document asks and I read the entry point rather than the summary: `Packet::decode` takes `max_packet_size`, `FixedHeader::decode` applies it **from the fixed header alone** — refusing an over-large declaration having consumed and reserved nothing — and the body is then *borrowed* rather than copied. The one allocation left on the decode path is a `Box` for CONNECT's Will, and the reason is recorded where it is taken: inlining a second property set widened every `Packet` variant, `PUBLISH` included, by 336 bytes for a field that appears once per connection.
note: one decision in it is worth more than its size and is tested both ways: inside a packet whose declared length **has fully arrived**, a field that runs off the end is `PacketLengthMismatch` and never `Incomplete`, because reporting `Incomplete` there parks a reader loop on bytes the peer has already finished sending. `Incomplete` stays the one variant that is not a verdict about the connection (`is_violation()` false), which is exactly what a reader loop needs to distinguish. The VBI minimality rule is the one B-062's vector 20 corrected — more than one byte with a trailing `0x00` — and identifier 128 is read as a VBI and rejected as 128 rather than mistaken for a one-byte code.

### B-140 — The codec's remaining thirteen packet types
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 31fc404 | needs: [B-139]
acceptance: PUBLISH, PUBACK, PUBREC, PUBREL, PUBCOMP, SUBSCRIBE, SUBACK, UNSUBSCRIBE, UNSUBACK, PINGREQ, PINGRESP, DISCONNECT and AUTH encoded and decoded with their flags, packet identifiers, reason codes and properties; repetition is a Protocol Error for every property except User Property and Subscription Identifier, an unknown identifier or a wrong type is a Malformed Packet, and a golden vector per packet type plus the extended fuzz target assert it — with the codec still dependency-free and weida-free.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b140-mqtt-codec-packets
note: merged as 31fc404 — **merged by commit hash (168037c) rather than by branch name, because `refs/heads/b140-mqtt-codec-packets` had been moved off this work to `dc6cf3a` by the time I merged**; the commit itself was intact and its parent is main `4b1d54c`, so nothing was lost, but the branch ref no longer names the item. Gate green: **1148 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`; `cargo tree -p weida-mqtt-codec` still prints exactly one node. All thirteen remaining packet types, 149 tests in the crate, and golden vectors 5-19 of `docs/adapters/mqtt5.md` §10.1 now asserted in both directions so all 23 rows live in the test file — row 9 decode-only by construction, because it is the long spelling of row 8 and the encoder emits the short one.
note: four decisions worth keeping, each also in the module documentation. **One reason-code type per packet**, closed subsets with all 256 bytes asserted, `PUBREC`/`PUBCOMP` as aliases where the specification shares the list — so `0x92` stays a PUBREL code and not a PUBACK one, which a single flat enum would have lost. **The omissible tails**: the four acknowledgements decode in two-, three- and four-byte spellings and encode in the shortest, DISCONNECT likewise, and **AUTH deliberately has no short form**, because every AUTH must repeat the CONNECT's Authentication Method ([MQTT-4.12.0-5]) so a two-byte AUTH could not be conformant. **The subscription-options byte carries three different verdicts** — Malformed for reserved bits 6-7 and for QoS 3, Protocol Error for Retain Handling 3 — with an exhaustive pass over all 256 values, and No Local on a `$share/` filter refused in the codec because the filter is in the same packet.
note: and the part I would have asked for if it had not been offered: **three rules are deliberately not enforced, each named where a later item picks it up** — a zero-length Topic Name needs the alias table (B-146), a Subscription Identifier in a client-to-server PUBLISH needs the direction (B-144), and DISCONNECT's "zero then non-zero Session Expiry is a Protocol Error" needs the CONNECT that came before (B-142). A sans-I/O codec has no state for any of them, and saying so is better than a check that half works. The fuzz corpus grew a 15-seed set, one valid packet per type each asserted decodable, plus a second target for the four countless-list payloads, where an off-by-one shortens a list rather than failing.

### B-141 — The client: connection, negotiation and keep-alive
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 80cee30 | needs: [B-140]
acceptance: `crates/mqtt/weida-mqtt` on `weida-runtime`: CONNECT as the first packet and CONNACK awaited before anything but AUTH, both declarative property sets exchanged with the server's Receive Maximum, Maximum QoS, Maximum Packet Size, Topic Alias Maximum and the five availability flags honoured — using an unavailable feature is this client's own error and never reaches the wire — Server Keep Alive overriding the client's value when present, PINGREQ sent when nothing else has been within the interval, and a server DISCONNECT's reason code surfaced as a named error rather than as a closed socket.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b141-mqtt-connection
note: merged `--no-ff` as 80cee30, one commit (b1b4416), no conflicts; gate green: **1248 tests** (1206 + 42), 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`, and `cargo tree -p weida-mqtt` reaches **neither `weida` nor `weida-protocol`** (0013 §4.2 checked, not assumed). One task owns the socket, so MQTT's per-connection acknowledgement-ordering obligations ([MQTT-4.6.0-2] to [MQTT-4.6.0-4]) fall out of the structure instead of being enforced; `ServerLimits` reads the CONNACK **once** and substitutes §11's default for every absent property, so absence is resolved in one place rather than at each call site; `ServerLimits::require` refuses each of the five availability flags **locally** with the code the server would have sent (0x9A, 0x9B, 0x9E, 0xA1, 0xA2) and a test asserts the wire afterwards shows only CONNECT and DISCONNECT. A server DISCONNECT is `Error::ServerDisconnected` with its code and a bare close is a distinct `Error::ConnectionClosed`, so the two are never conflated.
note: **two bugs its own tests caught, and both are the kind only a scripted server finds.** The reader was **not cancel-safe**: it grew its buffer and truncated after the read, so the event loop's `select` dropping the read future mid-way left zero bytes and the next decode read them as reserved packet type 0 — it now reads into a scratch buffer and appends only what arrived, making a cancelled read a no-op. And the keep-alive timer was restarted per loop turn, so **a chatty server could keep resetting a silent client's timer** until the server closed the connection at 1.5× — exactly the failure the mechanism exists to prevent; it now measures from the last **write**, which is what [MQTT-3.1.2-20] says. Both are the sort of defect a green suite hides until someone writes an adversarial peer.
note: the AUTH exchange of §4.12 is implemented **at connect time rather than deferred**, and the reason is sound: "CONNACK awaited before anything but AUTH" is only testable if AUTH is actually answered. The method is checked against the CONNECT's on every AUTH and on the CONNACK ([MQTT-4.12.0-5]), and an AUTH to a client that named no method is `Error::UnexpectedPacket` ([MQTT-4.12.0-6]). Three ceilings are sourced from the protocol (send quota from Receive Maximum, outbound alias table from Topic Alias Maximum) and two are **ours because the protocol has none** — the Subscription Identifiers one delivery may carry, and the queue of deliveries waiting for the application — each named in `Limits` and documented as ours; the two unquantified timers, a missing CONNACK and a missing PINGRESP, are fields with finite defaults and a test each that the client gives up rather than hangs.

### B-142 — The session: Clean Start, Session Expiry, Session Present and takeover
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 890ac59 | needs: [B-141]
acceptance: the client half of the session state — QoS 1 and 2 messages sent but not fully acknowledged, QoS 2 messages received but not fully acknowledged — held across connections under one Client Identifier, Clean Start 1 discarding and Clean Start 0 resuming, `Session Present` 1 with no local state closing the connection and 0 with local state discarding it, Session Expiry Interval set on CONNECT and revised on DISCONNECT with "zero then non-zero is a Protocol Error" enforced before sending; retransmission happens **on reconnect only**, with the original packet identifiers and DUP 1, and a test asserts nothing is resent inside a live connection.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b142-mqtt-session
note: merged `--no-ff` as 890ac59, one commit (7b996dc), 1750 insertions against 37 deletions, no conflicts; gate green: **1324 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **The application owns the session, and that is the item's argument rather than an implementation detail**: weida declined session state outright ([0008](decisions/0008-session-identity.md) §4.5, "a retained session is remote-controlled state that the stream core has no owner for"), so a client library that holds the *client half* has to say who the owner is — here it is the caller, which is what lets it outlive a connection. Session Present is the full matrix of 3.2.2.1.1 with each row a test **on the wire**: Clean Start 1 discards before the CONNECT, Session Present 0 with local state discards on arrival and then resends nothing, and Session Present 1 with **no** local state fails the connect as `Error::SessionPresentWithoutState`.
note: the test that earns [MQTT-4.4.0-1] asserts an **absence**, which is the only way that rule can be tested: three exchanges in flight, a one-second keep-alive, three intervals of idling, and the wire carries nothing but CONNECT, PINGREQ and DISCONNECT. Worth having, because the ecosystem disagrees loudly — EMQX retries after 30 s and Paho Python republishes after a reconnect even with `clean_session=True`, which its own documentation calls non-compliant. What *is* resent is asserted on decoded bytes rather than packet types: the original Packet Identifier with DUP 1, **in send order rather than identifier order** (tested with the identifiers deliberately out of order), and a QoS 2 exchange past its PUBREC resends only the PUBREL and never the PUBLISH.
note: two things stay out of the session **on purpose** and each is documented where a reader would look for it: the send quota and Receive Maximum, "explicitly not session state" (§4.9), and the Topic Alias table, which MUST NOT cross connections — so `StoredPublish` has no `topic_alias` field at all and a resent message carries its full Topic Name, which is the structural version of the rule. The codec's deferred DISCONNECT rule is now enforced before sending (a non-zero Session Expiry where CONNECT declared zero or none is refused locally, with the test asserting the refused DISCONNECT never reached the wire while shortening to zero stays permitted), and both tables have ceilings taken from the right side: the inflight identifier table from the **server's** Receive Maximum (the client's 64 becoming the server's 2, then `Error::QuotaExhausted`), and the inbound QoS 2 table from the Receive Maximum **this client declared**, with a server that exceeds it reported as `Error::ReceiveMaximumExceeded` carrying 0x93 rather than a table that grows.

### B-143 — QoS 1 and QoS 2 with the Receive Maximum quota
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 1c0183d | needs: [B-142]
acceptance: both delivery state machines in both directions — PUBLISH/PUBACK, and PUBLISH/PUBREC/PUBREL/PUBCOMP — over one packet-identifier space per direction per session, freed on PUBACK, PUBCOMP, a PUBREC of 0x80 or above, or SUBACK/UNSUBACK; the send quota is the peer's Receive Maximum counted in QoS 1 and 2 PUBLISH packets and nothing else, and exhausting it stalls the sender rather than exceeding it; a QoS 2 exchange interrupted by a reconnect completes across the resumed session with neither loss nor duplication, which is the test that earns this item.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b143-mqtt-qos
note: merged `--no-ff` as 1c0183d, one commit (21adcd2), 2003 insertions against 33 deletions, no conflicts; gate green: **1456 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. Both state machines in both directions, and the outcome type has **four** values rather than a boolean because the three QoS levels certify different things — with **none of them named `Stored`**, since "no acknowledgement certifies durability" [mqtt5 §12/P7], and 0x10 "No matching subscribers" carrying its own test as a *success*. A PUBREC of 0x80 or above ends the exchange without a PUBREL and leaves nothing to resend, asserted on the wire; inbound, a repeated QoS 2 PUBLISH is answered with a second PUBREC and **not delivered again**, and a PUBREL for an unheld identifier is answered 0x92 with the connection surviving.
note: **the design bug its tests caught is the kind that ships**: the session had a single `quota` field used for *both* outbound allocation and the inbound QoS 2 table, and those are two different numbers — the send quota is the **server's** Receive Maximum (3.2.2.3.3) while the receive table's ceiling is the **client's own** declared value (3.1.2.11.3). So a CONNACK that simply omitted Receive Maximum set the quota to the 65,535 default and **silently raised this client's own inbound ceiling from 2 to 65,535**, which is a bound evaporating because a peer said nothing. `set_send_quota` now touches the send side only, and a test asserts the client's 64 stays 64 while the server's 2 replaces the send quota. The flood test surfaced it by *timing out instead of reporting*, which is worth remembering as a symptom.
note: one methodological note I want kept, because it is a trap I would have fallen into: a "lost PUBREC" written as send-then-close is a **race** between the write and the close, so the test would be deciding by timing which packet was lost. The harness therefore grew accept-without-answering acts, so a lost acknowledgement is a server that accepted and said nothing. The four lost-packet cases then differ in *outcome* rather than in setup — lost PUBLISH and lost PUBREC both resend the PUBLISH with DUP 1 and differ only in the delivery count (1 either way, which is the no-duplication half); lost PUBREL and lost PUBCOMP both resend only the PUBREL and differ only in the server's answer, 0x00 against 0x92, with the client required to **complete** on 0x92 rather than hang. The harness counts onward deliveries with its own duplicate suppression, so "exactly once across the whole recovery" is an assertion rather than a hope. The quota's stall is asserted the same concrete way: the third publish's future stays pending, its packet stays off the wire, and a QoS 0 publish **overtakes** it — which is what "the unit is one QoS 1 or 2 PUBLISH and nothing else" means in practice.

### B-144 — Subscriptions: the `+` and `#` filters and the subscription options
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 977a000 | needs: [B-142]
acceptance: SUBSCRIBE and UNSUBSCRIBE with per-filter options — maximum QoS, No Local, Retain As Published, Retain Handling 0/1/2 and 3 refused as a Protocol Error — plus an optional Subscription Identifier reported on every delivery it caused; matching asserted with the specification's own worked examples (`sport/tennis/player1/#` matching the parent level, `sport/+` matching `sport/` but not `sport`, `/finance` matching `+/+` and `/+` but not `+`, and a leading-wildcard filter never matching a `$` topic); a per-filter SUBACK reason code, and re-subscribing a filter replacing it without losing messages.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b144-mqtt-subscriptions
note: merged `--no-ff` as 977a000, one commit (e2e080c), 1965 insertions against 15 deletions, no conflicts; gate green: **1477 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The matching clause is honoured literally: `the_specifications_worked_examples` asserts `sport/tennis/player1/#` against the **parent level itself**, `sport/+` matching `sport/` and **not** `sport`, `/finance` against `+/+` and `/+` and **not** `+`, and a separate test establishes that the `$`-topic rule is about the filter's **first character** rather than about wildcards anywhere in it — `#` and `+/broker/uptime` refused against `$SYS` while `$SYS/#` and `$SYS/+/uptime` match.
note: **the finding that would have shipped: the Packet Identifier space is one space for three packet types ([MQTT-2.2.1-3]) and the quota is not.** A single `next_free` over the publish table alone would have let a SUBSCRIBE take an identifier an unacknowledged QoS 1 PUBLISH was still holding. Fixed with a separate `control` set that `next_free` also consults, and the test asserts **both halves at once**: with Receive Maximum 1 and a publish holding the only quota, the SUBSCRIBE still goes out — the quota counts QoS 1 and 2 PUBLISH packets and nothing else — *and* its identifier differs from the publish's. The two rules pull in opposite directions, so a test checking one would have passed on either bug; that is the test design worth copying.
note: two smaller decisions that are right for stated reasons. **Position is the only binding between a SUBACK code and a filter** — the acknowledgement carries no filters — so a count mismatch is *unreadable* rather than merely odd, and it earns a named error (`AcknowledgementLengthMismatch { packet_type, sent, received }`) instead of being zipped short; the harness takes raw SUBACK bytes precisely so a script can name a disagreeing count. And **a refused filter is not recorded**: `Subscriptions::record` drops an error code rather than storing it, so the client's mirror never claims a subscription the server declined, asserted as 3 filters in, 2 in the mirror, call successful. The mirror is documented as a mirror and not an authority — §4.1 lists subscriptions under the *server's* session state — and `clean_start_discards_the_subscription_mirror` keeps that honest. Retain Handling 3 is **unrepresentable in the type**, so the codec's decode-side refusal is the only path to one, and the test says so rather than pretending to exercise it.

### B-145 — Retained messages and the Will
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done a1054ad | needs: [B-144]
acceptance: publishing with RETAIN, a zero-byte retained payload as the delete that is itself not stored, and a receiver that can tell a retained delivery from a live one under both Retain As Published settings and all three Retain Handling values; the Will carried in CONNECT with its properties, QoS, retain flag and Will Delay Interval, discarded by DISCONNECT 0x00 and requested anyway by DISCONNECT 0x04 — with a test asserting the Will fires on an abortive close, does not on an orderly one, and is not published when the session is resumed inside its delay.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b145-mqtt-retain-will
note: merged `--no-ff` as a1054ad, one commit (8eee071), 810 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **1491 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **The design bug it fixed was its own, shipped in B-142, and the reason is worth more than the fix: [MQTT-3.2.2-4] is unusable read literally.** §4.1's client-side Session State lists only the unacknowledged QoS 1/2 exchanges, so `accept_connack` refused `Session Present` 1 whenever the client happened to hold no messages — which obliged a client that connects with Clean Start 0 and Session Expiry 300, publishes nothing, drops and reconnects to **close on its own correct resumption**. The fix adds the third piece of client-side state the specification's enumeration omits: whether a session exists on the server at all, decided by whether the last CONNECT declared a non-zero Session Expiry Interval, cleared by Clean Start 1 with the rest. All four rows are tested, including the one that still *earns* `SessionPresentWithoutState` — a session that declared no expiry really does end with its connection. Filed onward as a candidate for B-153's "internal inconsistencies" list: the conformance statement and §4.1's enumeration cannot both be taken at face value.
note: **the Will Retain asymmetry is structural and no validation can close it**, and the item says so instead of hiding it: a publish with RETAIN against Retain Available 0 is refused locally, but the *Will* travels inside the CONNECT — before the CONNACK that carries the declaration exists — so there is nothing to check it against, and the only honest answer is the server's 0x9A surfaced as a named error. The test asserts both halves **side by side** so the asymmetry reads as a property rather than an omission. On the Will generally the item is equally disciplined: no client ever sees its own Will, so "the Will fires" is not an observable of this crate; what is observable is how the connection ended, and one test asserts the three endings on the wire across three consecutive connections — DISCONNECT 0x00, DISCONNECT 0x04 asking for it anyway, and dropping the handle so the transport closes with **nothing written** — with two DISCONNECTs for three connections as the assertion. The broker half is B-148/B-149's and the test file says so rather than letting a scripted server pretend.
note: `RetainedOrigin` names the loss Retain As Published creates, with a third value `Unknowable`: under RAP 0 the RETAIN flag is the verdict, under RAP 1 it reports the publisher's flag and a cached copy becomes indistinguishable from a live one — the same wire bytes read twice in one test, once per setting. Retain As Published exists for bridges, and the cost is that a bridge cannot also tell which of its inbound messages were snapshots. That is the right way to record a loss: a value in the type, not a sentence in a document.

### B-146 — Shared subscriptions, topic aliases and request/response
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 566e9eb | needs: [B-143, B-144]
acceptance: `$share/{ShareName}/{filter}` subscribed **as a client**, with a ShareName containing none of `/`, `+`, `#`, No Local refused on it, and no retained message expected at subscribe; Topic Alias per direction and per connection, bounded by the peer's Topic Alias Maximum, established by a non-zero-length Topic Name, never zero and never carried across a reconnect; and request/response with Response Topic and Correlation Data, deriving the response topic from CONNACK's Response Information where the server offered it and saying so where it did not.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b146-mqtt-shared-alias-reqrep
note: merged `--no-ff` as 566e9eb, one commit (56aeec3), 1144 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **1617 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **Topic aliases are two tables with two different bounds, and one table would have been a security hole** — [MQTT-3.3.2-8] says mappings are not shared between directions, so the CONNACK's number says what the *server* accepts from us and the CONNECT's says what *we* accept from it; a shared table would accept an alias the peer was never permitted to send, and the inbound bound is exactly what stops a peer sending alias 40,000 against our declared 4 and making us allocate a 40,000-entry map. Both refusals earn 0x94 and the test asserts **the map is still empty afterwards**, so the refusal demonstrably precedes the allocation.
note: the retransmission test is the one that proves [MQTT-3.3.2-7] and it could not be written any other way: publish twice with the second aliased to a zero-length Topic Name, lose the connection with a QoS 2 exchange in flight, resume, and assert the resend carries the **full** Topic Name with no alias and DUP 1. A stored message that kept its alias would resend a zero-length Topic Name with a stale alias — a Protocol Error at the server — and **within one connection aliasing is indistinguishable from correctness**, so only the reconnect can catch it. `StoredPublish` was already built without an alias field in B-142, so this confirms that design rather than repairing it. **No eviction in the outbound table, argued rather than defaulted:** once the server's maximum topics hold aliases, further topics go out whole, because any policy that beats "keep the first N" would have to know the caller's next topic and a wrong guess costs a full Topic Name on *every* publish. The test asserts the fifth publish is unaliased **and** that alias 1 still resolves, which is precisely what no eviction buys.
note: two honest findings recorded rather than smoothed. **A shared delivery is indistinguishable from a non-shared one on the wire** — there is no shared-delivery flag — so a client subscribed to both `state/+` and `$share/g/state/+` has exactly one way to tell which subscription delivered a message, the Subscription Identifier; the test delivers two byte-identical PUBLISHes differing only in that identifier and asserts matching cannot separate them either, because `$share` is not considered when matching. And **`Client::response_topic` returns `Option` with deliberately no fallback**: without CONNACK's Response Information a client has no protocol-level way to learn which topics it may publish replies on, and inventing `$response/{client_id}` would invent a namespace the server never granted and its authorization rules will refuse. The test asserts the client *did* ask, got nothing, reports `None`, and that the documented answer — setting `Message::response_topic` from out-of-band knowledge — works. Correlation stays entirely the client's, since the server forwards both properties unaltered and keeps no table.

### B-147 — TLS and enhanced authentication
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 88870b9 | needs: [B-141]
acceptance: TLS on 8883 with the certificate configuration a client needs, User Name and Password in CONNECT including 5.0's password without a user name, and the AUTH exchange of 4.12 — a client that sets Authentication Method sends nothing but AUTH or DISCONNECT until CONNACK, and re-authentication over a live connection works; a server that does not implement AUTH is reported by its reason code rather than by a hang, which is the failure mode the sheet records for two large deployments.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b147-mqtt-tls-auth
note: merged `--no-ff` as 88870b9, one commit (d09ee84), 1276 insertions against 26 deletions; gate green: **1653 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. This item introduces a **default-on `tls` feature**, and the worker ran the configuration the standing gate does not cover without being asked — as W4 did for `weida-amqp` — so I re-ran it here too: `clippy -p weida-mqtt --no-default-features --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean and `cargo test -p weida-mqtt --no-default-features` **142 tests** passing with the three TLS integration tests `cfg`'d out. `cargo tree -p weida-mqtt` links **no OpenSSL and no `native-tls`** in either configuration, which is what `unsafe_code = "forbid"` rests on, and `tokio-rustls` rides the rustls 0.23 already in the graph.
note: four things worth keeping. **The trust anchors are not in the crate** — `TlsOptions` takes the caller's `rustls::ClientConfig`, the same decision and the same argument W4 made for AMQP — and the test generates its own certificate with `rcgen`, completes a **real** handshake, then asserts the User Name and Password read on the server side arrived *inside* it, because "the credentials travel encrypted" is a claim only a real handshake can carry; a second test trusts a certificate for `some.other.host`, validates against `localhost`, and asserts the failure is reported rather than papered over. **`TlsOptions::server_name` exists for a concrete reason** — the connect address is often an IP literal or a tunnel endpoint no certificate names, and without an override a caller must choose between not validating and not connecting — and an unusable name is refused in `validate()` rather than at the TLS upgrade, which is where it was first put: the test caught it, because the dial fails first and the caller would have been told "connection refused" for a configuration fault.
note: **re-authentication is asymmetric in a way that is easy to get wrong**, and the test pins it: the client opens with AUTH **0x19** and continues with **0x18**, so a client that sent 0x18 first would be continuing an exchange that does not exist; the test asserts the two codes in order *and* that a publish goes through afterwards, because the specification is explicit that other traffic continues during the exchange. A second re-authentication while one is running now **fails the older waiter** rather than leaving it pending forever, because AUTH packets carry no identifier and two concurrent exchanges are indistinguishable on the wire. The authenticator became `Arc<dyn Authenticator>` for it, which is the honest consequence of state that outlives the handshake. And the sheet's named failure mode turned out to be **two** servers: one answers CONNACK 0x8C, one never answers at all — and since the specification gives no deadline for the second, the bound is this client's `connect_timeout` with the test asserting `Error::Timeout("CONNACK")` rather than a hang.
note: the commit also **deletes a tautological assertion the worker had written in B-141** — `assert!(x.is_err() || x.is_ok())`, which asserts nothing — and replaces it with the actual behaviour: an unbracketed IPv6 literal is split on its last colon and parses as host `::1`. Deleting one's own bad test unprompted is the habit LOOP §6 asks for and the rarest one to see.

### B-148 — Interop against `rumqttd`, both roles
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done b6d104c | needs: [B-143, B-145]
acceptance: the client against `rumqttd` started through the process supervisor with a `ready` condition and stopped in the same item on success and on failure alike ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2), over everything that broker implements — QoS 0, 1 and 2, retained messages, the Will, sessions and retransmission — with our client on both sides of the broker so publisher and subscriber roles are both ours; what `rumqttd` 0.20.0 cannot exercise is named in the test file rather than worked around, because its own checklist leaves MQTT 5 unchecked.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b148-mqtt-interop-rumqttd
note: merged `--no-ff` as b6d104c, one commit (ae5deb2), 974 insertions; gate green: **1655 tests**, 0 failed, **13 ignored** (the twelve interop tests plus the 1 GiB memory test), fmt, clippy in every configuration, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **I ran the interop here rather than accepting the report**, and the first attempt failed all twelve with `ConnectionRefused`, which is the honest shape: the file does not start the broker, the operator does, and the doc comment carries the command. So I started `rumqttd` through the process supervisor with a TCP `ready` condition on 21884, ran `cargo test -p weida-mqtt --test interop_rumqttd -- --ignored --test-threads 1` → **12 passed, 0 failed in 3.16 s**, and stopped the broker in the same item. Our client is on **both** sides of the broker in every test.
note: **the item's own premise was wrong and the measurement corrects it in our favour.** `rumqttd` 0.20.0 *does* speak MQTT 5: its `generate-config` enables a `[v5.1]` listener by default, it answers a 5.0 CONNACK **with properties**, declares `Topic Alias Maximum` 4096 and reports Subscription Identifiers back on every delivery. The project's README checklist is out of date, not the build. So three things filed for B-149 are already measured here — topic aliases end to end with the broker resolving ours, Subscription Identifiers ([MQTT-3.3.4-4] honoured, which **no scripted server can prove**), and properties end to end — and B-149 is narrowed accordingly in its own note.
note: **three broker disagreements, each asserted where it happens so a fixed version fails loudly**, and the first is the one to read: a delivery arrives at the **subscription's granted maximum rather than the minimum of that and the publish's QoS**, so a QoS 0 publish into a QoS 2 subscription arrives at **QoS 2** — an upgrade [MQTT-3.8.4-8] forbids in either direction. What proves it is a *pair* of tests, because the downgrade direction is correct: the broker is applying the granted value, not taking a minimum, and either test alone would have looked like conformance. Also: **Retain Handling 2 is ignored** (the option byte does reach the wire, which the codec test asserts, so the omission is the broker's), and **DISCONNECT 0x04 publishes no Will** while the 0x00 half demonstrably works. One build fact for the sheet: `cargo install rumqttd --version 0.20.0 --locked` **does not build on rustc 1.98** — its pinned `metrics` fails borrow checking with E0521 — while without `--locked` it builds in about 30 s; the doc comment carries the working command.
note: **and one finding about our own client, now measured against a real broker rather than a scripted one**: a process that restarts with no persisted session state and reconnects Clean Start 0 finds the broker **still holding its session**, and must close — which is not a corner case but what every restart of a client without a durable store does. The test asserts the refusal by name and then takes the only available answer, Clean Start 1. It is the same [MQTT-3.2.2-4] tension B-145 found, and it is also why the session tests generate a unique Client Identifier per run: a fixed one makes the second run start against state the first left, the same rule biting the test instead of the client. That pair — B-145's literal reading and this restart case — is the material B-153 should carry to the sheet.

### B-149 — MQTT 5 interop against a 5.0 broker
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done d145036 | needs: [B-148]
