acceptance: everything B-148 cannot reach because its broker speaks 3.1.1 — properties end to end, reason codes, Session Expiry, shared subscriptions, topic aliases, Retain Handling and the availability flags — against a broker claiming full 5.0 support (Mosquitto), under the supervisor and stopped in the same item, `#[ignore]` with the install command in the doc comment where it is absent ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2); every disagreement is recorded as measured against a named version rather than inferred.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b149-mqtt-interop-mqtt5
note: **premise corrected by B-148's measurement, and the scope is smaller than filed.** `rumqttd` 0.20.0 speaks 5.0 with properties, so properties end to end, topic aliases end to end and Subscription Identifiers are **already measured** against a real broker in B-148 and must not be re-filed here as if unproven. What genuinely remains for a 5.0 broker: **AUTH**, the availability *flags* as declarations, **shared subscriptions**, **Session Expiry as a timer** rather than as a declaration, and **Retain Handling 1**. `which mosquitto` is empty on this machine, so that half stays `#[ignore]` with the install command per LOOP §2 — and W3 will first try the same route that worked for `rumqttd` and report what it finds rather than assuming the package is the only way in.
note: merged `--no-ff` as d145036, one commit (4959315); gate green: **1668 tests**, 0 failed, **37 ignored**, fmt, clippy in every configuration including `-p weida-mqtt --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **I ran it here**: `rmqttd` started under the process supervisor with a TCP `ready` condition on 21885, `cargo test -p weida-mqtt --test interop_mqtt5 -- --ignored --test-threads 1` → **12 passed, 0 failed in 5.71 s**, broker stopped in the same item. `--test-threads 1` is load-bearing and the worker said so: several tests use a Client Identifier takeover or a shared group and interfere otherwise.
note: **the broker is `rmqtt` 0.23.1 rather than Mosquitto, and the substitution is the right call.** `which mosquitto` is empty and the package is not installed; installing a system package is outside LOOP §2. `rmqtt` is a `cargo install` binary, claims full 5.0 and — the part that decides it — **declares its capabilities in CONNACK**, which is exactly the gap B-148 left open: Maximum QoS 2, Retain Available 1, Receive Maximum 8, Maximum Packet Size 1 MiB, Topic Alias Maximum 16, all three subscription flags, Server Keep Alive 30 s, **every one of them absent from `rumqttd`'s**. The Mosquitto commands stay in the module documentation so the named broker can be measured where it exists. What is now measured end to end: shared subscriptions (two group members plus one ordinary subscriber, one publication, **one** delivery to the group and one to the ordinary subscriber — that count is the only thing separating a group from a filter), topic aliases both ways against a declared 16, **Retain Handling 1** which B-148's broker could not separate from 0, Session Expiry **as a timer** (1 s gone after 1.6 s, 300 s not — the pairing is what makes it a measurement rather than a sleep), properties forwarded unaltered and in order with repeated User Property keys, and the server's Receive Maximum becoming the send quota while the client's own declared 200 is untouched — **B-142's split-quota bug measured against a real declaration**. The refusal half runs against a second listener declaring Maximum QoS 1 and Topic Alias Maximum 0.
note: **three disagreements, each asserted where it happens.** A **retained message is sent to a shared subscription**, which §4.8.2 forbids. A **Client Identifier takeover closes the transport with no DISCONNECT** — no 0x8E, no reason code — which is precisely the 3.1.1 behaviour 5.0 exists to replace, from a broker claiming full 5.0; our client reports it by name rather than hanging, which is what it owes. And **Retain Available and Shared Subscription Available are global rather than per listener** in this broker, because they are plugin state, which is why the refusal tests use Maximum QoS and Topic Alias Maximum instead. **One trap worth the sheet:** with those plugins absent the broker declares 0 for both and our client then refuses a retained publish with 0x9A and a shared subscription with 0x9E **before the wire** — correct on both sides, and indistinguishable from a broken test; the worker hit it and the fixture and documentation now say so.
note: and the **paired build fact** for B-153, which is better than either half alone: `cargo install rmqttd --version 0.23.1` fails **without** `--locked` (a resolved `pulsar` gained a field its egress-bridge plugin does not set), while `cargo install rumqttd --version 0.20.0` fails **with** `--locked` (its pinned `metrics` fails borrow checking on rustc 1.98). Two brokers, two pinning failures, opposite directions. Still unmeasured and **named rather than implied**: AUTH, because neither broker offers an enhanced-authentication mechanism — rmqtt's JWT and HTTP plugins authenticate CONNECT credentials rather than running §4.12's exchange — so 4.12 remains measured only against the scripted server; and Response Information, asked for and offered by neither.

### B-150 — The parity table against MQTT 5.0
kind: spec | size: 60 | status: done c2bc905 | needs: [B-146, B-148]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/mqtt.md` in the shape `docs/libraries/zmq.md` set — every packet type, every property, every subscription option, every reason-code group, the three QoS levels and the transports, each present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason — with the client/broker line of [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) drawn explicitly so a reader learns why there is no server column; no row says "partial" without naming what is missing.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b150-mqtt-parity
note: merged `--no-ff` as c2bc905, one commit (4052368), `docs/libraries/mqtt.md` new at 378 lines plus its index row; **one additive conflict** in the index's Documents table where W4's `amqp.md` and `nats.md` rows met this one, both sides kept. Gate green: **1668 tests**, 0 failed, 37 ignored, fmt, clippy in every configuration, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **13 sections, because it has one the others do not: §0, "Why there is no server column", before §1** — and the way it draws that line is the item's best decision. MQTT's topology is strictly asymmetric (retained storage, the session store, subscription routing, shared-subscription selection and the Will's delay timer are the server's, and a client cannot implement half of them), so the document introduces a **fourth verdict, `server-side`**, used in 11 rows, and §0 states plainly that server-side is *the one verdict a parity table can hide a gap behind* — which is why every such row names what the client **sends** and which interop test measured what the server then did. A row that only said "absent" would be lying in one direction and "present" in the other.
note: **the evidence was checked mechanically rather than by eye, and the check caught five errors in the first draft**: a script resolved every relative link, every `crates/...` path and every named test symbol against the repository, and found three wrong reason-code counts (CONNACK 22 not 17, PUBACK 9 not 7, DISCONNECT 29 not 33) plus **two invented test names**. All five now read off the test that actually asserts the counts. That is the single most valuable habit in any of the five parity documents: a table's numbers are exactly as trustworthy as the tool that checked them.
note: three sections carry findings forward to where a reader meets the field rather than leaving them in this log. **§9.2** states that `Receive Maximum` is two different numbers and that one field serving both dissolves the inbound cap — B-143's bug, written down at the field. **§9.3** says Will Retain cannot be refused at configuration time and why that is structural. **§9.5** is [MQTT-3.2.2-4]: read literally it obliges a correctly-resuming idle client to close, so the document states what this client tracks instead and that a process restarting without a durable store must use Clean Start 1, with the interop test that measures it. §10 puts all five broker disagreements in one table with the specification sentence beside the measurement, the two opposite `cargo install` pinning failures, and the rmqtt plugin trap — and says plainly that **AUTH is measured only against our own scripted server**, a weaker claim than a foreign one, stated rather than implied.
note: **correction, filed against my own note above:** I told W3 its row ordering held through the conflict resolution, and it did not — my resolution appended MQTT after `nats.md`, in merge order, while W3 had placed it between `nng.md` and `amqp.md`, in the order LOOP §9's Phase B builds the protocols (ZMTP, SP, MQTT, AMQP, NATS). W3 checked its tips against main rather than taking my word and found the difference. The content was identical to the character, so nothing was lost, but the claim was wrong and the ordering intent was the better one: a reader should meet the documents in the sequence the roadmap introduces them, not in the sequence the merges happened to land. Restored on merging B-153.

### B-151 — `weida-mqtt-py`: the asyncio surface
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 6acdecd | needs: [B-111, B-148]
acceptance: `crates/mqtt/weida-mqtt-py` on `weida-py-core`: connect, publish, subscribe and an async iterator of deliveries carrying topic, QoS, the retain flag, the properties and the subscription identifiers, with reason codes as distinct exception classes; a QoS 2 publish awaited to its PUBCOMP from Python, and the whole round trip run against the broker B-148 supervises.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b151-mqtt-py-asyncio
note: merged `--no-ff` as 6acdecd, one commit (3b19026); gate green at **1668 tests**, 0 failed, 37 ignored — **after one re-run**, because the first pass hit B-180's `drain` flake again (`drain.rs:382`), which is filed and not this item's. fmt, clippy in every configuration including `-p weida-mqtt-py --features extension-module`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **Both Python proofs run here:** `develop.sh` → **33 passed** with `rumqttd` under the supervisor, and `package.sh` built `weida_mqtt-0.1.0-cp39-abi3-manylinux_2_34_x86_64.whl`, installed it into a fresh virtualenv with `PATH` scrubbed to `/usr/bin:/bin` and ran `smoke.py` — "the wheel needs no Rust toolchain" established rather than expected. Broker stopped in the same item. The acceptance clause is asserted the way it should be: `Completion.kind == "complete"` is the PUBCOMP and nothing else, with all three kinds asserted side by side so the string separates a binding that resolved on the write ("sent") from one that resolved on the PUBREC ("acknowledged") — which is why `Completion` is a class rather than a boolean.
note: **a sixth broker disagreement, found by Python and by the error variant B-144 added.** `rumqttd` 0.20.0's UNSUBACK carries **one** reason code however many filters the UNSUBSCRIBE named, and none at all where nothing was removed, while [MQTT-3.11.3-1] requires one per filter in order. Position is the only thing binding a code to a filter, so the count is **unreadable rather than merely odd**, and `AcknowledgementLengthMismatch` fired instead of zipping short and telling the caller the second filter had been removed with the first filter's code. The test asserts both the mismatch and that one filter per UNSUBSCRIBE interoperates. W3 correctly did not edit the merged `docs/libraries/mqtt.md`; **it belongs in §10's table and in B-153, and both now have it** (see B-153's note).
note: five design notes worth keeping. **Reason codes as classes, one per name rather than per packet**: 36 coded classes plus 15 for the failures MQTT gives no byte, all under `MqttError`, each carrying `.reason_code`, so `except NotAuthorized` catches 0x87 wherever it arrived — with two **exhaustive matches** over the codec's enums, so a code added to the library is a compile error rather than a silent base class. **`weida-py-core` caught a real bug at import**: `ProtocolError` was in both lists (the 0x82 code and the name for `Error::Protocol`), the foundation refused the duplicate, and the fix is better than the draft — `Error::Protocol(e)` now maps onto the DISCONNECT code name of `e.reason_code()`, so the class a caller catches is the code this client would have sent, decided once in the codec. **`Vec<u8>` is `bytes` in PyO3**, so the SUBACK/UNSUBACK codes needed a newtype: bytes on the wire, integers in Python, and the newtype is where the two part company. **TLS is off deliberately** — the trust anchors are the caller's `rustls::ClientConfig` and this surface cannot yet accept one, so linking the stack would be linking it for nothing; `is_encrypted` is always `False` **and says so** rather than being absent, which is a row for the future `mqtt-py.md`. And `Event` is `#[non_exhaustive]`, so the async iterator **skips an unknown variant rather than guessing it is a delivery**.

### B-152 — `weida-mqtt-py`: the sync surface
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done 2a69e8e | needs: [B-151]
acceptance: the synchronous Python surface over the same client, no asyncio loop required in the process and no second implementation of any protocol behaviour, with a receive timeout where the async surface has cancellation; B-151's QoS 2 round trip runs unchanged in synchronous form.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b152-mqtt-py-sync
note: merged in **two** commits, because the first gate was **red**: `959ba08` merged `--no-ff` as 29e3fef, then `RUSTDOCFLAGS="-D warnings" cargo doc --workspace --no-deps` failed with "public documentation for `weida_mqtt_py` links to private item `sync`" (`lib.rs:54` linking `[`sync`]` against `mod sync;` at line 100). I did not revert; I reported the error line and left the item `in_progress`, W3 reproduced it after `cargo clean -p weida-mqtt-py` and the fix merged as 2a69e8e. **The cause is worth more than the fix: rustdoc's incremental cache made a doc-only change invisible, so W3's run in its worktree was silent against a stale artifact.** The clean run then found a **second** break nobody's gate covered — `[`TlsOptions`]` in `transport.rs`'s module doc, a `#[cfg(feature = "tls")]` type, whose intra-doc link cannot resolve with the feature off, shipped in B-147 and invisible to every default-features run. Filed as B-184.
note: gate green on 2a69e8e from a **cleaned** target (8.1 GiB removed here): **1673 tests**, 0 failed, 37 ignored; fmt; clippy workspace, `-p weida --no-default-features`, `-p weida-mqtt --all-features`; and rustdoc with `-D warnings` in **three** configurations — `--workspace --no-deps`, `-p weida-mqtt --no-default-features`, `-p weida-mqtt --all-features`. Python ran here: `develop.sh` → **45 passed** with `rumqttd` under the supervisor, `package.sh` built the abi3 wheel and ran the extended `smoke.py` from a scrubbed `PATH`; broker stopped in the same item. The crate gained an off-by-default **`blocking`** feature, like `weida-zmq`'s.
note: **the acceptance clause "no second implementation of any protocol behaviour" is what decided the design, and it pushed the facade out of the binding and into the library.** `weida-mqtt/src/blocking.rs` is the synchronous MQTT — one `block_on` per method over the async client — and `weida_mqtt.sync` is argument conversion around it, so the QoS machines, session, send quota, aliases, keep-alive and every refusal are decided exactly once and the library gains a synchronous surface too. `Message`, `Subscription`, `ConnectOptions`, `Session`, `Delivery` and `Completion` are the **same classes** in both Python surfaces; only the three handles differ, because only they do.
note: four findings. **The future is polled on the calling thread** (`futures::executor::block_on`), as `weida-zmq`'s facade does and for the same reason — the reactor's threads drive the connection, so a caller parking one would wait for a worker waiting for it; `two_threads_each_block_on_their_own_connection` proves it with `worker_threads=1`, where a facade using the reactor's own `block_on` would deadlock. **`futures::executor::block_on` has no timer**: the first `recv_timeout` used `tokio::time::timeout` and panicked with "there is no reactor running", so `Deliveries` now carries the `Exec` and the deadline goes through `Exec::within`, which enters the runtime's handle before creating the sleep — the timer is the runtime's, which is what the doc comment already claimed. **Every test in `tests/blocking.rs` is a plain `#[test]`**, and that is the point: a `#[tokio::test]` runs *inside* a runtime, so a facade that secretly needed an ambient one would pass; the first test asserts `Handle::try_current().is_err()` before touching anything. And **`publish` deliberately has no timeout**, asserted rather than documented — `test_publish_has_no_timeout_argument_and_the_omission_is_deliberate` expects a `TypeError`, because a publish abandoned mid-exchange is still session state on both sides and an argument named `timeout` would promise otherwise. The GIL claim is a **count**, not a comment: a second thread increments a counter while the first blocks in `recv`, asserting it ran more than ten times.

### B-153 — Research: what the MQTT implementation taught the sheet
kind: research | size: 45 | status: done 5b0f8d2 | needs: [B-149]
acceptance: [research/mqtt5.md](research/mqtt5.md) updated with what building the client and running it against two brokers established, in the sheet's own protocol-native vocabulary and with no sentence mentioning weida ([research/README.md](research/README.md) rule 1), every addition attributed to a new numbered source naming the broker versions and what was observed; §13's incompatibility list gains what the runs measured, and the specification's two internal inconsistencies are marked confirmed or not by an implementation that had to choose between them.
note: workstream W3, worktree ../weida-w3, branch b153-mqtt-sheet-update
note: **the material this item must carry is now six broker disagreements, two specification tensions and two build facts, and they are all measured.** The disagreements, each with its assertion already in the tree: (1) a delivery arrives at the subscription's **granted maximum rather than the minimum** of that and the publish's QoS, so QoS 0 into a QoS 2 subscription is *upgraded* — `rumqttd` 0.20.0, B-148, proved by a **pair** of tests because the downgrade direction is correct; (2) **Retain Handling 2 is ignored**, same broker, with the codec test showing the option byte does reach the wire; (3) **DISCONNECT 0x04 publishes no Will** while 0x00 works, same broker; (4) a **retained message is delivered to a shared subscription**, which §4.8.2 forbids — `rmqtt` 0.23.1, B-149; (5) a **Client Identifier takeover closes the transport with no DISCONNECT and no reason code**, same broker, which is the 3.1.1 behaviour 5.0 exists to replace; and (6) **UNSUBACK carries one reason code however many filters the UNSUBSCRIBE named**, and none where nothing was removed, against [MQTT-3.11.3-1]'s one-per-filter-in-order — `rumqttd` 0.20.0, found from Python in B-151. Since position is the only binding between a code and a filter, (6) is *unreadable* rather than untidy, which is why the client reports a length mismatch instead of zipping short.
note: the two **specification tensions** an implementation had to choose between, both from this run: **[MQTT-3.2.2-4] read literally obliges a correctly-resuming idle client to close** (B-145 found it against a scripted server, B-148 measured it against a real broker as the ordinary case of a process restarting without a durable store), because §4.1's client-side session-state list omits "whether a session exists on the server at all"; and **`Receive Maximum` is two different numbers** (§3.2.2.3.3 the server's, §3.1.2.11.3 the client's own) which B-143 found by having one field serve both and dissolving the inbound cap. Plus the **two build facts**, which are better paired than separate: `cargo install rmqttd --version 0.23.1` fails **without** `--locked` while `cargo install rumqttd --version 0.20.0` fails **with** it. And the two absences to state rather than imply: **AUTH is measured only against our own scripted server** because neither broker offers a mechanism, and **Response Information** is offered by neither.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 5b0f8d2, one commit (c8c1499) stacked on the B-152 rustdoc fix so the merge carried both: `docs/research/mqtt5.md` **+87 −4**, one file, docs-only. Gate green here: **1673 tests**, 0 failed, 37 ignored, fmt, clippy workspace, rustdoc with `-D warnings` both `--workspace` and `-p weida-mqtt --no-default-features`. **I checked the sheet's two rules by counting rather than reading:** zero occurrences of "weida" in the file ([research/README.md](research/README.md) rule 1), and the source list runs **1..66 with no gaps** and nothing cited-but-undeclared — the two new records, [65] `rumqttd` 0.20.0 across thirteen scenarios and [66] `rmqtt` 0.23.1 across twelve, each naming version, date, platform, rustc, transport and configuration. I also read §13's `rumqttd` row and §§848-864 rather than accepting the summary.
note: **the clause I most wanted to land is landed: both of the specification's internal inconsistencies are now marked confirmed by an implementation that had to choose.** Appendix B's [MQTT-3.1.3-1] payload order is not merely non-normative but **unusable** — a CONNECT built to it puts the Will Topic where the Will Properties' length byte belongs and cannot be parsed, while both brokers accept the normative body's order and answer 0x00 with all four Will properties. And Appendix B's inverted Retain Handling 1 row predicts the opposite order of events from what `rmqtt` does (first subscribe delivers, second does not). "Both are appendix errata" is a stronger result than "the body is authoritative", and only writing a client could produce it. Two further tensions are recorded as **found by writing a client rather than by reading**: [MQTT-3.2.2-4] against §4.1's enumeration, and `Receive Maximum` as two independent numbers eleven pages apart.
note: §13's Rust table now says `rumqttd` 0.20.0 speaks **"3.1.1 and 5"** with the project's own checklist quoted *and* marked out of date — flagged in the row as the one place in the sheet where a measurement overrides a project's documentation, which is exactly the honesty a reader needs to check it. `rmqtt` 0.23.1 gains a row as the only broker in the table that **declares** a full capability set, with the plugin-versus-listener scope and the startup panic on a missing plugin config noted, and the paragraph claiming no pure-Rust MQTT 5 broker now names two — neither offering AUTH, so **4.12 has no pure-Rust peer at all**. §5 and §8 gained measured notes where a reader meets the rule rather than only in §13, including that §4.8.2's "retained messages are never sent to shared subscriptions" is not honoured by `rmqtt` and a client cannot rely on the exclusion.
note: **W3 closes here: B-062 + B-139..B-153, sixteen items** — the MQTT 5 codec, the client, two interop harnesses against two real brokers, the parity table, both Python surfaces and this sheet update. Its `weida-mqtt-py` parity document is not filed; see B-183's shape for the equivalent gap on `weida-nng-py`.

### B-154 — `weida-amqp-codec`: the AMQP 1.0 type system
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 155aef5 | needs: []
acceptance: `crates/amqp/weida-amqp-codec` with an **empty `[dependencies]`**: the Part 1 primitive types with their format codes in fixed and variable width, described types, composite types, arrays, lists and maps, decoded from every form the specification permits and encoded in one canonical form; golden vectors from the specification's own examples, a fuzz target, and nothing allocated before a declared size or element count is checked against a caller-supplied bound — a list header declares its count, which is remote input.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b154-amqp-types
note: merged `--no-ff` as 155aef5, one commit (806e55d), 3770 insertions; **two conflicts, both additive, both sides kept** — the workspace member list (W1's `weida-py-core` block met this crate) and the workspace dependency list, with the member line placed beside the other protocol families rather than after the PyO3 block so 0013 §4.1's grouping still reads. Gate green: **870 tests** (815 + 55), 0 failed, 2 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The empty `[dependencies]` is real and `cargo tree -p weida-amqp-codec` prints exactly one line, the crate itself — not even `weida-core`, which the manifest comment says in prose and the tree now proves.
note: the "nothing allocated before a declared count is checked" half is the reason this item exists, and it is built the way INVARIANTS wants rather than with a constant: `Limits { max_elements, max_depth }` is an **argument to every decode entry point**, the declared count is refused above `max_elements` before any element is looked at, and `reserve_bounded` additionally clamps the reservation to the octet region the header itself declared — so a nine-octet `list32` announcing four billion elements cannot make the decoder reserve more than the nine octets can hold. Depth is bounded separately because nesting spends the call stack rather than the heap. Both defaults are justified against the specification's own widest composite (`attach`, fourteen fields; `unsettled`, the one map Part 2 §2.6.3 leaves unbounded).
note: the golden vectors are checked by a test rather than counted by a reader: `the_table_covers_every_encoding_the_specification_defines` asserts 39 rows *and* cross-checks the table's format codes against the crate's own code list, so a row added without an encoding fails too. Figure 1.19 decodes field by field and re-encodes without its optional trailing null, and Figure 1.2 is **refused** because its string descriptor is reserved — a published example that a lenient codec would have accepted.

### B-155 — The frame header and the nine performatives
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 4b1d54c | needs: [B-154]
acceptance: the 8-byte frame header (`SIZE`, `DOFF`, `TYPE`, two type-specific bytes) with `SIZE` < 8 or `DOFF` < 2 malformed and the extended header skipped, AMQP (`0x00`) and SASL (`0x01`) frames distinguished, the 8-octet protocol headers for `%d0`, `%d2` and `%d3`, and all nine performatives — `open` `0x10` through `close` `0x18` — plus the SASL bodies encoded and decoded as described types; a golden vector per performative, the fuzz target extended, and the 512-octet pre-negotiation minimum honoured as the bound before `open` has been read.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b155-amqp-performatives
note: merged `--no-ff` as 4b1d54c, one commit (5ee2aca), 4847 insertions, no conflicts (the crate was already a workspace member, so this branch touched no shared manifest). Gate green: **1076 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`; `cargo tree -p weida-amqp-codec` still prints exactly one node. The pre-negotiation bound is the clause I checked in the code: `MIN_MAX_FRAME_SIZE` = 512 is both the initial ceiling *and* the permanent floor, and `decode_header` computes `max_frame_size.max(MIN_MAX_FRAME_SIZE)` — so a peer pipelining a four-gigabyte frame ahead of its `open` is refused on eight octets, and a caller **cannot make the check stricter than the specification allows** by advertising less than 512. `frame::write` refuses an over-large frame and writes **nothing** when it does, which the test asserts rather than assuming.
note: one design choice worth recording: `protocol_header` **reports** the version rather than judging it, so a `%d3` answer to a `%d0` request is readable as the peer demanding a security layer instead of arriving as an opaque error — which is exactly what B-157's negotiation needs to say honestly. Fuzz targets went from two to four (value, composite, frame, performative) and the deterministic `fuzz_smoke` suite from three tests to six; the fuzz crate is its own workspace, as `weida-zmtp`'s is, so `--workspace` does not build it and it needs nightly.

### B-156 — The message sections and the delivery states
kind: adapter | size: 60 | status: done acbf50e | needs: [B-155]
acceptance: header, delivery-annotations, message-annotations, properties, application-properties, the three body forms (`data`, `amqp-sequence`, `amqp-value`) and footer encoded and decoded in the order the specification fixes, plus the terminal delivery states `accepted`, `rejected` with its error, `released` and `modified` with its flags; a multi-frame message is reassembled from its transfer fragments and bounded before allocation by a caller-supplied `max-message-size`.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b156-amqp-message
note: merged `--no-ff` as acbf50e, one commit (a4b3eed), 1897 insertions, no conflicts; gate green: **1180 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The reassembly bound is the clause I read: `Reassembly::new(max_message_size)` takes it as a constructor argument in the same shape as `Limits`, `accept` compares `already + fragment` against it **before the buffer grows by a single octet**, and `max_message_size_is_checked_before_the_buffer_grows` asserts the refused fragment was not appended. Zero means no limit, which is what the field's own default means — and a caller passing zero has said so rather than omitted something.
note: two decisions worth keeping. The section order of Part 3 §3.2 is **enforced** rather than tolerated: out-of-order, duplicate and mixed body sections are each their own error, because a decoder that sorted them would be accepting a message whose bare part it could not have hashed. And `Decoded::bare` reports the bare message as a **slice of the input** rather than as a re-encoding, for the same reason — a footer signature covers the exact octets that arrived, so handing back a canonical re-encoding would quietly break every signature check built on it. `modified`'s three fields stay `Option` so "unset" and "false" do not collapse, and `increments_delivery_count` is exposed as the rule a redelivery limit counts on.

### B-157 — The client: version negotiation, SASL, TLS and `open`
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 1af03f5 | needs: [B-155]
acceptance: `crates/amqp/weida-amqp` on `weida-runtime`: the 8-octet header sent immediately on connect and the partner's answer read as its demand — a `%d3` reply to a `%d0` request means a security layer is mandatory, and the client reports that rather than retrying blind — the SASL dialog with ANONYMOUS and PLAIN inside 512-octet frames and all five outcome codes, TLS layered by `%d2` or reached as a bare `amqps` listener, then `open` on channel 0 with `container-id`, `max-frame-size`, `channel-max` and `idle-time-out`; the idle timeout is answered with an empty frame at half the advertised interval, and `close` is the last thing ever written.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b157-amqp-connection
note: merged `--no-ff` as 1af03f5, one commit (f10cf2b), **one additive manifest conflict** against W3's `weida-mqtt` line, both kept; gate green: **1279 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. This item introduces a **default-on `tls` feature**, so I ran the configuration the standing gate does not cover: `clippy -p weida-amqp --no-default-features --all-targets -- -D warnings` is clean and `cargo test -p weida-amqp --no-default-features` passes **30 tests** with the one TLS integration test `cfg`'d out. `cargo tree -p weida-amqp` reaches neither `weida` nor `weida-protocol`, and the new `tokio-rustls` rides the rustls 0.23 already in the graph through quinn rather than adding a second TLS stack.
note: the decision I would have wanted argued and which is argued: **the trust anchors are not in the crate**. `Connection::connect_tls` takes the caller's `rustls::ClientConfig`, because which certificates an application trusts is the application's decision and a library that picks for it is a library that cannot be audited. The TLS test generates its own certificate with `rcgen` and completes a **real** handshake rather than asserting a type. Part 2 §2.2's four outcomes are four separate tests and four separate error variants — `%d3` to a `%d0` is `SecurityLayerRequired` and is **never retried**, `AMQP 0 0 9 1` is `VersionMismatch`, non-header octets are `BadProtocolHeader`, and silence is our own `HandshakeTimeout` because the protocol gives no deadline. The idle timeout is **two independent clocks**, ours-to-send emitting an empty frame at half the peer's interval and ours-to-enforce closing with `amqp:connection:forced` and an explanation, with zero-means-unset folded into `None` so no caller re-derives it.
note: three defaults deliberately differ from the specification's and each is a named constant with its reason — max-frame-size 128 KiB against "no limit", idle-time-out 60 s against "unset", channel-max 255 against 65535 — and the bounds are split the right way: the session table by the protocol's own `channel-max`, and **ours where the protocol has none**, namely `connection::OUTGOING_QUEUE`, `sasl::MAX_ROUNDS` for the "zero or more" challenge loop Part 5 leaves unbounded, and `max_resolved_addresses` for the resolver's answer. A mechanism the server did not offer is refused rather than downgraded, which is the SASL half of the same rule.

### B-158 — The session: `begin`, `end` and the session windows
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 1a58656 | needs: [B-157]
acceptance: a session begun on the lowest free outgoing channel and answered with `remote-channel`, the two directions numbered independently, the six flow-control variables maintained so the peer's `incoming-window` is never exceeded, `end` with and without an error, and the `DISCARDING` state silently discarding input until the partner's `end` instead of acting on it; a test drives the window to zero and asserts the sender stalls rather than overruns, and a frame on an unmapped channel ends the session with the error the specification names.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b158-amqp-session
note: merged `--no-ff` as 1a58656, one commit (8e81faf), no conflicts; gate green: **1353 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations **and** `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, whose test run is green too, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. The stall test is the one the acceptance asked for and it asserts the part that matters: `the_window_drives_to_zero_and_the_sender_stalls_rather_than_overruns` walks a window of 3 down to 0, asserts `may_send` goes false, asserts `record_sent` refuses **and** that `next_outgoing_id` did not advance — so a caller who ignored `may_send` still cannot overrun. `window.rs` holds all six flow-control variables of Part 2 §2.5.6 as one type including **both branches** of the recomputation formula, and the fallback branch is the one that decides whether a session can send its first frame at all; transfer-ids are RFC 1982 serial numbers with a test transferring through the wrap at 2^32.
note: **one session carries two channel numbers, not one**, and the driver keeps two maps rather than assuming they agree — the answering `begin` arrives on the peer's outgoing channel and names ours in `remote-channel`. The test is concrete about it: the client begins on 0 and 1, the server answers on 4 and 9, and `Session::incoming_channel` reports the peer's. `DISCARDING` is a state value rather than a comment, asserted by ending with an error, having the server send a `flow`, and checking the window **did not move**.
note: **the judgement call the worker asked me to confirm, and I confirm it.** A channel *above* our advertised `channel-max` is `amqp:connection:framing-error`, which Part 2 §2.7.1 names. A channel *inside* the range that no session was begun on is named nowhere in the text — §2.7.1 covers only the out-of-range case — so the client closes with `amqp:not-allowed` and puts the channel number in the description, with `session::UNMAPPED_CHANNEL_CONDITION` documenting that the condition is **ours**. That is the right treatment: a defensible reading, marked as a deviation rather than quoted as a rule, and it goes in the parity table as one. The bounds are also split correctly, which is the subtle half: links per session by the protocol's own `handle-max` (advertised as 255 rather than the specification's 2^32), the session table by the **peer's** `channel-max` rather than ours — because that is the number that says which channels the peer will accept, with a test showing a peer advertising 0 refusing the second `begin` with `amqp:resource-limit-exceeded` **before it reaches the wire** — the incoming window at 400, and `SESSION_QUEUE` = 64 as ours for the frames the specification leaves entirely outside any window.

### B-159 — The link: `attach`, `detach`, termini and the settle modes
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 3e905ec | needs: [B-158]
acceptance: links attached in both directions with a name unique among the links of that direction between the two containers, `source` and `target` termini carrying `address`, `durable`, `expiry-policy`, `timeout` and `dynamic`, handles allocated lowest-free with an `attach` on a handle in use answered by `close` carrying `amqp:session:handle-in-use`, `snd-settle-mode` and `rcv-settle-mode` negotiated, and an errored link endpoint detached with its error and its handle never silently reused; a second attach of the same link name steals it and the loser is closed with `amqp:link:stolen`.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b159-amqp-link
note: merged `--no-ff` as 3e905ec, one commit (21e21f6), no conflicts; gate green: **1437 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `link.rs`, `terminus.rs` and `owned.rs`: attach and detach, both termini with their five fields, the **two independent handle spaces**, the steal answered with `amqp:link:stolen`, `handle-max` as the link table's bound and an errored handle **poisoned for the life of the session** rather than recycled — which is the clause most easily got wrong, because reuse is invisible until two links disagree about which one a `disposition` meant. Nine integration tests against a scripted server.
note: **one thing landed that the item did not name, and it is ungated: the commit also carries 2,824 lines of `crates/nats/weida-nats`** — error, options, message, request, subject, subscription and transport — while `[workspace] members` still lists only `weida-nats-codec`. So the NATS client is **in the tree and invisible to the gate**: `cargo test --workspace` never compiles it, and the 1437 tests above do not include a line of it. The worker's own note says the member line is held back in its worktree for B-165. I merged rather than rejecting the commit, because the AMQP half is the item and is complete, but this is recorded as the risk it is: **until B-165 lands with its member line, that directory is unproved code**, and the first thing B-165's merge must do is make the gate see it. If B-165 does not arrive, the directory is to be removed rather than left as a monument.
note: **closed, and closed the right way**: W4 chose to delete rather than to attach a member line, in `chore(nats): drop the ungated in-progress client` (merged here as eaa5c66, 2871 lines removed, gate green at 1456 tests with `crates/nats/` back to the codec alone). The reason it gave is the one that decides it: `cargo build -p weida-nats` had **never been run once** — nine files written, `tests/` empty, breakage expected in `connection.rs` — so a member line would have attached the gate to code that could not pass it, which is the same hole with a label on it. The work is not lost; it continues on `b165-nats-client` in a **separate worktree** with its own `CARGO_TARGET_DIR`, and that is also the fix for the root cause: the crate no longer exists in `../weida-w4`, so no `git add -A` can reach it and nobody has to toggle a member line in and out of the manifest around each gate run, which is what produced the hole. B-165 will arrive as one commit carrying the crate **and** its member line, gated together.

### B-160 — `transfer` and link credit
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4459034 | needs: [B-156, B-159]
acceptance: link credit counted in **messages** and granted only by the receiver through `flow`, with `delivery-count` and `link-credit` maintained at both ends, `drain` honoured and `echo` answered; a sender with no credit stalls instead of sending, transfers with `more` set reassemble into one delivery that never interleaves with another on the same link, `max-message-size` is refused before allocation, and the session's transfer window applies **on top of** the link's credit, because the two schemes are independent and simultaneously active.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b160-amqp-transfer-credit
note: merged `--no-ff` as 4459034, one commit (1449d5d), 2848 insertions against 44 deletions; gate green: **1599 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `credit.rs` holds the four variables of Part 2 §2.6.7 with the absolute-limit arithmetic in RFC 1982 serial numbers and `drain`, 14 unit tests; `delivery.rs` holds `Delivery` and `Sent`; and the acceptance's hardest clause — **the two flow-control schemes are independent and simultaneously active** — is two tests rather than a sentence: a sender with **four units of link credit and a one-frame session window sends nothing** until a `flow` refreshes the window, and a 1600-octet message through a 512-octet frame costs **one unit of credit and four window units**. That is the pair I would have asked for.
note: **the bug its tests found is a scheduling bug with a wire consequence**: the driver's biased `select` preferred the command channel over the outbound queue, so `close` **overtook a transfer a sender had already completed** and the frame was dropped as having raced the close. "`close` MUST be the last frame ever written" makes it last; it is not a licence to discard what was queued before it — the Close arm now flushes the outbound queue first. A rule about ordering read as a rule about precedence is a mistake worth naming, because the code looked correct either way.
note: one deliberate choice where the specification offers two, recorded as a choice: **a delivery arriving past the credit this end had granted is handled and logged rather than answered with `amqp:link:transfer-limit-exceeded`**, because the sender sent under credit we granted and then withdrew, and dropping it would lose a message to our own race. I accept it — a receiver that punishes a peer for its own withdrawal is a receiver that loses messages on every credit reduction — and it belongs in B-163's parity table as a stated deviation. One codec addition rode along: `Reassembly::message_format()`, because the format arrives on the first frame and the frame that completes a delivery legitimately carries none. W4 also reports one full-workspace run printing "error: 1 target failed" with **no failing test and no failing result**, clean on three subsequent runs; it did not claim it away and neither do I — it matches the load-sensitive shape of B-180.

### B-161 — Dispositions, settlement and the unsettled map
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done dc86111 | needs: [B-160]
acceptance: an unsettled map at each end with settlement idempotent and one-way, `disposition` over ranges of delivery-ids carrying `accepted`, `rejected`, `released` or `modified`, and each settle-mode combination behaving as specified — settled on send, settled on disposition, and the `second` dance where the receiver waits for the sender's settlement; each test asserts what the mode *proves* to the application (the message is durable-or-not, the peer will or will not redeliver) rather than that a frame arrived.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b161-amqp-disposition
note: merged `--no-ff` as dc86111, one commit (1a53840), 2261 insertions against 9 deletions; gate green: **1641 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `settlement.rs` holds the owned `Outcome` with **`received` deliberately excluded — it is a progress report, not an answer** — the delivery-id-keyed unsettled map with serial-arithmetic ranges, and 11 unit tests; the link gains `accept`/`reject`/`release`/`modify`, a `settle(first, last, outcome)` that covers a range in one frame, and `unsettled()`/`settled(id)`. Every one of the 13 integration tests asserts **what the exchange proves** rather than that a frame arrived: accepted and rejected do not come back, released and modified do, rejected and modified-with-delivery-failed count against a redelivery limit while accepted and released do not, and settled-on-send makes `settled(id)` an **error with the reason in the message**, because an application that could wait for that answer would wait forever.
note: three judgement calls, all three right and all three argued. **The `second` dance is driven by the driver rather than the application**: once a receiver publishes an outcome without settling, the sender owes a settling disposition and nothing is left to decide, so exposing a call would only invent a way to deadlock. **A settling disposition may carry no state at all** — it confirms what was already said — so `record_theirs` falls back to the outcome *this* end published; without that fallback the receiver's own `accepted` would be lost at the very moment it became final. And the **unsettled map is bounded only on the sender's side** (`DEFAULT_MAX_UNSETTLED`, 2048), with the module saying why: a receiver's map cannot grow except against credit it granted itself, so the protocol already bounds it, while a receiver that keeps granting credit and settles nothing would grow a *sender's* map without limit. That is the asymmetry argued from who controls the growth, which is the reasoning B-181 found missing elsewhere.

### B-162 — Interop against `fe2o3-amqp` and RabbitMQ
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 430f30a | needs: [B-161]
acceptance: sender and receiver roles against `fe2o3-amqp` as the pure-Rust peer that always runs, plus RabbitMQ 4.x — which speaks 1.0 natively on 5672 — under the process supervisor and `#[ignore]` with the reason where it is absent ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2); the four things the sheet says implementations refuse are probed rather than assumed — `rcv-settle-mode=second`, transactions, link resumption and terminus expiry — and each outcome, including the address syntax each peer demanded, is recorded as measured against a named version.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b162-amqp-interop
note: merged `--no-ff` as 430f30a, one commit (5c2814e), 1740 insertions against 15 deletions; gate green: **1665 tests**, 0 failed, **17 ignored**, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `fe2o3-amqp` 0.17 is a **dev-dependency with only its `acceptor` feature**, so the foreign peer always runs: 8 tests, nothing ignored, no broker and no C toolchain, and `cargo tree -p weida-amqp` still reaches neither `weida` nor `weida-protocol` and pulls no second TLS stack. RabbitMQ is genuinely absent here — `which rabbitmq-server`, docker and podman all empty — so its 4 tests are `#[ignore]`d behind one `needs_rabbitmq!()` macro carrying install commands for Arch, Debian and docker, with the harness starting and stopping the broker itself on its own port and waiting for the socket. **The module documentation says in those words that nothing in that file has been observed**, so B-163's table will record the RabbitMQ column as *not run* rather than as compatible. That is the distinction most interop documents lose.
note: **interop found three real bugs in this client, which is what interop is for.** (1) We **never answered a peer-initiated `detach`** — a link endpoint is destroyed only when both ends detach, and `fe2o3`'s `close()` does not return until the answer lands, so a peer whose application held the link waited forever; the fix answers with the peer's own `closed` flag and none of our error. (2) A `flow` for a handle whose link had just been cleanly detached ended the **session** with `amqp:session:unattached-handle` — that frame is a *race* with the detach, not a fault, and `fe2o3` really sends one; cleanly detached handles are now remembered and their frames discarded, while a handle detached **with an error** correctly draws `amqp:session:errant-link`, which Part 2 §2.6.5 names and which was not being distinguished at all. (3) A client that detached and immediately re-attached the same name **stole its own just-detached link**, sending `amqp:link:stolen` for a link the application itself closed and poisoning the handle. All three have deterministic scripted regression tests beside the interop ones, and one existing test was **strengthened** rather than adjusted: `an_errored_detach_poisons_its_handle` now reads the answering detach first, which asserts presence where it used to encode absence.
note: four measurements for B-163's table, each against **`fe2o3-amqp` 0.17.0 by name**: `rcv-settle-mode=second` is **honoured** by this peer, so the sheet's "implementations refuse it" is *not universal*; a terminus `expiry-policy=never` with `timeout=600` comes back **echoed rather than substituted**; a duplicate link name is **refused rather than stolen**, so this peer does not implement Part 2 §2.6.1's steal; and transactions are absent by construction on our side, because Part 4's target is a `coordinator` while `LinkOptions::target` is typed as a `Target` — the probe establishes the other half, that **no `amqp:txn:` capability is claimed by either end**. Four facts, each with a version attached, is what makes a parity row defensible.

### B-163 — The parity table against the OASIS core
kind: spec | size: 60 | status: done ff8031a | needs: [B-162]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/amqp.md` in the shape `docs/libraries/zmq.md` set — every performative, every message section, both credit schemes, the settle modes and delivery states, the SASL mechanisms and the transports, each present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason — with Part 4 transactions, link resumption and the three coexisting filter families named absent and the evidence for why, and the client/broker line of [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) stated once; no row says "partial" without naming what is missing.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b163-amqp-parity
note: merged `--no-ff` as ff8031a, one commit (6e3bb39): `docs/libraries/amqp.md` new at 301 lines, its index row, **and 38 lines of `link.rs`** — so this is not a docs-only item and the gate ran: **1668 tests**, 0 failed, 17 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. Counted here: **12 sections**, and the two occurrences of "partial" are both legitimate — one is the definition-of-done row quoting the rule, the other describes `received` as the state that "describes how far a **partial** body got". No row claims partial.
note: **the table found a claim the code had not implemented, which is exactly what a parity table is for.** `settlement.rs` *documented* that `send` waits at `DEFAULT_MAX_UNSETTLED`, and `take_credit` did not check it — the doc was written before the code was true. Fixed in this same commit: `take_credit` now takes the settled flag and requires room in the map for an **unsettled** delivery, since a settled one is recorded nowhere. That is the second time in this run that writing a document about the code corrected the code (B-093's option count was the first), and it is the argument for the parity table being a deliverable rather than a courtesy.
note: two rows I read closely because they are where such a table usually lies. **One row states what is *not* enforced rather than claiming presence**: Part 3 restricts application-properties *values* to simple types, this decoder checks only that the section is a map, so a nested value is carried rather than refused — the only "present with a hole" row in the document, and it names the hole. And the **transactions row is absent by construction rather than by refusal**: Part 4's target is a `coordinator`, a different described type, and `LinkOptions::target` is typed as a `Target`, so no value of the API can reach one — with the further fact that **Part 6, Distributed Transactions, was never published**, so one of the four capability names denotes nothing. The RabbitMQ column is recorded as **not run** in four places rather than as compatible, which is what B-162's unobserved file requires.

### B-164 — `weida-nats-codec`: the text protocol
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 9b9eb36 | needs: []
acceptance: `crates/nats/weida-nats-codec` with an **empty `[dependencies]`**: `INFO`, `CONNECT`, `PUB`, `HPUB`, `SUB`, `UNSUB`, `MSG`, `HMSG`, `PING`, `PONG`, `+OK` and `-ERR` encoded and decoded, CRLF-delimited with the declared byte count read before the payload and the payload never scanned for a terminator, the `NATS/1.0` header block with repeated names and preserved case, and a caller-supplied `max_payload` checked from the declared count before any allocation; a golden vector per verb and a fuzz target.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b164-nats-codec
note: merged `--no-ff` as 9b9eb36, one commit (f9b7f2a), no conflicts; gate green: **1400 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `cargo tree -p weida-nats-codec` prints exactly one node — the **fifth** codec in the tree with an empty `[dependencies]`, and the rule of 0013 §4.3 has now held for ZMTP, SP, MQTT, AMQP and NATS alike. All twelve verbs, 47 crate tests plus 8 golden vectors and 4 fuzz-smoke tests, with the fuzz crate its own workspace as the others are. **The payload is never scanned for a terminator**: the declared count comes off the control line, the body is taken by length, and the trailing CRLF is required at exactly the expected offset — with a test whose payload contains a CRLF in the middle, which is the test that makes the claim mean something.
note: three of this codec's bounds exist because **the protocol bounds nothing** there, and each is a caller argument on `Limits` checked from the declared count before any allocation: a header block declares no entry count at all (`a:\r\n` is four octets, so a 1 MiB block can ask for a quarter-million vector slots), a JSON array announces no length so `connect_urls` is capped while its elements are read, and `MAX_JSON_DEPTH` = 8 exists only so an unknown `INFO` field carrying an object can be **stepped over without recursing a peer into the stack**. The four `DEFAULT_*` values are documented as starting values rather than caps, which is the distinction that matters when the caller can raise them.
note: one published disagreement is recorded rather than silently resolved, which is the right instinct: `max_control_line` is documented as 1024 in the client protocol reference's error table and compiled as **4096** in `nats-server`. The crate names the larger and says why in the constant's doc comment — this bound applies to lines a *server* sends us, so the implementation's number is the one that decides whether we can read a real server at all. It belongs in `docs/libraries/nats.md` (B-169) and possibly in the sheet (B-174), and both items now have it waiting.

### B-165 — The client: `INFO`, `CONNECT`, TLS and the pings
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done fca6b72 | needs: [B-164]
acceptance: `crates/nats/weida-nats` on `weida-runtime`: the server's `INFO` read first, `CONNECT` answered with the selected capabilities and one of token, user and password, JWT, or an NKey signature over the server's nonce; TLS completed before ordinary traffic where `INFO` requires it; `PING`/`PONG` in both directions with the number of unanswered pings bounded rather than infinite; later asynchronous `INFO` handled outside the handshake, including `connect_urls` and the `ldm: true` drain notice; and the server's `max_payload` enforced locally so an oversized publish fails before the wire.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b165-nats-connection
note: merged `--no-ff` as fca6b72 — **one commit (6d70c00) carrying B-165, B-166 and B-167 together**, 7364 insertions over 16 files, and the workspace manifest is **in it**: `"crates/nats/weida-nats"` in `members` and `weida-nats` in `[workspace.dependencies]` land with the code, which is the rule this run set after the ungated-crate episode. Gate green here: **1573 tests** (1491 + 82), 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`; `weida-nats` itself is 40 lib + 18 handshake + 12 subscriptions + 10 request-reply + 2 doctests. `cargo tree -p weida-nats` reaches `weida-core`, `weida-nats-codec`, `weida-runtime`, `tokio`, `tokio-rustls` and **no `weida`, no `weida-protocol`**.
note: three decisions worth keeping. **NKey and JWT nonce signing is a caller-supplied closure rather than a dependency**, so the crate has *no cryptographic dependency at all* — the same rule `weida-amqp` applied to trust anchors, and the right one: key material is the application's. **There is no reconnect loop**, for the reason the sheet gives rather than by omission. And **`NoResponders` (the 503) stays distinct from `RequestTimeout`**, because "nobody is listening" and "nobody answered in time" are different facts about the system and collapsing them is how a client teaches its users to retry the wrong thing.

kind: code | size: 90 | status: done fca6b72 | needs: [B-165]
acceptance: `SUB` with a client-chosen `sid` and an optional queue group, `UNSUB` with and without a message count, `MSG`/`HMSG` dispatched to the subscription that asked for it, and subject matching where `*` is exactly one token and `>` is one or more trailing tokens and must be last — `orders.*` matching `orders.created` and not `orders.eu.created`, `orders.>` matching both; two subscribers in one queue group receive one copy between them per publication while an ordinary subscriber beside them receives its own, which is the test that separates a group from a subject.

kind: code | size: 60 | status: done fca6b72 | needs: [B-166]

### B-168 — Interop against `nats-server`
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 248cd7c | needs: [B-167]
acceptance: publish, subscribe, queue groups and request-reply against `nats-server` started through the process supervisor with a `ready` condition and stopped in the same item on success and on failure alike, with an `async-nats` peer on the other side of that server for every case so the exchange is ours against an independent client rather than ours against ourselves; where the `nats-server` binary is absent the whole file is `#[ignore]` with the install command in its doc comment ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2), and the item states plainly that there is no pure-Rust substitute — an `async-nats` peer is a client and needs the same server — so absence means no interop rather than a weaker one.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b168-nats-interop
note: merged `--no-ff` as 248cd7c, one commit (1ee3433), 862 insertions; gate green: **1668 tests**, 0 failed, **25 ignored**, fmt, clippy in the workspace configuration and `-p weida-nats --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **`nats-server` is not reachable on this machine and nothing in the file has been run** — I confirmed it here: `which nats-server` and `which nats` are both empty, and docker and podman are absent too — so all **8 tests are `#[ignore]`d** behind one `needs_nats_server!()` macro carrying four install routes, and the module documentation says so in the same words B-162's says about RabbitMQ. The item's own acceptance asked for that honesty and got it.
note: **the NATS-specific half of the honesty is the part worth keeping**, and it is stated where a reader will hit it: there is **no pure-Rust substitute**, so absence means *no interop at all* rather than a weaker one. `fe2o3-amqp` ships an `acceptor` — a foreign *server* — which is why AMQP interop runs anywhere; `async-nats` is a **client**, and two clients cannot talk to each other, because in Core NATS the whole of routing, queue-group selection and request-reply lives in the server. So `async-nats` is the independent peer on the far side of a real server and never a stand-in for one, and the documentation says explicitly that the handshake, subscription and request-reply suites are assertions about **the bytes this client writes**, checked against the protocol reference, and **not** evidence that a real server accepts them. Distinguishing "verified against a peer" from "verified against a document" is the distinction most interop chapters quietly lose.
note: what the eight would establish is written where each test is, and two of them are designed rather than assembled: the wildcard test's point is that **the server and this client's own `subject::matches` agree**, and the queue-group test holds one subscriber of *each* client with 20 jobs and asserts **the count rather than the split**, because a queue group is "one of the set" and not a load-balancing promise. The server would start on a readiness condition read off its own "Server is ready" line rather than a sleep, and stop in `Drop` so a panicking test leaves nothing behind. Two dependency notes, both checked: `async-nats` brings `tokio-rustls` and `tokio-websockets` which resolve to the **same rustls 0.23** already in the graph — no second TLS stack — and `cargo tree -p weida-nats --no-dev-dependencies` reaches neither `weida` nor `weida-protocol`; `futures-util` arrives as a dev-dependency for one reason, that `async-nats`'s `Subscriber` is a `Stream` while ours is an inherent method **on purpose**.

### B-169 — The parity table against Core NATS
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done 78f35e2 | needs: [B-168]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/nats.md` in the shape `docs/libraries/zmq.md` set: every client verb, the two subject wildcards, queue groups, request-reply with inboxes, the authentication mechanisms and the transports, each present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason; JetStream, clusters, gateways and leaf nodes are named absent as server features with what a client would need to reach them, and the client/broker line of [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) is stated once.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b169-nats-parity
note: merged `--no-ff` as 78f35e2, one commit (5a7dd6f): `docs/libraries/nats.md` new at 217 lines plus its index row, docs-only by the diff, and the code is unchanged since B-168's green gate at 1668 tests. **11 sections**, one occurrence of "partial" and it is not a row claiming it. **The thing to read first is §10: clause 4 of 0013 §4.7 is recorded as `NOT met`.** "Interop in both roles against an independent implementation" cannot be claimed — `nats-server` is absent and `async-nats` is a client, so there is no substitute — and rather than softening the clause or dropping it, the document says "NOT met, and stated rather than worked around" and names exactly what would change it: install the binary and run the `--ignored` suite. **That makes this the first library document in the repository with five of six**, and it is the honest number. §1 goes further and splits every claim into two kinds — read from the protocol reference and asserted against a scripted server that replays its byte sequences, or read from the code — saying in those words that the 40 unit plus 40 scripted tests are "assertions about the bytes this client writes and reads, not evidence that a real server accepts them".
note: two rows are judgement rather than transcription and both are right. **JetStream is absent but the row says it adds no verb** — it is `$JS.API` request/reply over the same twelve, with acknowledgements as payloads — so a caller learns the *shape* of what is missing rather than only its name. And **clusters are "absent as a feature, present as the one thing a client sees"**, because `INFO.connect_urls` is retained and readable and that is the whole client-visible surface of a cluster. The subject rules include the four degenerate forms **refused rather than normalised**, the 503 stays distinct from a timeout in the table as it is in the code, and the bounds table is marked the protocol's or ours line by line.
note: **this closes W4 — B-154..B-169, sixteen items: two codecs, two client libraries, two interop harnesses and two parity tables.** Its bindings (B-170..B-173) remain `ready` and depend only on `weida-py-core`, which is on main from W1's B-111.

Python ran here: `develop.sh` → **17 passed**, `package.sh` built `weida_amqp-0.1.0-cp39-abi3-manylinux_2_34_x86_64.whl` and ran one accepted transfer from a fresh venv with cargo, rustc and maturin off `PATH`. No index conflict: the `amqp-py.md` row landed after `amqp.md` in the table I had just reordered by Phase B, which is where it belongs.
note: **the acceptance said "against the peer of B-162", and W4 made that literal rather than convenient**: `crates/amqp/weida-amqp/examples/fe2o3_peer.rs` runs `fe2o3-amqp` 0.17 as a child process, so three of the seventeen Python tests exchange with a **foreign implementation** — send-to-`accepted` on both surfaces and a delivery settled from Python — rather than with a script of ours. A binding tested only against its own library proves the argument conversion; this proves the protocol.
note: **two library changes came with it, and the first is a public-API decision worth reading.** `weida_amqp::Error` **drops `#[non_exhaustive]`**, with the reasoning written at the type: a binding that maps every variant onto its own vocabulary can only be checked for drift if a new variant is a compile error downstream, and `#[non_exhaustive]` forces a wildcard arm that would swallow it into the base class. The cost — adding a variant becomes a breaking change — is the honest description of adding a failure a caller may have to handle. Verified at `error.rs:94-102`. Second, `connection::State::refusal()` is new and `Connection::begin` uses it, so a connection the peer closed with `amqp:resource-limit-exceeded` reports **that condition** instead of "the connection is gone" — which is what let the condition-as-a-class test fail honestly instead of passing for the wrong reason.
note: **`weida_nats::Error` is still `#[non_exhaustive]` on main** (`crates/nats/weida-nats/src/error.rs:43`), so the two libraries disagree until B-172 lands the same change. I checked rather than took the announcement's word for it; flagged to W4, and it resolves itself with the NATS binding.

### B-172 — `weida-nats-py`: the asyncio surface
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 48c71cb | needs: [B-111, B-168]
acceptance: `crates/nats/weida-nats-py` on `weida-py-core`: connect, publish with optional headers, subscribe returning an async iterator of messages with subject, `sid`, reply-to and payload, queue-group membership, `unsubscribe` with a message count, and `request` with a mandatory timeout that raises rather than hangs — the no-responder status among the exception classes; the round trip runs against the server B-168 supervises.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b172-nats-py-asyncio
note: in flight beside B-174 rather than after it: W4's announcement of B-170 cited "the same decision the NATS binding made for `weida_nats::Error`", which is work on `b172-nats-py`, so both are running and B-174 is W4's own. Confirmed with W4 on merging B-170.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 48c71cb together with B-173 — one branch (`b172-nats-py`, tip 2d2e08b, two commits) from a second worktree of this repository, so no rebase was needed: the objects were already here. Gate green on the standing gate: **1678 tests**, 0 failed, 37 ignored; fmt; clippy workspace, `-p weida --no-default-features`, `-p weida-nats --no-default-features`; rustdoc `--workspace` with `-D warnings`. Python here: `develop.sh` → **36 passed, 2 skipped**, the two skipped being the interop pair that needs the `nats-server` nobody has. **It closes the disagreement I flagged on B-170**: `weida_nats::Error` drops `#[non_exhaustive]` with the reasoning written at the type, matching `weida_amqp::Error`, so the two libraries agree again and a variant added to either is a compile error in its binding.
note: **the extra doc configuration found a pre-existing break, which is B-184's second sighting in two items.** `RUSTDOCFLAGS="-D warnings" cargo doc -p weida-nats --no-default-features --no-deps` fails with two unresolved intra-doc links, **both older than this merge**: `crate::Connection::connect_tls` (`crates/nats/weida-nats/src/error.rs:109`) and `[`upgrade_tls`]` (`crates/nats/weida-nats/src/transport.rs:26`) — both naming `#[cfg(feature = "tls")]` items, so the workspace run with `tls` on has never seen them. They arrived with the NATS client (B-165..B-167) and survived four items. I did **not** hold these items for it: their own gate is green and the defect is neither theirs nor reachable by the gate as written. Asked W4 for the two-line fix on its live branch; tracked as **B-186** so it cannot be lost.
note: **`crates/nats/weida-nats-py` ships no `package.sh` and no `smoke.py`**, where `weida-zmq-py`, `weida-mqtt-py` and `weida-amqp-py` all do — so this is the one binding of four whose wheel has never been built, installed into a clean virtualenv and run with the Rust toolchain off `PATH`. `develop.sh` exists and passes. Not an acceptance failure (B-172 does not name packaging) but a parity gap in the one claim these bindings exist to make; filed as **B-187**.

### B-174 — Research: what the AMQP and NATS implementations taught their sheets
kind: research | size: 45 | status: done bca987e | needs: [B-162, B-168]
acceptance: [research/amqp10.md](research/amqp10.md) and [research/nats.md](research/nats.md) updated with what building the two clients and running them against foreign peers established, each in its own sheet's protocol-native vocabulary and with no sentence mentioning weida ([research/README.md](research/README.md) rule 1), every addition attributed to a new numbered source naming the peer versions and what was observed; the AMQP sheet's "known incompatibilities" list gains what the RabbitMQ run measured, and every claim the runs could **not** settle is left standing as an open question rather than quietly resolved.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b174-amqp-nats-sheet-update
note: merged `--no-ff` as bca987e, two commits (1ed0108), four files: `docs/research/amqp10.md` +24, `docs/research/nats.md` +12, `docs/research/README.md` and one code fix. Gate green here at **1678 tests**, 0 failed, 37 ignored — **after one re-run**, because the first pass hit B-182's `survey` flake at `survey.rs:104` (`discarded_late()` left 0, right 1), the unowned NNG flake W2 left behind. fmt, clippy workspace and `-p weida --no-default-features`, rustdoc `--workspace` and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`. **I checked both sheets' rule 1 by counting: zero occurrences of "weida" in either file**, and read the three new source records in full rather than their summaries.
note: **the acceptance's hardest clause was "every claim the runs could not settle is left standing as an open question rather than quietly resolved", and both sheets honour it in bold.** `amqp10.md` [67] is a measurement record of the shape I asked for — peer, version, date, platform, rustc, transport, the peer's feature set (`acceptor` on, `transaction` off), eight scenarios, and the 8 KiB split attributed to an acceptor built with `max_frame_size(1024)`. RabbitMQ's four published non-supports get their own bullet at §13 saying **"Not measured, and still only documentation"**, with the open question named: whether each non-support is a refusal a client can read or a silent substitution. `nats.md` [30] states in bold that **no `nats-server` binary was available** and that `nats`, `docker` and `podman` were absent too, so nothing attributed to it is an observation of a server — the additions are the places the reference is silent and an implementer must choose, which is the honest scope and the same verdict B-169's parity table reached from the other side.
note: **the best single finding is at §1 and it is an interop one:** a `flow` arrived for a handle that had just been cleanly detached, and the specification's unattached-handle and errant-link remedies both read as "end the session" — so an implementation that does not separate a detach race from a fault kills healthy sessions. Beside it: `rcv-settle-mode=second` **honoured** by this peer, which demotes the incompatibility list's entry from "implementations refuse this" to "these three brokers refuse this"; a duplicate link name **refused** rather than stolen, against Part 2's steal; terminus expiry echoed; the coordinator capability discoverable at attach. And [31] of `nats.md` is the move I would copy: reading `nats-server`'s own `server/sublist.go` turned three claims that would have been `[inference]` into cited facts, including one the documentation only implies — **a token after `>` makes a subject invalid at the server** rather than matching anything.
note: **one code commit rides along and it deletes a vacuous assertion**: the transactional probe asserted "no capability starting with `amqp:txn:`", which could never fail — Part 4's coordinator capabilities are `amqp:local-transactions` and its four relatives, while `amqp:txn:` prefixes the transactional *state* and error conditions. It now checks the five real names, still passes, and the sheet's bullet says which prefix is which. A test that cannot fail is worse than no test, and W4 found this one by writing the sentence that had to cite it.
note: **W4 closes here: B-154..B-174, twenty-one items** — the AMQP 1.0 codec and client with interop against a pure-Rust peer, the NATS codec and client with its harness, two parity tables, four Python surfaces and both sheet updates. **That is all sixty-six filed items of [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) merged across four workstreams.** Left behind, all filed: B-182 (W2's survey flake, unowned), B-183 and B-185 (the two missing binding parity documents), B-184 (the gate's doc configurations), B-186 (two `cfg`-ed doc links in `weida-nats`, the tree's last known red) and B-187 (`weida-nats-py`'s missing wheel proof).
